# Sūrah Muḥammad (Chapter 47) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sūrah Named After the Messenger It Names**

Thirty-eight verses long, revealed in Madinah, Sūrah Muḥammad carries a name of extraordinary rarity in the Qur'an: it is one of only four places in the entire revealed text where the Prophet ﷺ is mentioned by his own personal name — *"and believe in what has been sent down unto Muhammad—and it is the truth from their Lord"* (47:2). The other three are 3:144 (*"Muhammad is only a messenger; messengers have passed away before him"*), 33:40 (*"Muhammad is not the father of any of your men, but the Messenger of God and the Seal of the Prophets"*), and 48:29 (*"Muhammad is the Messenger of God"*). Four occurrences out of more than six thousand verses. The Qur'an addresses its bearer constantly — *"O Prophet,"* *"O Messenger,"* *"O you enwrapped,"* *"O you covered"* — yet names him almost never, and when it does, it does so in contexts of definition: defining his mortality, defining his office, defining the finality of his mission, and here, in Sūrah 47, defining the object of belief itself. Belief in God is not complete, the verse says, unless it includes belief *in what has been sent down unto Muhammad*. The name is not an honorific decoration; it is a doctrinal coordinate. A chapter that will spend its length examining who is sincere and who is not, whose deeds count and whose do not, opens by planting in the ground the standard against which all sincerity is measured.

The sūrah is also known by a second name in the early tradition: *al-Qitāl*, "Fighting," taken from verse 20's rebuke of those in whose hearts is a disease and who recoil when a definitive sūrah mentioning fighting is revealed. Al-Suyūṭī records both names in his *al-Itqān*. That a single chapter should be called both "Muḥammad" and "Fighting" is itself instructive, and the sūrah's interior logic explains why: the allegiance to the Messenger and the willingness to act upon that allegiance are treated here as one indivisible reality. The hypocrites of Madinah, whom the sūrah dissects with surgical precision, professed the name and flinched at the cost. The chapter is therefore an extended examination of the gap between profession and performance — the gap in which, the sūrah insists, a whole life can be lost.

**All Madinan, with One Verse of the Road**

Scholars are unanimous that Muḥammad is a Madinan sūrah. A minority position, transmitted through al-Ālūsī and Ibn al-Jawzī, holds that the sūrah is early Madinan with the single exception of verse 13, which is reported to have been revealed during the Hijrah itself — in the hours after the Prophet ﷺ left Makkah, before he entered Madinah, and therefore technically belonging to the Makkan period by the criterion of place. The report is poignant and worth recording in full, because it gives the sūrah one verse with a biography attached. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī transmit that after the Prophet ﷺ departed from Makkah he turned back toward the city and said:

> *"By God, you are the most beloved land to God and the most beloved land to me. Were it not that your people had expelled me from you, I would not have left you."*

Then came the verse: *"And how many a town greater in strength than thy town which cast thee out have We destroyed, having none then to help them?"* (47:13). The consolation is addressed to a man standing on the road with his home behind him and his future unformed: the cities that expelled God's messengers were greater in power than this one, and they were destroyed. Whatever the precision of the occasion, the placement of the verse inside a Madinan sūrah performs a structural function. It stands at the hinge of the chapter — after the discussion of the two destinies (vv. 11–12) and before the examination of the hypocrites' inner state (vv. 14–30) — and it inserts into a Madinan argument a Makkan memory: the pain of expulsion, and the divine precedent that expulsion is not the last word.

**The Architecture of the Chapter**

The sūrah moves in a clear sequence, and seeing the sequence is seeing the argument.

*First* (vv. 1–3), the two destinies are stated in their essence: the disbelievers who turn away from God's path — their deeds are made to go astray; the believers who act righteously and believe in what was sent down to Muhammad — their evil deeds are covered over and their state set aright. The reason is given: one group follows falsehood (*bāṭil*), the other follows truth from their Lord (*ḥaqq*). Thus God sets forth for mankind *their likenesses* — *amthālahum*.

*Second* (vv. 4–7), an exhortation to fighting, with the detailed legislation of the battlefield and of captives, closing with the promise that those slain in God's way will not have their deeds made to go astray, that God will guide them, set their state aright, and admit them into a Garden *having made it known to them* — followed by the sūrah's most quoted covenant: *"If you help God, He will help you and make firm your steps."*

*Third* (vv. 8–11), the state of the disbelievers: wretchedness, the nullification of deeds, the reason (they were averse to what God sent down), the invitation to travel and look at the ruined towns, and the closing verdict — God is Master of those who believe, and the disbelievers have no master.

*Fourth* (vv. 12–15), the contrast of final ends, culminating in the sūrah's great parable of the Garden with its four rivers and its counterpart of boiling liquid that tears apart the bowels.

*Fifth* (vv. 16–30), the longest movement, a sustained anatomization of the hypocrites and the diseased heart: those who listen and then ask "What did he say?"; those whose hearts God has sealed; the awaited Hour coming suddenly; the command to know *lā ilāha illā Allāh*; those who wish for a sūrah and faint when fighting is mentioned; those who would work corruption and sever the womb; the locked hearts; those who turn back after guidance became clear; the whispered alliance with those averse to revelation; the angels striking faces and backs; the rancor that God will expose; the tone of speech by which they will be known.

*Sixth* (vv. 31–35), the trial: God will test until He knows who strives and who is patient; the opponents of the Messenger will not harm God in the least; obey God and obey the Messenger and let not your deeds be in vain; those who die in disbelief will not be forgiven; do not falter and call for peace while you have the upper hand.

*Seventh* (vv. 36–38), the concluding turn from the battlefield to the purse: this life is play and diversion; God will not ask you for your wealth; were He to ask and press you, you would be miserly; behold, you are called to spend, and whoever is miserly is miserly against himself; God is the Rich and you are the poor; and if you turn away, He will replace you with a people who will not be your likes.

Read as a whole, the sūrah is a descending spiral of tests, each one narrower than the last and therefore harder: first the test of allegiance (vv. 1–3), then the test of the body in battle (vv. 4–7, 35), then the test of the tongue in the assembly (vv. 16, 30), then the test of the heart in secret (vv. 19, 26, 29), and finally the test of the hand on the wealth (vv. 36–38). The one who passes all of them is the one whose deeds are not made to go astray.

**The Refrain of Nullified Deeds**

Five times in thirty-eight verses the sūrah returns to a single verb-pair, and this repetition is the chapter's spine. *Aḍalla aʿmālahum* — "He made their deeds go astray" — in verses 1 and 8. *Aḥbaṭa aʿmālahum* — "He made their deeds come to naught" — in verses 9, 28, and 32. And against them, one negation of the negation: *"And as for those who are slain in the way of God, He will not make their deeds go astray"* (47:4), and *"He will not deprive you of your deeds"* (47:35).

The two Arabic verbs are not synonyms, and the commentators distinguished them. *Aḍalla*, from *ḍ-l-l*, means to cause to stray, to lose, to go missing — as one loses a camel in the desert, or a traveler loses the road. A deed that is "made to go astray" is not destroyed so much as *lost*: it wanders off from its owner and never arrives at its destination. *Aḥbaṭa*, from *ḥ-b-ṭ*, originally describes the swelling and ruin of an animal that has grazed on a poisonous plant — the belly distends, the animal appears fat and healthy, and then it dies. It is one of the most vividly physical verbs in the Qur'anic vocabulary of failure. A deed that is *ḥābiṭ* looks substantial from the outside and is rotten from within.

Placed together, the two verbs describe the whole pathology of religious fraud: some deeds get lost on the road to God, and some deeds swell with an appearance of life while containing death. The sūrah is, in this sense, a treatise on the *reality* of human action as distinct from its *appearance* — the intrinsic connection, as the introduction to the source commentary notes, between deeds and the state of the heart that produced them. Verse 33 draws the practical conclusion in a single negative command: *"let not your deeds be in vain"* — *wa lā tubṭilū aʿmālakum*. The verb *abṭala*, a third term in the family, means to render void. The believer is thus instructed to guard his deeds against three distinct kinds of ruin: getting lost, swelling with poison, and being voided — and the sūrah will show that all three have the same cause.

**The Diseased Heart and the Sealed Heart and the Locked Heart**

Alongside the refrain of deeds runs a refrain of hearts, and here too the sūrah is precise in its gradations. Verse 16 speaks of hearts God has *sealed* — *ṭabaʿa ʿalā qulūbihim*, from the root *ṭ-b-ʿ*, to stamp, to press a seal upon wax. Verses 20 and 29 speak of hearts in which there is a *disease* — *fī qulūbihim maraḍ*. Verse 24 asks whether hearts have *locks* upon them — *am ʿalā qulūbin aqfāluhā*, with the definite noun *al-aqfāl* and the attached pronoun making the phrase strikingly concrete: "their locks," as though each diseased heart carries its own padlock. And elsewhere the Qur'an speaks of *rust* — *"No indeed; rather what they have earned has covered their hearts"* (83:14), the word *rāna* meaning a film that gathers over a surface.

Ibn al-Jawzī, as the source commentary notes, arranged these in an order of severity: the locked heart is the most severely afflicted, beyond the reach of guidance; the rusted and the sealed hearts are less diseased. The gradation matters pastorally. A Muslim reading the sūrah is meant to ask not "which of these people am I?" in a spirit of condemnation of others, but "which condition is my own heart in?" — and the answer is discoverable, because the sūrah gives the diagnostic test in verse 24: *do they not contemplate the Qur'an?* The heart that no longer receives meaning from the recitation is the heart with a lock upon it. This is why al-Ghazzālī, in the *Iḥyāʾ*, listed four obstacles that prevent a listener from understanding the Qur'an — preoccupation with the correctness of pronunciation alone, dogmatic partisanship for a school, persistence in sin and pride, and the assumption that the transmitted commentaries exhaust the Book's meaning. The obstacles are not intellectual limitations; they are locks, and their keys are moral.

**God's Knowledge as the Sūrah's Quiet Guarantor**

Four times the sūrah interrupts its narrative with a declaration of divine knowledge, and the effect is cumulative. *"God knows your coming and going, and your abode"* (47:19). *"And God knows their keeping of secrets"* (47:26). *"And God knows your deeds"* (47:30). *"And We shall test you until We know those among you who strive and those who are patient, and We shall test your proclamations"* (47:31). The last of these raises the classical theological question of how God can come to "know" what He already knows, and the answer given unanimously by the commentators — Ibn al-Jawzī, al-Rāzī, and before them the whole tradition — is that the knowing here means *making manifest in existence* what was already known in eternity: the test does not inform God, it reveals the tested. God's knowledge is not increased; the servant's reality is displayed, to himself, to his community, and on the Day when the secrets of the breasts are brought out.

This is a doctrine with enormous psychological consequence, and the sūrah exploits it deliberately. The hypocrite's entire strategy is concealment: secret consultations (v. 26), a tone of speech that differs from the heart (v. 30), an outward obedience covering an inward rancor (v. 29). Against that strategy the sūrah sets four declarations of knowledge, and then one declaration of exposure: *"Or do those in whose hearts is a disease suppose that God will not expose their rancor?"* (47:29). The word *aḍghān* — rancors, deep-seated grudges — is plural and denotes hatreds that have been buried long enough to harden. The sūrah's promise is that the burial is temporary. This is not a threat only; it is, for the sincere, a profound relief: nothing you have done in secret for God is lost, and nothing you have concealed from God is hidden.

**Why This Sūrah Matters Now**

Sūrah Muḥammad addresses a condition that is not confined to seventh-century Madinah: the condition of belonging to a community of belief while privately withholding from it. Every age produces its own version of the man who listens to the sermon and then asks a companion outside, "What did he say?" — present in body, absent in attention, unmarked in the register but unreached by the message. Every age produces those who pray for a decisive command and then pale when it arrives, who want religion to require of them only what they have already decided to give. Every age produces the miser who is miserly against himself without knowing it.

The sūrah's remedy is stated in its nineteenth verse, which stands at the exact center of its argument and is the pivot on which everything before and after turns: *"Know, then, that there is no god but God, and ask forgiveness for thy sin and for the believing men and the believing women."* Knowledge of divine unity, followed immediately by the confession of one's own need for forgiveness, followed immediately by intercession for other believing men and women — the three acts in that order. Ibn Taymiyyah's entire intellectual project can be read as an elaboration of the first clause: that *tawḥīd* is not a proposition assented to but a reality that reorganizes the will. The second clause prevents that reorganization from becoming self-righteousness. The third prevents it from becoming self-absorption.

The commentary that follows treats each of the thirty-eight verses as an independent discourse, in strict order, exactly as mandated: every verse is quoted individually from the sūrah's canonical text and then expanded through Arabic language and morphology, the classical commentators — al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Zamakhsharī, al-Rāzī, Ibn ʿAṭiyyah, Ibn al-Jawzī, al-Ālūsī, Ibn ʿĀshūr, al-Saʿdī — authenticated Prophetic reports with their collections identified, the logic of the teaching, and its bearing upon ordinary life. Verses the source treats in groups (20–21, and to a degree 36–38) are separated and given full individual treatment. Nothing has been shortened because the chapter is long. The sūrah's own warning against faltering — *"So do not falter and call for peace while you have the upper hand"* — is honored in the manner of the reading: unhurried and complete, from *"Those who disbelieve and turn from the way of God"* to *"and they will not be the likes of you."*

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:1

> **Those who disbelieve and turn from the way of God—He has made their deeds go astray.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Opening Verdict of the Chapter**

The sūrah named after the Messenger begins not with him but with his opposites, and not with their punishment but with the fate of their *deeds*. This is a deliberate and revealing choice. Before Sūrah Muḥammad legislates anything about fighting (v. 4), consoles anyone about expulsion (v. 13), or exposes any hypocrite (vv. 16, 29–30), it establishes the metaphysical ground rule that will govern everything that follows: actions have consequences in an order of reality the actor cannot see, and the consequence of turning away from God is that one's own actions turn away from one. The verse is short — five words in Arabic — and it is the thesis statement of the chapter.

*Alladhīna kafarū wa ṣaddū ʿan sabīli Allāhi aḍalla aʿmālahum.* The structure is a conditional-free declaration: two verbs describing the disbelievers, one verb describing what God does in response. There is no "if" and no "then"; the sentence is spoken as settled fact, in the past tense, about a matter still unfolding in history. Al-Ṭabarī reads the whole verse as a report of what God has already done to the works of those who rejected Him and blocked His path: their deeds have been made to stray, wasted, void of the benefit their owners expected.

**Ṣadda ʿan: Turning Away and Blocking Others**

The verb *ṣadda ʿan* — "to turn from" — carries a double sense that the commentators were careful to preserve, and both senses are active here. Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī in his *Mufradāt alfāẓ al-Qurʾān* and al-Zamakhsharī in the *Kashshāf* note that the form is used both transitively and intransitively: it can mean *to turn oneself away from* something, and it can mean *to turn others away from* it, to block, to obstruct, to stand in the door. The Qur'an uses both meanings, sometimes in one breath: *"Those who disbelieve and turn [people] from the way of God"* (22:25), and *"And they turn away from it and forbid it"* (6:26).

Taking the two meanings together — as the source commentary does — the verse describes a disposition rather than a single act. The disbeliever is not merely a private skeptic who declines to believe; he is an obstacle. His unbelief has a social direction. It works upon others: by argument, by ridicule, by persecution, by the silent pressure of example, by the institutional power of a tribe or a state that makes belief costly for other people. Makkah in the years before the Hijrah was exactly this: a city where the message was not simply rejected but actively suppressed, where believers were tortured on hot sand, where a boycott starved a clan for three years in a ravine, where the Prophet ﷺ himself was driven out. The verse speaks into that situation and names it precisely.

This double sense carries a logical weight worth stating plainly. A person who merely disbelieves harms himself. A person who also blocks others harms them, and multiplies the harm geometrically, because every person he obstructs may in turn obstruct others. Islam therefore treats the obstruction of guidance as among the gravest of sins — heavier, in a sense, than ordinary sin, because ordinary sin consumes the sinner's own capital while obstruction spends the capital of other souls. The Prophet ﷺ said, in the report recorded by Muslim:

> *"Whoever sets a good precedent (sunnah) in Islam will have its reward and the reward of those who act upon it after him, without that detracting from their rewards in any respect; and whoever sets an evil precedent in Islam will bear its burden and the burden of those who act upon it after him, without that detracting from their burdens in any respect."* (Muslim 1017, from Jarīr ibn ʿAbd Allāh)

The same principle, inverted, is what *ṣadda ʿan sabīl Allāh* names. The blocker of good has not merely committed a private fault; he has inaugurated a chain.

**Sabīl Allāh: The Road, and Who Owns It**

*Sabīl* — way, road, path — is added here to God's own name: *sabīl Allāh*, "the way of God." The genitive construction is important. The road does not belong to the travelers. It is not a human invention, a cultural product, or the property of any party; it is God's road, laid down by Him, leading to Him. That single phrase answers a question modern readers bring to every religious claim: by what right does anyone tell me how to live? The Qur'anic answer is not "because we are many" or "because it works" but "because the road is His." One does not legislate for a highway one does not own; one follows the signs of its builder.

The phrase also appears repeatedly in the sūrah's discussion of striving and spending — *"those slain in the way of God"* (47:4), *"help God"* (47:7), *"spend in the way of God"* (47:38) — so that *sabīl Allāh* becomes the sūrah's organizing image: there is a road, there are those who walk it, those who block it, and those who spend their lives and wealth upon it. The hypocrites, whom the sūrah will examine at length, are the people who stand at the side of the road talking about it without walking.

**Aḍalla Aʿmālahum: The Lost Deed**

The verb *aḍalla* is the intensive-causative of *ḍalla*, from the root *ḍ-l-l*, whose basic sense is to stray, to be lost, to go missing. The Qur'an uses the root for the lost animal and the lost traveler: *"And He found you lost (ḍāllan) and guided"* (93:7); Moses says of himself, *"I did it then, while I was of those astray"* (26:20). To say that God *aḍalla aʿmālahum* — "He made their deeds go astray" — is to borrow the image of a man leading a camel through the desert and waking to find the animal gone: the deed has wandered off from its owner and will not arrive where it was meant to arrive.

The commentators unpacked this into three distinct operations, and the source commentary gathers them well.

*First*: their evil deeds will not achieve their intended effect. The plot fails, the persecution does not extinguish the message, the campaign to turn people from God's road does not close the road. God says elsewhere: *"And they planned, and God planned; and God is the best of planners"* (3:54), and *"They plot a plot, and I plot a plot"* (86:15–16). The disbelievers of Makkah spent twenty years trying to make the Qur'an disappear; the Qur'an outlived every one of them and is recited today by hundreds of millions who have never heard of them.

*Second*: whatever good deeds they performed will bring them no benefit in the Hereafter. This is the hardest clause for the modern conscience, and it deserves an honest treatment rather than an evasion. The Qur'an is explicit that the works of those who die rejecting God will not be counted for them: *"And We shall turn to whatever deeds they did, and We shall make them as dust scattered"* (25:23); *"Those whose efforts have gone astray in the life of this world while they suppose they are doing good"* (18:104). The principle is not that God is stingy with reward — He is described as rewarding a single good deed tenfold to seven hundredfold and beyond — but that a deed is a relational act, and its value depends upon the relation in which it is offered. A gift made in the name of a person who is denied cannot be credited to that person's account. Al-Qurṭubī's treatment of this point is calm and juridical: the condition of acceptance of a deed is faith, as the condition of a building is its foundation; remove the foundation and the structure has nothing to stand on, however beautiful its walls.

*Third*: they mistake evil deeds for good deeds, because the evil of their deeds has been made to seem fair unto them. This is the psychological dimension, and it is the most frightening of the three, because it describes a state in which the person cannot detect his own condition. The Qur'an names the mechanism at 9:37: *"The postponement [of the sacred month] is an increase in disbelief, whereby those who disbelieve are led astray; they make it lawful one year and unlawful another, in order to conform to the number that God made sacred, thus making lawful what God made unlawful. The evil of their deeds is made fair-seeming to them."* The verb *zuyyina* — "made to seem fair, beautified, decorated" — describes a cosmetic operation upon the moral imagination: the thing is not changed, only its presentation. A person in this state does not feel like a sinner; he feels like a reformer, a realist, a man of the world. He feels, in the exact language of 18:104, that he is *doing good*.

Muslim records the Prophet ﷺ describing this condition from the other side — the believer who discovers on the Day of Judgment that his apparent wealth of good works has been spent:

> *"Do you know who the bankrupt (al-muflis) is?"* They said, "The bankrupt among us is the one who has no dirham and no goods." He said: *"The bankrupt of my community is the one who comes on the Day of Resurrection with prayer, fasting, and alms — and comes having reviled this one, slandered that one, consumed the wealth of this one, shed the blood of that one, and beaten this one. So this one is given from his good deeds and that one from his good deeds; and when his good deeds run out before what is owed is settled, their sins are taken and cast upon him, and he is thrown into the Fire."* (Muslim 2581, from Abū Hurayrah)

The *muflis* is not the one without deeds but the one whose deeds have gone astray — dispersed to creditors he did not know he had. The verse in Sūrah Muḥammad applies the same logic at its outermost limit: the person who turned from God's road and turned others from it has contracted debts to every soul he obstructed, and his capital cannot cover them.

**Why the Deed Strays: Sincerity and Conformity**

The Islamic doctrine of the accepted deed is stated in two conditions, and Ibn Taymiyyah formulated them with characteristic precision: a deed is accepted only if it is sincere (*mukhliṣ*) — done for God's face alone — and correct (*muwāfiq*) — done in accordance with what God prescribed. Both conditions fail in the case of verse 1. The disbeliever's deed is not done for God at all, so the first condition fails at the root; and it is not done in accordance with His way, since he has turned from that way, so the second fails too. Al-Fuḍayl ibn ʿIyāḍ (d. 187/803), commenting on *"that He may test you, which of you is best in deed"* (67:2), said that "best" means *"the most sincere and the most correct,"* explaining that a sincere deed that is incorrect is not accepted, and a correct deed that is insincere is not accepted.

The logic is not arbitrary. Consider an ordinary human analogue that anyone recognizes: a subordinate who performs an impressive piece of work but does it in defiance of his employer's instructions, for the credit of a rival firm, and reports it to no one. The work may be excellent in itself. It will not be entered in his employment record, it will not earn him a promotion, and it may well earn him a dismissal. Nothing unjust has happened. The record belongs to the firm, the criteria belong to the firm, and the worker chose to work for someone else. God's ownership of the record of deeds is more absolute than any employer's, and His criteria are not negotiable — but unlike the employer of the analogy, He gives notice of the criteria in advance, in a book, and repeats the notice for twenty-three years.

**The Past Tense and the Open Door**

One grammatical feature of the verse has occupied the commentators and deserves emphasis, because it prevents the verse from becoming a weapon of despair. *Aḍalla aʿmālahum* is in the past tense: "He *has made* their deeds go astray." Al-Qurṭubī and others note that this leaves open the possibility that those who disbelieve may repent in the future and be forgiven. The verse is a statement about the present condition of a present state, not an eternal sentence upon named individuals. God's nullification of the deeds of unbelief is a description of what unbelief does; it is not a decree that any particular human being must remain in unbelief until death.

This reading is confirmed by verse 34 of the same sūrah, which specifies the actual limit: *"Truly those who disbelieve and turn from the way of God, then die while they are disbelievers, God will not forgive them."* The unforgivable element is dying in that state. Until death, the door stands open, and the sūrah's own second verse immediately describes what happens when a person walks through it: the same God who made their deeds go astray *"has absolved them of their evil deeds and set their state aright."* The two verses are meant to be read as one door with two sides.

The most famous illustration of the transition is ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, who came to the Prophet ﷺ to pledge allegiance and drew his hand back, asking first to be forgiven for what had gone before, and received the answer recorded by Muslim:

> *"Do you not know that Islam destroys what preceded it, and that Hijrah destroys what preceded it, and that the ḥajj destroys what preceded it?"* (Muslim 121)

ʿAmr had been one of the most effective obstacles of his generation — he had been sent by Quraysh to the Negus in Abyssinia to have the Muslim emigrants extradited, and he had fought at Uḥud and at the Trench. Everything in verse 1 applied to him in the years before his conversion. Then he entered Islam, and the deed that had gone astray was gathered back, and the man who had tried to obstruct the road became one of its commanders. Verse 1 is not a list of the damned; it is a description of a state, and states change.

**The Mirror in Verse Four**

The verse is constructed to be read against its own counterpart eleven words later in the sūrah's argument. Verse 4 closes: *"And as for those who are slain in the way of God, He will not make their deeds go astray"* — *wa-lladhīna qutilū fī sabīli Allāhi fa-lan yuḍilla aʿmālahum*. The same verb, the same object, negated, with the addition of the emphatic particle *lan*, which in Arabic denies a future absolutely: "He will *never* let their deeds go astray." Between these two clauses the entire sūrah unfolds. Those who turn from the way of God lose their deeds; those who are killed upon the way of God never lose theirs. The two roads have the same name in the two sentences — *sabīl Allāh* — and opposite outcomes, and the difference between them is not success or failure by worldly measurement but direction: whether the life was oriented toward the road or away from it.

There is a lesson in this for anyone who has ever watched an apparently wasted life of faithfulness. The believer who gives his years to prayer, to his family, to honesty in trade, to the care of a sick parent, and who sees none of it rewarded in this world — whose children are ungrateful, whose business fails, whose health declines — is performing deeds that the visible world scores as zero. Verse 1 and verse 4 together state that the visible world is not the scoring system. The deeds have not gone astray. They are in transit.

**The Modern Form of Ṣadd**

It would be a mistake to read the verse only as a description of seventh-century Makkah. *Ṣadd ʿan sabīl Allāh* has modern instruments that the classical commentators could not have imagined, and the sūrah's diagnosis applies to them without adjustment. A culture that makes religious practice socially expensive is performing *ṣadd*; so is an employer who schedules unavoidable obligations across the prayer time; so is a curriculum that presents faith only as pathology; so is a household in which a young person's sincerity is met with mockery rather than questions. None of these involves violence, and all of them involve the turning of souls away from a road. The verse's warning runs in both directions at once: those who block the road have their own deeds blocked, and the blocking is a heavier matter than the person doing it usually supposes.

Conversely, the same verse carries an unspoken promise to those who *open* the road. If obstruction of guidance is a chain of harm that continues after the obstructer's death, facilitation of guidance is a chain of benefit that continues after the facilitator's. The teacher who explains one verse, the parent who answers a child's honest question without ridicule, the friend who makes the mosque less intimidating for a newcomer — each of them has set a precedent in Islam, and its reward runs without diminution from those who act upon it afterward. Verse 1 names the ruin of the closed door. The whole of the sūrah that follows is an invitation to walk through the open one.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:2

> **And those who believe and perform righteous deeds, and believe in what has been sent down unto Muhammad—and it is the truth from their Lord—He has absolved them of their evil deeds and set their state aright.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Other Side of the Same Door**

Verse 1 described a loss; verse 2 describes a recovery, and the symmetry between them is exact and deliberate. Both verses open with *alladhīna* — "those who" — and both close with two verbs whose subject is God. In verse 1: those who disbelieved and turned away, and God made their deeds go astray. In verse 2: those who believed and acted righteously and believed in what was sent down to Muhammad, and God absolved them of their evil deeds and set their state aright. The sūrah has thus, in two sentences, laid out its entire anthropology. Human beings are defined by what they follow — falsehood or truth, as verse 3 will state explicitly — and God is defined by what He does in response. Nothing else in the chapter will add a third category.

There is also a numerical asymmetry worth noticing, because the Qur'an's structures are rarely accidental. The disbelievers are described by two verbs (*kafarū, ṣaddū*) and receive one divine response (*aḍalla*). The believers are described by three verbs (*āmanū, ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāt, āmanū bi-mā nuzzila ʿalā Muḥammad*) and receive two divine responses (*kaffara ʿanhum, aṣlaḥa bālahum*). The path of belief asks more and gives more. It requires assent of the heart, action of the limbs, and specific acceptance of this revelation — and it returns both the removal of the past and the rectification of the future. The path of rejection requires less and returns less. This is the arithmetic of the two roads, and the sūrah will hold to it for thirty-six more verses.

**Āmanū wa ʿAmilū al-Ṣāliḥāt: The Inseparable Pair**

The phrase *"those who believe and perform righteous deeds"* is the most frequently repeated collocation in the Qur'an. It occurs in some fifty places, and the conjunction *wa* joining the two elements has been read by the commentators — al-Zamakhsharī among them — as indicating that neither element stands alone. Faith without works is incomplete; works without faith are unaccepted. The pairing appears in the same order every time, belief first, which is itself a teaching: the act is a consequence of the conviction, not a substitute for it. A person who performs the motions without the conviction is the hypocrite whom this sūrah will spend verses 16 to 30 dissecting, and a person who has the conviction without the motions is the one whom verse 20 will describe as fainting at the mention of fighting.

*Al-ṣāliḥāt* — "the righteous deeds" — is the plural of *ṣāliḥah*, from *ṣ-l-ḥ*, the root ofصلاح, soundness, rightness, fitness, the opposite of *fasād* (corruption), which the sūrah will invoke at verse 22 (*"would you perchance work corruption upon the earth"*). A deed is *ṣāliḥ* when it is sound in itself, fitting to its object, and conducive to the good order of the one who does it. The word is used for food that has not spoiled, for a limb that functions, for a person who is wholesome. Applied to action, it carries the sense of an act that *works* — not in the utilitarian sense of producing pleasure, but in the sense of a joint that fits its socket. The Qur'an's ethic is in this respect closer to medicine than to law: deeds are described as healthy or diseased, nourishing or poisonous, and the sūrah's own verse 12 will say of the disbelievers that they *"eat as cattle eat"* — an image of consumption without nourishment of the higher kind.

**The Second Belief: Why the Qur'an Names Muhammad Here**

The verse asks of the believer something beyond general faith: *"and believe in what has been sent down unto Muhammad."* Why the repetition of *āmanū*? The person who believes in God already believes in revelation. The commentators — al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī — offer complementary answers, and together they make a substantial theological point.

*First*, the repetition specifies the object. General belief in God is not the same as belief in *this* Book. There were, in seventh-century Arabia, many who affirmed a supreme deity, who accepted that God had sent messengers before, and who nonetheless rejected Muhammad ﷺ. The Qur'an's own report of their reasoning is recorded: *"And they say: O thou unto whom the Reminder has been sent down, thou art indeed possessed"* (15:6), and *"Is the Reminder sent down unto him among us?"* (38:8). The second *āmanū* closes that gap: believing in God includes believing that this particular revelation, delivered to this particular man, is from Him.

*Second*, the repetition marks a rank. Al-Rāzī's reading is that the verse describes a progression: belief as such, then the deeds that express it, then belief in the specific revelation that guides those deeds. Each step is a further determination of the first. A person who stops at general theism has not yet arrived at the place where guidance becomes actionable, because action requires particular commands, and particular commands require a particular source.

*Third* — and this is the point the sūrah's very name makes — the naming of Muhammad ﷺ in a verse about the acceptance of deeds establishes that the Messenger is not incidental to the message. He is named in only four verses of the Qur'an (3:144; 33:40; 47:2; 48:29), and each naming performs a definitional work. Here the definition is epistemic: what was sent down *unto Muhammad* is the criterion. The name itself is instructive. *Muḥammad* is the intensive passive participle of *ḥ-m-d*, "to praise" — not simply "praised" but "the one repeatedly and abundantly praised," a form denoting intensity and repetition. Al-Qāḍī ʿIyāḍ devoted his famous *al-Shifāʾ* to the Prophet's rights and attributes, and among the observations gathered in that tradition is that the name describes what the bearer became: praised on earth by his community in every call to prayer and every testimony of faith, and praised in the heavens by the angels. The verse that makes belief in his revelation a condition of absolution attaches that belief to his name.

**The Parenthetical: God Confirms His Own Book**

Between the condition and the reward, the verse inserts a short declarative sentence of its own: *"and it is the truth from their Lord"* — *wa-huwa al-ḥaqqu min rabbihim*. Grammatically this is an interjection (*iʿtirāḍ*), a clause that interrupts the main sentence to affirm something about it. Its function is not informational — God does not need to convince the reader — but testimonial. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the phrase confirms the revelation independently of the believers' belief in it: whether they believe or not, it *is* the truth from their Lord. Their belief adds nothing to its truth; it only connects them to it.

The choice of *Rabb* rather than *Allāh* in this clause is worth pausing upon. *Rabb* is the Name of nurturing lordship — the one who originates, sustains, educates, and brings to completion. The revelation is described as coming *from their Rabb*: from the One who raised them, who knows what they are made of, who has been tending them since before they could speak. This is why the revelation can be trusted as guidance — it comes from the Maker of the thing being guided. A manual written by someone other than the manufacturer may be sincere and even useful; a manual written by the manufacturer is authoritative. The Qur'an presses this argument repeatedly: *"Does He not know, Who created? And He is the Subtle, the Aware"* (67:14).

*Al-ḥaqq* — the truth, the real, the fixed — is one of the Qur'an's central words and also one of God's Names: *"That is because God is the Truth (*al-ḥaqq*), and that what they call upon besides Him is falsehood (*al-bāṭil*)"* (22:62). Verse 3 of this sūrah will pick up exactly this pair: the disbelievers *followed al-bāṭil*, the believers *followed al-ḥaqq from their Lord*. The two words are the two poles between which the entire chapter is stretched. *Bāṭil* is not merely the false in the sense of factually incorrect; it is the void, the unsubstantial, that which has no ground to stand on and therefore perishes — *"Truth has come and falsehood has vanished; truly falsehood is ever vanishing"* (17:81). To follow *bāṭil* is to build on sand, and the sūrah's refrain of nullified deeds is the record of what happens to buildings on sand.

**Kaffara ʿAnhum: Covering, Not Merely Pardoning**

*"He has absolved them of their evil deeds"* renders *kaffara ʿanhum sayyiʾātihim*. The verb *kaffara* comes from *k-f-r*, whose root meaning is *to cover, to conceal, to veil*. The same root produces *kāfir* — the disbeliever, so called because he covers over the truth he recognizes, or covers over God's blessings by refusing to acknowledge them; and *al-kāfir* for the farmer, because he covers the seed with soil; and *kufr* for the night, because it covers the land in darkness; and *kuffār* for the covering of a helmet or the sheathing of a sword. Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī, whose lexical work the source commentary cites, explains that *takfīr* in the religious sense is the covering over of evil deeds such that they are rendered as though they had never been committed.

The distinction between *takfīr* (absolution, covering) and *maghfirah* (forgiveness) drawn by al-Iṣfahānī is subtle and pastorally significant. In forgiveness, the sin is not punished but it remains on record: the servant will see it on the Day of Judgment and know that he was spared. In absolution, the sin is covered over — removed from the register, as it were — so that it is as if it had not occurred. The second is the greater grace, and the sūrah assigns it here to those who believe, act righteously, and accept the revelation. Anyone who has ever been genuinely forgiven for something shameful recognizes the difference between being pardoned and being released from the memory of the offense; the Qur'an promises the second.

The theological basis is the doctrine, stated in the report of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ already cited in verse 1 and confirmed elsewhere in Muslim, that entering Islam wipes out what preceded it. Al-Qurṭubī reads *"He has absolved them of their evil deeds"* in this verse as covering all the evil deeds performed before belief. The Prophet ﷺ also said, in the ḥadīth recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

> *"God has written down the good deeds and the evil deeds."* Then he explained: *"Whoever intends a good deed and does not do it, God writes it with Him as a complete good deed; and whoever intends it and does it, God writes it with Him as ten good deeds, up to seven hundred times, and many times over. And whoever intends an evil deed and does not do it, God writes it with Him as a complete good deed; and whoever intends it and does it, God writes it as one evil deed."* (al-Bukhārī 6491; Muslim 131, from Ibn ʿAbbās)

The disproportion between the multiplication of good and the single counting of evil is itself a form of *takfīr* built into the accounting: the ledger is designed so that mercy outweighs. When verse 2 adds the covering of past evil to that structure, the position of the new believer becomes clear — he begins, in the language of the tradition, with a clean page and a running account in his favor.

**Aṣlaḥa Bālahum: The Rectification of the Inner State**

The second divine response is *"and set their state aright"* — *wa-aṣlaḥa bālahum*. The noun *bāl* is unusual and rich. Al-Ṭūsī, cited in the source commentary, notes that it can mean the state, the condition, the heart, or the mind. Al-Zamakhsharī understands the phrase to mean that God helps them in following the teachings of Islam after embracing the religion — the absolution deals with the past, the rectification with the future. The verb *aṣlaḥa* is the same root *ṣ-l-ḥ* that produces *al-ṣāliḥāt* in the first half of the verse: those who perform sound deeds are themselves made sound. The circle closes.

What does a "rectified state" mean concretely? The commentators point to the interior conditions without which religious practice collapses: a heart that is settled rather than agitated, a mind that can hold a resolution, an intention that does not have to be re-decided every morning, a conscience that functions. Anyone who has watched a person recover from addiction, or from a period of serious moral chaos, recognizes the transformation: it is not only that the bad acts stop, but that the inner machinery starts working again. Sleep becomes possible. Promises become keepable. The person stops needing to lie about small things. This is *iṣlāḥ al-bāl* in ordinary human terms, and the verse attributes it to God's action rather than to the person's effort.

The Qur'an elsewhere describes the same gift in stronger terms: *"Except those who repent, believe, and perform righteous deeds — for those, God will replace their evil deeds with good deeds; and God is ever Forgiving, Merciful"* (25:70), which al-Rāzī connects directly to this verse. Replacement is beyond covering: the site of the old deed becomes the site of a new one. The man who spent his years in fornication becomes the man who spends them in marriage and the raising of children; the woman who used her tongue in slander becomes the one who uses it in remembrance; the trader who cheated becomes the trader whose honesty is a public good. Nothing in the past is erased from memory, but everything in the past is redirected.

**The Psychology of the Verse: Why Both Graces Are Needed**

Consider why the verse promises two things rather than one. Absolution alone would leave the believer forgiven but unchanged — pardoned, and then returned to the same disordered inner life that produced the offenses. Rectification alone would leave the believer improved but burdened — living better, and carrying the weight of everything he used to be. Human beings cannot sustain either condition. A person who is told "you are forgiven" but given no help will fall back, because the causes remain. A person who is told "improve yourself" but given no release will despair, because the record remains. The verse joins the two, and in joining them it describes the complete structure of Islamic soteriology: God removes the liability and God repairs the liability-maker.

This is why the Islamic tradition has always treated conversion and repentance as both a legal event and a therapeutic one. The jurists discuss what is wiped out; the spiritual writers — al-Ghazzālī in the *Iḥyāʾ*, Ibn al-Qayyim in *Madārij al-Sālikīn* — discuss what is rebuilt. Ibn al-Qayyim's central image of the heart as a house that must be emptied before it can be furnished captures the same double operation: *takfīr* empties, *iṣlāḥ al-bāl* furnishes. A believer who receives only the first becomes the complacent convert who thinks his past has been settled and therefore owes nothing; a believer who pursues only the second becomes the exhausted self-improver who has no peace. The sūrah offers both in one breath.

**The Verse as a Test of the Reader**

Read from inside the sūrah's argument, verse 2 is not merely a promise but a diagnostic. It sets three conditions, and every Muslim reader will find that he satisfies them unequally. Belief — yes, in general. Righteous deeds — partially, inconsistently. Belief in what was sent down to Muhammad — and here the sūrah asks the sharpest question of all, the one verses 16 and 24 will press: does one actually *receive* the revelation, or does one sit in its presence and leave asking what was said?

The two divine responses are correspondingly graded. The believer whose faith is nominal will find his evil deeds covered — God's mercy is not withheld from the community of faith — but will not necessarily find his state rectified, because rectification follows the sincerity of the deeds, and insincere deeds do not reshape the heart. This is why the sūrah will keep returning to the theme of *ṣidq*, truthfulness: verse 21, *"were they true to God, it would be better for them"*; verse 31, *"We shall test your proclamations."* The promise of verse 2 is real and available, but it is not automatic. It is a covenant, and covenants have conditions.

The most practical application of the verse is the one the earliest community understood: this is the verse a person recites when he is about to begin again. After a failure, after a lapse, after a year in which nothing went right, the Muslim does not have to construct a new theology of second chances. He has one, in five lines, in the second verse of Sūrah Muḥammad, and it is stated in the past tense as though it had already been done — *"He has absolved them of their evil deeds and set their state aright"* — because in the divine reckoning, what God has promised is more real than what has not yet happened.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:3

> **That is because those who disbelieve follow falsehood and because those who believe follow the truth from their Lord. Thus does God set forth for mankind their likenesses.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Causal Particle: Revelation Explains Itself**

The verse opens with *dhālika bi-anna* — "that is because" — the same explanatory formula that appeared at the head of verse 2's counterpart and that runs through the sūrah (vv. 3, 9, 11, 22-implied, 25-implied, 26, 28, 32). The formula performs a service that deserves to be named explicitly: the Qur'an does not merely pronounce verdicts, it gives reasons. Having stated in verse 1 that the deeds of those who disbelieve have been made to go astray, and in verse 2 that those who believe have been absolved and rectified, the revelation now steps back and says *why*. Al-Ṭabarī reads the whole verse as the justification (*taʿlīl*) of the preceding ruling: God rendered their deeds void and covered the believers' sins *because* of what each group followed.

This structural habit of the Qur'an — decree followed by explanation — has been noted by Ibn Taymiyyah as one of the marks distinguishing divine legislation from arbitrary command. A ruler who issues orders without reasons trains subjects in servility; a Lord who gives reasons trains servants in understanding. The Qur'an repeatedly invites the reader to ask *why*: *"Thus does God make clear unto you His signs, that haply you may reflect"* (2:219); *"that mankind may stand forth in justice"* (57:25). Verse 3 is a small instance of a vast pattern, and the pattern matters because it addresses a permanent human objection to religious obligation: the suspicion that the command exists merely to assert authority. Here the command is grounded in the nature of the thing followed.

**Ittibāʿ: Religion as Following**

Both halves of the verse use the same verb: *ittabaʿa*, from *t-b-ʿ*, to follow, to pursue, to walk behind. The disbelievers *followed* falsehood; the believers *followed* the truth. The Qur'an's choice of this verb rather than one of possession or manufacture is deeply significant. Human beings, on this account, are not primarily originators of truth; they are followers of it. The religious question is not "what have you constructed?" but "what are you walking behind?"

This is why *ittibāʿ* became a technical term in Islamic thought. The Qur'an makes it the test of love itself: *"Say: If you love God, then follow me, and God will love you and forgive you your sins"* (3:31). Ibn Kathīr calls this verse the judge (*al-ḥākimah*) over every claimant of love for God: whoever claims to love God but does not follow the Messenger is refuted by it. The great imams are known by the same word — the *muttabiʿūn*, those who follow; and the science of jurisprudence is organized around *ittibāʿ al-dalīl*, following the evidence. Even the word for the community's earliest generation, *al-Ṣaḥābah* and after them *al-Tābiʿūn*, uses the root: *Tābiʿūn* literally means "Followers."

The logic embedded in the verb is worth stating. To follow is to accept that the direction is not yours to choose. It involves a surrender of initiative in exchange for a guarantee about the destination: you do not know the road, but the one you follow does. This is exactly what makes following rational in ordinary life — a patient follows a surgeon's instructions he does not fully understand, a passenger follows a pilot's procedures he could not execute, a student follows a proof he cannot yet reproduce. The follower is not thereby debased; he is using the only instrument available to him, which is the recognition of who knows. Disbelief, on the Qur'an's account, is not a superior intellectual achievement but a refusal to follow — and the refusal, verse 3 says, has a direction of its own.

**Al-Bāṭil: What Falsehood Actually Is**

*Al-bāṭil* is one of the Qur'an's most consequential words, and it is usually undertranslated as "falsehood." The root *b-ṭ-l* denotes that which perishes, comes to nothing, is wasted, has no substance or continuing existence. A *bāṭil* contract in Islamic law is not merely irregular; it is void from the outset, as though it never existed, and it produces no legal effects whatsoever — as distinct from a *fāsid* contract, which is defective but may be repaired. The same word describes idolatry: *"That is because God is the Truth, and that what they call upon besides Him is the falsehood, and that God is the Exalted, the Great"* (22:62).

To say the disbelievers "followed falsehood" is therefore to say something more precise than that they held incorrect opinions. It is to say they invested their lives in what cannot hold them. The image the Qur'an supplies elsewhere is exact: *"He sends down water from the sky, and the valleys flow according to their measure, and the flood carries a swelling scum... Thus does God set forth the truth and the falsehood. As for the scum, it passes away as a thing of no account; but as for that which benefits mankind, it remains upon the earth"* (13:17). The *bāṭil* is the foam: it covers the surface, catches the light, looks for a moment like the whole of the river — and then it is gone without having watered anything.

This explains verse 1. The deeds of those who follow *bāṭil* go astray not because God is arbitrary but because they were attached to something that itself has no being. A man who builds an enormous structure on a foundation of foam will watch the structure wander off; the fault is not in the building. The Qur'an presses the point with a rhetorical question: *"Say: Are those who know and those who do not know equal?"* (39:9), and again in this same sūrah at verse 14: *"Is one who stands upon a clear proof from his Lord like one whose evil deeds are made to seem fair unto him?"*

**Al-Ḥaqq Min Rabbihim: Truth With an Address**

The believers follow *"the truth from their Lord"* — *al-ḥaqqa min rabbihim*. Two features of this phrase carry weight.

The first is the definite article on both sides: *al-bāṭil* and *al-ḥaqq*. Not "a falsehood" and "a truth," but *the* falsehood and *the* truth. The Qur'an's moral universe is not a marketplace of equally weighted options; it is a binary with a single axis, and every human life is oriented along it. Modern readers often find this stark, but the sūrah is not making a claim about the complexity of ideas — it is making a claim about the structure of reality. There is what is real and what is not, and there is no third thing. Ibn Taymiyyah built a great part of his epistemology on this pairing, arguing that *al-ḥaqq* is one while *al-bāṭil* is many, because truth corresponds to a single reality while falsehood can deviate in endless directions. The one road to a destination is single; the ways of missing it are innumerable.

The second is the addition of *min rabbihim* — "from their Lord." Truth here is not an abstraction floating in the world awaiting discovery by anyone clever enough; it is a specific communication from a specific Sender to specific recipients. *Rabbihim* — *their* Lord — makes the relationship personal. The same Lord who created them has addressed them, and the address is the truth. This is the Qur'an's answer to the modern problem of how anyone can claim access to moral truth: not by unaided reason constructing it, and not by intuition feeling it, but by receiving a communication from the One who made the receiver. Reason's role, in this framework, is not to generate the truth but to recognize its credentials and to work out its implications — which is precisely the function verse 24 will demand: *"Do they not contemplate the Qur'an?"*

**Yaḍribu Allāhu Amthālahum: The Setting Forth of Likenesses**

The final clause — *"Thus does God set forth for mankind their likenesses"* — *ka-dhālika yaḍribu Allāhu li-l-nāsi amthālahum* — closes the opening triad of the sūrah and opens the sūrah's second great theme.

The verb *ḍaraba* literally means to strike, and *ḍaraba mathalan* — "to strike a parable" — is the Qur'an's fixed idiom for setting forth a likeness. The image behind the idiom is often explained by the commentators as the striking of a coin: the die is struck into the metal and an image appears, so that the coin carries a likeness by which it is known. A parable is thus a struck image of a reality, made portable so that it can travel into a mind that has never seen the reality itself.

*Amthālahum* — "their likenesses" — is ambiguous in a productive way, and the commentators divided. Al-Qurṭubī takes it to mean that God has made clear the difference between good and evil deeds, setting forth the likeness of each so that they can be told apart. Al-Rāzī connects it more directly to the distinction just drawn in the verse between those who follow falsehood and those who follow truth: God sets forth the likeness of each group — their state, their end, their inner reality — so that mankind may recognize them. Ibn ʿAṭiyyah and others note the reflexive reading: God strikes for mankind the likeness *of themselves*, showing human beings what they look like from the outside when they follow falsehood and when they follow truth.

This last reading gives the clause its sharpest edge. The parables of the Qur'an are not illustrations of distant matters; they are portraits. When the Qur'an says of those who were entrusted with the Torah and did not carry it that their likeness is *"the likeness of the donkey carrying books"* (62:5), it is not describing an animal — it is describing a reader. When it says of the disbelievers that their likeness is *"one who kindles a fire, and when it has lit all around him, God takes away their light and leaves them in darkness, seeing nothing"* (2:17), it is describing a soul. Verse 3 announces the method: what you are about to read is a mirror, and it is held up to mankind, not to some other species.

The Qur'an states its own purpose in this method several times, and the statements are explicit about what the parables are for:

> *"And these parables We set forth for mankind, but none grasps them except those who know."* (29:43)

> *"And We set forth for mankind in this Qur'an every kind of parable, that haply they may reflect."* (39:27)

The condition attached in 29:43 — *except those who know* — is important, because it means the parable is not self-executing. It requires a prepared mind, and the sūrah will go on to describe what disqualifies a mind: a sealed heart (v. 16), a diseased heart (v. 20), a locked heart (v. 24). The likeness is struck, the coin is placed in the hand, and the question is whether the recipient looks at it.

**Why Likenesses and Not Definitions**

There is a reason the Qur'an so often argues by likeness rather than by definition, and verse 3's placement of the phrase immediately after the truth/falsehood distinction suggests it. Definitions work upon the intellect alone; likenesses work upon the imagination, the memory, and the emotions as well. A person may assent to a proposition about justice and remain unmoved by it; a person who is shown the likeness of a just man and an unjust man, side by side, in images drawn from his own daily experience — a river, a fire, a donkey, a garden, cattle at a trough — carries the comparison away with him and finds it waiting at the moment of decision. The parable is the form in which truth survives the gap between understanding and acting.

This is exactly the problem Sūrah Muḥammad is addressing. The hypocrites it dissects are not people who lack information. They heard the Qur'an recited in the Prophet's own voice, sat in his assembly, and went out asking one another what he had said (v. 16). Their failure was not intellectual. It was a failure of the imagination to be reached, and verse 3 declares the remedy God has supplied: likenesses. Verse 15 will be the sūrah's supreme example — four rivers in a garden set against a boiling liquid that tears apart the bowels — and the reason the sūrah devotes so much space to it is that no definition of the Hereafter could reach a heart that a picture of thirst cannot.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Method Statement**

Read structurally, verse 3 does three jobs at once. It explains verses 1 and 2. It names the two objects of following between which every human life must be divided. And it announces the literary method by which the rest of the chapter will proceed. Everything after verse 3 is, in a sense, the working out of *amthālahum*: the parable of the battlefield and the captives (v. 4), the parable of the ruined town (vv. 10, 13), the parable of cattle eating (v. 12), the parable of the two gardens and the two drinks (v. 15), the parable of the listener who forgets (v. 16), the parable of the locked heart (v. 24), the parable of the angel seizing the soul (v. 27), the parable of the miser and the replaced people (vv. 37–38). The sūrah has told the reader, in its third verse, how it intends to argue.

The practical instruction for a reader of the Qur'an follows directly. When one encounters a parable, one should not treat it as ornament or as a concession to a simple audience. One should do what verse 24 commands — *yata-dabbarū*, contemplate, look through and behind — and ask what likeness is being struck of one's own state. The man who reads *"the likeness of those who disbelieve is as one who kindles a fire"* and does not ask whether he is sitting in the dark of his own extinguished fire has received the parable and refused the likeness. God strikes the coin; the reader must look at the face on it.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:4

> **When you meet those who disbelieve, strike at their necks; then, when you have overwhelmed them, tighten the bonds. Then free them graciously or hold them for ransom, till war lays down its burdens. Thus [shall it be]. And if God willed, He would take vengeance upon them, but that He may test some of you by means of others, [ . . . ]. And as for those who are slain in the way of God, He will not make their deeds go astray.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Hardest Verse and How It Must Be Read**

This is the verse on which the sūrah's second name, *al-Qitāl*, depends, and it is the verse most likely to be quoted out of its context by those who wish either to attack Islam or to defend a distortion of it. It must therefore be read with the full apparatus that the Islamic tradition itself brought to it: the grammar, the occasion, the surrounding verses, the Prophetic practice that implemented it, the juristic restrictions placed upon it, and the interpretive disagreement among the classical scholars about its relation to other verses. Nothing is served by evasion, and nothing is served by isolating the clause from the sentence in which it stands — for the same verse that commands severity in battle also legislates mercy toward the prisoner, declares the war to have an end, affirms that God did not need human beings to fight it, and closes with a promise to those killed upon God's road.

The verse has four movements: the encounter and the strike; the overwhelming and the binding; the disposition of captives; and the theological frame. Each deserves its own treatment.

**Fa-Ḍarb al-Riqāb: The Grammar of a Battle Command**

*"When you meet those who disbelieve, strike at their necks"* renders *fa-idhā laqītumu alladhīna kafarū fa-ḍarba al-riqāb*. The grammatical form of the second half is unusual and deliberate. Rendered literally, as Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Ṭabarī all note, it is not an imperative verb but an accusative verbal noun: "then a striking of the necks" — understood as *fa-ḍribū al-riqāb ḍarban*, "strike the necks with a striking." Arabic rhetoricians treat this construction, the absolute object (*al-mafʿūl al-muṭlaq*), as conveying emphasis and specificity: the act is named as a noun, which makes it vivid and complete, and it is confined by that very naming to the act itself.

The commentators drew two conclusions from the form. The first is severity: the mention of the neck rather than the body generally conveys, as al-Qurṭubī says, the gravity and severity of the fighting — this is not a skirmish to be conducted with restraint once the swords are out, because a battlefield fought half-heartedly is more lethal to the half-hearted than one fought decisively. The second is limitation: the nominal form indicates the brevity of the act. It is confined to battle and is not a continuous command. There is no standing instruction here to seek out necks; there is an instruction governing the moment of armed encounter, *idhā laqītum* — "when you meet," the conditional particle fixing the rule to a specific circumstance.

*Riqāb* is the plural of *raqabah*, the neck, and its use is a metonymy (*majāz*) of the kind Arabic eloquence loves: the part is named for the whole, and the part named is the one whose disabling ends the fight most quickly. Ibn ʿĀshūr observes in *al-Taḥrīr wa-l-tanwīr* that the expression is a rhetorical intensification for the decisive strike in combat, and that its very vividness serves the deterrent purpose — an army known to fight decisively is fought against less often. The same verse, read by a potential aggressor, is a reason not to attack; read by a Muslim soldier, it is an instruction not to hesitate. Deterrence and discipline are one act in the language of the Qur'an.

**The Occasion: A Community That Had Been Forbidden to Fight**

The verse cannot be understood apart from the sequence of Qur'anic legislation on fighting, and the sequence is itself a lesson. For thirteen years in Makkah the Muslims were commanded to endure. The revelation of that period is unambiguous: *"Repel that which is evil with that which is better"* (41:34); *"So be patient with a beautiful patience"* (70:5); *"And be patient over what befalls you"* (31:17). It is reported that in Makkah a group of Muslims asked the Prophet ﷺ why they could not fight the disbelievers even though they were being oppressed, and he answered that he had not been commanded to fight. The companions ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf and others described themselves in that period as being restrained from combat while being tortured.

Then, in Madinah, permission was granted: *"Permission is given to those who fight because they have been wronged, and truly God is powerful to help them — those who were expelled from their homes without right, only because they said, 'Our Lord is God'"* (22:39–40). Then came the obligation to defend: *"And fight in the way of God those who fight you, and transgress not; truly God loves not the transgressors"* (2:190). Then came the fuller legislation of Sūrah 8 at Badr and Sūrah 9 in the year of Tabūk. Verse 4 of Sūrah Muḥammad sits within this arc, in the Madinan period when a besieged community of emigrants and helpers was under sustained armed attack by a coalition that had already fought it at Badr, would fight it at Uḥud, would besiege it at the Trench, and had driven it out of its homes and confiscated its property.

The historical context is not a defense that dissolves the text; it is the condition of its meaning. A verse addressed to a community under armed attack, forbidding it to hesitate when attacked, is not the same utterance as a general license to kill. The jurists were explicit that the ruling of fighting in Islam is tied to its cause — aggression, expulsion, the obstruction of God's road, the breaking of treaty — and that when the cause is absent the ruling is absent. Ibn Taymiyyah's treatment in *al-Siyāsah al-Sharʿiyyah* and in his juristic rulings on fighting insists that combat is directed at combatants and at those who support combat, not at human beings as such.

**Athkhantumūhum: Overwhelming Before Capturing**

*"Then, when you have overwhelmed them, tighten the bonds"* — *ḥattā idhā athkhantumūhum fa-shuddū al-wathāq*. The verb *athkhana*, from the root *th-kh-n*, means to wound deeply, to inflict heavy losses, to overcome decisively — the noun *thikhan* denotes thickness and density, so the verb conveys making the wound thick, i.e., dealing a crushing blow. Al-Ṭabarī understands it as overcoming those whose necks were not struck and making them captives; Ibn Kathīr allows the more severe reading of inflicting great slaughter. The two readings differ in degree but agree on sequence, and the sequence is the legal point.

*Fa-shuddū al-wathāq* — "tighten the bonds." *Wathāq* is the thing with which one is bound, from *w-th-q*, the root of *thiqah*, trust and firmness; a *wathīqah* is a document that binds parties, an oath that binds a person. Here it is literal: the rope of the prisoner. Al-Ṭabarī explains that the bonds are tightened so that the captive can neither cause further harm nor escape.

The juristic conclusion the commentators drew is precise and, in the history of warfare, remarkable: *prisoners are to be taken only after victory has been decisively achieved.* Al-Qurṭubī connects the verse to 8:67:

> *"It is not for a prophet to take captives until he overwhelms [his enemy] in the land."* (8:67)

The verse at 8:67 was revealed after Badr, when some of the Companions favored taking ransom for the Qurayshī prisoners while others, notably ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, favored execution, and the Qur'an addressed the decision in terms that disciplined the community's conduct of war. The principle established is that the objective of the fighting is the neutralization of the threat — *ithkhān* — and not the accumulation of captives for profit. A force that takes prisoners while the battle is still undecided endangers itself and devalues the lives it is protecting. The rule is therefore simultaneously strategic and moral: it forbids the substitution of plunder for purpose.

**Mannan or Fidāʾ: The Disposition of the Prisoner**

*"Then free them graciously or hold them for ransom"* — *fa-immā mannan baʿdu wa-immā fidāʾ*. The doubled *immā* presents two options: *mann*, a gracious release without compensation, from the root *m-n-n*, to confer a favor; and *fidāʾ*, ransom or exchange, from *f-d-y*, to redeem, the same root as *fidyah* (the compensation paid for a missed fast) and *fadiyya* (a substitute offered in another's place).

The commentators — al-Jalālayn, al-Qurṭubī, Ibn Kathīr — note that the verse thereby permits release as an act of grace, ransom for money, and exchange for Muslim prisoners held by the other side. It does not *command* release, and it does not exhaust the possibilities: prisoners may also be held, and in certain cases executed, alternatives the Prophet ﷺ practiced at various times according to circumstances.

The historical record is worth setting out, because it demonstrates that the verse was implemented as a framework requiring judgment rather than as a single fixed rule. At Badr (2/624) some prisoners were ransomed, some were released on the condition that they teach the children of Madinah to read and write — a ransom in the form of literacy, recorded by al-Bukhārī in the context of the Battle of Badr's captives — and two were executed for specific prior crimes: al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith, who had tortured believers and composed verses mocking the Prophet, and ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ, who had attempted to strangle the Prophet ﷺ during the prayer in Makkah. At Banū Qurayẓah (5/627) the tribe that had betrayed the defensive pact during the siege of Madinah was judged by Saʿd ibn Muʿādh according to the law of their own scripture, and the men who had fought were executed. After Ḥunayn (8/630) the Prophet ﷺ released the thousands of captives of Hawāzin when their delegation asked him to, granting what was his own share and urging the Companions to do likewise. In the expedition against the Banū al-Muṣṭaliq prisoners were ransomed through marriage — Juwayriyah bint al-Ḥārith, whose marriage to the Prophet ﷺ led her whole tribe to be freed.

The pattern across these cases is one of judgment applied to circumstance, and al-Qurṭubī formulated the hermeneutic that makes sense of it. He notes, as the source commentary records, that abrogation need only be invoked where two verses are irreconcilable in their legal intent; but where it is possible to implement both under different circumstances, as here, there is no abrogation. Whether to execute an implacable adversary, to continue holding a captive, or to release one must thus be decided case by case. This is a principle of immense general importance in Islamic jurisprudence: the existence of differing rulings on the same subject is usually a sign of differentiated circumstances rather than of contradiction, and the reflex to declare one text cancelled by another should be the last resort rather than the first.

Those who did argue for abrogation pointed to 8:57, 9:5, and 9:36. The verse of the sword in 9:5 — *"And when the sacred months have passed, slay those who associate partners with God wherever you find them"* — was understood by some early authorities to have superseded the options of ransom and release. But the great majority of jurists, including al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr, read 9:5 within its own stated context — the Qurayshī polytheists who had violated the treaty of Ḥudaybiyah and attacked the Muslims' allies — and read 47:4 as the continuing general framework for the treatment of captives once hostilities are over. The four schools of law accordingly preserve a range of options for the ruler regarding prisoners of war, which is precisely what 47:4 legislates.

**The Restrictions the Tradition Placed Around the Command**

It would be a serious omission to present the verse without the Prophetic and caliphal restrictions that the tradition universally attached to fighting. The Messenger ﷺ is reported in Muslim to have instructed his commanders:

> *"Fight in the name of God and in the way of God; fight those who disbelieve in God. Fight and do not steal from the spoils, do not break your pledge, do not mutilate, and do not kill a child."* (Muslim 1731, from Buraydah ibn al-Ḥuṣayb)

And in the report of Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq to Usāmah ibn Zayd's army, transmitted in the *Muwaṭṭaʾ* of Mālik and elsewhere, the first Caliph laid down the rules that became the basis of the Islamic law of war:

> *"Do not betray, do not steal from the spoils, do not mutilate, do not kill a child, an aged person, or a woman. Do not cut down or burn palm trees, do not cut down fruit-bearing trees, do not slaughter a sheep or a cow or a camel except for food. You will pass by people devoted to worship in cells; leave them and that to which they are devoted."* (Mālik, *al-Muwaṭṭaʾ*; also in ʿAbd al-Razzāq's *Muṣannaf*)

The Prophet ﷺ himself, on seeing a woman killed in one of the expeditions, said: *"She was not fighting"* — and forbade the killing of women and children (recorded by Abū Dāwūd 2669 and Ibn Mājah 2841 from Rabāḥ ibn al-Rabīʿ). These restrictions are not later humanitarian additions imposed upon an earlier text; they are the Prophetic implementation of the same sūrah that contains verse 4, and they define the meaning of *ḍarb al-riqāb* for the tradition: the strike is directed at those who fight, in the moment of fighting, until the fighting ends.

**Ḥattā Taḍaʿa al-Ḥarb Awzārahā: The War Ends**

*"Till war lays down its burdens"* — *ḥattā taḍaʿa al-ḥarb awzārahā*. *Awzār* is the plural of *wizr*, a load or burden, and by extension the heavy armor and weapons a warrior carries — the same word that appears in *"God will forgive you your sins"* contexts as *wizr* meaning a weight of guilt. Al-Qurṭubī reports both readings: the burdens are literally the weapons, or they are all the hardships war entails. Either way the image is of war as a porter setting down his load — the personification is striking, and it makes war the subject of the sentence rather than its object. War is a thing that carries a weight and eventually puts it down; the verse's horizon is the end of the carrying.

The commentators note that the clause can be read either as "until war ceases" or as "so that war will cease," and many understand it as a reference to the time when God alone is worshipped. This second reading is theologically significant: the purpose of the fighting is not conquest but the removal of the conditions that make fighting necessary. The verse thus contains its own termination clause. A war conducted under 47:4 has an end built into its mandate, and that end is the laying down of the burden.

The Prophet ﷺ demonstrated this in practice. The most important treaty of his career — Ḥudaybiyah (6/628) — was made from a position in which the Muslims had been prevented from performing the ʿumrah, and its terms appeared to the Companions to be humiliating: they would return that year, they would hand back any Qurayshī who came to them as a Muslim, and the document would not bear the phrase "Messenger of God." ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb objected forcefully and later said that he had never doubted as he doubted on that day. Yet the treaty produced ten years of security during which the number of Muslims multiplied several-fold, and the Qur'an called the day of its signing *"a manifest victory"* (48:1). The lesson the tradition drew is that the objective is not the defeat of the enemy but the establishment of conditions in which guidance can travel. Verse 4 of Sūrah Muḥammad is followed by verse 5 promising guidance, and the sūrah's next chapter is named al-Fatḥ, "The Victory," revealed about a treaty.

This is also the verse against which the sūrah's own verse 35 must be read — *"So do not falter and call for peace while you have the upper hand"* — and the reconciliation of the two is one of the classical discussions. Al-Qurṭubī notes that 8:61 (*"And if they incline toward peace, incline thou toward it, and trust in God"*) is read by some as abrogating 47:35, but that the sounder position — the same hermeneutic he applied to verse 4 — is that the two describe different conditions: when the enemy sincerely inclines to peace, peace is to be accepted; when the community has the upper hand and the opponent seeks peace as a stratagem to regroup, weakness must not be shown. Circumstance, not contradiction.

**"And If God Willed, He Would Take Vengeance Upon Them"**

The theological heart of the verse is its parenthetical: *"Thus [shall it be]. And if God willed, He would take vengeance upon them, but that He may test some of you by means of others."* *Wa-law yashāʾu Allāhu la-ntaṣara minhum* — God could have taken vengeance upon them Himself, without any human fighting.
