The commentators are unanimous on the sense: divine power is not in question; the question is why human beings are made the instrument.

The answer given is *li-yabluwa baʿḍakum bi-baʿḍ* — "that He may test some of you by means of others." The verb *balā* means to test, to try, to put through an ordeal, and it is one of the Qur'an's central words for the human condition: *"He who created death and life that He may test you, which of you is best in deed"* (67:2); *"And We made some of you a test for others: will you be patient?"* (25:20); *"And We shall surely test you with something of fear and hunger, and loss of wealth and lives and fruits; and give glad tidings to the patient"* (2:155). The parallel verse in the law of striving is 5:48:

> *"And had God willed, He would have made you one community, but [He willed otherwise], that He might try you in that which He has given you. So vie with one another in good deeds."* (5:48)

The logical structure deserves careful statement, because it is a genuine philosophical claim about the purpose of human agency. God's power is not increased by human action, and God's justice does not require human execution. The testing is not for God's information — He knows the outcome before it occurs, as verse 31 will insist — but for the actualization of the tested. A test in the Qur'anic sense does not discover what is unknown; it brings into existence what was potential. Courage does not exist as an actuality until there is something to be courageous about. Sacrifice does not exist until there is something to sacrifice. Generosity does not exist until there is something to give. Patience does not exist until there is something to endure. A world in which God simply destroyed the oppressors at the moment of oppression would be a world without these qualities — a world of spectators rather than of moral agents.

This is the deepest reason the verse gives for the fighting, and it explains why the sūrah's treatment of it is framed by promises about deeds: those slain in God's way will not have their deeds go astray, God will guide them, rectify their state, and admit them to a Garden made known to them. The battlefield is presented not as an end but as the most extreme instance of the sūrah's governing concern — what becomes of a human deed.

Ibn Taymiyyah's discussion of this principle, developed across his writings on divine wisdom, makes the point that God's command of human beings to act, when He could act without them, is an instance of His mercy toward them: He gives them the dignity of causality, the honor of being instruments of a purpose greater than themselves, and the reward attached to that instrumentality. The alternative — a universe in which nothing requires human participation — would deprive human life of its moral weight entirely.

**"He Will Not Make Their Deeds Go Astray"**

The verse closes with the negation of the sūrah's opening: *"And as for those who are slain in the way of God, He will not make their deeds go astray."* The reading in the canonical transmission is *qutilū* — "were slain," passive — while a variant reading transmits *qātalū*, "fought." Both are attested, and the passive is the one preserved in the standard text. The emphasis falls, accordingly, not on the fighting but on being killed in it: the one whose life ends on God's road in the discharge of that obligation.

*Fa-lan yuḍilla aʿmālahum* — with *lan*, the particle of absolute future negation: *never* will He let their deeds go astray. Against verse 1's *aḍalla aʿmālahum*, in the past tense and unemphatic, this is the strongest possible contrast. The person who turned from God's road loses his deeds; the person who is killed upon it can never lose his. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr connect this to the wider Qur'anic promise: *"And do not say of those who are slain in the way of God, 'They are dead'; rather they are living, but you do not perceive"* (2:154), and *"Think not of those who are slain in the way of God as dead; rather they are living, provided for by their Lord, rejoicing in what God has given them of His bounty"* (3:169–170).

The reason for this promise is not difficult to see, and it is the reason the sūrah gives everywhere: the deed is defined by its direction. A person who gives his life upon God's road has performed the ultimate act of *ittibāʿ* — the following described in verse 3 — and there is no possibility of his deed wandering off, because the deed and its destination coincide. He died going somewhere, and the dying was the going.

For the ordinary reader who will never stand on a battlefield, the verse retains its force through the principle it states rather than through its occasion. The sūrah uses the widest vocabulary of striving elsewhere — verse 31 speaks of *"those among you who strive and those who are patient,"* and the Qur'an describes striving with wealth and with the self (49:15; 9:41) as well as with the sword. The person who is worn out by an honest life, who gives his strength to the care of others, who loses money rather than cheat, who endures ridicule for a practice he will not abandon, is performing deeds upon God's road, and the promise attached to those deeds is the same one: they will not go astray. Nothing spent for God is lost. That is the sentence the whole verse exists to say.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:5

> **He will guide them and set their state aright,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Promise in the Future Tense**

Verse 4 ended with a negation — *He will not make their deeds go astray* — and verse 5 continues the same sentence into positive content: *sayahdīhim wa-yuṣliḥu bālahum*, "He will guide them and set their state aright." The source commentary notes that the future tense here indicates a promise, and the letter *sīn* prefixed to the verb is one of the two markers of futurity in Arabic (the other being *sawfa*, which denotes a more distant future). The *sīn* brings the action near and makes it certain: it *will* happen, and soon in the reckoning of the one to whom it is promised.

The verse is short enough that a reader may pass over it, yet it contains the whole of the sūrah's eschatology in two verbs. And the fact that it repeats the second verb of verse 2 — *aṣlaḥa bālahum*, "He set their state aright" — is not redundancy but escalation. In verse 2 the rectification was in the past tense: God *has* absolved them and *has* set their state aright, an accomplished fact in the present life of the believer. In verse 5 it is in the future: God *will* set their state aright, in the life to come. The same gift is promised in both worlds, and the second is the consummation of the first.

**Guidance Is Not One Thing**

The commentators distinguish several kinds of guidance in the Qur'an, and the distinctions matter for understanding which guidance verse 5 promises.

The first is *hidāyat al-dalālah* — the guidance of indication, of showing the road. This is the guidance given to every human being through revelation and reason, and it is attributed to the Prophets: *"And truly you guide unto a straight path"* (42:52). It can be offered to a person who rejects it, which is why the Qur'an tells the Prophet ﷺ at 28:56: *"Truly you guide not whom you love, but God guides whom He wills."*

The second is *hidāyat al-tawfīq* — the guidance of enablement, of actually placing the heart upon the road and keeping it there. This belongs to God alone: *"Whomever God guides, he is guided; and whomever He leads astray, you will find no protector for him"* (18:17). It is the guidance promised in verse 17 of this sūrah to those who are already guided: *"But those who are guided, He increases them in guidance."*

The third is the guidance of the Hereafter — direction within the world to come. This is the sense in which verse 5 is most commonly read, and the Qur'an speaks of it explicitly: *"Guide them to the path of the Blaze"* (37:23) is the command concerning the guilty, while of the believers it is said, *"And they will be guided unto goodly speech, and they will be guided unto the path of the Praiseworthy"* (22:24). Guidance in the next world is a real navigation: there are stations to pass, a bridge to cross, gates to be admitted through, and dwellings to be found. The believer who was guided in this world will be guided in that one, and the guidance will be continuous with what came before.

Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr read verse 5 within this frame: those slain in God's way will be guided — to the crossing, to their places, to the Garden itself — and their state will be set aright in the fullest possible sense, since nothing in the Hereafter is disordered for them. The two verbs are therefore sequential: guidance is the direction, and rectification of the state is the condition of the one being directed. A traveler who is shown the road but arrives exhausted, wounded, and in disarray has been guided but not made whole. God promises both.

**Why the Reward of Guidance Is More Guidance**

There is a principle embedded in verse 5 that runs through the whole Qur'an and deserves to be drawn out, because it explains a great deal about how Islam understands spiritual progress. The reward of a good deed is another good deed; the reward of guidance is more guidance. Verse 17 of this same sūrah states it: *"But those who are guided, He increases them in guidance and grants them their reverence."* And elsewhere: *"And God increases in guidance those who are guided"* (19:76); *"And those who are guided aright, He increases them in guidance and grants them their reverence"* (18:13 — in the account of the Companions of the Cave: *"We increased them in guidance"*); *"And as for those who believe in God and His messengers, they are the sincere ones"* (57:19). The principle also runs in the opposite direction, which is why the sūrah warns so insistently about the sealed and locked heart: *"And We turn their hearts and their sights, as they did not believe in it the first time"* (6:110).

The logic is that of a capacity that grows by use. Guidance is not a possession that can be stored; it is an activity of the heart in relation to truth, and like every activity of a living faculty it strengthens or atrophies. A muscle exercised becomes capable of more exercise; a language spoken becomes easier to speak; a conscience consulted becomes quicker to answer; a conscience overridden becomes slower. Ibn al-Qayyim developed this into the organizing structure of *Madārij al-Sālikīn*, his commentary on the stations of the spiritual path, arguing that every act of obedience produces in the heart a light that makes the next act easier, and every act of disobedience produces a darkness that makes the next sin more likely — until, in the extreme case the Qur'an describes, the heart becomes incapable of receiving anything at all.

Verse 5 thus promises something more than safe arrival. It promises a person who is *more guided* than he was, whose capacity for direction has been enlarged by the very journey. The one who gave his life on God's road does not merely reach the destination; he becomes the kind of being for whom the destination is natural.

**Rectification of the State: What Bāl Means in the Hereafter**

*Bāl* — rendered here as "state" — was discussed at verse 2, where al-Ṭūsī noted that it can mean the condition, the heart, or the mind. The word is rare in Arabic and rarer still in the Qur'an, appearing in a handful of places: 47:2 and 47:5 in this sūrah, and in the interrogative idiom *mā bāluka* — "what is the matter with you?" — used by the Qur'an in the speech of Moses' sister (20:60 in the Pharaoh's mouth: *"What is the matter with you, O Samiri?"* — actually 20:95) and elsewhere. Its core sense is that about which one is concerned: the state of affairs, the matter at the center of one's attention.

To say that God will set a person's *bāl* aright in the Hereafter is therefore to say that everything about which he was concerned — his condition, his mind, his affairs, his relations, his unfinished business — will be put in order. The Qur'an describes the disordered alternative in vivid terms: the inhabitants of the Fire crying out for water, for relief, for death, for return; the person whose book is given in his left hand wishing he had never been given it; the one who says *"would that I had sent forth for my life"* (89:24). This is *fasād al-bāl* — the ruin of the state — and it consists precisely in the impossibility of setting anything right, because the time for setting things right has passed.

The promise of verse 5 is the exact inverse: arrival without disarray. The believer will not come to the Garden confused, unprepared, ashamed, or in need of explanation. The tradition adds a detail that makes this concrete, recorded by Muslim from Abū Hurayrah:

> *"The first group to enter Paradise will be upon the form of the moon on the night of the full moon, and those after them upon the form of the brightest star in the sky... They will not spit, nor blow their noses, nor relieve themselves. Their vessels there will be of gold and silver, their combs of gold, the fuel of their censers will be aloeswood, and their sweat will be musk. For each of them there will be two wives... No disagreement among them, no hatred; their hearts one heart, glorifying God morning and evening."* (al-Bukhārī 3245; Muslim 2834)

Note what the report removes: bodily disorder, emotional disorder, social disorder. Nothing is out of place. That is *iṣlāḥ al-bāl* rendered in images, and it is what verse 5 promises in two words.

**The Verse Between the Battlefield and the Garden**

Structurally, verse 5 performs a transitional work that is easy to miss. Verse 4 is the sūrah's most severe verse — the encounter, the strike, the bonds, the ransom. Verses 5 and 6 are its immediate sequel, and they contain nothing but mercy: guidance, rectification, entry into the Garden, a Garden made known. Verse 7 then returns to the community's obligation: *if you help God, He will help you.*

The arrangement teaches something about how the Qur'an wants the subject of fighting to be held in the mind. The severity is not the point; it is a means, and the point is stated on either side of it. Before the command, the sūrah establishes that those who turn from God's road have lost their deeds (vv. 1–3). After the command, it establishes that those who die upon God's road have their guidance completed and their state set right (vv. 5–6). The reader is thus never allowed to lose sight of the fact that what is at stake in the fighting is not territory or vengeance but the direction of souls and the fate of deeds. This is why the sūrah's vocabulary never leaves the language of *sabīl* — the way, the road: those who turn from it, those slain upon it, those who help God upon it, those who spend upon it. Everything in the chapter is about roads and where they end.

For the reader who will never be called to armed combat, verse 5 remains a promise about his own end. The believer whose life is spent in ordinary obedience — the prayer performed on time, the child raised with patience, the debt repaid, the elderly parent carried, the truth told at cost — has set his face in a direction, and the sūrah promises that the direction will be honored. He will be guided at the moment when guidance matters most, and the state he has been tending all his life, usually badly and always incompletely, will be set aright by the One who undertook to set it right. The verb is His, not ours. That is the whole comfort of the verse.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:6

> **and cause them to enter the Garden, having made it known to them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verb That Makes Paradise Familiar**

*"And cause them to enter the Garden, having made it known to them"* — *wa-yudkhiluhumu al-jannata ʿarrafahā lahum*. The final verb of this short sequence is the most unusual and the most discussed. *ʿArrafa* is the doubled intensive form of *ʿarafa*, to know — so "He made it known to them," "He caused them to know it fully," "He made it recognizable to them." The choice of the intensive form rather than the simple *ʿarrafahum bihā* or *aʿlamahum* has occupied the commentators, and their readings converge on a single theme: the Garden that the believer enters is not a strange place. It is a place he already knows.

Al-Rāzī in *Mafātīḥ al-Ghayb* gathers the interpretations, and the source commentary records the principal one: after being guided and having their state set aright, God brings the believers into the Garden, and then each person knows his own place in Paradise. Al-Qurṭubī connects the verse directly to a Prophetic report which supplies the detail:

> *"When the believers have been saved from the Fire, they will be stopped at a bridge between the Fire and the Garden, where they will retaliate against one another for the injustices done among them in the world. Then when they are purified and cleansed, they will be permitted to enter the Garden. And by Him in Whose Hand lies the soul of Muhammad, everybody will recognize his dwelling in the Garden better than he recognizes his dwelling in this world."* (al-Bukhārī 2440, 6535, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī)

The comparison at the end of the report is the interpretive key to the verse. Recognition of one's home in Paradise will exceed recognition of one's home in this world. The believer will not need directions, a guide, or an introduction. He will arrive as a person arrives at the house he has lived in for forty years — the hand finds the light-switch in the dark, the foot knows the stair that creaks, the body is at home before the mind has finished thinking.

**Why the Knowledge Comes First**

Three readings of *ʿarrafahā lahum* are transmitted, and they operate on different time scales.

*The first* places the knowing in the world: God made Paradise known to them in this life through His description of it, so that they longed for it, worked for it, and endured the loss of this world's comforts for its sake. On this reading the verse is a statement about the efficacy of the Qur'an's own depictions of the Garden — and Sūrah Muḥammad will supply one of the most detailed of them nine verses later (47:15). The believer who has read the description of the rivers of water, milk, wine, and honey has been given knowledge of a place he has never seen, and that knowledge is the engine of his patience. Ibn al-Qayyim's whole account of the spiritual path in *Madārij al-Sālikīn* depends on this mechanism: the heart moves toward what it has been made to see. A person cannot desire a good he cannot imagine, which is why revelation spends so much of its length upon description rather than command. The command tells the will what to do; the description tells the heart what to want.

*The second* places the knowing at the arrival: God will make each person know his dwelling, his rank, his companions, his place in the assembly. The tradition adds that the believer will be given a marker, a scent, a light by which he finds his way, and that his knowledge will be immediate and unerring. This reading gives the verse its eschatological weight, and it is the reading the ḥadīth above supports.

*The third*, reported from among the early interpreters and noted by al-Qurṭubī, connects *ʿarrafahā* to *ʿarf*, the Arabic word for fragrance — a scent that carries and is recognized from a distance. On this reading the Garden is made known to its inhabitants by its perfume: they smell it before they see it, and the smell is a knowledge. The Qur'an speaks of the Garden's fragrance elsewhere: *"And when he turned toward the direction of Midian, he said: It may be that my Lord will guide me upon the right road"* (28:22) is unrelated, but the report of the scent of Paradise is explicit — the Prophet ﷺ said, as recorded in al-Bukhārī from Abū Hurayrah in the account of the first group's entry, and in al-Tirmidhī:

> *"The scent of Paradise is smelled from a distance of forty years' journey, and whoever kills a person who has a covenant [with the Muslims] will not smell its scent."* (Ibn Mājah 3170; Aḥmad, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr; the covenant clause is in al-Bukhārī 3166)

The three readings are not competing but cumulative, and the intensive form of the verb accommodates all of them: the Garden was made known in advance through description, it is made known at arrival through recognition, and it is made known through a fragrance that precedes it. In each case the point is the same. God does not usher the believer into an unfamiliar reward. He brings him home.

**The Bridge and the Settling of Accounts**

The ḥadīth of Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī introduces an element the verse presupposes: before the entry, there is a stopping at a bridge between the Fire and the Garden, where injustices done among believers in the world are retaliated — that is, settled. This is the station known in the tradition as the *qiṭārah* or the final reckoning of mutual wrongs (*al-maẓālim*), and its purpose is purification. No believer enters the Garden carrying an unresolved grievance against another believer, and none enters carrying a grievance another holds against him. Everything is settled, and then — the report says — *they are purified and cleansed*.

This detail illuminates verse 5's phrase *"and set their state aright."* The rectification includes the rectification of relationships. The Muslim who died owing an apology, or who was owed one, will not spend eternity in the awkwardness of an unfinished quarrel. The tradition is emphatic that nothing of that sort survives into the Garden: *"No disagreement among them, no hatred; their hearts one heart"* (al-Bukhārī 3245). Verse 6's "having made it known to them" thus completes a process that began with guidance, continued with rectification of the inner state, and now arrives at recognition of one's own place among people with whom all accounts have been closed.

There is a practical instruction here for the living, and the Islamic tradition has always drawn it. The Prophet ﷺ asked his Companions:

> *"Do you know who the bankrupt is?"* — and after their answer, he explained: *"The bankrupt of my community is the one who comes on the Day of Resurrection with prayer, fasting, and alms, and comes having reviled this one, slandered that one, consumed the wealth of this one, shed the blood of that one, and beaten this one..."* (Muslim 2581)

The warning is that the debts between people are settled from the currency of good deeds, and that a person may arrive wealthy in worship and leave insolvent. The bridge mentioned in the ḥadīth of Abū Saʿīd is where that settlement occurs. The believer who wishes to enter the Garden with his deeds intact should settle his accounts before the bridge, in this world, while the other party is still alive and still reachable — a principle the jurists made into a chapter of their own, the chapter of *al-maẓālim*, wrongs requiring restitution.

**The Logic of a Reward That Is Known**

Why does the verse emphasize knowing at all? The entry into the Garden would be complete without the recognition. The answer lies in the nature of the reward being described, and it is an answer the Qur'an gives repeatedly.

Paradise, in Islamic teaching, is not a generic pleasure delivered to interchangeable recipients. It is differentiated: ranks, degrees, dwellings, proximity. *"Look at how We have preferred some of them over others"* (17:21); *"They will not desire removal from it"* (18:108); and the report that each person will know his dwelling better than his house in this world. Differentiation is only a good if it is *perceived*. A person placed in a superior position without knowing it gains nothing from the superiority; a person who knows that the position is his, and knows that he was not deceived about it, gains the whole of it. Recognition is thus not an addition to the reward but the organ by which the reward is experienced.

This is also why the verse says *ʿarrafahā lahum* rather than "He showed them the Garden." Knowing is interior; showing is exterior. The believer will not merely be presented with a spectacle; he will possess a knowledge, and knowledge is the only possession that survives death — which is one reason the Qur'an ranks it so high: *"Say: My Lord, increase me in knowledge"* (20:114); *"God raises in degrees those of you who believe and those given knowledge"* (58:11); *"Are those who know and those who do not know equal?"* (39:9). The one who spent his life learning the revelation will arrive in a place he has been studying. The verse 15 that follows is, in this light, a piece of that knowledge being given — an instalment of *taʿrīf al-jannah*, the making-known of the Garden, delivered to the believers of Madinah while they were still on earth and still in danger.

**The Sequence of the Three Verbs**

Verses 4 through 6 form a single sentence in the canonical text, and the order of its three verbs is worth observing: *He will guide them* (v. 5a), *and set their state aright* (v. 5b), *and cause them to enter the Garden, having made it known to them* (v. 6). Al-Rāzī notes the sequence explicitly: first guidance, then rectification, then entry. Each prepares the next.

The order is not arbitrary, and it mirrors the order of the spiritual life in this world. Guidance is the direction — knowing which way to go. Rectification of the state is the fitness — being made capable of the journey, with the heart settled, the relations repaired, the debts discharged. Entry is the arrival — and arrival is only enjoyable to one who has been prepared for it. A person taken from a life of chaos into a life of order experiences the order as deprivation; a person taken from a life of order into a greater order experiences it as relief. This is why the Qur'an insists so much on the rectification of the inner state in this life: the Hereafter does not change people, it reveals and completes them. The Garden is made known to those who made it known to themselves first.

The same sequence explains a much-discussed feature of Islamic eschatology: the report that people will be resurrected in the state in which they died (*"You will be gathered as you were created"* — cf. al-Bukhārī 3349 on the resurrection barefoot and uncircumcised), and the Prophet's ﷺ general statement recorded by Muslim:

> *"When the son of Adam dies, his deeds are cut off except three: a continuing charity, a knowledge by which people benefit, or a righteous child who prays for him."* (Muslim 1631, from Abū Hurayrah)

The deeds continue to travel with the person because they have made him what he is. Verse 6 promises that what arrives will recognize where it has arrived.

**For the Reader Outside the Battlefield**

The verses 4 through 6 were revealed to a community under arms, and the promise at their center concerns those slain in God's way. But the sūrah's own vocabulary elsewhere widens the promise. Verse 31 speaks of *"those among you who strive and those who are patient"* — *al-mujāhidīn* and *al-ṣābirīn* — and the Qur'an defines striving broadly: *"And strive in God's cause with the striving that is His due"* (22:78); *"Those who believe and emigrate and strive in the way of God with their wealth and their selves are greater in degree with God"* (9:20). Striving with wealth and with the self covers a great deal of ordinary life.

The person who prays Fajr in the cold while his household sleeps, the mother who raises children alone, the trader who refuses a profitable fraud, the student who studies a religious text he finds difficult, the old man who walks to the mosque on failing legs — each of these is striving upon God's road in the sense the Qur'an intends, and the promise of verses 5 and 6 attaches to the striving: guidance, rectification of the state, and an entry into a Garden that has already been made known. The final clause of verse 6 should therefore be read as an address to every reciter. The Garden is being made known to you now, in this verse, in this sūrah, in the descriptions that follow. The knowledge is being given in advance, so that the arrival will not be a surprise but a homecoming — so that, as the report says, you will know your dwelling there better than you know your dwelling here.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:7

> **O you who believe! If you help God, He will help you and make firm your steps.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Conditional at the Center of the Sūrah**

After six verses of description — what God does to the deeds of those who turn away, what He does for those who follow, what He will do for those slain upon His road — the sūrah turns to address the reader directly for the first time: *yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū*, "O you who believe!" The formula occurs eighty-eight times in the Qur'an and always introduces a matter requiring action. What follows here is the sūrah's most frequently quoted sentence, seven words in Arabic, and it is a conditional: *in tanṣurū Allāha yanṣurkum wa-yuthabbit aqdāmakum* — "if you help God, He will help you and make firm your steps."

The structure is that of a contract, and it is one of the clearest statements anywhere in revelation of the principle that divine aid is not dispensed irrespective of human effort. God does not say, "I will help you"; He says, "if you help Me, I will help you." The antecedent is the servant's act and the consequent is the Lord's. Anyone who has lived under a condition knows what this means: the promise is real, the promise is certain, and the promise is not unconditional.

**How Can God Be Helped?**

The first question the verse raises is theological, and the commentators answered it immediately and unanimously. Al-Jalālayn and al-Qurṭubī explain that "if you help God" means if you help God's religion and His Messenger; al-Zamakhsharī adds that it means helping the party of God. God is not helped in the sense in which a strong man is helped by another who adds to his strength. He is *al-Ghanī*, the Rich, self-sufficient, in need of nothing — the sūrah will say so explicitly at its close: *"God is the Rich, and you are the poor"* (47:38). To "help God" is to help what is His: His cause, His law, His Messenger, His servants, the establishment of what He has commanded and the removal of what He has forbidden.

The Qur'an uses the same idiom without embarrassment elsewhere, and each use clarifies it:

> *"O you who believe! Be helpers of God, as Jesus son of Mary said unto the disciples: Who will be my helpers unto God? The disciples said: We are the helpers of God."* (61:14)

> *"And God will surely help those who help Him. Truly God is Strong, Almighty."* (22:40)

The last clause of 22:40 is significant: *God is Strong, Almighty* — the verse affirms His absolute sufficiency in the same breath as it promises to help those who help Him. The help is therefore not needed by God; it is needed by the helper. This is the logical key to the whole verse. The one who is asked to help is the one who benefits from helping, because the act of helping aligns him with a reality that cannot fail. A person who assists a losing side shares its loss; a person who assists a side that cannot lose has placed himself where victory must pass. God has made His own cause the cause that must prevail, and He invites human beings to attach themselves to it — not because He requires them but because they require the attachment.

The same principle is stated in the Prophet's ﷺ teaching about human relationships, in one of the most widely transmitted of all his sayings:

> *"God is in the aid of the servant so long as the servant is in the aid of his brother."* (Muslim 2699, from Abū Hurayrah)

The symmetry is exact and it is not accidental: help is reciprocated by help, and the condition of receiving is giving. Ibn Taymiyyah draws the general rule from such texts in his discussions of *tawakkul* and *sabab*: God has ordained that effects follow their causes, and He has made the servant's own effort a cause He honors, not because the effect depends upon it in itself but because the servant's dignity consists in being a cause.

**The Ways of Helping God's Religion**

What does helping look like in practice? The Qur'an supplies the answer in the verse the commentators most often cite alongside this one:

> *"Were it not for God's repelling people, some by means of others, monasteries, churches, synagogues, and mosques, wherein God's Name is mentioned much, would have been destroyed. And God will surely help those who help Him."* (22:40)

The mechanism of divine support in the world is human beings supporting one another, and the institutions listed are places of worship. To help God's religion is, concretely, to keep such places standing and functioning: to build and maintain them, to attend them, to teach in them, to fund them, to protect them, to make them welcoming. The verse's enumeration is notable for including the houses of worship of other revealed communities, which is why Ibn Taymiyyah and others cited it in the classical discussion of the protection of non-Muslim places of worship under Muslim rule.

Beyond the institutions, the tradition enumerates the ways of helping: supporting the Messenger and his mission (which after his death means supporting his Sunnah — teaching it, practicing it, defending it against misrepresentation); establishing justice, since the purpose of revelation is *"that mankind may stand forth in justice"* (57:25); commanding the good and forbidding the evil, which the Qur'an makes the defining activity of the community: *"You are the best community brought forth for mankind, commanding the good, forbidding the evil, and believing in God"* (3:110); spending wealth in God's way, which is the subject of the sūrah's final three verses; and speaking the truth where it is unwelcome, *"fearing no reproach of any reproacher"* (5:54).

The Prophet ﷺ described the range of helping in a way that includes the weakest and the least equipped:

> *"Help your brother, whether he is an oppressor or oppressed."* A man said, "O Messenger of God, I help him when he is oppressed, but how do I help him when he is an oppressor?" He said: *"You hold his hand [and prevent him] from oppression — that is your helping him."* (al-Bukhārī 2444, from Anas ibn Mālik)

The helping of God's religion includes restraining those who claim to act in its name and act against it. In an age when a great deal of what is done in the name of religion is done against it, this is not a marginal application of the verse but a central one.

**Yanṣurkum: The Help That Comes Back**

The consequent — *yanṣurkum*, "He will help you" — uses the same root as the antecedent, *n-ṣ-r*, producing one of the Qur'an's characteristic reciprocations (*al-mushākalah*), in which the servant's word is returned to him in God's mouth. The effect is that the two acts appear as one transaction, though their magnitudes are infinitely unequal. The servant's help is finite, partial, mixed with self-interest, interrupted by fatigue; the Lord's help is absolute, and the Qur'an elsewhere names its forms: victory over enemies, provision in difficulty, firmness in trial, opening of the heart, ease after hardship.

The help promised is not necessarily the help requested. This is the point at which the verse has been most often misunderstood in history, by those who assumed that helping God's religion guarantees worldly success on their own terms. The Qur'an's own record contradicts that assumption: the believers were helped at Badr and tested at Uḥud; they were helped at the Trench and starved in the boycott; the Prophet ﷺ himself was helped by victory in his lifetime and lost his wife, his uncle, and sixty of his Companions. Ibn Kathīr's commentary on 22:40 notes that divine help comes in the form God chooses, at the time God chooses, and includes deliverance, patience, and reward in the next life as fully as it includes battlefield victory. The Prophet ﷺ's own summary of the matter is in al-Bukhārī:

> *"How strange is the affair of the believer! Truly his affair is all good, and this is for no one except the believer. If good befalls him he gives thanks, and that is good for him; and if harm befalls him he is patient, and that is good for him."* (Muslim 2999, from Ṣuhayb)

The help of God is therefore the help that makes every condition good, which is a greater thing than the help that removes bad conditions.

**Wa-Yuthabbit Aqdāmakum: The Firmness of the Feet**

The second promise is more specific and more physical: *"and make firm your steps"* — *wa-yuthabbit aqdāmakum*. *Aqdām* is the plural of *qadam*, the foot; *yuthabbit* is the doubled intensive of *th-b-t*, to be firm, stable, fixed, immovable. The image is of feet planted on ground that will not give way.

The Qur'an uses this phrase in exactly the situations one would expect. Before the Battle of Badr, the believers prayed:

> *"Our Lord, pour patience upon us, and make firm our feet, and help us against the disbelieving people."* (2:250)

And they prayed again in the face of a larger army:

> *"Our Lord, forgive us our sins and our excess in our affair, and make firm our feet, and help us against the disbelieving people."* (3:147)

And at Badr God addressed the angels:

> *"Truly I am with you; so make firm those who believe."* (8:12)

And of the believers generally:

> *"God makes firm those who believe with the firm word, in the life of this world and in the Hereafter; and God sends the wrongdoers astray, and God does what He wills."* (14:27)

The commentators — al-Qurṭubī and al-Jalālayn as recorded in the source — read the firmness of the feet in 47:7 in two complementary ways: walking upon the straight path, and standing firm while fighting. Both are literal and both are metaphorical at once. On the battlefield, the soldier whose feet slip is dead; on the path of life, the person whose resolve slips is lost. The doubling of the verb's form (*yuthabbit* rather than *yathbut*) indicates the intensity and repetition of the making-firm: it is not a single stabilization but a continuous one, as the ground keeps changing under the feet.

Al-Ṭabarī and others note that the phrase is also an allegory for the firmness of the heart in faith, which is the Qur'anic usage at 14:27 and in the Prophet's ﷺ most characteristic supplication, transmitted in al-Tirmidhī and elsewhere and said to be his most frequent duʿāʾ:

> *"O Turner of the hearts, make my heart firm upon Your religion."* (al-Tirmidhī 2140, from Anas ibn Mālik; also Ibn Mājah 3834)

ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr asked him, "O Messenger of God, do our hearts turn?" and he answered:

> *"Yes. There is no human being created by God but that his heart is between two of the fingers of the Most Merciful: if He wills He makes it firm, and if He wills He lets it go astray."* (Aḥmad; al-Tirmidhī 2141)

The connection to verse 7 is exact. The one who helps God's religion is promised that his feet will be made firm — and the tradition makes clear that the firmness is God's gift rather than the believer's achievement. The effort is ours and the stability is His, which is why the effort is commanded and the prayer for firmness is also commanded. Ibn Taymiyyah's treatment of this paradox, developed throughout his work on divine decree and human agency, holds the two together without dissolving either: the servant truly acts and truly chooses, and God truly creates the action and truly knows its outcome — and the correct posture toward both facts is the posture of the one who works and prays.

**The Verse and the Hypocrites**

Read within the sūrah's argument, verse 7 has a specific audience. Verses 1–6 have distinguished the disbelievers and the believers and promised the slain their reward. Verse 7 addresses the living believers who have not been slain and tells them what to do next. The verses that follow — 8 through 11 — return to the disbelievers' wretchedness, and verses 16 through 30 turn to the hypocrites who wanted a sūrah and then flinched at fighting. Verse 7 stands between these two groups as the criterion.

The hypocrites, as the sūrah will describe them, were not people who refused to help God's religion openly; they were people who helped it selectively, when it cost them nothing. Verse 20 shows them asking for a revelation, and verse 21 shows the alternative that would have been *"more fitting for them: obedience to God and honorable speech."* The conditional of verse 7 excludes that posture. *If* you help God — completely, when it is costly, in the matter He specifies rather than the matter you prefer — *then* He will help you. The one who helps conditionally has not satisfied the antecedent, and the consequent does not follow.

This is why the verse has functioned in Islamic history as a test of movements claiming divine support. Every generation has produced groups that assert God is on their side, and the verse supplies the only reliable criterion: look at what they are doing for His religion, in His terms, at cost to themselves. The claim of divine help is not established by victory, since God tests with both outcomes; it is established by the antecedent. Muslim records the Prophet ﷺ saying:

> *"God does not look at your forms and your wealth, but He looks at your hearts and your deeds."* (Muslim 2564, from Abū Hurayrah)

The two things God looks at are precisely the two things verse 7 names: the heart that intends the help, and the deed that performs it.

**The Modern Application of a Battlefield Verse**

The ordinary reader of this verse will not be standing in a shield-wall. The helping it commands is available everywhere and almost entirely unremarked upon. It is the father who teaches his child to pray and stays awake to do it. It is the professional who refuses to falsify a report because his work is a trust. It is the person who defends a Muslim colleague's right to pray at work. It is the one who funds the well, the school, the mosque, the orphan — the sūrah will return to this at verse 38. It is the one who corrects a misquotation of the Qur'an in a public forum with patience and evidence rather than fury. It is the one who keeps a family tie that has gone cold, since the sūrah makes the severing of the womb a mark of turning away (v. 22). It is the one who tells a friend the truth he does not want to hear, in the manner the verse's own phrase *"honorable speech"* (v. 21) prescribes.

Every one of these is helping God's religion in the sense the verse intends, and every one of them is followed by the same promise: help, and feet made firm. The person who has done this kind of work knows from experience what the promise means. He has found strength at moments when he had none, openings where there were walls, and a steadiness in situations that made others panic. He has also found that the steadiness was not a feeling of confidence but the absence of the collapse he expected. That is *tathbīt al-aqdām* in its ordinary form: not the absence of shaking ground, but feet that hold.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:8

> **As for those who disbelieve, wretched are they, and He has made their deeds go astray.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Taʿsan Lahum: The Sentence of Ruin**

Verse 1 said of the disbelievers that God made their deeds go astray. Verse 8 says it again — and adds a word of its own. *Wa-lladhīna kafarū fa-taʿsan lahum wa-aḍalla aʿmālahum*: "As for those who disbelieve, wretched are they, and He has made their deeds go astray." The addition is *taʿsan lahum*, and it is one of the most densely loaded phrases in Qur'anic Arabic.

Al-Qurṭubī, as the source commentary records, explains that it is an emphatic expression conveying ruin, sorrow, misery, revilement, destruction, defeat, failure, ignominy, evil, and misfortune. The list is not a translation but an unpacking: the phrase compresses an entire field of calamity into two words. Linguistically *taʿs* comes from the root *t-ʿ-s*, whose sense is to be low, to fall, to be cast down — the opposite of *intaʿasha*, to rise up and recover. The classical lexicographers explain that *taʿisa* describes a man thrown from his mount or fallen on the ground, and *taʿsan lahu* is an invocation of that condition: may he fall and not rise; may he be cast down and find no one to raise him.

The grammatical construction deserves notice. *Taʿsan* is an accusative verbal noun standing in place of a verb, in the same class of construction as *ḍarba al-riqāb* in verse 4 — "a striking of the necks." Arabic uses this form for emphatic declaration, and the effect is that the sentence does not describe wretchedness but pronounces it. It is not a report that these people happen to be in a bad state; it is a verdict delivered in the form of a curse, and the preposition *lahum* — literally "for them" — carries an irony the translation cannot easily hold: *this is what is theirs*, this is their allotment, this is what has been assigned to them.

**Why the Repetition of Verse One?**

The obvious question is why the sūrah returns to verse 1's statement. Six verses of material — the legislation of battle, the promise of guidance, the entry into the Garden made known, the conditional of divine help — intervene between the two, and then the sūrah comes back to where it started and says the same thing with an addition.

The commentators' answer is structural. Verses 4 through 7 were addressed to the believers, and verse 7 in particular ends on a note of enormous encouragement: *if you help God, He will help you and make firm your steps.* A hearer who has just been promised divine support may naturally ask the converse question: and what of those who oppose Him? Are they not also powerful, numerous, wealthy, victorious in skirmishes, seemingly untouched? Verse 8 answers before the doubt has finished forming. The encouragement given to the believers is immediately paired with the verdict upon their opponents, so that the two realities are held in the mind together.

This pairing technique runs through the sūrah and is one of its defining features. Verses 1–2 (deeds lost / deeds covered). Verses 4–8 (the slain will not have their deeds go astray / the disbelievers are wretched and their deeds go astray). Verses 11–12 (God is Master of the believers / the disbelievers eat as cattle eat and their abode is the Fire). Verse 14 (the one on a clear proof / the one whose evil deeds are beautified). Verse 15 (the four rivers / the boiling liquid). Verse 17 (those guided are increased / those sealed are not). The sūrah thinks in contrasts, and verse 3 explains why: *Thus does God set forth for mankind their likenesses.* The contrast is the likeness.

The repetition also has a rhetorical function the Arabic tradition calls *al-takrīr* — reiteration for the sake of fixing the meaning in the heart. A statement heard once may be noted; the same statement heard again, after an interval filled with other material, is remembered. The Qur'an uses reiteration deliberately and defends the practice: *"And We have reiterated in this Qur'an, that haply they may reflect"* (17:41); *"Thus do We reiterate the signs"* (7:58, and many places). Ibn al-Qayyim wrote in *al-Tibyān fī aqsām al-Qurʾān* that repetition in the Qur'an is never mere repetition, because each restatement arrives in a new context with new material around it and therefore says something the first occurrence did not. Here the second occurrence adds *taʿsan lahum* — the note of personal ruin — which the first did not have.

**The Addition: From Lost Deeds to a Ruined Person**

Verse 1 described what happened to the *deeds*. Verse 8 describes what happens to the *people*. The progression is theologically important, because it states the relation between the act and the actor that the whole sūrah is exploring.

A deed that goes astray is a loss. A person who is wretched is a condition. The Qur'an's account of sin is not that isolated acts carry isolated penalties; it is that acts accumulate into character, and character into destiny. Verse 1 says the deeds have gone missing; verse 8 says the owner is ruined — and the second follows from the first. A man whose life's work has wandered off from him, who has spent forty years on projects that arrive nowhere, does not merely lack the products of those years; he lacks the years.

The same progression appears in the sūrah's treatment of the heart. Verse 16 says God has sealed their hearts; verse 23 says He has made them deaf and blind; verse 24 asks whether hearts have locks upon them; verse 25 says Satan has seduced them and they have been granted respite; verse 29 asks whether they suppose God will not expose their rancor. Each statement describes a condition produced by acts, and each is worse than the one before. The Islamic doctrine of the hardening of the heart — developed at length by Ibn al-Qayyim in *al-Dāʾ wa-l-dawāʾ*, "The Disease and the Cure" — is precisely this: sin is not a discrete event but a deposit, and deposits accumulate into a covering, and the covering into a seal, and the seal into a lock. The Qur'an's own vocabulary tracks the stages: *rāna* (a film gathering over, 83:14), *khatama* (a seal pressed upon, 2:7), *ṭabaʿa* (a stamp, 47:16), *aqfāl* (locks, 47:24).

Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī, the great ascetic of Basra (d. 110/728), is reported to have said of this progression:

> *"They kept committing sins until their hearts became blind to them, and then they committed more until the sin became dear to them."*

The wretchedness pronounced in verse 8 is not an arbitrary punishment attached to a technical offense; it is the natural condition of a person who has lost the use of the faculties by which life is lived well.

**The Occasion and the Immediate Audience**

Many commentators connect verse 8 to the aftermath of Badr, when the leading men of Quraysh — Abū Jahl, Umayyah ibn Khalaf, ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah, Shaybah ibn Rabīʿah, al-Walīd ibn ʿUtbah, and others who had led the persecution in Makkah — lay dead on the field, and their bodies were cast into the well of Qalīb. Al-Bukhārī records that the Prophet ﷺ stood at the edge of the well and called them by name:

> *"O Abū Jahl ibn Hishām, O Umayyah ibn Khalaf, O ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah, O Shaybah ibn Rabīʿah! Have you found what your Lord promised you to be true? For I have found what my Lord promised me to be true."* ʿUmar said, "O Messenger of God, how do you speak to bodies in which there are no spirits?" He said: *"By Him in Whose Hand is my soul, you do not hear what I say better than they do — only they cannot reply."* (al-Bukhārī 3976; Muslim 2873, from Ibn ʿUmar)

Whatever the precise occasion of verse 8, this is the historical moment the verse describes: men who had been the most powerful figures in Arabia, whose deeds — their poetry, their alliances, their campaigns, their persecution of the believers — had been made to go astray, and who were now in a well. The phrase *taʿsan lahum* was heard by the Companions in a world where its referents were visible. The Companions did not need to be persuaded that the mighty can fall; they had walked past the well.

**The Two Sides of One Sentence**

The verse's own construction places the curse and the nullification in a single sentence joined by *wa*, and the order matters: *wretched are they, AND He has made their deeds go astray.* The wretchedness is stated first. This is the reverse of verse 1, where the nullification of the deeds came first and was the whole of the statement.

The reversal suggests a causal reading that the commentators offer: their wretchedness is *because* their deeds have gone astray, and it is also expressed *in* that going astray. A person whose work does not arrive anywhere is wretched in the most literal sense, whatever his external circumstances. This is why the Qur'an can describe wealthy, powerful, militarily successful people as wretched, and can describe a persecuted minority living in mud-brick houses on the edge of a desert as the recipients of divine help. The assessment is not based on visible conditions.

The Prophet ﷺ stated the same reversal in a saying preserved by Muslim:

> *"The world is a prison for the believer and a paradise for the disbeliever."* (Muslim 2956, from Abū Hurayrah)

The believer in a prison is free in the dimension that counts; the disbeliever in a paradise is imprisoned in it, because everything he has will be taken and nothing he has done will arrive. Verse 8 is the second half of that saying rendered as a verdict.

**Reading the Verse Against Despair and Against Contempt**

Two errors face a reader of this verse, and the tradition has always warned against both.

The first is despair — the believer who reads *taʿsan lahum* and wonders whether it applies to him, because he knows his own deeds are imperfect and his own heart is unstable. The answer the sūrah gives is in its own structure: verse 8 is about those who *disbelieve*, and verse 2 is about those who *believe and act righteously*. The dividing line is not the absence of failure but the presence of faith and of effort in its direction. The believer's deeds are covered over, not lost (v. 2), and the promise of verse 7 is addressed to him precisely because he is trying to help God's religion and knows he is doing it badly. The verse that pronounces ruin upon the disbeliever is, for the believer, a description of a road he is not on.

The second is contempt — the reader who uses the verse as a license to despise particular people. This the sūrah forbids by its own verse 1's past tense, which leaves the door of repentance open, and by verse 34, which specifies that the unforgivable condition is *dying* in disbelief: *"Truly those who disbelieve and turn from the way of God, then die while they are disbelievers, God will not forgive them."* The verdict of verse 8 is a verdict upon a state, not upon a named soul. Every person alive is still in the state's reach in either direction. The sūrah itself supplies the illustration: Khālid ibn al-Walīd, the most effective cavalry commander ever fielded against the Muslims, who broke their line at Uḥud, embraced Islam in 7/628 and died a Muslim in 19/642 — and the Prophet ﷺ said of him, as recorded by Aḥmad and al-Tirmidhī:

> *"What a good servant of God is Khālid ibn al-Walīd — a sword among the swords of God."* (al-Tirmidhī 3846; Aḥmad, graded ḥasan)

A man about whom verse 8 could have been spoken in 3 AH became a man about whom that saying was spoken in 8 AH. The verse describes the road; the people are still walking.

**The Wretchedness That Is Invisible**

The deepest force of *taʿsan lahum* lies in the fact that the wretchedness it pronounces is not felt by the wretched. This is the theme the sūrah develops at verse 12 — *"those who disbelieve enjoy themselves and eat as cattle eat"* — and at verse 14 — *"one whose evil deeds are made to seem fair unto him."* The person under the verdict of verse 8 may be having a good year. He may be prosperous, healthy, popular, and pleased with himself. He may be, in the Qur'an's own phrase, *"rejoicing in what they have of knowledge"* (40:83) and surrounded by what he considers evidence of divine favor.

The Qur'an names this condition *istidrāj* — being led on by degrees: *"And those who deny Our signs — We shall lead them on by degrees from where they know not. And I grant them respite; truly My plan is firm"* (7:182–183); *"Do they think that what We provide them of wealth and children — We hasten unto them in good deeds? No, but they do not perceive"* (23:55–56). And the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"When you see God giving a servant from the world what he loves while he persists in disobedience, that is only a leading on by degrees (istidrāj)."* Then he recited: *"So when they forgot that by which they were reminded, We opened unto them the gates of every thing, until when they rejoiced in what they were given, We seized them suddenly, and lo, they were in despair."* (6:44) (Aḥmad; al-Ṭabarānī, from ʿUqbah ibn ʿĀmir)

Verse 8's pronouncement is therefore not a prediction about how the disbeliever will feel in this world. It is a statement about what he is. The fallen man who does not know he has fallen is fallen all the same, and the phrase *taʿsan lahum* is spoken over him by the One who can see the ground. This is why the sūrah immediately follows with verse 10 — *"Have they not journeyed upon the earth and observed how those before them fared in the end?"* — supplying the visible evidence for an invisible verdict: the ruined towns along the caravan route are the proof that wretchedness is real even while it is unfelt.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:9

> **That is because they were averse to that which God sent down, so He made their deeds come to naught.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reason Beneath the Reason**

Verse 8 pronounced wretchedness and the loss of deeds; verse 9 gives the cause, and the cause it gives is not what a modern reader expects. It is not that they were ignorant, or that they lacked evidence, or that they were born into a tradition that misinformed them. It is that *they were averse*: *dhālika bi-annahum karihū mā anzala Allāhu fa-aḥbaṭa aʿmālahum* — "that is because they were averse to that which God sent down, so He made their deeds come to naught."

The verb *kariha*, from the root *k-r-h*, means to dislike, to be averse to, to find something repugnant or burdensome. It denotes not ignorance but *disinclination*. The person described here has encountered the revelation and has a reaction to it — and the reaction is negative at the level of appetite rather than at the level of information. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the aversion described implies aversion to all revealed books and religions, not merely to the Qur'an: the same disposition that rejected Muhammad ﷺ rejected Moses and Jesus, and would reject the next messenger if there were one.

This diagnosis is one of the Qur'an's most consistent and most uncomfortable claims about unbelief: that its root is usually not intellectual but volitional. The Qur'an repeatedly describes people who *know* and yet reject — *"They denied them wrongfully and arrogantly, while their souls were convinced of them"* (27:14); *"And they recognized it, but they rejected it wrongfully and in arrogance"* (2:146 is of a related sense); *"Is it then My speech that you marvel at, and laugh and do not weep?"* (53:59–60). It describes the reaction of the Qurayshī chiefs not as confusion but as offense: *"Is the Reminder sent down unto him among us?"* (38:8) — the objection is not to the content but to the choice of recipient. And it describes the emotional register of the rejection with remarkable precision: *"And when you recite the Qur'an, We place between you and those who do not believe in the Hereafter a hidden barrier"* (17:45), and *"They say: Our hearts are wrapped. Rather, God has cursed them for their disbelief"* (2:88).

Aversion, in other words, is treated as a moral fact before it is a cognitive one. The verse does not say "they were mistaken"; it says "they disliked." This matters enormously for how a Muslim understands disagreement about religion, because it means that the most effective response to aversion is not always more argument. Argument addresses the intellect; aversion lives in the appetite, and the appetite is reached by other means — by character, by beauty, by kindness, by the visible goodness of the believer's life, by patience with hostility. The Qur'an's own instruction to its Messenger in the face of aversion is repeatedly a command about conduct rather than about rhetoric: *"Repel with that which is better, and lo, he between whom and you was enmity shall become as though he were a devoted friend"* (41:34); *"Go, both of you, unto Pharaoh; truly he has transgressed. And speak unto him a gentle word, that haply he may take heed or fear"* (20:43–44).

**Aḥbaṭa: The Swollen Animal**

Verse 9 introduces the second of the sūrah's two verbs of nullification: *aḥbaṭa aʿmālahum*, "He made their deeds come to naught." The verb comes from *ḥ-b-ṭ*, and its literal meaning is one of the most vivid images in the Qur'anic vocabulary of failure. The Arabs used *ḥabiṭa* of an animal that grazes upon a poisonous plant — *al-ḥubāṭ* — and swells: the belly distends, the creature appears bloated and fat, and then it dies. The disease was known to the bedouin herders because it killed camels that looked, from a distance, to be thriving.

The commentators — al-Zamakhsharī, al-Rāzī, Ibn al-Jawzī — record this etymology, and it explains the choice of verb in a way that no translation fully conveys. To say of a deed that it is *ḥābiṭ* is to say that it *looked* like something and was not. The deed had volume, presence, a shape that impressed observers; it had, in fact, everything except life. The verse is therefore not merely a statement that the disbelievers will not be rewarded. It is a statement that their works were already dead while they were being performed, and that the swelling was the first symptom.

This is why the Qur'an pairs *ḥubūṭ* with appearance so often, and why verse 12 of this sūrah immediately supplies the image: those who disbelieve *"enjoy themselves and eat as cattle eat"* — the swollen animal at the trough, consuming, and the consumption not becoming nourishment of the kind that matters. The sūrah's whole indictment is contained in the pairing of the two images: the deeds swell and the doers eat, and neither produces anything.

**The Distinction Between Aḍalla and Aḥbaṭa**

The sūrah uses both verbs and, as noted in the introduction, they are not synonyms. *Aḍalla* (vv. 1, 8) is from *ḍ-l-l*, to stray, to be lost: the deed wanders away from its owner and never reaches its destination. *Aḥbaṭa* (vv. 9, 28, 32) is from *ḥ-b-ṭ*: the deed swells, spoils, and perishes in place.

The difference is instructive. A lost deed is a deed that went somewhere and could not be found; a ruined deed is a deed that never had substance to begin with. The two verbs together cover the complete range of religious failure. Some people's works are lost because they directed them to the wrong end — they labored, and the labor went to a destination that cannot pay. Other people's works are ruined because they were corrupt in themselves — performed for show, performed in disobedience, performed with a heart that disliked the thing it was doing. The first is an error of direction; the second is an error of substance. Verse 9 assigns the second to those who were averse to revelation, and the assignment is logical: a person who dislikes what God sent down will perform deeds shaped by that dislike, and the shape will be visible in the deed however impressive its scale.

Al-Rāzī draws the connection explicitly in his commentary on this verse: the nullification follows the aversion as an effect follows its cause, because a deed is only sound when the intention behind it is sound, and the intention cannot be sound in a heart that is averse to its source. Ibn Taymiyyah's formulation of the same principle in his writings on sincerity is that the deed is to the intention as the body is to the spirit: remove the spirit and the body becomes carrion, however well-formed it was.

**Where Else the Qur'an Speaks of Nullified Deeds**

The source commentary lists the parallel passages, and the list is worth reading through, because it shows how systematic the doctrine is:

> *"And whoever of you turns away from his religion and dies while a disbeliever — those are they whose deeds have come to naught in this world and the Hereafter, and those are the companions of the Fire, therein abiding."* (2:217)

> *"Truly those who disbelieve in the signs of God and kill the prophets without right, and kill those among mankind who command justice — give them glad tidings of a painful punishment. Those are they whose deeds have come to naught in this world and the Hereafter, and they shall have no helpers."* (3:21–22)

> *"Say: Shall We inform you of those who are the greatest losers in deeds — those whose effort goes astray in the life of this world while they suppose that they are doing good?"* (18:103–104)

> *"And it was revealed unto thee and unto those before thee: If thou associate partners with God, thy deed shall surely come to naught, and thou shalt surely be among the losers."* (39:65)

The last of these is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ himself in the hypothetical form, and it establishes the principle in its most absolute terms: even the deeds of the greatest of the Prophets would be nullified by association of partners with God. The rule is not about persons but about the relation between the act and its Lord.

Two further passages supply the imagery. Of the disbelievers' works on the Day of Judgment:

> *"And We shall turn to whatever deeds they did, and We shall make them as dust scattered."* (25:23)

And of the miser who spends without faith:

> *"The likeness of what they spend in this life of this world is the likeness of a wind wherein is a biting cold that strikes the harvest of a people who wronged themselves, and destroys it. God wronged them not, but they wrong themselves."* (3:117)

The wind with the biting cold is the frost that kills a crop at the moment it was about to ripen: the farmer's whole year of labor destroyed in one night, and the destruction caused not by God's injustice but by the crop's exposure. The image is the agricultural counterpart of the *ḥubāṭ*: in both cases what looked like a harvest was never going to be one.

**The Two Worlds of Nullification**

Verse 9's parallel at 2:217 and 3:22 adds a specification the source commentary notes: God makes their deeds come to naught *in this world and in the Hereafter*. The nullification is not postponed.

In this world it operates in ways that are visible to anyone who looks. The person averse to revelation loses the guidance that would have ordered his life; he loses the peace that comes from a settled relation to his Maker; he loses the framework that would have made his suffering meaningful; he loses the community that would have carried him in grief. He may gain wealth, position, and pleasure — the sūrah acknowledges this at verse 12, *"those who disbelieve enjoy themselves"* — but the gaining does not amount to a life that arrives anywhere. Verse 13 of this sūrah supplies the historical proof: towns greater in strength than Makkah were destroyed, and they had enjoyed themselves too.

In the Hereafter the nullification is total and final: there is no credit, no reduction of the sentence, no partial recognition. This is the hardest part of the doctrine for the modern conscience, and the honest response to it is not to soften it but to state its conditions accurately. The nullification applies to the one who dies in the state of aversion, as verse 34 specifies; it does not apply to the one who turns, as verse 2 specifies. It applies to the one who *rejected* what he recognized, as verse 25 describes (*"those who turn their backs after guidance has been made clear to them"*); the tradition has always distinguished this from the one who never received the message in a form capable of being recognized, and the great majority of classical scholars held such a person to be among those tested on the Day of Judgment rather than among those condemned in advance. God does not punish before the messenger comes: *"And We never punish until We send a messenger"* (17:15); *"Lest you should say: There came to us no bringer of glad tidings and no warner"* (5:19).

**The Reflected Case: Aversion Within the Community**

The verse speaks of those who were averse to what God sent down, and its immediate referents are the polytheists of Makkah and the opponents of the Prophet ﷺ. But the Islamic tradition has never limited the verse's application to them, and the reason is that aversion to revelation exists in graduated forms within every believing community.

There is the aversion of the person who accepts the whole revelation except the part that costs him — and the Qur'an names this directly: *"Do you, then, believe in part of the Book and disbelieve in part? What is the reward of those who do so among you, but disgrace in the life of this world, and on the Day of Resurrection they shall be returned unto the most severe punishment?"* (2:85). There is the aversion to a specific ruling, which the tradition distinguishes carefully from struggling with it: the believer who finds a command hard and still obeys is not the one described here; the believer who decides the command does not apply is. There is the aversion to hearing a matter discussed — the changing of the subject, the joke that ends the conversation, the irritation at the one who raises it. And there is the aversion the sūrah itself diagnoses at verse 16, which is not rejection but inattention: the person who listens, leaves, and asks a companion, "What did he say?"

The pastoral use of verse 9 is therefore not to identify others but to examine the appetite. Ibn al-Qayyim's advice in *al-Fawāʾid* is direct: watch what you dislike hearing, because your dislikes are a map of your heart's condition. The thing a person cannot bear to have said to him is usually the thing he most needs said to him, and the degree of his aversion is a measure of the ground the aversion has taken. Verse 9 states the consequence without softening: the deeds of the averse come to naught. The nullification begins in this world, in the loss of the guidance the person cannot bear to receive.

**The Cure the Sūrah Offers**

The sūrah does not leave the diagnosis without the treatment, and the treatment is stated at verse 17: *"But those who are guided, He increases them in guidance and grants them their reverence."* Against the aversion to revelation, the Qur'an prescribes the increase of guidance — not a single decision but a cumulative process — and *taqwā*, the reverent guarding that verse 15 makes the qualification for the Garden. The person who finds a command repugnant is not told to wait until he feels differently; he is told to obey and to keep company with the revelation until the aversion is worn down by acquaintance.

This is a principle with wide application, and the Prophet ﷺ stated it in the report preserved by al-Bukhārī from Abū Hurayrah, in the ḥadīth qudsī:

> *"God said: Whoever shows enmity to a friend of Mine, I have declared war upon him. And My servant does not draw near to Me with anything more beloved to Me than what I have made obligatory upon him; and My servant continues to draw near to Me with supererogatory works until I love him. And when I love him, I am his hearing with which he hears, his sight with which he sees, his hand with which he strikes, and his foot with which he walks."* (al-Bukhārī 6502)

The mechanism described is exactly the reversal of verse 9. The person who performs the obligatory acts — initially perhaps against his inclination — is drawn nearer until the inclination changes and the act becomes beloved. The aversion is not the final word about the heart; it is the starting condition that practice alters. Verse 9 pronounces the ruin of the one who lets the aversion govern him. The whole of the Qur'anic discipline of practice exists so that no one has to.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:10

> **Have they not journeyed upon the earth and observed how those before them fared in the end? God destroyed them; and the disbelievers shall have the like thereof.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to Travel and Look**

Verse 9 stated an inner condition — aversion — and verse 10 turns the reader outward, to the ground. *Afa-lam yasīrū fī al-arḍi fa-yanẓurū kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu alladhīna min qablihim*: "Have they not journeyed upon the earth and observed how those before them fared in the end?" The interrogative *a-* plus the negative *lam* produces the rhetorical question whose expected answer is "yes, they have" — and the reproach lies in the fact that having journeyed, they did not observe.

The Qur'an's phrase *sīrū fī al-arḍ* — "journey in the earth" — is one of its most distinctive idioms, and the source commentary rightly notes that it is repeated many times: 6:11; 12:109; 16:36; 22:46; 27:69; 29:20; 30:9, 42; 35:44; 40:21, 82; 47:10. Sometimes it is an imperative: *"Say: Journey in the earth and see how was the end of those who denied"* (6:11). Sometimes it is the reproach of this verse: have they not journeyed? The audience of the revelation were traders whose caravans ran from Yemen through Makkah to Gaza, passing the ruins of the destroyed peoples on both legs of the journey. They did not need to be told the ruins were there; they camped beside them. The verse is addressed to people who had seen the evidence and drawn no conclusion.

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī note that *those before them* is understood as a reference to the ruins left by previous tribes destroyed despite being of greater might than those who opposed the Prophet ﷺ, with the cross-references at 35:44 and 40:21, 82:

> *"Have they not journeyed in the earth and observed how was the end of those before them? They were greater than them in strength, and they tilled the earth and built upon it more than these have built, and their messengers came unto them with clear signs. And God would not wrong them, but they wronged themselves."* (30:9)

> *"Have they not journeyed in the earth and observed how was the end of those before them? They were more numerous than these, and greater in strength, and in the monuments they left upon the earth. What they earned availed them not."* (40:82)

The repeated specification — greater in strength, more numerous, more skilled in construction — is not incidental. It removes the most common excuse for ignoring a warning, which is that it applied to weaker people in easier circumstances. The ruins they passed were the ruins of the strong.

**Kayfa Kāna ʿĀqibah: The Question of the End**

The verse directs the observation to a specific feature: *ʿāqibah*, the end, the outcome, the final result. The word comes from *ʿ-q-b*, the root of the heel and of what follows — *ʿaqiba* means to come after, to succeed. The *ʿāqibah* of a matter is what comes after it, and in Qur'anic usage it means the final state: *"And the end belongs to the reverent"* (7:128; 20:132); *"As for those who are wretched, they shall be in the Fire"* (11:106); *"And the ʿāqibah is for the reverent"* (28:83).

The choice of this word is the analytical heart of the verse. The Qur'an does not ask the traveler to look at the *beginnings* of the destroyed peoples — their wealth, their poetry, their military power, their architecture. It asks him to look at their *ends*. The reason is a methodological one that any student of evidence recognizes: a process is only understood by its termination. A building in mid-construction tells you nothing certain about whether it will stand; a building that has stood for four hundred years and then collapsed tells you everything. The Qur'an directs the observer to the completed data.

This is why verse 22:46 supplies the full psychological analysis of the observation:

> *"Have they not journeyed in the earth, and have hearts with which to understand and ears with which to hear? For truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but it is hearts within breasts that go blind."* (22:46)

The verse locates the organ of seeing in the heart rather than the eye, and it makes the journeying useless without the heart's participation. A caravan can pass Madaʾin Ṣāliḥ a hundred times and remain blind. The blindness is not optical; it is the condition the sūrah describes at verse 23 (*"making them deaf and blinding their sight"*) and at verse 24 (*"or do hearts have their locks upon them?"*).

**Dammara Allāhu ʿAlayhim: The Verb of Total Destruction**

*"God destroyed them"* renders *dammara Allāhu ʿalayhum*. The verb *dammara* is the doubled intensive of *d-m-r*, and the preposition *ʿalā* — "upon them" — is significant: the destruction came down upon them from above. Al-Rāghib explains *tadmīr* as the destruction of a thing together with its traces, so that nothing remains to be rebuilt. The Qur'an uses the same verb of the peoples of ʿĀd and Thamūd and of Pharaoh: *"And We destroyed what they were making, and what they used to build"* (27:36, of the people of Pharaoh); *"And how many a town We destroyed while it was wrongdoing, so it fell in upon its roofs, and how many a well abandoned and a lofty palace!"* (22:45).

The historical referents were well known to the audience. ʿĀd, the people of Hūd, with their pillars — *"Iram of the pillars, the like of which was never created in the lands"* (89:7–8) — whose ruins the caravans passed in the Ḥaḍramawt. Thamūd, the people of Ṣāliḥ, who *"carved houses out of the mountains"* (26:149), whose rock-cut tombs at al-Ḥijr — Madaʾin Ṣāliḥ, today's al-ʿUlā — stood directly on the caravan road between Makkah and Syria. The people of Lot, whose cities were overthrown. Midian, the people of Shuʿayb. Pharaoh, whose monuments the audience knew from the reports of Egypt. Each was a civilization with architecture, agriculture, military power, and a sense of permanence; each ended in *tadmīr*.

The Prophet ﷺ gave his Companions the practical ruling for passing through such places, recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim on the occasion of the expedition to Tabūk, when the army passed al-Ḥijr:

> *"Do not enter upon these people who were punished except while weeping; for if you do not weep, then do not enter upon them, lest what befell them befall you."* (al-Bukhārī 3380; Muslim 2980, from Ibn ʿUmar)

And in the version of al-Bukhārī he ordered the dough the Companions had kneaded with water from the well of Thamūd to be given to the riding animals, and the water drawn from that well to be poured out, except the water of the well of the she-camel. The instruction contains the whole theology of verse 10: the ruins are to be looked at, and the looking is to produce weeping, and the weeping is the heart's participation in the observation. A person who walks through the tombs of Thamūd and feels only archaeological interest has journeyed in the earth and not observed.

**Wa-Lil-Kāfirīna Amthāluhā: The Likeness Returns**

The final clause — *"and the disbelievers shall have the like thereof"* — *wa-li-l-kāfirīna amthāluhā* — completes the verse by projecting the pattern forward. The destroyed peoples had an end; the present disbelievers will have *the like of it*. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī read this as meaning they will suffer a fate like that of those before them.

The word used is *amthāl*, the plural of *mathal* — likeness, similitude, parable, precedent — and it is the same word that appeared at verse 3: *"Thus does God set forth for mankind their likenesses"* (*amthālahum*). The recurrence is deliberate and it does important work. In verse 3, *amthāl* referred to the likenesses God strikes for mankind — the parables by which the difference between the followers of truth and the followers of falsehood is made visible. In verse 10, *amthāl* refers to the likeness that awaits the disbelievers: the same fate, the same pattern, the same end. The word thereby joins the two meanings of *mathal* that English separates — a parable is a likeness in speech, and a precedent is a likeness in events. The destroyed towns are both: they are parables in the Qur'an's telling, and they are precedents in history, and their double function is what makes the argument work.

The Qur'an states this principle of recurrence explicitly:

> *"That is because God would not change a blessing He has bestowed upon a people until they change what is in themselves; and truly God is Hearing, Knowing.
