[Theirs is] the like of the custom of Pharaoh's people and those before them: they denied the signs of their Lord, so We destroyed them for their sins, and We drowned Pharaoh's people — and all of them were wrongdoers."* (8:53–54)

And: *"The custom of God with those who passed away before — and you will not find any change in the custom of God"* (33:62); *"The custom of God who has sent before — and you will not find in the custom of God any alteration"* (48:23). *Sunnat Allāh* — the custom, the way, the established procedure of God — is a fixed term in the Qur'an, and verse 10 is an appeal to it. History is not a series of unrelated accidents; it is a governed process with a regularity that can be observed by anyone who journeys and looks.

**The Logic of Historical Evidence in the Qur'an**

Why does the Qur'an argue from history rather than only from metaphysics? The reason is epistemic, and it is worth stating because it makes the verse available to every reader in every age.

Metaphysical arguments can be disputed endlessly, because their premises are not visible. Historical outcomes cannot. A person who rejects the argument for the soul's immortality has lost nothing by rejecting it; a person who ignores the record of destroyed civilizations has ignored data. The Qur'an's method here is empirical in the strict sense: it points at observable phenomena — ruins along a trade route — and asks the observer to draw the inference the phenomena support. The inference is not that every prosperous civilization will be destroyed by an earthquake or a wind, but that prosperity without reverence does not confer permanence, and that the peoples who were most confident of their permanence were the ones whose ruins the audience was standing beside.

There is a further layer, and it is the one Ibn Khaldūn (d. 808/1406) built an entire science upon. In his *Muqaddimah* he took the Qur'anic command to journey in the earth and observe the ends of previous peoples as the methodological warrant for the study of history as a discipline with laws — what he called *ʿilm al-ʿumrān*, the science of civilization. He argues that civilizations have lifecycles, that luxury softens the qualities that produced strength, that the collapse follows a predictable pattern, and that the historian who studies the pattern can read his own society's position within it. Ibn Khaldūn understood himself to be doing what verse 10 commands: journeying, observing the *ʿāqibah*, and drawing the conclusion for those still on the road.

**The Modern Reader's Journey**

The audience of this verse had physical ruins within a fortnight's walk. The modern reader has something more extensive and more difficult to interpret: the entire recorded history of the species, available without travel. Every reader of this verse lives in a world where the *ʿāqibah* of empires is documented in detail — Rome, Persia, the ʿAbbāsids, the Andalusian caliphate, the Ottomans, and the twentieth century's successive superpowers, each of which was certain of its permanence while it lasted.

The observation the verse demands is not antiquarian. It is the observation of a pattern that is visible in the smallest units as well as the largest: the family that prospered and then scattered because its members stopped keeping the ties the sūrah commands at verse 22; the business that grew and then failed because its owners stopped observing the limits; the individual who built a life on a foundation of aversion to what God sent down and watched it swell and spoil, exactly as the verb *aḥbaṭa* describes. The *mathal* — the likeness — operates at every scale, because the *sunnah* operates at every scale.

What the verse asks is therefore not difficult, and it is not optional. It asks the reader to look at endings rather than beginnings. Most human beings evaluate their choices by how those choices feel at the start: the new position, the new relationship, the new country, the new practice, the new abandonment of an old constraint. The Qur'an asks the reader to evaluate by how things end, and to gather that information from the record of those who have already ended. This is the entire discipline of what the tradition calls *al-naẓar fī al-ʿawāqib* — considering outcomes — and it is the mark of wisdom in Islamic ethics. The Prophet ﷺ said, as recorded by al-Tirmidhī:

> *"The wise man is the one who calls himself to account and works for what comes after death; and the helpless man is the one who follows his own caprice and hopes upon God."* (al-Tirmidhī 2459, from Shaddād ibn Aws)

The verse closes with a warning shaped as a statement of fact: *the disbelievers shall have the like thereof.* The likeness is not a threat issued in anger; it is a pattern reported by a witness who has seen it operate before. The towns are still along the road. The command is still to journey and look.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:11

> **That is because God is Master of those who believe, and because the disbelievers have no master.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mawlā: The Word That Holds Many Meanings at Once**

Verse 10 ended with the destruction of the disbelieving peoples and the promise of the like for those who follow them. Verse 11 asks why, and answers with a single word: *mawlā*. *Dhālika bi-anna Allāha mawlā alladhīna āmanū wa-anna al-kāfirīna lā mawlā lahum* — "That is because God is Master of those who believe, and because the disbelievers have no master."

*Mawlā* is one of the richest and most contested words in Arabic. Al-Rāghib al-Iṣfahānī and the lexicographers after him enumerate its senses: master, lord, owner; patron, protector, guardian; helper, supporter, ally; benefactor and the one benefited; the one who frees a slave and the slave who is freed; close kin, cousin, nephew; heir; neighbor; friend; son-in-law. The word runs the entire range from the top of a relationship to the bottom of it, and it does so because its root *w-l-y* denotes *proximity* — being near, being next to, being the one who stands beside. A *walī* is one who is close, and closeness in the social world produces every one of the listed relations: the one close to you in power protects you, the one close to you in blood inherits from you, the one close to you in affection helps you.

Applied to God, the word carries its highest sense: the one whose nearness is absolute, whose protection is effective, whose help cannot fail. The Qur'an uses it of God repeatedly:

> *"God is your Master, and He is the best of masters and the best of helpers."* (8:40)

> *"And if you both turn unto God, then truly your hearts have inclined; but if you back each other against him, then truly God is his Master, and Gabriel, and the righteous among the believers, and the angels after that are his helpers."* (66:4)

> *"Say: Nothing befalls us except what God has decreed for us; He is our Master, and upon God let the believers rely."* (9:51)

The believers' own supplication in al-Baqarah closes with the same word: *"You are our Master, so help us against the disbelieving people"* (2:286).

**The Symmetry That Is Not a Symmetry**

The verse is built as a balanced sentence — *because God is Master of those who believe, and because the disbelievers have no master* — but the balance conceals an asymmetry that is the whole of its meaning. The first half names an actual relation with an actual party. The second half names the absence of a relation, and the absence is absolute: *lā mawlā lahum* — there is no master for them. Not a weaker master, not a false master that will disappoint them, but *no* master.

Al-Qurṭubī draws the implication recorded in the source commentary: anyone who does not recognize God as master, protector, and helper will have no master or protector, since they will find no protector or helper for themselves apart from God. The Qur'an states the same conclusion in a series of rhetorical questions:

> *"Say: Who will protect you from God if He intends evil for you, or intends mercy for you? And they will find, besides God, no protector and no helper."* (33:17)

> *"Or who could protect you from Him?"* (67:20)

> *"And they have no protector or helper besides God"* (42:31, in the context of the Day when the punishment arrives).

The logic is not that God refuses to protect the disbeliever as an act of retaliation. It is that protection is a relation, and a relation requires two parties. The person who has declined the relation has not been deprived of it by the other party; he has removed himself from it, and there is no third party available to step in, because there is no power other than His. This is why the Qur'an describes the idols and patrons of the disbelievers with such relentless deflation: *"And those whom they call upon besides Him do not possess anything, not even a membrane on a date-stone"* (35:13); *"Say: Call upon those you supposed besides Him — they have no power to remove harm from you, nor to change it"* (17:56).

**The Occasion at Uḥud: A Shouted Argument**

Al-Qurṭubī records a specific occasion for the verse, and the story is one of the most vivid in the whole of the sīrah literature. At the Battle of Uḥud (3/625), after the archers left their posts and Khālid ibn al-Walīd's cavalry came around the mountain, the Muslims were broken and the idolaters gained the upper hand. Al-Bukhārī records from al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib:

> *"When the idolaters gained the upper hand over the Muslims, they shouted from afar: A day for a day — we have ʿUzzā and you have no ʿUzzā!"* The Prophet ﷺ said: *"Answer them."* They said, "What shall we say?" He said: *"Say: God is our Master and you have no master."* (al-Bukhārī 4043)

The exchange is worth examining closely, because it shows what the verse is actually about. The Qurayshī slogan — *yawm bi-yawm*, "a day for a day" — refers to Badr: this day balances that day. Their theological claim follows: *we have ʿUzzā and you have no ʿUzzā.* ʿUzzā was the goddess enshrined at Nakhlah near Makkah, one of the three chief deities of the Quraysh alongside al-Lāt and Manāt, and the Qur'an names her with contempt: *"Have you considered al-Lāt and al-ʿUzzā, and Manāt, the third, the other?"* (53:19–20).

The claim being made was not merely that they had a patron the Muslims lacked. It was that the battlefield result proved it: we are winning today, therefore our goddess is real and yours is not. This is the argument from success that human beings make in every age, and the Prophet ﷺ's instructed reply refuses the premise without denying the fact. He does not tell the Companions to shout "we are winning" — they were not. He tells them to shout the statement of verse 11: *God is our Master, and you have no master.* The truth of the claim is not indexed to the day's events.

Al-Qurṭubī holds that verse 11 was revealed on that day; the majority hold the sūrah is Madinan without that specification, and the report functions in any case as the perfect illustration. The believers had been routed on the field and were shouting the sentence anyway, because the sentence was about a relation and not about a result.

**What It Means to Have No Master**

The disbeliever's condition as the verse describes it is not primarily one of guilt but of *abandonment* — and the abandonment is self-inflicted. To have no *mawlā* is to stand in the world without anyone whose nearness guarantees your protection. The Qur'an pictures this state in a series of images.

The man whose idols fail him at the moment of need: *"And when harm touches mankind, they call upon their Lord, turning unto Him; then when He lets them taste mercy from Him, lo, a party of them associate partners with their Lord"* (30:33). The temporary return to God in crisis, and the abandonment of Him in relief, is itself a description of having no master — the person who calls on anyone available and trusts none of them.

The man alone in the dark: *"Or like deep darkness in an abysmal sea, covered by waves, above which are waves, above which are clouds — darkness upon darkness; when he puts forth his hand he can hardly see it. And he for whom God appoints no light, for him there is no light"* (24:40). The verse ends with the same negation as verse 11: no light appointed, no master appointed, and therefore nothing to see by and no one to stand beside.

The man who discovers the absence too late: *"And they will come forth unto their Lord... and the disbelievers will say: Would that we were dust"* (78:40); *"And the affair has been decided, and they will say: Is there any way of return?"* (42:44).

The practical consequence in this life is one the tradition describes constantly: anxiety. A person with no *mawlā* must supply for himself everything a patron supplies — protection, provision, meaning, and the assurance that the universe is not indifferent. This is why the Qur'an presents reliance (*tawakkul*) as the natural state of the believer and its absence as the natural misery of the other: *"Upon God let the believers rely"* (3:122, 160; 5:23; 9:51). Ibn Taymiyyah observed in his discussions of the heart's attachment that the human soul cannot function without something it relies upon, and that the soul which has not attached itself to God attaches itself to created things, which then fail it, and the failure is experienced as the instability of the soul itself. The person with no master is not free; he is unprotected, and he feels it.

**God as Master of Those Who Believe**

The first half of the verse is a statement of enormous comfort, and the preposition matters: God is *mawlā* of those who believe — not of everyone in the same way. He is the Creator, Provider, and Sustainer of all, and His lordship (*rubūbiyyah*) is universal. But *walāyah* — the nearness of protection, help, and friendship — is specified in the Qur'an as a relation with the believers: *"God is the Master of those who believe; He brings them forth from the darkness into the light. And as for those who disbelieve, their masters are the false deities; they bring them forth from the light into the darkness"* (2:257).

The contrast in 2:257 is exact and complete: the believers have a Master who moves them toward light; the disbelievers have masters who move them toward darkness. Verse 11's *lā mawlā lahum* — they have no master — should be read alongside 2:257's statement that their masters are the false deities. The resolution is that the false patrons are not masters in the real sense, because they cannot protect; the person who has them has, for practical purposes, nothing. The Qur'an's phrase at 22:73 makes the point with a devastating image:

> *"O mankind! A parable is set forth, so listen to it: truly those whom you call upon besides God will never create a fly, even if they gathered together for it. And if the fly snatched something from them, they could not recover it from it. Weak is the seeker and the sought."* (22:73)

A patron who cannot retrieve a crumb from a fly is not a patron. *Lā mawlā lahum* is not a deprivation; it is a statement of what they actually have.

The believer's position is therefore not a matter of feeling protected but of being protected, and the difference between the two is the difference between verse 11 and the anxiety that ignores it. Al-Bukhārī records the Prophet's ﷺ statement about the certainty of the divine patronage:

> *"Know that if the whole community gathered to benefit you, they could not benefit you except with something God has written for you; and if they gathered to harm you, they could not harm you except with something God has written against you. The pens have been lifted and the pages have dried."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516, from Ibn ʿAbbās; graded ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ)

This is the operative content of having a Master: not that nothing will happen to you, but that nothing can happen to you outside a governance that is on your side.

**The Verse and the Community's Self-Understanding**

Verse 11 closes the sūrah's second movement (vv. 8–11), which began with wretchedness and ended with the absence of a patron. The four verses form an argument: the disbelievers are wretched (v. 8) because they were averse to revelation and their deeds came to naught (v. 9); their predecessors were destroyed and they will have the like (v. 10); and the reason for both is that they have no master while the believers do (v. 11).

For the community of Madinah, which was outnumbered, out-funded, and under continuous threat, the verse was not a theological abstraction but the ground of its morale. The believers had lost their homes, their property, and their kin in Makkah. Their patron was not a tribe — the tribal patronage system of Arabia was exactly the institution they had left behind, and verse 22 will address the rupture of kinship ties directly. Their patron was God. This is what made the emigration possible and what made the community's survival explicable to itself.

The same function is available to the individual reader. Every human being has moments in which the ordinary supports fail: the employer who does not renew, the friend who disappears, the diagnosis that arrives, the child who will not answer. Verse 11 is the sūrah's answer to those moments, and it is not an answer about the removal of the difficulty. It is an answer about the presence of a Master in it. The disbelievers have no master — and the sentence is spoken not to gloat but to make the believer aware of what he has, at the moment when it does not look like much.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:12

> **Truly God causes those who believe and perform righteous deeds to enter Gardens with rivers running below, while those who disbelieve enjoy themselves and eat as cattle eat; and the Fire shall be an abode for them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Appetites**

Verse 11 stated the difference between the two groups in terms of patronage. Verse 12 states it in terms of appetite, and the contrast it draws is one of the most economical in the Qur'an: the believers are admitted into Gardens with rivers running below; the disbelievers enjoy themselves and eat as cattle eat, and the Fire is their abode.

The verse is constructed so that the two halves are not parallel in length. The believers receive one clause — admission into Gardens. The disbelievers receive three: they enjoy themselves, they eat as cattle eat, and the Fire is an abode for them. The imbalance is expressive: the reward of the believers is stated simply because it needs no elaboration, while the condition of the disbelievers is described in detail because it requires explanation. A reader might otherwise assume that people who enjoy themselves and eat well are the successful ones. The verse spends its words dismantling that assumption.

**Yatamattaʿūn: Enjoyment Without Return**

*"Those who disbelieve enjoy themselves"* renders *alladhīna kafarū yatamattaʿūn*. The verb is the tenth form of *m-t-ʿ*, the root of *matāʿ* — a provision, a furnishing, a temporary enjoyment of something that will pass. *Tamattaʿa* means to take enjoyment, to make use of something for a period, and the Qur'an uses it repeatedly in a context of limitation: *"Enjoy yourselves for a while; truly you are criminals"* (77:46); *"Let them eat and enjoy themselves, and let hope divert them; for they will come to know"* (15:3); *"Whoever disbelieves — I shall let him enjoy himself for a little, then I shall compel him unto the punishment of the Fire, and wretched is the destination"* (2:126).

The tenth form carries a sense of seeking or obtaining the enjoyment, and its recurrent pairing with a stated limit gives it a particular shade: enjoyment taken as though it were the whole of what is available. The verse does not condemn enjoyment as such. Islam is not an ascetic religion, and the Qur'an is explicit that the good things of this world are lawful: *"Say: Who has forbidden the adornment of God which He has brought forth for His servants, and the good things of provision?"* (7:32); *"O you who believe! Do not make unlawful the good things God has made lawful to you, and do not transgress"* (5:87). The Prophet ﷺ said, as recorded by Muslim:

> *"This world is sweet and green, and God has made you successors in it, so He will look at how you act."* (Muslim 2742, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī)

What verse 12 identifies is not enjoyment but *absorption* in enjoyment — the state of a person for whom the enjoyment has become the terminus rather than a provision for the journey. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Qurṭubī explain that the disbelievers' enjoyment is unrestrained by any reference to what comes after it, and that this is the specific quality condemned. The believer enjoys and gives thanks, and the thanks connects the enjoyment to its Source and to its end. The disbeliever enjoys and stops there, and the stopping is the whole problem.

**Yaʾkulūna Kamā Taʾkulu al-Anʿām: The Cattle at the Trough**

*"And eat as cattle eat"* — *wa-yaʾkulūna kamā taʾkulu al-anʿām*. *Anʿām* is the collective plural for the grazing livestock: camels, cattle, sheep, goats. The simile is one of the Qur'an's sharpest and it appears more than once:

> *"And the likeness of those who disbelieve is the likeness of one who shouts at what hears nothing but a call and a cry — deaf, dumb, blind; they do not understand."* (2:171)

> *"Or dost thou suppose that most of them hear or understand? They are naught but like cattle — rather, they are further astray in the way."* (25:44)

> *"And they have hearts with which they do not understand, and eyes with which they do not see, and ears with which they do not hear. Those are like cattle — rather, they are further astray. Those are the heedless."* (7:179)

The source commentary records the classical explanation: to eat as cattle eat means to have no concern other than filling the belly — and by implication satisfying the carnal desires — while being heedless of the Hereafter. Al-Jalālayn and al-Qurṭubī both note this.

What exactly does the simile indict? Not the eating. Cattle are not sinful for eating; they are doing what they were made to do. The indictment lies in what cattle *cannot* do while eating. An animal at a trough does not ask where the food came from, does not thank the one who provided it, does not share it deliberately with another, does not consider whether it is lawful, does not moderate its consumption for a purpose beyond itself, and does not remember that it will die. Every one of these omissions is available to a human being, and every one of them is a capacity that distinguishes him from the animal. The simile therefore does not say that the disbeliever is an animal; it says that in the specific act of consumption he has declined to exercise what makes him more than one.

The Prophet ﷺ expressed the same idea in a physiological image recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

> *"The believer eats in one intestine, and the disbeliever eats in seven intestines."* (al-Bukhārī 5393; Muslim 2060, from Ibn ʿUmar and Abū Hurayrah)

The tradition reports the occasion: a man who used to eat enormously came to the Prophet ﷺ and became a Muslim, and thereafter ate little. The saying is not a claim about anatomy; it is about the relation between appetite and satisfaction. The one whose eating is ordered by belief is satisfied by less, because his eating serves a life whose meaning lies elsewhere. The one whose eating is the meaning requires more, because nothing else is supplying it. Anyone who has observed the difference between a person who eats to live and a person who lives to eat will recognize the observation, and anyone who has been the second person will recognize it more uncomfortably.

The Qur'an's most extended treatment of the same theme is at 57:20:

> *"Know that the life of this world is but play and diversion, and adornment, and mutual boasting among you, and vying for increase in property and children — the likeness of a rain whose vegetation impresses the farmers; then it withers such that you see it turn yellow; then it becomes chaff. And in the Hereafter is a severe punishment, and forgiveness from God and good pleasure. And the life of this world is naught but the enjoyment of delusion."* (57:20)

The agricultural image at the end — the crop that impresses the farmers, yellows, and becomes chaff — is the plant counterpart of the *ḥubāṭ* of verse 9: something that looks like a harvest and turns out to be straw. Sūrah Muḥammad returns to this at verse 36: *"The life of this world is but play and diversion."*

**Mathwan Lahum: The Abode**

*"And the Fire shall be an abode for them"* — *wa-l-nāru mathwan lahum*. *Mathwā* is the place where one settles and stays, from *th-w-y*, to lodge, to reside; a *mathwā* is a dwelling that one does not leave. The Qur'an uses it of both destinations: *"Is not the Fire an abode (mathwā) for the disbelievers?"* (29:68); *"And the Garden is the abode"* in other contexts; of Hell specifically: *"their abode (maʾwā) is the Fire"* and *"wretched is the abode"* in several places. The word's force is permanence: not a passing through, not a temporary lodging, but a settled residence.

The contrast with the believers' clause is exact. They are *caused to enter* Gardens with rivers running below — *yudkhilu*, a verb of admission, of being brought in by another. The disbelievers' Fire is stated as a *mathwā* — a place that is theirs, an abode assigned. The passive construction in the first half is important: the believers do not enter the Garden by their own power; God admits them. Muslim records the Prophet's ﷺ statement of this:

> *"None of you will enter Paradise by his deeds."* They said, "Not even you, O Messenger of God?" He said: *"Not even me, unless God envelops me in mercy and favor from Him."* (al-Bukhārī 6463; Muslim 2816, from Abū Hurayrah)

The admission is grace. The verse's first clause, read in that light, is not a payment for services rendered but an act of divine generosity toward those who believed and did what was sound — and the whole of the Qur'anic economy of reward rests upon that generosity, since the deeds themselves were enabled by Him.

**The Saying That Summarizes the Verse**

Al-Qurṭubī records, as the source commentary notes, a saying that has circulated in the tradition in this connection:

> *"In the world the believer makes provision, the hypocrite preens, and the disbeliever revels."*

The threefold division matches the three groups this sūrah examines: the believers (vv. 2, 5–7, 12a), the disbelievers (vv. 1, 8–12b), and the hypocrites (vv. 16, 20–21, 25–30). Each is characterized by what it does with the world. The believer treats it as provision — *zād*, the food for a journey, which is taken in the quantity the journey requires. The hypocrite treats it as a stage — *yazanū*, preens, decorates himself for an audience. The disbeliever treats it as the destination — *yatamattaʿ*, takes his enjoyment, and stops.

The believer's posture is described by the Prophet ﷺ in the report preserved by al-Bukhārī from Ibn ʿUmar:

> *"Be in this world as though you were a stranger or a traveler along a road."* And Ibn ʿUmar used to say: "When you are in the evening, do not wait for the morning; and when you are in the morning, do not wait for the evening. Take from your health for your illness, and from your life for your death." (al-Bukhārī 6416)

The stranger does not furnish his lodging as though he would die in it. He keeps his bags near the door. This is the practical meaning of eating in one intestine, and it is not misery: the stranger enjoys his lodging fully precisely because he is not trying to make it permanent.

**Gardens With Rivers Running Below**

The believers' clause refers the reader to the Qur'an's most repeated description of Paradise: *jannāt tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār* — Gardens with rivers running below. The phrase occurs in more than thirty places (2:25; 3:15; 4:57; 5:119; 9:72, 89, 100; 10:9; 13:35; 14:23; 16:31; 18:107; 20:76; 22:23; 25:15; 29:58; 39:20; 47:15; 48:5; 57:12; 61:12; 64:9; 65:11; 66:8; 85:11; 98:8). Its repetition is itself a form of instruction, and it is the repetition the sūrah announced at verse 3: God sets forth likenesses for mankind.

*Jannah* comes from *j-n-n*, to cover, to conceal — the same root as *jinn* (beings concealed from sight) and *janīn* (the unborn child concealed in the womb) and *junnah* (a shield). A *jannah* is a garden whose ground is covered by its vegetation, a place of shade and concealment: the opposite of exposed, scorching, visible ground. The Arabian imagination of paradise is not abstract; it is the walled garden with water in it, the one thing the desert does not provide. The phrase "rivers running below" specifies that the water is not carried in but flows — permanent, unmanaged, self-renewing. The Garden is thus described in the vocabulary of the thing its first audience most lacked, and the sūrah will extend the description at verse 15 into four distinct rivers.

The report of the Prophet's ﷺ description of the Garden supplies the content of the promise:

> *"God said: I have prepared for My righteous servants what no eye has seen, no ear has heard, and no human heart has conceived."* (al-Bukhārī 3244; Muslim 2824, from Abū Hurayrah)

Abū Hurayrah added: "Read if you wish: *No soul knows what is kept hidden for them of the coolness of eyes, as a recompense for what they used to do* (32:17)." The point of the report is that the Qur'an's descriptions — gardens, rivers, milk, honey, wine — are not exhaustive catalogues but indications, and the reality exceeds the image. The likeness is struck so that the heart can move; it is not a specification so that the mind can calculate.

**Why the Verse Puts the Two Pictures Side by Side**

The rhetorical effect of verse 12 depends on the juxtaposition, and the juxtaposition is doing precise work. The reader is shown the disbelievers enjoying themselves — a genuinely attractive picture, and one that matches much of observable reality — and is then shown the believers being admitted into Gardens. Between the two, no argument is offered. The verse does not say the disbelievers are wrong because their enjoyment is illusory, or because it will end, or because it is sinful. It simply sets the two pictures next to each other and lets the reader compare their scales.

The comparison is what the sūrah calls a *mathal* — a likeness — and the method was announced at verse 3. The believer's provision is compared to the disbeliever's revelry, and the question the reader must answer is which of the two pictures he would rather be inside. This is the Qur'an's characteristic way of arguing with appetite: it does not deny the appetite's claim, it offers the appetite a larger object. Ibn al-Qayyim described the mechanism in *al-Fawāʾid*: the heart cannot be emptied of love, only redirected; the way to remove a lesser love is to install a greater one.

The practical application of verse 12 is available to anyone with an appetite and a calendar. The verse asks the reader to notice how he eats — literally and figuratively — and whether the eating is ordered toward anything. It asks him to notice the difference between the meal taken with gratitude and attention and the meal consumed while distracted, and it tells him that the difference is not trivial but is the difference between two destinations. It asks him to notice whether he is making provision, preening, or reveling. And it offers, in four words, the alternative: Gardens with rivers running below.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:13

> **And how many a town greater in strength than thy town which cast thee out have We destroyed, having none then to help them?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse Spoken to a Man Who Had Just Left Home**

Verse 13 is the sūrah's most personal verse. It is the only one in which the Prophet ﷺ is addressed by reference to his own town, his own expulsion, and his own situation at a specific hour — and the tradition reports that the hour was the Hijrah itself. Al-Ālūsī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qurṭubī all record that this verse was revealed at the beginning of the migration from Makkah to Madinah, which would make it, on the minority view, the one Makkan verse inside a Madinan sūrah. The occasion is transmitted with a detail that the commentators preserve in full:

> After the Prophet ﷺ left Makkah, he turned to the city and said: *"By God, you are the most beloved land to God and the most beloved land to me. Were it not that your people had expelled me from you, I would not have left you."* (al-Tirmidhī 3925; Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ālūsī on 47:13)

Then the verse came: *"And how many a town greater in strength than thy town which cast thee out have We destroyed, having none then to help them?"*

The juxtaposition of the saying and the verse is what gives the verse its power. The saying is not a complaint; it is a declaration of love for the place that had just tried to kill its speaker. The verse answers not by disputing the love but by reframing the expulsion: the town that cast you out is not stronger than towns God has already destroyed, and there will be no one to help it.

It is worth dwelling on the emotional register, because it is rare in the Qur'an to see the Prophet's ﷺ personal feeling given this much space. He loved Makkah. He said so out loud, on the road, having just been driven out of it by people who had boycotted his clan, tortured his companions, plotted his assassination, and forced him to leave at night with a bounty on his head. And the revelation that came did not tell him his love was misplaced. It told him that the town would answer for what it had done. Love of homeland and the justice owed by the homeland's oppressors are held in the same verse without either cancelling the other.

**Wa-Kaʾayyin: How Many**

The verse opens with *kaʾayyin* — an indefinite quantifier meaning "how many," used in the Qur'an several times and always in contexts of multitude: *"And how many a prophet fought, and with him many devoted men, and they did not lose heart for what befell them in the way of God"* (3:146); *"And how many a living creature does not carry its own provision — God provides for it and for you"* (29:60); *"And how many a town was stronger than your town"* (this verse); *"And how many a town We destroyed while it was wrongdoing"* (22:45).

The word's function is quantitative and it is emphatic: not one town, not a few, but many. The reader is being told that the pattern is not exceptional. Expulsion followed by destruction is not a rare event in the divine governance of history; it is a recurrent one, and the recurrence is what makes it a pattern rather than an accident. The verse thus performs the same work as verse 10 — appeal to the *sunnat Allāh* — but does so from inside the Prophet's own biography rather than from the ruins along the caravan road.

**Ashaddu Quwwatan: Stronger in Strength**

*"Greater in strength"* — *hiya ashaddu quwwatan*. *Quwwah* is power, strength, capacity — the same word used at 30:9 (*"they were greater than them in strength"*) and 40:82 (*"greater in strength, and in the monuments they left upon the earth"*). The comparison is explicit and it is the verse's central claim: the town that cast the Prophet ﷺ out was *less* strong than towns already destroyed.

For the audience this was not an abstraction. Makkah was a trading city of perhaps a few thousand people, whose power rested on its control of the Kaʿbah, its caravan networks, and its alliances. The destroyed towns the Qur'an names were, by every measure the Arabs had available, greater: ʿĀd with *"Iram of the pillars, the like of which was never created in the lands"* (89:7–8); Thamūd *"who carved the houses out of the mountains"* (26:149) and *"who made in the land great monuments"*; Pharaoh *"possessor of the stakes"* (89:10), whose dominion ran over Egypt and whose buildings still stood. The verse asks the Makkan elite to compare themselves honestly with these, and the honest comparison is unfavorable to them.

There is an argument in the comparison that Ibn ʿĀshūr identifies as one of the Qur'an's characteristic methods, *qiyās al-awlā* — the argument from the stronger case: if God destroyed those who were stronger than you for the same offense, then your destruction for that offense is not in doubt but in the order of things. The form of the argument is used repeatedly in the Qur'an: *"Are you more difficult to create, or the heaven?"* (79:27); *"Is the creation of you greater, or the creation of the heaven?"* (79:27); *"Do they not see that God who created the heavens and the earth is able to create the like of them?"* (17:99); *"Is not He who created the heavens and the earth able to create the like of them?"* (36:81). In each case the greater is cited to establish the lesser. Here the greater destructions establish the coming one.

**Qaryatika Allatī Akhrajatka: The Town That Cast You Out**

The phrase *"thy town which cast thee out"* — *qaryatika allatī akhrajatka* — is grammatically striking and the commentators have noted why. The expulsion is attributed to the town itself, in the feminine singular, as though the place were the agent. Strictly, the people expelled him; the Qur'an says as much elsewhere: *"and those who drove the Messenger out"* (9:40), and *"your people have driven me out."* The attribution to the town is a metonymy of the kind Arabic eloquence favors — naming the place for its inhabitants — but it also does something more.

It makes the town a party to the crime. A place is not morally neutral; the Qur'an consistently treats locations as bearing the character of what is done in them. Sodom is *"the overthrown towns"* (53:53); Madyan is addressed as a people and destroyed as a place; the Masjid al-Ḥarām is holy because of what occurs in it; the earth itself will testify on the Day of Judgment: *"On that day it shall tell its tidings, because your Lord has inspired it"* (99:4–5). The town that casts out a messenger takes on the guilt of the casting out, and the verse speaks to it as a responsible party.

This has a consequence the tradition drew for the sanctity of Makkah itself, which remains the most honored place on earth despite what its people did. The Prophet's ﷺ own words, in the report preserved by al-Bukhārī on the day of the Conquest:

> *"O Makkah, how good you are, and how beloved you are to me! Were it not that your people drove me out of you, I would never have dwelt anywhere but in you."* (al-Tirmidhī 3925, from Ibn ʿAbbās; the sense is also in al-Bukhārī 1586 in another wording)

And his declaration on the same day: *"Truly God has made this city sacred on the day He created the heavens and the earth; it is sacred by God's sanctification until the Day of Resurrection"* (al-Bukhārī 1833; Muslim 1353). The town is both the object of the verse's warning and the object of its speaker's love and its Lord's sanctification. The guilt is the people's; the honor is the place's; and the verse holds both without confusion.

**Ahlaknāhum Fa-Lā Nāṣira Lahum: Destruction and the Absence of Help**

*"Have We destroyed them, having none then to help them"* — *ahlaknāhum fa-lā nāṣira lahum*. The verb *ahlaka*, to destroy, is used of the peoples throughout the Qur'an, and the concluding clause *fa-lā nāṣira lahum* — "and there was no helper for them" — is the negative counterpart of verse 11: *"the disbelievers have no master."* The two verses are separated by one and they say the same thing from two angles. Verse 11 states the principle: no *mawlā*. Verse 13 states the historical demonstration: no *nāṣir* when the destruction came.

*Nāṣir* is the active participle of *n-ṣ-r*, the root of verse 7's promise — *"if you help God, He will help you."* The same root appears here in the negative: there was no one to help them. The Qur'an repeats this formula about the destroyed peoples with a persistence that suggests it is meant to be remembered: *"And there was none to help them"* (28:81, of Qārūn); *"And they had no helpers besides God"* (11:101); *"And you have no helpers besides God"* (2:107). The point is not that help was offered and refused; it is that no help existed to be offered. The patrons, allies, and tribal protectors on whom these civilizations had relied were, at the moment of destruction, not there — exactly as verse 11 predicted.

The Qur'an generalizes the observation:

> *"And how many a town have We destroyed while it was wrongdoing, so it fell in upon its roofs, and how many a well abandoned and a lofty palace! Have they not journeyed in the earth...?"* (22:45–46)

Note the details: roofs collapsed inward, a well left with no one to draw from it, a palace raised high and now empty. The verse 22:45–46 immediately follows with the command to journey and the diagnosis of the blind heart, which is where Sūrah Muḥammad's own verse 10 has already been. The two sūrahs are working through the same material in the same order.

**The Verse as Consolation**

Read in its reported occasion, verse 13 is a consolation delivered to a man at the lowest point of his public life. He had lost his wife Khadījah and his uncle Abū Ṭālib in the year of sorrow; he had been rejected by Ṭāʾif and pelted until his sandals ran with blood; he had been driven out of the city he loved; he was now traveling toward a town that had accepted him but that he had never seen, with a small band of followers and no army, no treasury, and no alliance. The verse tells him three things.

First: you are not the first. Towns greater than this one cast out their messengers, and the Qur'an names them. Second: they were destroyed. The expulsion did not end the story; it began the reckoning. Third: none helped them. Whatever the apparent power of the party that expelled you, it will not avail them when the time comes.

This is the structure of consolation the Qur'an uses with its Prophet repeatedly: *"And indeed messengers were denied before thee, and they were patient over that wherein they were denied, and were harmed, until Our help came unto them"* (6:34); *"And We know that your breast is straitened by what they say"* (15:97); *"Has there not come upon man a period of time when he was not a thing mentioned?"* (76:1). The consolation is never "it will not hurt"; it is always "it has happened before, and here is how it ended."

Eight years after this verse, the Prophet ﷺ re-entered Makkah with ten thousand men, without bloodshed, and stood at the door of the Kaʿbah before the same Quraysh who had expelled him, and asked them: *"What do you think I will do with you?"* They said, "Good — a noble brother, son of a noble brother." He said: *"I say to you what Joseph said to his brothers: 'No reproach upon you today.' Go, for you are free"* (Ibn Hishām, *al-Sīrah*; the Qur'anic quotation is 12:92). The verse's warning was fulfilled in the town's humiliation, and the humiliation was followed by mercy — which is the pattern the Qur'an describes at verse 1 of the next sūrah, revealed on that same road: *"Truly We have given you a manifest victory"* (48:1).

**Expulsion and the Right of Return**

The verse carries a legal and moral weight that the Islamic tradition has taken seriously. Expulsion from one's homeland is treated in the Qur'an as an injury of the first order, alongside killing: *"Why should you not fight in the way of God, and for the oppressed men, women, and children who say: Our Lord, bring us forth from this town whose people are oppressors, and appoint for us from Your presence a protector, and appoint for us from Your presence a helper?"* (4:75). The emigrants (*al-muhājirūn*) are ranked in the Qur'an above others precisely because of what they lost: *"Those who believed and emigrated and strove in the way of God with their wealth and their selves are greater in degree with God, and those are the triumphant"* (9:20).

The verse's placement of expulsion and destruction in the same sentence establishes a principle of accountability: the party that drives people from their homes answers for it. This is why the classical jurists included expulsion among the injuries that justify response, and why the Qur'an's permission to fight names it as the cause: *"those who were expelled from their homes without right, only because they said: Our Lord is God"* (22:40). The phrase *"without right"* (*bi-ghayri ḥaqq*) is the criterion — expulsion is an injury when it lacks justification, and the Qur'an's account of every prophetic expulsion is that it lacked justification entirely.

For the reader who has never been expelled from a country, the verse retains its force at the scale at which expulsion actually occurs in most lives: the person pushed out of a job by someone less competent, the family displaced from a neighborhood, the student made to feel unwelcome in an institution, the believer driven from a community by people who claimed to represent it. The verse's promise is not about the recovery of the position; it is about the accountability of those who took it. Towns stronger than yours have been destroyed for less, and there was no one to help them.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:14

> **Is one who stands upon a clear proof from his Lord like one whose evil deeds are made to seem fair unto him and like those who follow their caprices?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Answers Itself**

Verse 14 is a rhetorical question of a form the Qur'an uses often and always to the same effect: *a-fa-man kāna ʿalā bayyinatin min rabbihi ka-man zuyyina lahu sūʾu ʿamalihi wa-ttabaʿū ahwāʾahum* — "Is one who stands upon a clear proof from his Lord like one whose evil deeds are made to seem fair unto him and like those who follow their caprices?" The expected answer is a flat "no," and the question exists not to elicit the answer but to make the hearer say it.

The parallel questions in the Qur'an establish the type:

> *"Is one who believes like one who is iniquitous? They are not equal."* (32:18)

> *"Say: Are the blind and the seer equal? Or are the darknesses and the light equal?"* (13:16)

> *"Are those who know and those who do not know equal?"* (39:9)

> *"Is the blind equal to the seer? Will you not then reflect?"* (6:50)

In each case the Qur'an is refusing an equivalence that its opponents were implicitly asserting. The Makkans treated the Prophet ﷺ and Abū Jahl as two men with two opinions; the Qur'an says they are not two opinions but two states of being, and the difference between them is the difference between sight and blindness. Verse 14 performs the same refusal inside Sūrah Muḥammad's argument, and it does so with a precision the parallel verses do not: it names *three* categories, not two.

**ʿAlā Bayyinah: Standing Upon the Proof**

The first category is *"one who stands upon a clear proof from his Lord"* — *man kāna ʿalā bayyinatin min rabbihi*. Two features of the phrase matter.

The preposition *ʿalā* — "upon" — is spatial. The person is not merely in possession of a proof; he is *standing on* it, as one stands on ground or a foundation. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the construction as indicating establishment and stability: the proof is beneath him, supporting his weight, and he is not going to move. The image contrasts directly with the verse's other two figures, one of whom is deceived by an appearance and the other of whom is carried along by appetite. Neither of them is standing on anything.

*Bayyinah* comes from *b-y-n*, to be clear, distinct, separated, evident. It is the noun of that which makes a thing manifest — a clear sign, a decisive argument, an unmistakable indication. The Qur'an uses it of the revealed proofs: *"We sent them with clear proofs and books"* (3:184); *"Here is a clear proof from your Lord"* (7:73); of the Qur'an itself: *"This is a clear light"* (4:174), *"clear verses"* (*ayāt bayyināt*, 3:118 and often); and of the inner certainty given to the believer. Al-Qurṭubī, as the source commentary records, understands it as the revelation, or as affirmation and certainty; al-Jalālayn takes it simply as a definitive argument from his Lord. The readings converge: a *bayyinah* is something that makes the matter evident, whether from outside (revelation) or from within (the certainty revelation produces).

The addition of *min rabbihi* — "from his Lord" — completes the phrase. The proof is not self-generated. It is received from the One who made the person and who knows what he was made for. This is the epistemology of the whole verse in four words: the person who stands firm stands on something given to him from outside himself, and the two who do not stand firm are relying on something produced inside themselves — the beautification of their own deeds, the direction of their own appetites.

**Zuyyina Lahū Sūʾu ʿAmalihi: The Beautified Evil**

The second category is *"one whose evil deeds are made to seem fair unto him"* — *man zuyyina lahu sūʾu ʿamalihi*. The verb *zuyyina* is the passive of *zayyana*, from *z-y-n*, to adorn, beautify, decorate. The same root produces *zīnah* — adornment, ornament, the decoration a woman wears or a city displays: *"We made the stars an adornment (zīnah) for the heaven"* (37:6); *"The day of the festival, when the people are gathered, and the day of adornment (yawm al-zīnah)"* (20:59).

The passive voice raises the question the commentators address: who does the beautifying? The Qur'an attributes the act to three agents in different passages, and the attributions are not contradictory but describe one operation from three angles.

Satan: *"And Satan made their deeds fair-seeming unto them and barred them from the way"* (29:38); *"Satan made fair-seeming unto them what they were doing"* (6:43); *"And when Satan made their deeds fair-seeming unto them and said: There is none who can overcome you today"* (8:48, of the Quraysh at Badr).

God, in the descriptive sense of His decree permitting the state: *"Thus We have made fair-seeming unto every community their doing"* (6:108); *"And thus We made fair-seeming unto Pharaoh the evil of his deed"* (40:37). The theologians explain this consistently: God creates the state and its conditions, and the person's own choice is what places him in it — the same distinction the Qur'an draws at 10:44: *"Truly God does not wrong mankind in anything, but mankind wrong themselves."*

The self, in the passages that describe the appetite's own work: *"Is one whose evil deed is made fair-seeming unto him, so that he considers it good?"* (35:8).

The result in all three descriptions is the same and it is one of the Qur'an's most precise psychological observations: the person does not experience his evil as evil. He experiences it as reasonable, attractive, justified, even admirable. He sees it *fair-seeming*. This is why correction so often fails — the person being corrected does not share the premise that there is anything to correct.

Al-Rāzī's analysis of the verse, recorded in the source commentary, draws the crucial distinction between the second and third categories: the one whose evil deeds are beautified may still have doubt, and may therefore reflect upon and question his evil deeds, and in doing so be closer to the one who stands upon a clear proof. Beautification is an operation on the appearance, and an appearance can be examined — the person may notice that something about his self-justification does not quite hold. The third category has no such opening.

**Ittabaʿū Ahwāʾahum: The Followers of Caprice**

*"And like those who follow their caprices"* — *wa-ttabaʿū ahwāʾahum*. The plural *ahwāʾ* is the plural of *hawā*, from *h-w-y*, to fall, to plunge, to have an inclination — the root denotes a downward movement and an appetite. *Hawā* is the inclination of the soul toward what it wants, without reference to whether it is good; *ahwāʾ* are the many such inclinations, shifting and conflicting.

The Qur'an treats *hawā* as the most dangerous of the soul's faculties precisely because it does not present itself as a danger. It presents itself as preference, taste, feeling, authenticity. The warnings are severe and repeated:

> *"And who is more astray than one who follows his caprice without guidance from God?"* (28:50)

> *"Have you seen him who takes his caprice as his god? God has led him astray knowingly, and has set a seal upon his hearing and his heart, and placed a covering over his sight. Who will guide him after God? Will you not then take heed?"* (45:23)

> *"O David, We made you a successor in the earth; so judge between mankind with truth, and do not follow caprice, lest it lead you astray from the path of God."* (38:26)

> *"But as for him who feared the station of his Lord and forbade the soul from caprice, truly the Garden is the refuge."* (79:40–41)

The verse 45:23 is the fullest diagnosis and it deserves attention: the one who takes his *hawā* as his *ilāh* — his god — is the person whose appetite has taken the position that belongs to the divine. He is not an atheist; he has a god, and the god is what he wants. The consequences listed in the verse follow in order: God lets him stray knowingly (*aḍallahu Allāhu ʿalā ʿilm*), a seal is set on his hearing and his heart, a covering is placed on his sight — and the verse ends with the question that Sūrah Muḥammad's verse 24 will ask in different words: *who will guide him after God?*

Note the sequence in 45:23: the idolization of appetite *precedes* the sealing. The seal is not the cause of the straying but its confirmation. This is the Qur'an's consistent account of hardening, and it preserves human responsibility: the person arrives at the sealed state by a series of his own choices, each of which was small and each of which was his. Verse 14's third category — those who follow their caprices — are therefore further from the *bayyinah* than those whose deeds are merely beautified, because they have stopped asking whether the direction is right. They follow. The verb is the same *ittabaʿa* used at verse 3: the disbelievers *followed* falsehood, the believers *followed* the truth. Verse 14 tells us what the followers of caprice are following.

**Why the Sequence of the Three Matters**

Al-Rāzī's observation that the second category is closer to salvation than the third gives the verse an unexpected pastoral use. It provides a diagnostic scale.

At the first position stands the person on a clear proof: he has revelation, he has certainty, and his position is stable because his foundation is not his own.

At the second stands the person whose bad deeds look good to him. He is deceived, but the deception is a deception — there is a difference between the thing and how it appears to him, and that difference is a point of access. He can still be startled. He can still meet an argument, a loss, a death, a verse that he cannot explain away. This is the position of most sinners, including most believing sinners, and the sūrah's treatment of them elsewhere (vv. 2, 25, 33) leaves the door open.

At the third stands the person following his appetites, who no longer has a position to be startled out of, because he is not standing anywhere — he is moving, and the movement is governed by desire. The Qur'an's verdict on this condition is the harshest in verse 14's implied answer and the most explicit in 45:23: sealed, covered, and beyond guidance except by God.

The scale runs in one direction and the sūrah's whole argument is that movement along it is voluntary. Verse 16 describes the sealing happening to those who listen and do not understand; verse 17 describes the opposite movement: *"those who are guided, He increases them in guidance."* The person who examines the beautified appearance of his own deeds moves up the scale; the person who stops examining and starts following his preferences moves down it. Verse 24 gives the instrument of examination: *"Do they not contemplate the Qur'an?"*

**The Verse and the Modern Condition**

It would be a mistake to read verse 14 as a description of seventh-century pagans. The three categories it names map onto the modern condition with uncomfortable accuracy, and the third category in particular has acquired institutions.

The first category — the person on a clear proof — is the one who has received a revelation, understood its credentials, and built his life on it. He is increasingly rare, and the rarity is not primarily intellectual.

The second category — the person whose evil looks fair to him — describes the ordinary state of a moral culture that has renamed its vices. Greed is called ambition; promiscuity is called freedom; cruelty is called honesty; avarice is called prudence; the abandonment of a spouse is called self-actualization. The beautification is linguistic and it is effective, and the person inside it does not feel like a sinner. He feels like a modern person. The access point the verse identifies is the doubt al-Rāzī mentions: the moment when the renamed thing fails to deliver what the new name promised, and the person notices.

The third category — the followers of caprices — describes a social order built upon the monetization of appetite. An economy whose growth depends on the continual manufacture of new desires is a system for the industrial production of *ahwāʾ*. The person living inside it does not merely have preferences; his preferences are engineered, measured, predicted, and sold back to him, and the experience of choosing is the experience of following. The Qur'an's word for the governing principle of such a life is *hawā*, and its verdict is that the person who is governed by it has taken it as his god.

This is why verse 14's question is not rhetorical only. It asks the reader to identify which of the three he is standing on, and the identification is possible by a simple test: when the revelation says something you do not like, what happens? The person on a clear proof accepts it and adjusts. The person whose deeds are beautified argues with it, and the arguing is a sign that he is still engaged. The person who follows his caprice does not argue — he changes the subject, or he decides the text means something else, or he stops reading. The third response is the most comfortable and the most dangerous, and it is the one verse 14 places furthest from the proof.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:15

> **The parable of the Garden that has been promised to the reverent: therein lie rivers of water incorruptible, rivers of milk whose flavor does not change, rivers of wine delicious for those who imbibe, and rivers of purified honey. Therein they partake of every fruit and of forgiveness from their Lord. [Are they] like one who abides in the Fire and those who are made to drink a boiling liquid which then tears apart their bowels?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Great Parable**

Verse 15 is the longest verse in Sūrah Muḥammad and its rhetorical center. It is the *mathal* that verse 3 announced: *"Thus does God set forth for mankind their likenesses."* The knowledge of the Garden promised at verse 6 — *"having made it known to them"* — is delivered here, in this life, to readers who will never enter it except through belief in this description.

The verse opens with *mathalu al-jannati* — "the parable, the likeness, the description of the Garden." The Arabic construction is grammatically unusual and the commentators have discussed it: *mathal* is the subject and what follows is its explication, so that the sense is "the description of the Garden promised to the reverent is this: therein lie rivers..." The word *mathal* signals in advance that what is coming is a likeness and not a specification — an image struck for the heart's benefit, using the vocabulary of a world that knows thirst.

**Why These Four Drinks**

The four rivers are water, milk, wine, and honey. The choice is not arbitrary, and the Arabian context explains it immediately. These were the four drinks of the Ḥijāz, and they occupied four distinct places in the life of its people.

Water was survival. In a land where the well determined the settlement, where the caravan's route was a sequence of water-points, where a tribe's war could begin over a watering-place, water was the first necessity and its corruption the first disaster. A stagnant pool breeds disease; a desert traveler who drinks from one may die of what he drank to live.

Milk was nourishment. The bedouin's wealth was his herd, and the herd's product was milk, which supplied the protein that the date-and-grain diet did not. Milk spoils faster than anything else the desert provides — it sours within hours in the heat — and the souring was a daily experience of impermanence.

Wine was intoxication. The pre-Islamic Arabs drank heavily and their poetry celebrates it constantly; wine is the subject of some of the finest of the old odes. Its effects were known in detail: the pleasure, the headache, the loss of judgment, the shame of the morning, the ruin of the purse, the violence of the drunkard. Islam prohibited it in stages (2:219; 4:43; 5:90–91), and the final prohibition is emphatic — *"an abomination of Satan's handiwork."*

Honey was luxury and medicine. It was the only sweetener widely available, it was expensive, it kept indefinitely, and it was used medicinally: *"There comes forth from their bellies a drink of varying colors, wherein is healing for mankind"* (16:69). Its production required the labor of bees and the intervention of the beekeeper, and its harvest involved straining out wax, debris, and the bodies of insects.

Each of these four was desired, each had a defect, and the verse removes the defect while keeping the desire. This is the structure of the Qur'an's descriptions of Paradise generally, and Ibn Taymiyyah stated the principle in his discussion of the unseen: the names are shared with the things of this world while the realities differ. God does not promise a water unlike water; He promises water without the property of water that ruins it.

**Water Incorruptible: Ghayri Āsin**

*"Rivers of water incorruptible"* — *anhārun min māʾin ghayri āsin*. The word *āsin* is the active participle of *asana*, to become stagnant, corrupt, changed in smell and taste. Al-Jalālayn explains it as water whose nature, in contrast to the water of this world, does not change; al-Qurṭubī as water whose smell and taste do not change. The negation *ghayr* makes the phrase "not-stagnant, not-corrupting."

The specification is exactly what a desert audience needed. Water in Arabia went bad: cisterns grew foul, wells silted, stored water developed a taste. The believer's image of abundant water was therefore never quite the image of good water. The verse joins them — abundance that does not spoil — and in doing so it names the general principle of the Garden: the permanence of the good. Everything in this world that is desirable is also perishable, and the perishability is what makes possession anxious. The Garden removes the perishability and thereby removes the anxiety.

**Milk Whose Flavor Does Not Change**

*"Rivers of milk whose flavor does not change"* — *anhārun min labanin lam yataghayyar ṭaʿmuh*. Al-Qurṭubī explains: milk that does not sour, as the milk of this world does. The verb *yataghayyar*, from *gh-y-r*, to change, is used with the taste as its subject — the flavor itself does not alter.

The image is domestic and it is profound. Anyone who has kept milk in a hot climate without refrigeration knows the speed of the change: fresh in the morning, doubtful by noon, unusable by evening. Milk was therefore the paradigm of a good that cannot be held. The verse promises a river of it that stays. And note that the milk is in a *river* — it is not drawn from an animal, not carried in a skin, not measured out. It flows.

**Wine Delicious for Those Who Imbibe**

*"Rivers of wine delicious for those who imbibe"* — *anhārun min khamrin ladhdhatin li-l-shāribīn*. *Khamr* is the Qur'an's word for the intoxicant, from the root *kh-m-r*, to cover or conceal — the same root as *khumār* (the head-covering) and *khimār*, because wine covers the intellect. *Ladhdhah* is the noun of delight, pleasure, the satisfaction of an appetite — from *l-dh-dh*, and the doubled consonants give the word a sensual weight in Arabic.

The crucial qualification is *li-l-shāribīn* — "for those who drink." The deliciousness belongs to the drinking and not to the aftermath. The Qur'an states the contrast with this world's wine explicitly elsewhere:

> *"Therein no headache will they have from it, nor will they be stupefied."* (56:19)

> *"They will be given to drink of a wine sealed, whose seal is musk — and let those who vie vie for that — and its mixture is of Tasnīm, a spring wherefrom those brought near drink."* (83:25–28)

> *"And there will be circulated among them a cup of a wine white, delicious to the drinkers. No harm is in it, nor from it are they intoxicated."* (37:45–47)

The negations in these verses — no headache, no stupefaction, no harm, no intoxication — are a precise inventory of what this world's wine costs. The Garden's wine keeps the pleasure and removes the price. This is a general principle of the Qur'an's eschatology and it deserves statement because it answers a common objection: the Garden is not the renunciation of earthly goods but their purification. Nothing enjoyed lawfully here is lost there; what is lost is only the harm that was mixed into it.

There is also a moral symmetry in the promise, and the tradition noted it. Wine was prohibited to the Companions in stages, and when the final prohibition came they gave it up — the reports describe the streets of Madinah running with it as the jars were broken. The people who abstained for God's sake are promised a better version of what they abstained from. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whoever abstains, God will make him chaste; whoever seeks sufficiency, God will suffice him; whoever is patient, God will make him patient — and no one is given a gift better and more ample than patience."* (al-Bukhārī 6470; Muslim 1053, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī)

The abstainer is not deprived; he is deferred. Verse 15 is the deferral's specification.

**Honey Purified**

*"Rivers of purified honey"* — *anhārun min ʿasalin muṣaffā*. *Muṣaffā* is the passive participle of *ṣaffā*, to be clear, pure, unmixed; the doubled form means clarified, filtered, freed of sediment. Al-Qurṭubī's explanation is that this is not earthly honey, which is made by bees and must then be harvested and filtered because of impurities, but honey created directly by God.

The distinction matters. Earthly honey, however good, comes with wax, with the legs of bees, with dust, with the taste of whatever the bees happened to find. It is *filtered* — a word that implies an original contamination. The Garden's honey is *muṣaffā* without ever having been contaminated, and the difference is between a thing cleaned and a thing made clean. The whole of the Qur'anic description of Paradise operates on this distinction: not repair but original perfection.

**The Four Rivers of the Night Journey**

The source commentary records that some equate these four rivers with the rivers seen by the Prophet ﷺ during the Night Journey and Ascension, and the report is in al-Bukhārī from Mālik ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿah:

> *"I was raised to the lote tree of the boundary and saw four rivers, two of which were flowing out and two of which were flowing in. I asked: What are these four rivers, O Gabriel? He said: As for the two hidden rivers, they are two rivers in Paradise; and as for the two apparent rivers, they are the Nile and the Euphrates."* (al-Bukhārī 3887; Muslim 164)

And in the longer account of the same journey, the choice of drinks:

> *"Then I was brought three vessels: one containing milk, one containing wine, and one containing honey. I took the milk and drank it, and it was said: You have taken the natural disposition (fiṭrah) — you and your community."* (al-Bukhārī 3887; Muslim 162)

Muslim also records the Prophet's ﷺ statement connecting the world's great rivers to the Garden:

> *"Sayḥān and Jayḥān, and the Nile and the Euphrates, are all from the rivers of Paradise."* (Muslim 2839, from Abū Hurayrah)

The connection, if pressed, gives verse 15 an unexpected dimension: the rivers the audience drank from — the Euphrates at least, known to the caravans — are described in the tradition as issuing from the same source as the rivers of the Garden. The water of this world and the water of the next are not unrelated; the one is a shadow of the other. This is why the Qur'an describes the Garden's water as *ghayr āsin*: the difference is not of kind but of corruption. The world's river is the same river with mortality in it.

**Every Fruit and Forgiveness**

The verse then makes a pairing that is easy to read past and that the commentators found remarkable: *"Therein they partake of every fruit and of forgiveness from their Lord"* — *wa lahum fīhā min kulli al-thamarāti wa-maghfiratun min rabbihim*.

The grammatical construction places *maghfirah* — forgiveness — in the same series as *al-thamarāt*, the fruits. The believers have, in the Garden, of every fruit *and* forgiveness. Forgiveness is listed among the Garden's provisions, as though it were a food.

The point the commentators draw is that forgiveness is not merely the ticket of admission but a continuing delight. The believer in Paradise is being forgiven — continuously, and he knows it. This is why the Qur'an describes the inhabitants' speech: *"And they said: Praise be to God who has fulfilled His promise to us and made us inherit the earth, dwelling in the Garden where we will"* (39:74); *"And We shall remove whatever of resentment is in their breasts"* (7:43); *"And they said: Praise be to God who has guided us to this, and we would not have been guided had God not guided us"* (7:43). The joy of the Garden includes the joy of knowing that one did not earn it and that the shortfall was covered. That knowledge is not humiliating in the Garden; it is one of its pleasures, because the covering is by a Lord who is named here *rabbihim* — their Lord, the One who raised them.

Ibn al-Qayyim drew out this pairing in his discussion of the soul's two hungers: the hunger for good and the hunger to be acquitted of evil. Every human being carries both. The Garden satisfies both, and the verse says so in a single clause that most readers pass without stopping. If the Garden had only fruit, the believer would eat in it with the memory of his failures. It has forgiveness too, and the eating is untroubled.

**The Counter-Parable: The Boiling Liquid**

The verse then turns, and the turn is the starkest in the Qur'an. *"[Are they] like one who abides in the Fire and those who are made to drink a boiling liquid which then tears apart their bowels?"* — *ka-man huwa khālidun fī al-nāri wa-suqū māʾan ḥamīman fa-qaṭṭaʿa amʿāʾahum*.

*Ḥamīm* is water at the extreme of heat — boiling, scalding. The Qur'an uses it repeatedly for the drink of the Fire: *"And they will be given to drink of boiling water, so it will cut their bowels"*; *"This — let them taste it: boiling water and pus"* (38:57); *"They will have no food except from a bitter thorn that neither nourishes nor avails against hunger"* (88:6–7); *"And if they call for water, they will be given water like molten brass that scorches the faces — wretched is the drink, and evil the resting place"* (18:29).

*Amʿāʾ* is the plural of *miʿā*, the intestine, the bowel. *Fa-qaṭṭaʿa* is the intensive form of *qaṭaʿa*, to cut, and the doubled form means to cut into pieces, to shred. The image is of the drink entering and destroying the organ of digestion from within.

The source commentary notes that although other verses speak of the boiling drink, none provides as stark a contrast. The reason is the deliberate pairing: four rivers of purified drink against one of boiling liquid; a bowel that receives honey against a bowel that is torn apart. The verse is not describing two places; it is describing two digestions.

The theological point that the tradition draws is the one the source commentary states through al-Kāshānī and confirms with 4:10:

> *"Truly those who consume the property of orphans unjustly only consume fire in their bellies."* (4:10)

The consumption is identical in form to the sin: what is swallowed in this world as forbidden gain is swallowed in the next as fire. The same organ, the same act, a different substance. And the conclusion the Qur'an draws is that this is not divine cruelty:

> *"Truly God does not wrong mankind in the least, but mankind wrong themselves."* (10:44)

The boiling liquid is not an external punishment imposed on an innocent appetite; it is the appetite's own object, disclosed. The person who consumed everything without regard to its source discovers what he was actually consuming. Verse 12 said the disbelievers *"eat as cattle eat"*; verse 15 shows what that eating becomes.

**The Sufi Reading: The Four Rivers as Four Kinds of Knowledge**

The source commentary records the interpretation of al-Kāshānī and other Sufi commentators, who read the four rivers as four kinds of divinely granted knowledge by which the heart is enlivened. The reading is allegorical and it is offered in the tradition as an additional layer, not as a replacement for the literal one — a principle Ibn Taymiyyah insisted upon in his treatment of such interpretations: the inner meaning is acceptable where it does not deny the outer.

Al-Kāshānī's scheme, as recorded, is this. The *water incorruptible* is knowledge that does not change with the defects of delusions (*wahmiyyāt*), doubts (*tashkīkāt*), and differences in corrupt tenets — the knowledge of those who have reached the station of the heart. The *milk whose flavor does not change* is a lower degree of knowledge, not altered by being mixed with caprice or innovation, by differences between schools of law, or by the tribalism of those attached to creeds — the knowledge pertaining to proper actions, which prevents disobedience and depravity. The *rivers of wine* are the kinds of love for the Divine Attributes and the Divine Essence, delicious for those who are complete (*kāmil*), who have reached the station of witnessing the beauty of the manifestations of the Attributes and of the Essence — the ardent lovers of Absolute Beauty, in the station of the spirit. And the *rivers of purified honey* are holy delights descending upon the heart, luminous gleams, and the raptures of realization in the states and stations of wayfarers and seekers; and those who drink from them are fewer than those who drink from the wine, for *"not everyone who tastes the delight of honey tastes the sweetness of wine."* On this reading, *"they partake of every fruit"* means they partake of every delight from the manifestations of the Divine Acts, Attributes, and Essence.

Whether or not one follows the scheme in its detail, its underlying observation is sound and it is confirmed by the plain sense of the verse: knowledge, love, and spiritual delight are described throughout the Islamic tradition in the vocabulary of drink, because they do for the heart what water and milk and wine and honey do for the body. The Qur'an itself uses the image: *"And We sent down from the sky blessed water"* (50:9), and the revelation is repeatedly called water that revives dead land (2:164; 13:17; 39:21). The heart that receives it is watered; the heart that does not is the hard ground on which the water runs off. And al-Kāshānī's counterpart for the Fire's drink follows the same logic: the person in Hell is made to drink the boiling liquid of his own caprice — the appetite that governed him in life becomes the substance that destroys him in death.

**The Verse as an Argument**

Verse 15 is not decoration, and it is not a bribe. It is an argument of the kind the Qur'an most often makes — an argument addressed to the appetite in order to reach the will. The sūrah has been discussing hypocrisy, aversion, sealed hearts, and lost deeds, and it has been unable, throughout, to reach the person who does not *want* to be reached. Verse 15 changes the register. It stops arguing about truth and falsehood and starts describing thirst and its quenching.

This is why the verse is so long, so concrete, and so physical. It is aimed at the part of the reader that is not persuaded by propositions. The man who hears "the deeds of the disbelievers have gone astray" may shrug; the man who hears "rivers of milk whose flavor does not change" and has drunk sour milk in a hot tent understands something about his own life that the first statement did not reach. The Qur'an's eschatological descriptions are, in this sense, therapeutic instruments, and verse 3 tells us what they are: likenesses struck for mankind.

The final question of the verse — *are they like one who abides in the Fire?* — puts the reader in the position of having to answer. And the answer determines which of the two digestions he is currently engaged in. The Garden's inhabitants partake of every fruit and of forgiveness. The Fire's inhabitants are given a boiling liquid that shreds them. The verse does not say which the reader is; it says the two are not alike, and leaves him to locate himself.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:16

> **Among them are those who listen unto thee. Then when they depart from thee they say to those who have been given knowledge, "What did he say?" It is they whose hearts God has sealed and who follow their caprices.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Portrait of the Man Who Was Present and Absent**

Verse 16 opens the sūrah's longest movement (vv. 16–30), a sustained examination of the hypocrites of Madinah, and it begins with one of the most exact portraits in the Qur'an. It is not a portrait of an enemy, an open denier, or a persecutor. It is a portrait of an attendee.

*Wa-minhum man yastamiʿu ilayka* — "and among them are those who listen unto thee." The pronoun in *minhum*, "among them," refers back to the disbelievers of the preceding verses, and within that group the sūrah now identifies a subclass: the ones who come. They sit in the assembly. They hear the recitation in the Prophet's ﷺ own voice, with his pauses and his weeping and the particular timbre the Companions described as unmatched by any human voice. They are present.

*Ḥattā idhā kharajū min ʿindika qālū li-lladhīna ūtū al-ʿilma mādhā qāla ānifan* — "then when they depart from thee they say to those who have been given knowledge: 'What did he say?'" The verb *yastamiʿu* is the tenth form of *s-m-ʿ*, and the difference between the tenth form and the simple *samiʿa* (to hear) is significant: *istamaʿa ilā* means to listen *to*, to attend, to turn one's ear toward. These are not people who happened to overhear; they came to listen. And then they leave and ask a companion what was said.

*Ānifan* is an adverb meaning "just now, a moment ago." The word's presence is devastating. It was minutes ago. The speaker has just walked out of the room and is asking someone who was in the same room what was said. This is not a question about a lecture attended a year before; it is a question about a speech that ended sixty seconds earlier.

**Who Are "Those Given Knowledge"?**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī identify *alladhīna ūtū al-ʿilma* as the Prophet's Companions — the believers who understood what they heard. Al-Zamakhsharī reads the same. The identification produces a scene worth reconstructing: the hypocrites leave the Prophet's ﷺ assembly and turn to the sincere Companions, presumably to save face — they cannot admit they were not attending, and asking "what did he say?" in a general tone allows them to appear merely to have missed a detail.
