The Companions must answer, and the answer reveals nothing to the questioner, because the questioner is not asking in order to understand. He is asking in order to have asked.

There is an alternative reading in the tradition: that the questioners are genuinely confused and are asking for clarification, and that the verse's verdict concerns the *result* of their listening rather than its motive. Al-Rāzī discusses both. But the conclusion of the verse — *"It is they whose hearts God has sealed"* — settles the matter: whatever the tone of the question, the condition being described is the condition of the person for whom revelation produces no retention.

**Ṭabaʿa Allāhu ʿAlā Qulūbihim: The Sealed Heart**

*"It is they whose hearts God has sealed and who follow their caprices"* — *ulāʾika alladhīna ṭabaʿa Allāhu ʿalā qulūbihim wa-ttabaʿū ahwāʾahum*. The verb *ṭabaʿa* is from *ṭ-b-ʿ*, to stamp, to impress a seal, to press a die into wax or clay. The seal in the ancient world was the mark of closure and authentication: a sealed document could not be opened without the seal's destruction, and a sealed jar's contents were guaranteed by the seal. To say that God has stamped upon a heart is to say that the heart has been closed, and closed in a way its owner cannot undo.

The Qur'an uses the same image repeatedly, and the source commentary correctly directs the reader to 2:7:

> *"God has set a seal (khatama) upon their hearts and their hearing, and over their eyes is a covering, and they shall have a great punishment."* (2:7)

> *"And We have placed coverings over their hearts lest they understand it, and in their ears a deafness. And if thou call them unto guidance, they will never be guided."* (18:57)

> *"God has sealed their hearts and their hearing, and over their eyes is a veil."* (45:23)

Al-Qurṭubī's explanation of 47:16, as the source records, is precise: that God seals their hearts means that although they hear the words and profess belief outwardly, they are blinded by their desire to follow their caprices. Thus they cannot understand, and they do not act in accord with what they hear. The sealing is therefore not an arbitrary act that makes innocent people incapable; it is the confirmation of a disposition they have already formed. The clause order in the verse makes this clear: *God has sealed their hearts* AND *they follow their caprices*. The two statements are joined, and the Islamic theological tradition reads them in both directions at once — the following of caprice produces the seal, and the seal makes the following easier. The Qur'an states the same reciprocity at 61:5:

> *"And when they turned away, God turned their hearts away; and God guides not the corrupting people."* (61:5)

The turning is theirs first, and then it is God's; and both statements are true, and the sūrah will insist on both again at verse 25.

**The Connection to Verse Fourteen**

Verse 16 ends with the same phrase that ended verse 14's third category: *wa-ttabaʿū ahwāʾahum* — "and they follow their caprices." The repetition is deliberate and it establishes the diagnosis. The man who listens to the Qur'an and leaves asking what was said is not suffering from an intellectual limitation. He is suffering from an appetite that has taken the position of authority in his soul, and the appetite has no use for the recitation. It hears it, finds nothing in it that serves its purposes, and discards it within the hour.

This is why the sūrah treats the condition as culpable rather than as unfortunate. A person who cannot understand a difficult argument is not blameworthy. A person who does not retain a speech because he was not actually attending to it — because his attention was elsewhere, occupied by his own concerns — is. The verse's verdict of sealing is a verdict on a chosen state.

**The Warning Addressed to Believers**

The source commentary notes something important: this verse can also be understood as a message to believers that they must be attentive when the Qur'an is recited, lest they hear the words without understanding them or heeding their meaning. The tradition has consistently read the Qur'an's descriptions of the hypocrites as warnings to the believers rather than as descriptions of others, on the principle stated by Ibn ʿAbbās and repeated by al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī: the Qur'an was revealed to be read against oneself.

The application is immediate for anyone who has ever sat in a sermon thinking about something else. The physical posture of listening, the attendance at the gathering, the presence in the room — none of these constitute listening. The measure is retention and effect: what did you carry out of the room, and what did you do differently because of it? The hypocrites of verse 16 carried nothing out, and the sūrah names the condition. A believer who finds that years of Friday sermons have left him with no memory of any of them has been given, in this verse, a diagnostic rather than a condemnation — and the diagnostic is meant to be acted upon.

**Al-Ghazzālī on the Four Obstacles**

The source commentary records al-Ghazzālī's treatment of this verse in the *Iḥyāʾ ʿUlūm al-Dīn*, in the section on the recitation of the Qur'an, where he lists four obstacles that prevent those who listen to the Qur'an from understanding it. The list is one of the most useful pieces of practical psychology in the Islamic tradition, and each item deserves unpacking.

*First*: confining one's focus to the correct pronunciation of the letters. Al-Ghazzālī is not saying that tajwīd is worthless — he devotes considerable attention to it elsewhere — but that a recitation whose entire conscious content is the accuracy of articulation has left no attention for meaning. The person who spends the whole prayer tracking his *madd* and *ghunnah* may finish without having understood a word. This is a real and widespread condition, and it is a form of the verse's problem: the words are received at the level of sound and never reach the level of sense.

*Second*: dogmatic adherence to a particular school of thought (*madhhab*). The reader who has decided in advance what the verse must mean, because his school says so, cannot receive what the verse says. Al-Ghazzālī's point is not anti-madhhab — he was a Shāfiʿī jurist — but anti-prejudice: the school is a means of understanding, and when it becomes a filter that prevents understanding it has inverted its function. The man who hears a verse and thinks only "so-and-so refuted that reading" has done what the hypocrites did: he left the assembly with nothing from the Qur'an.

*Third*: persistence in sin, or being characterized by pride and worldly passions. This is the obstacle the verse itself names, and al-Ghazzālī's placement of it third is deliberate: the moral condition governs the epistemic one. A person whose life is organized around an ongoing disobedience will find, without understanding why, that the Qur'an does not reach him. He can recite it fluently and remain unmoved. The tradition states the mechanism in a report attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās and widely quoted by the commentators:

> *"Sin rusts the heart as water rusts iron."*

And the Qur'an states it as fact: *"No indeed; rather what they have earned has covered (rāna) their hearts"* (83:14).

*Fourth*: reading the outward commentary and thinking that the Qur'an contains only those meanings transmitted from early commentators such as Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd. This is al-Ghazzālī's most surprising and most challenging item, and it is often misunderstood. He is not disparaging the transmitted exegesis — his own work depends on it — but warning against the assumption that the transmitted meanings exhaust the Book. The Qur'an is addressed to every generation and every individual, and the reader who concludes from the commentaries that everything worth saying has been said has closed the Book while holding it open. The verse's own question at 47:24 — *"Do they not contemplate the Qur'an?"* — is a command to every reader, not only to the first ones.

**The Scene in the Assembly**

The verse's power lies in its specificity, and it is worth attending to what the scene would have looked like to its first hearers. The Prophet's ﷺ assembly in Madinah — in the mosque with its palm-trunk pillars and its mud floor, or in his house, or on a journey — was a gathering in which the revelation was sometimes delivered on the spot. A man would be sitting, and the state would come upon the Prophet, and the verses would be recited, and the scribes would write. The Companions described the weight of the moment and the clarity of what followed.

Into that assembly came men who were not there for it. They had reasons to be present — social standing, tribal diplomacy, curiosity, the desire to be seen among the followers. They sat through the recitation. And when it was over they walked out and asked someone what had been said. The verse preserves the scene permanently, and it does so in order to describe a condition that has never been confined to that assembly.

Every religious community in every age has its verse-16 attendees. The person baptized and present and unformed. The student of religion who completes the course and remembers nothing. The reader who has read the Book four times and cannot say what it told him. The listener who asks, on leaving, "what was the point?" The verse is not surprised by them; it describes them, and it names the cause — not stupidity, not bad teachers, not difficult text, but the sealed heart and the followed caprice. And because the cause is named, the cause can be addressed: the heart is sealed by following caprice, and it is opened by ceasing to.

Verse 17 supplies the counterpart in the very next breath: *"But those who are guided, He increases them in guidance."* The two verses are a single diagnosis with a single prescription, and the difference between the two groups is not capacity. It is direction.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:17

> **But those who are guided, He increases them in guidance and grants them their reverence.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counterpart to the Sealed Heart**

Verse 16 ended with a diagnosis: hearts sealed, caprices followed. Verse 17 begins with *wa* — "and" or "but" — and gives the other case: *wa-lladhīna htadaw zādahum hudan wa-ātāhum taqwāhum*, "but those who are guided, He increases them in guidance and grants them their reverence."

The contrast is exact in structure. Verse 16 describes a group in terms of what they did (*listened*, *departed*, *asked*) and then what God did to them (*sealed*). Verse 17 describes a group in terms of what God did to them (*increased*, *granted*), and their own act is compressed into a single verb — *ihtadaw*, they guided themselves, they sought and found the way. The two verses together present the sūrah's central doctrine of spiritual causality: the human act is real and it is small; the divine response is real and it is large; and the response is proportioned to the act, not in magnitude but in kind.

The verb *ihtadā* is the eighth form of *h-d-y*, and the eighth form in Arabic carries a reflexive or acquisitive sense: to seek guidance for oneself, to become guided, to take the way. It is not the passive of being guided; it is the act of the one who orients himself. The same form appears in the Qur'an's most basic description of the believer's relation to revelation: *"Say: God's guidance, that is the guidance"* (2:120); *"And whoever seeks guidance, they seek guidance for their own souls"* (17:15, in the related sense of *ihtadā*: *fa-inna yuḥtadī li-nafsih* — "whoever is guided is guided for his own soul"). The one who takes the first step is then given the second, and the second is greater than the first.

**The Doctrine of Increase**

*"He increases them in guidance"* — *zādahum hudan*. The Qur'an states this principle repeatedly, and the repetition establishes it as a law rather than an occasional favor:

> *"And We increased them in guidance."* (18:13, of the Companions of the Cave: *"They were youths who believed in their Lord, and We increased them in guidance"*)

> *"And God increases in guidance those who are guided."* (19:76)

> *"And when a sūrah is sent down, there are those among them who say: Which of you has this increased in faith? As for those who believe, it has increased them in faith, and they rejoice."* (9:124)

> *"And when His signs are recited unto them, they increase them in faith, and upon their Lord they rely."* (8:2)

The mechanism is stated most clearly in the Qur'an's own image of the opening of the breast:

> *"Whomever God wills to guide, He opens his breast unto Islam; and whomever He wills to lead astray, He makes his breast narrow and constricted, as though he were climbing into the sky."* (6:125)

The report of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd supplies the Prophetic elaboration, transmitted in the *Musnad* of Aḥmad and in al-Ḥākim:

> *"When the light enters the breast, it expands and opens."* They said, "O Messenger of God, is there a sign of that?" He said: *"Turning away from the abode of delusion toward the abode of eternity, and preparing for death before death comes."* (al-Ḥākim, *al-Mustadrak*; Aḥmad, *al-Musnad*; Ibn Abī Shaybah)

The two signs named are the same two movements Sūrah Muḥammad describes: the reassessment of this world as *delusion* (v. 36: *"the life of this world is but play and diversion"*; v. 12: *"those who disbelieve enjoy themselves"*) and the preparation for death before it arrives (v. 18: *"its portents have already come"*). The increase in guidance is therefore not the accumulation of information; it is a change in the direction of desire.

**Why Guidance Increases**

The principle is not arbitrary, and the Islamic tradition explained its logic in a way that is worth setting out because it applies to every domain of human capacity.

Guidance is a relation between the heart and the truth, and relations of this kind strengthen by exercise. The person who acts on what he knows finds that he understands more; the person who refuses to act on what he knows finds that he understands less. This is not a mystical claim but an observable one, and it operates in every field. A musician who practices hears intervals he could not hear before practicing. A physician who takes cases perceives symptoms that the textbook description did not deliver to him. A person who keeps promises develops an intuition about what can be promised. In each case the capacity was produced by the exercise of a smaller capacity, and the increase was given to the one who used what he had.

The Qur'an states the same principle in its negative form, and the negative form is more frightening because it describes a decrease:

> *"And as for those in whose hearts is a disease, it increases them in filth upon their filth, and they die while they are disbelievers."* (9:125)

The identical verb — *zādahum* — with an identical object being increased, in opposite directions. The one who is guided is increased in guidance; the one with a diseased heart is increased in disease. The mechanism is the same in both cases, and it is the mechanism of a capacity that grows by use: whatever you feed becomes stronger. This is why the sūrah's discussion of the hypocrites is so insistent on the danger of small choices — the inattention in the assembly (v. 16), the flinch at the mention of fighting (v. 20), the whispered alliance (v. 26), the reluctance to spend (v. 37). Each is small and each is a feeding.

The saying widely circulated among the early scholars and recorded in the collections of Ibn ʿAbd al-Barr and others expresses the rule in its classical form:

> *"Whoever acts upon what he knows, God will make him inherit the knowledge of what he does not know."*

The wording is transmitted as a saying of the salaf rather than as a Prophetic report of established chain, but its content is drawn directly from verses such as this one and from 2:282: *"And be reverent toward God, and God will teach you."*

**Wa-Ātāhum Taqwāhum: He Gave Them Their Reverence**

The second clause is grammatically unusual and the commentators divided over it. *Ātāhum taqwāhum* literally reads "He gave them their taqwā" — and the question is what it means to be given one's own reverence.

Al-Zamakhsharī, as the source records, understands it in two ways: either God helps them attain reverence, or He gives them the rewards of their reverence. The first reading makes the clause a promise of enablement — the taqwā you are trying to achieve, He will give you the means to achieve. The second makes it a promise of requital — the taqwā you achieved, He will give you its wages.

A third reading, transmitted from among the early commentators, takes *taqwā* here as a verbal noun standing for its object: He gave them what they were guarding against, that is, He gave them the safety that taqwā seeks. On this reading the phrase means He granted them the security that is the goal of all their guarding.

All three readings are compatible, and together they describe the complete structure of the virtue. *Taqwā* — from *w-q-y*, to shield, to guard — is the disposition of one who lives aware that his path runs beside consequences and steers accordingly; the analogy the tradition favors is of a traveler through dangerous country who walks carefully, or a driver on a mountain road at night who is not terrified but is entirely attentive. The person who has this disposition is promised, in this verse, three things: that God will help him attain it further, that he will be given its reward, and that he will be given what it was guarding him toward.

There is a remarkable reflexivity in the clause that deserves emphasis. The reward of taqwā is taqwā. The one who guards himself is given more capacity to guard himself. This is not a tautology; it is the statement that the virtue is self-reinforcing under divine governance, and it explains why the Qur'an can make taqwā both the condition of receiving guidance and the product of receiving it: *"a guidance for the reverent"* (2:2); *"And be reverent toward God, and God will teach you"* (2:282); *"O you who believe, if you are reverent toward God, He will grant you a criterion (furqān)"* (8:29). The circle is not vicious; it is a spiral, and each turn is higher than the last.

**The Furqān: The Criterion Given to the Reverent**

Verse 8:29 deserves a paragraph of its own, because it names the specific gift that follows taqwā:

> *"O you who believe! If you are reverent toward God, He will grant you a criterion (furqān), and will absolve you of your evil deeds, and will forgive you; and God is of great bounty."* (8:29)

*Furqān* is from *f-r-q*, to separate, to distinguish — the same root as the Qur'an's own name *al-Furqān* (25:1), "the Criterion." A *furqān* is the faculty by which two things that look alike are told apart. To be given a furqān is to be given the capacity to distinguish truth from falsehood in a particular case, the lawful from the doubtful, the sincere advisor from the flatterer, the good opportunity from the trap.

This is precisely what the hypocrites of verse 16 lacked. They heard the recitation and could not distinguish it from any other speech; they left asking what had been said. The believer of verse 17 is given the faculty they lacked, and the verse specifies its source: not intelligence, not education, not age, but reverence. The most intelligent person in a room may lack the furqān entirely, and the simplest person may possess it, and the difference between them is the difference between 47:16 and 47:17.

Note also that 8:29 pairs the furqān with absolution of evil deeds — *yurīd... wa-yukaffir ʿankum sayyiʾātikum* — which is the same verb as 47:2's *kaffara ʿanhum sayyiʾātihim*. The two sūrahs describe the same package: reverence produces a criterion and produces the covering of sins, and both are aspects of the increase in guidance promised here.

**The Two Verbs in Sequence**

The order of the verse is worth observing: *zādahum hudan* then *wa-ātāhum taqwāhum*. Guidance first, reverence second. This is the order of reality, and it is the opposite of the order many people assume.

Most religious people assume that reverence comes first — that one must be pious in order to be guided — and they therefore try to manufacture piety as a precondition of understanding. The verse reverses this: the guided are increased in guidance, and then given their reverence. Reverence is a *product* of guidance, not its price. A person who has been shown the way and has taken it develops, by taking it, the guarding disposition that the way requires. He does not need to be reverent before he starts; he needs to start, and the reverence will be given.

This has enormous practical consequence for the person who feels insufficiently pious to begin. The sūrah's answer is that the guidance comes first and the taqwā follows, and that the one who waits for taqwā before seeking guidance will wait forever. The same structure appears in the Qur'an's own description of the revelation: *"a guidance for the reverent"* (2:2) — but the reverence in question is the beginning of reverence, the willingness to be careful, not its completion.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Prescription**

Within the sūrah's argument, verse 17 functions as the prescription following the diagnosis. Verses 14–16 have described the failure of understanding: the person on a clear proof set against the person whose deeds are beautified and the person following caprice; the man who listens and forgets; the heart God has sealed. A reader who has followed the diagnosis may reasonably ask what is to be done about it — and verse 17 answers without addressing the question directly.

The answer is that guidance increases. The person who is already oriented receives more orientation, and the more he receives the more he can receive, and the reverence he is trying to achieve is given to him as part of the same process. The sūrah is therefore not telling its reader to fix his heart by an act of will; it is telling him to keep taking the direction he has and to expect the heart to follow. The will's task is smaller than the heart's transformation, and the transformation is God's work: *He* increases, *He* grants.

The two verbs are both in the past tense in Arabic — *zādahum*, *ātāhum* — and the past tense here, as at verse 2, states the promise as an accomplished fact. What God has undertaken is done in the divine reckoning. The one who is guided has already been increased and has already been given his reverence; the experience of the increase arrives in time, but the gift precedes the experience. This is the same assurance the sūrah gives at verse 5 about the Garden and at verse 7 about help, and it is the sūrah's characteristic way of comforting the person who cannot yet feel what he has been promised.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:18

> **Do they await aught but that the Hour should come upon them suddenly? Yet its portents have already come. Then when it has come upon them, what will their remembrance do for them?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question About Waiting**

Verse 17 promised increase to the guided; verse 18 turns to those who are not, and asks them a question about time. *Fa-hal yanẓurūna illā al-sāʿata an taʾtiyahum baghtatan* — "Do they await aught but that the Hour should come upon them suddenly?" The verb *yanẓurūna* is from *n-ẓ-r*, to look, to wait, to expect — the same verb used of waiting for something one is attending to. The rhetorical form *a-... illā* — "do they [verb] anything except...?" — is one the Qur'an uses to reduce a person's options to the one he is avoiding: *"Do they await aught but that the angels should come unto them, or that thy Lord's command should come?"* (16:33); *"Do they await aught but the Hour, that it should come upon them suddenly?"* (43:66, almost the identical verse); *"They await but one cry which will seize them while they dispute"* (36:49).

The question is addressed to the disbelievers and hypocrites of the preceding verses, as al-Ṭabarī notes. And its content is a description of what they are actually doing. The person who postpones repentance, who delays the decision, who says "later," who assumes there will be another opportunity — is waiting. He may not call it waiting, but the verse does. His life is organized around an expectation he has not articulated, and the expectation is that the Hour will not come while he is still in a position to defer it.

The Qur'an's answer is that the deferral is exactly what will not be granted. The Hour comes *baghtatan* — suddenly, unexpectedly, without warning at the moment of arrival. The word is from *b-gh-t*, to come upon unawares, and the Qur'an uses it repeatedly for the manner of the Hour's arrival:

> *"They will ask thee about the Hour: When is its arrival? Say: The knowledge of it is only with my Lord; none shall reveal its time but He. Heavy is it in the heavens and the earth; it shall come upon you not but suddenly."* (7:187)

> *"And verily the Hour is coming; so forgive with a beautiful forgiveness."* (15:85)

> *"Or lest they should say when the punishment comes upon them suddenly: Our Lord, why didst Thou not send us a messenger, that we might follow Thy signs?"* (20:134)

> *"Rather, the Hour is their appointed time, and the Hour is more calamitous and more bitter."* (54:46)

The suddenness is not incidental to the Hour's description; it is its defining feature. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The Hour will be established while two men have spread a garment between them and are trading with it, and they will not be able to fold it; and the Hour will be established while a man is watering his livestock at his tank, and he will not be able to make them drink; and the Hour will be established while a man has raised a morsel to his mouth, and he will not be able to eat it."* (al-Bukhārī 6506, from Abū Hurayrah)

The three images — the unfinished transaction, the unwatered animals, the uneaten mouthful — describe interruption at the ordinary scale. Nothing dramatic precedes the event. The world is mid-sentence.

**Fa-Qad Jāʾa Ashrāṭuhā: The Portents Have Already Come**

*"Yet its portents have already come"* — *faqad jāʾa ashrāṭuhā*. *Ashrāṭ* is the plural of *sharaṭ*, a sign, a mark, a token — the same root as *sharṭ* in the juristic sense of a condition, and *ishrāṭ* in the sense of marking out. An *sharaṭ* is what indicates that something is near or that a condition has been met. The phrase *ashrāṭ al-sāʿah* became the standard Islamic term for the signs of the Hour, and the tradition developed an extensive literature on it, distinguishing the minor signs (*al-ʿalāmāt al-ṣughrā*) from the major ones (*al-kubrā*).

The verse's past tense — *jaʾa*, "has come" — is the crucial feature. The signs are not promised; they have arrived. The audience is being told that the evidence for the imminence of the Hour is already in front of them, and that their waiting is therefore not neutral but a refusal to read available data.

The source commentary records the interpretation most widely held: the sign referred to in this verse is most likely the coming of the Prophet ﷺ himself. And the Prophetic statement supporting this is among the most famous in the tradition:

> *"I was sent, and the Hour, like these two"* — and he held up his index finger and his middle finger. (al-Bukhārī 4936; Muslim 2950, from Sahl ibn Saʿd al-Sāʿidī)

The gesture is precise. The two fingers are adjacent; there is no third between them. The distance between the sending of Muhammad ﷺ and the coming of the Hour is the distance between the index and the middle finger — immediate in the scale of prophetic history, which spans millennia. And in another wording: *"The Hour has surpassed me as this surpasses this."*

The tradition gathers the other signs in the ḥadīth of Gabriel, in Muslim's collection:

> *"That the slave-girl should give birth to her mistress, and that you should see the barefoot, naked, destitute shepherds of sheep vying with one another in building tall structures."* (Muslim 8, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb)

The two signs have been discussed for fourteen centuries. The first is read variously as the reversal of social orders, or the widespread practice of selling one's own concubine after she bears his child, or the disobedience of children to parents so complete that the child treats the mother as a servant. The second is read as the transformation of desert pastoralists into builders of towers — a description that many modern commentators have noted with unease, since the Arabian Peninsula has within living memory become a place of the tallest structures on earth built by people whose grandfathers herded sheep.

Whether or not these identifications are correct in detail, the verse's argument does not depend on them. Its argument is that the evidence has been supplied and that the waiting is a posture toward supplied evidence.

**Fa-Annā Lahum Idhā Jāʾathum Dhikrāhum: The Uselessness of Late Remembrance**

The verse closes with a question that contains its own answer: *"Then when it has come upon them, what will their remembrance do for them?"* — *fa-annā lahum idhā jāʾathum dhikrāhum*. *Annā* is an interrogative of manner and place: how? whence? in what way? The construction *annā lahu* — "how is it for him?" — expresses impossibility: by what means, from where, could this possibly benefit him?

*Dhikrā* is the noun of *dh-k-r*, remembrance, mention, recollection — the same root as *dhikr*, the Qur'an's name for itself (*"and this is a blessed Reminder We have sent down"*, 21:50) and for the act of remembering God. The word here covers the whole field: remembering God, taking heed, reflecting, repenting, believing. The verse asks what any of it will accomplish when the Hour has already arrived.

The Qur'an answers the question in a series of explicit statements, and the statements establish a rule of divine governance:

> *"And repentance is not for those who commit evil deeds, until when death comes to one of them he says: Now I repent — nor for those who die while they are disbelievers."* (4:18)

> *"Their faith did not benefit them when they saw Our punishment — the custom of God which has passed among His servants; and there the disbelievers lost."* (40:85)

> *"And if thou couldst see when the guilty hang their heads before their Lord: Our Lord, we have seen and we have heard, so return us; we will do righteous deeds — truly now we are certain!"* (32:12)

The last of these is the sharpest. The request is not for mercy but for *return* — send us back, and now we will act. And the request is refused, as verse 23:99–100 states:

> *"Until, when death comes to one of them, he says: My Lord, return me, that I may do righteousness in what I left behind. No indeed! It is a word he speaks. And behind them is a barrier until the day they are resurrected."* (23:99–100)

*Barzakh* — the barrier, the isthmus — is the Qur'an's name for the state between death and resurrection, and its function in this verse is precisely to prevent return. The door that was open in life is closed by death, and the closing is not arbitrary. It is a consequence of what the door was for: a door that could be entered after the decision had been finalized would not have been a door but a formality.

Pharaoh is the Qur'an's case study, and the treatment is exact:

> *"And We brought the Children of Israel across the sea, and Pharaoh and his hosts followed them in rebellion and hostility, until when the drowning overtook him he said: I believe that there is no god but the One in whom the Children of Israel believe, and I am among those who submit. Now? Whereas you disobeyed before, and were among the corrupters?"* (10:90–91)

The single word *ālāna* — "now?" — carries the whole verdict. The belief is true; the timing makes it worthless. This is the principle verse 18 states as a question, and it is a principle with a rationale: belief at the moment of unavoidable evidence is not belief but recognition, and the moral value of belief lies in its being freely given when the evidence was not compelling.

The Prophet ﷺ stated the rule's outer limit in the report transmitted by al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

> *"The Hour will not be established until the sun rises from the west. When it rises and the people see it, all will believe — and that will be when faith will not benefit a soul that had not believed before, or had not earned good through its faith."* (al-Bukhārī 4635; Muslim 157, from Abū Hurayrah, citing 6:158)

**Why the Verse Comes Here**

The placement of verse 18 in the sūrah's argument is significant. Verses 14–17 have described the epistemic failure — the sealed heart, the followed caprice, the man who asks what was said — and verse 17 has given the prescription for the guided. Verse 18 then supplies the urgency. A diagnosis without a deadline produces reflection; a diagnosis with a deadline produces action.

The sūrah is here performing one of the Qur'an's characteristic moves: the pairing of the description of the Hereafter with the description of the present state of the heart. The heart that cannot retain a recitation (v. 16) is the same heart that will not retain a warning, and the warning is that the opportunity to retain anything is finite. The person who leaves the assembly asking "what did he say?" will leave the world asking a worse question, and the answer will not be available.

This is why the Islamic tradition made *dhikr al-mawt* — the remembrance of death — a discipline rather than a mood. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Remember often the destroyer of pleasures"* — meaning death. (al-Tirmidhī 2307; al-Nasāʾī, from Abū Hurayrah; graded ḥasan)

And he said:

> *"The intelligent one is he who subdues his own soul and works for what comes after death, and the incapable one is he who follows his soul's caprice and hopes upon God."* (al-Tirmidhī 2459, from Shaddād ibn Aws)

The second of these is the practical application of verse 18 and the diagnostic of verse 14 at once: the follower of caprice — the third category of verse 14 — is precisely the one who *hopes upon God* without acting, and the verse calls him incapable, *al-ʿājiz*. His incapacity is not intellectual; it is the inability to make his present conduct answerable to his future condition.

**The Modern Reader and the Sudden Hour**

Verse 18's argument is stronger for a modern reader than for its first audience, and the reason is worth stating. The Companions lived in a world where sudden death was routine: plague, childbirth, a wound, a fall, a raid. They did not need to be persuaded that the hour could come mid-sentence. The modern reader lives inside an apparatus designed to make death feel distant — hospitals that remove it from the house, statistics that make it an average rather than an event, medicine that extends the horizon decade by decade. The result is a lived assumption that the Hour will not arrive *baghtatan* for him.

The verse addresses exactly this assumption, and it does so with a precision that has not aged: the portents have already come. The evidence of mortality is not hidden; it is in the reader's own body, in the deaths of his parents' generation, in the disappearance of the world he grew up in, in the fact that the people he was at school with are now the age his parents were when he thought they were old. None of this is portent of the cosmic Hour in the technical sense, but it is the personal hour that verse 18 primarily concerns — and the personal Hour arrives with all the suddenness of the cosmic one, usually in the middle of an ordinary week, usually while a transaction is unfinished.

The Qur'an's instruction in the face of this is not anxiety but preparation, and preparation is not morbid. It is the ordinary practice of the believer: the prayer performed on time because there may be no later prayer; the apology made today because the person may not be reachable tomorrow; the debt repaid because the estate will be disputed; the charity given now because the wealth may not remain; the ties of kinship maintained because the sūrah will warn at verse 22 that the alternative is corruption on the earth. Every one of these is a response to *baghtatan*. The one who lives as though the Hour might arrive suddenly does not live fearfully; he lives *promptly*, and promptness is the whole of the practical wisdom verse 18 contains.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:19

> **Know, then, that there is no god but God, and ask forgiveness for thy sin and for the believing men and the believing women. God knows your coming and going, and your abode.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hinge of the Sūrah**

Verse 19 stands at the exact center of Sūrah Muḥammad's argument, and it is the only verse in the chapter that issues three commands in a single breath. *Fa-ʿlam annahu lā ilāha illā Allāh, wa-staghfir li-dhanbika wa-li-l-muʾminīna wa-l-muʾmināt; wa-llāhu yaʿlamu mutaqallabakum wa-mathwākum.* Know that there is no god but God. Ask forgiveness for your sin and for the believing men and the believing women. God knows your coming and going and your abode.

The particle *fa-* at the head — rendered "then" or "so" — connects the verse to what precedes, and the connection is instructive. Verses 14–18 have described the failure to understand, the sealed hearts, the followed caprices, the suddenness of the Hour, and the uselessness of late remembrance. Verse 19 answers all of it with three imperatives. The remedy for the sealed heart is knowledge of divine unity; the remedy for the burden of a life already spent is the seeking of forgiveness; and the assurance that makes both possible is that God knows every state you are in.

The address shifts within the verse. The first two commands are in the singular, addressed to the Prophet ﷺ — *fa-ʿlam*, *wa-staghfir*. The third statement is in the plural, addressed to the community — *mutaqallabakum wa-mathwākum*, *your* coming and going. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr note the pattern as characteristic: the Prophet ﷺ is addressed in his own person, and the ruling extends through him to all who follow him. The commands given to him are given to his community in him.

**Fa-ʿlam: The Command to Know, Given to the One Who Knows**

*"Know, then, that there is no god but God"* — *fa-ʿlam annahu lā ilāha illā Allāh*. The command to know, addressed to the recipient of revelation, raises an immediate question that the commentators confronted directly. The Prophet ﷺ already knew this. He knew it before the verse; he had been reciting it for years; it was the first word of the message he had brought. Why is he commanded to know it?

Al-Māwardī, as the source records, offers one answer: the verse means that what one has learned by way of reason and deduction, one must know with certainty — the transition from inferred knowledge to witnessed knowledge. Others, also recorded in the source, see it as a command to *say* "there is no god but God," as the form of remembrance mentioned in the previous verse. Both readings are sound, and a third, more comprehensive one is suggested by the verse's own structure.

Knowledge in the Qur'an is not a static possession. It is an act that must be renewed, and the renewal is not repetition but deepening. The same principle appears in the command *"Say: My Lord, increase me in knowledge"* (20:114) — addressed to the Prophet ﷺ, who is thereby commanded to ask for increase in the thing he most possesses. Ibn Taymiyyah developed the point extensively in his epistemology: the knowledge of *lā ilāha illā Allāh* has levels, and the highest of them is not additional information but additional penetration of the same information. The first level is assent that there is one God. The second is understanding what "one" excludes — every rival claimant, every dependency, every object of ultimate concern. The third is the reorganization of the will around that understanding, so that nothing is sought, feared, loved, or relied upon except Him. The fourth is the presence of that knowledge in every decision at the moment of decision. Very few human beings reach the third, and the command to *know* is a command to keep moving up.

*Lā ilāha illā Allāh* itself deserves parsing, because the verse names it as the object of knowledge. *Ilāh* is from *a-l-h* or *w-l-h*, to be worshipped, adored, turned to in need — an *ilāh* is a god, that which is worshipped and relied upon. The negation *lā ilāha* — "there is no god" — is absolute and indefinite in a negative context, which in Arabic grammar produces comprehensive negation: no god of any kind, in any respect. Then the exception: *illā Allāh* — "except God." The whole sentence is thus the denial of every claimant to worship followed by the affirmation of the one. The Prophet ﷺ described its weight:

> *"The best remembrance is: there is no god but God; and the best supplication is: praise be to God."* (al-Tirmidhī 3383, from Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh; graded ḥasan)

And he said of its effect:

> *"Whoever's last words are 'there is no god but God' will enter Paradise."* (Abū Dāwūd 3116; Aḥmad, from Muʿādh ibn Jabal; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Ḥākim)

**Wa-Staghfir Li-Dhanbika: The Prophet Commanded to Seek Forgiveness**

*"And ask forgiveness for thy sin"* — *wa-staghfir li-dhanbika*. This clause has been the subject of extensive theological discussion, because the Islamic tradition holds the Prophets to be protected (*maʿṣūm*) from sin in the sense that would compromise their message, and yet the Qur'an commands the greatest of them to seek forgiveness repeatedly: here, at 40:55 (*"So be patient; truly the promise of God is true, and ask forgiveness for thy sin"*), at 110:3 (*"then celebrate the praise of thy Lord and seek His forgiveness; truly He is ever Relenting"*), and at 48:2 (*"that God may forgive thee what has advanced of thy sin and what has been delayed"*).

The commentators resolved the question along several lines, all of which are transmitted in the classical works.

*First*, the command is an act of worship in itself, and the Prophet's ﷺ seeking of forgiveness is not the admission of a specific fault but the performance of the servant's proper posture before his Lord. The more a person knows God, the more acutely he perceives the shortfall of his own service relative to God's majesty, and the perception is itself the *dhanb* in question — the shortfall, not the transgression. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Truly my heart becomes covered (yughānu), and I seek forgiveness from God a hundred times in a day."* (Muslim 2702, from al-Agharr al-Muzanī)

Al-Nawawī explains in his commentary on Muslim that *yughānu* here means a covering arising from the Prophet's ﷺ continual preoccupation with the affairs of his community and the interests of the Muslims, which, though itself an act of worship, is a descent from the highest state of presence with God — and his return to that state is what he sought forgiveness for. This is the interpretation most widely accepted in the tradition.

*Second*, the command is legislative. The Prophet ﷺ is the exemplar, and what is commanded to him is commanded to his community through him. He seeks forgiveness so that seeking forgiveness becomes established practice, and the command is therefore addressed to the believers in his person.

*Third*, the *dhanb* refers to what the tradition calls the leaving of the better option (*tark al-awlā*) — acts that are not sins but that fall short of the highest available standard, which for a Prophet is measured differently than for others. The Prophet ﷺ's decisions at Badr concerning the prisoners, and his acceptance of the excuse of those who stayed behind at Tabūk, are discussed in the tradition in these terms.

Whatever reading is adopted, the clause's effect on the believer is immediate and enormous. If the sinless Messenger is commanded to seek forgiveness, then no one is exempt, and the practice of *istighfār* is not for the particularly wicked but for everyone. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Blessed is the one who finds in his record much seeking of forgiveness."* (Ibn Mājah 3818; al-Bayhaqī, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Busr; graded ṣaḥīḥ by some, ḥasan by others)

And:

> *"Whoever persists in seeking forgiveness, God will appoint for him a way out of every distress, a relief from every anxiety, and will provide him from where he does not reckon."* (Abū Dāwūd 1518; Ibn Mājah 3819, from Ibn ʿAbbās)

**Wa-Li-l-Muʾminīna Wa-Li-l-Muʾmināt: Intercession Made a Command**

The second half of the command extends it: *and for the believing men and the believing women*. The Prophet ﷺ is commanded not only to seek forgiveness for himself but to seek it for the whole community of believers, male and female, named separately.

The explicit mention of women deserves notice. The Qur'an's gendered plurals often use the masculine form inclusively; here both forms are stated, and the statement is a specification rather than a redundancy. The believing women are named as recipients of the Messenger's intercession in the same clause as the believing men, and the naming has been cited by Muslim women scholars across the centuries as one of the places where the Qur'an refuses to let them be absorbed into a general term.

The practice has precedent and sequel in the Qur'an itself. Abraham prays: *"Our Lord, forgive me and my parents and the believers on the day the reckoning stands"* (14:41). Noah prays: *"My Lord, forgive me and my parents, and whoever enters my house believing, and the believing men and the believing women"* (71:28) — the same double gendered form. The angels pray for the believers: *"Our Lord, Thou embracest all things in mercy and knowledge; so forgive those who repent and follow Thy way, and protect them from the punishment of the Blaze"* (40:7). And the believers are instructed to pray for their predecessors: *"Our Lord, forgive us and our brothers who preceded us in faith, and place not in our hearts any rancor toward those who believe"* (59:10).

Note what verse 59:10 asks for alongside the forgiveness: the removal of *ghill* — rancor, resentment — from the heart toward other believers. The word is a near-relative of the *aḍghān* — rancors — that Sūrah Muḥammad's verse 29 promises God will expose in the hearts of the hypocrites. The believer's prayer is that his heart be emptied of what the hypocrite's heart is full of. And the Qur'an reports that in the Garden the prayer is answered: *"And We shall remove whatever of resentment is in their breasts — brothers upon couches, facing one another"* (15:47).

The command to seek forgiveness for others is therefore not merely a kindness; it is a discipline that reshapes the one who performs it. A person who prays daily for the forgiveness of all believing men and women cannot easily continue to hold a grudge against any of them, because the prayer and the grudge occupy the same space. The tradition noted the reciprocity in a report preserved by Muslim:

> *"The supplication of a Muslim for his brother in his absence is answered. At his head is an appointed angel; whenever he supplicates for his brother with good, the angel says: Āmīn, and the like for you."* (Muslim 2732, from Abū al-Dardāʾ)

**Yaʿlamu Mutaqallabakum Wa-Mathwākum**

*"God knows your coming and going, and your abode"* — *wa-llāhu yaʿlamu mutaqallabakum wa-mathwākum*. *Mutaqallab* is the noun of place and time from *taqallaba*, to turn over, to move about, to shift — the same verb used of the turning of the heart (*"their hearts and their sights turn over"*, 6:110) and of day and night (*"and the turning over of those who disbelieve in the land"*, 3:196). *Mathwā* is the place of lodging, as at verse 12 (*"the Fire shall be an abode for them"*).

The commentators recorded two principal readings, both given in the source. Al-Māwardī and al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī understand it as waking activity and sleep — your movement by day and your stillness by night. Others, including al-Jurjānī, understand it as activity in this life and the state in the grave and the Hereafter — your coming and going in the world, and your final lodging. Both readings refer, as the source notes, to God's knowing all of one's states, because nothing is hidden from Him.

The verse thereby closes with the guarantee that underwrites the two commands. Knowledge of tawḥīd is hard because it must be maintained in every state, and the states are numerous and hidden — the private thought, the intention behind the kind word, the decision nobody saw. Forgiveness is hard to seek because it requires the naming of faults that one has concealed even from oneself. Both commands are possible only because God already knows the *mutaqallab* and the *mathwā* — every turning and every resting place. The concealment is therefore pointless, and the abandonment of concealment is the beginning of both knowledge and forgiveness.

The Qur'an states the same fact in a passage that reads like a commentary on this verse:

> *"And with Him are the keys of the unseen; none knows them but He. And He knows whatever is in the land and the sea. Not a leaf falls but He knows it, nor a grain in the darknesses of the earth, nor anything green or dry, but it is in a clear Book."* (6:59)

> *"It is the same to Him whether one of you conceals his speech or declares it, and whether he hides by night or goes forth by day."* (13:10)

**The Three Commands as a Complete Program**

The three elements of verse 19 form a sequence that the Islamic spiritual tradition recognized as complete in itself: knowledge, self-purification, and intercession for others.

The knowledge of *lā ilāha illā Allāh* establishes the reference point. Without it, everything else is arbitrary — the seeking of forgiveness would be directed at nothing, and the intercession for others would have no one to receive it.

The seeking of forgiveness removes the obstacle. A person who knows there is no god but God and does not seek forgiveness is a person holding an unexamined record; the record's weight makes the knowledge unusable, because the knowledge demands a change of direction and the direction cannot be changed while the weight is unaddressed.

The intercession for the believing men and women removes the last obstacle, which is the self's enclosure within itself. A person who seeks forgiveness only for himself has made his own salvation his horizon, and that horizon is itself a form of the *hawā* the sūrah condemns at verse 14. Praying for all believers expands the horizon to the whole community, and it is the community that the sūrah is concerned with — a community under attack, containing hypocrites, requiring mutual support, and dependent on its members' prayers for one another.

Ibn Taymiyyah's summary of the verse's place in the sūrah captures its function: it is the pivot on which the discussion turns from the exposure of hypocrisy to the instruction of sincerity. Everything before it describes what goes wrong; everything after it (vv. 20–38) describes the specific failures of the community and the specific commands to the community. In the middle stands the sentence that makes both possible: know that there is no god but God, and ask forgiveness.

The tradition records that the Prophet ﷺ acted on the verse. Abū Hurayrah reported:

> *"I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: By God, I seek forgiveness from God and repent to Him more than seventy times in a day."* (al-Bukhārī 6307)

And in another report, more than a hundred times (Muslim 2702). The one to whom the command was given performed it continuously, and the performance is the interpretation: knowing that there is no god but God is not achieved once, and seeking forgiveness is not achieved once. Both are the shape of a life.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:20

> **Those who believe say, "Would that a sūrah were sent down." Then when a definitive sūrah wherein fighting is mentioned is sent down, thou seest those in whose hearts is a disease looking at thee as if death had overcome them. More fitting for them**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Wish and the Arrival of the Thing Wished For**

Verse 20 opens a new movement in the sūrah and does so with a scene of extraordinary specificity. *Wa-yaqūlu alladhīna āmanū law-lā nuzzilat sūrah* — "and those who believe say: would that a sūrah were sent down." *Law-lā* with the past tense verb expresses a wish for something not yet obtained: if only a sūrah would be revealed; would that a sūrah come down.

The believers of Madinah are asking for more revelation. The request is, on its face, the most admirable one a religious community can make — and the verse proceeds to show that the request was not entirely what it appeared to be. The very next clause supplies the reversal: *"Then when a definitive sūrah wherein fighting is mentioned is sent down, thou seest those in whose hearts is a disease looking at thee as if death had overcome them."*

The structure is a trap sprung on the reader as well as on the people described. The first half produces assent — yes, of course, the believers want revelation. The second half discloses that some of those who wanted it did not want what it contained. The verse is thus an examination of religious enthusiasm, and its finding is that enthusiasm for the *idea* of revelation is a different thing from readiness to receive it.

**The Historical Context: A Community That Had Asked to Fight**

The source commentary supplies the background. It is said that the context is a time when some believers were hoping for a sūrah that would permit them to fight their enemies. While in Makkah, a group of Muslims had reportedly asked the Prophet ﷺ why they could not fight the disbelievers even though they were oppressed by them, and he answered that he had not been commanded to fight. When verses came enjoining fighting, many feared fighting even as they should have feared God (4:77). And 4:77 preserves the scene:

> *"Hast thou not considered those unto whom it was said: Hold back your hands, establish prayer, and pay the alms? Then when fighting was prescribed for them, lo, a party of them feared mankind as they should have feared God, or with greater fear, and said: Our Lord, why hast Thou prescribed fighting for us? Why didst Thou not appoint for us a nearer term?"* (4:77)

The psychology is precise and recognizable. Under oppression, the demand for permission to fight is a way of holding onto dignity — the oppressed person asserts that he would resist if resistance were lawful. The assertion costs nothing while it is hypothetical. When the permission arrives, the cost becomes real, and the same person asks why the term could not have been nearer, meaning: why now?

The Qur'an records the answer to the hypothetical enthusiasm at 2:216:

> *"Fighting is prescribed for you, and it is hateful to you. But it may be that you hate a thing and it is good for you, and it may be that you love a thing and it is evil for you. And God knows, and you know not."* (2:216)

*"And it is hateful to you"* — *wa-huwa kurhun lakum*. The verse does not rebuke the hatred; it acknowledges it as a fact and then subordinates it to knowledge. Fighting is genuinely hateful, and God does not pretend otherwise. What the verse objects to is the assumption that hating a thing is evidence that it is bad, and loving a thing evidence that it is good. The knowledge of which is which belongs to God.

**Sūrah Muḥkamah: The Definitive Chapter**

*"When a definitive sūrah is sent down"* — *fa-idhā unzilat sūratun muḥkamah*. *Muḥkamah* is the passive participle of *aḥkama*, the fourth form of *ḥ-k-m*, to make firm, to settle, to decide, to render precise. An *āyah muḥkamah* is a verse of settled, unambiguous meaning, carrying a definitive ruling — as distinct from a *mutashābihah*, a verse whose meaning is ambiguous or admits of multiple readings. The distinction is established at 3:7:

> *"He it is who sent down upon thee the Book, wherein are verses that are definitive (muḥkamāt) — they are the mother of the Book — and others ambiguous (mutashābihāt)."* (3:7)

The application of the term here is significant. The sūrah whose revelation the believers had been requesting arrives as *muḥkamah* — decisive, admitting of no evasion, requiring no interpretation. There is no room in it for the reader to find a comfortable alternative meaning. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the definitive character of the sūrah is precisely what makes the hypocrites' reaction possible: an ambiguous verse could be read in a way that avoided the obligation, and a definitive verse cannot.

*"Wherein fighting is mentioned"* — *wa-dhukira fīhā al-qitāl*. The verb is passive and minimal: fighting is *mentioned*. Not commanded in detail, not explained, not qualified — mentioned. And the mention alone produces the reaction the verse describes.

**Nazara al-Maghshiyyi ʿAlayhi Mina al-Mawt: The Look of the Dying Man**

*"Thou seest those in whose hearts is a disease looking at thee as if death had overcome them"* — *raʾayta alladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍun yanẓurūna ilayka naẓara al-maghshiyyi ʿalayhi mina al-mawt*. The simile is drawn with clinical accuracy. *Maghshiyy* is the passive participle of *ghushiya*, to be overcome, to faint, to be covered by unconsciousness — the same word used of the swoon of the person being tortured or of the person at the point of death. *Naẓara al-maghshiyyi ʿalayhi mina al-mawt* — the look of the one overcome by death — describes a particular expression: the fixed stare of a person whose attention has left the room and gone somewhere else, the eyes on you but not seeing you, the face drained.

The verse is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ in the second person singular — *thou seest*. He was watching these men. He saw the look come over their faces as the verses were recited, in the assembly, in real time. And the sūrah records what he saw, which means the look was visible to everyone present.

*Fī qulūbihim maraḍ* — "in whose hearts is a disease." The phrase occurs eleven times in the Qur'an (2:10; 5:52; 8:49; 9:125; 22:53; 24:50; 33:12, 60; 47:20; 47:29; 74:31), and the source commentary explains it as describing those of impure intentions (33:32) or those who secretly harbor deep religious hypocrisy or doubt (2:8–11; 5:52; 24:47–50). *Maraḍ* is physical disease used of a spiritual condition, and the metaphor is exact in its implications: a disease is not chosen at the moment of its symptoms, it develops, it has a course, it can be treated if treated early, and it kills if not.

The Qur'an's fullest description of the disease is at 2:8–10:

> *"And among mankind is he who says: We believe in God and the Last Day, while he is not a believer. They would deceive God and those who believe, and they deceive not but themselves, and they do not perceive. In their hearts is a disease, and God has increased them in disease; and they shall have a painful punishment for that which they used to lie."* (2:8–10)

Note the parallel with 47:17: *"those who are guided, He increases them in guidance"* — and here, *"God has increased them in disease."* The same mechanism of increase operates in both directions, exactly as at 9:124–125.

**The Verse's Judgment on the Enthusiasts**

Al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and al-Ṭabarī, as the source records, hold that this verse most likely refers only to the hypocrites. But the verse begins with *"those who believe say"* — and the saying is attributed to the believers. The construction raises a question the commentators addressed: are the ones who said "would that a sūrah were sent down" the same as the ones who fainted at the mention of fighting?

Two readings are possible, and both are transmitted. On the first, the sentence describes a sequence within a single group: some who claimed belief asked for a sūrah, and when it came with fighting in it, their true condition was exposed by their faces. On the second, the two clauses describe different people: the sincere believers asked for revelation and received it gladly, and the hypocrites — described separately in the second clause — looked at the Prophet ﷺ with the eyes of dying men when the same revelation arrived.

The second reading is favored by al-Qurṭubī. But the verse's rhetoric does not require the reader to choose, and the reason it does not require it is that the phenomenon it describes exists along a spectrum. Every community contains people who want revelation in general and particular people who do not want it in particular. The sincere believer asks for a sūrah and receives a sūrah containing a command he finds difficult, and he obeys — with fear, with reluctance, with the same *kurh* that 2:216 acknowledges — and his obedience despite the reluctance is his faith. The hypocrite asks for a sūrah and receives the same command, and his face reveals that he did not mean the request.

The difference between the two is not the presence of reluctance. It is what the reluctance produces. This is why the verse's next clause (v. 21) does not rebuke the fear but prescribes the alternative: *obedience and honorable speech.*

**Fa-Awlā Lahum: The Unfinished Sentence**

Verse 20 ends with three words that do not form a complete sentence in isolation: *fa-awlā lahum* — "more fitting for them," or, as al-Rāzī notes, some prefer to place a full stop at the end of verse 20 so that the clause means "[death] would be more fitting for them." The construction *awlā la-* is an idiom of preference and threat: *it is more proper for them*, *it would be better for them* — and in classical Arabic usage *awlā laka* is a known formula of warning, roughly "woe to you," "you are closer to destruction."

The ambiguity is deliberate and it is powerful. Read with verse 21 as a continuation — *more fitting for them would be obedience to God and honorable speech* — the phrase prescribes what would have been better. Read as a threat completed by the implied subject from the preceding simile — *death would be more fitting for them* — it pronounces a verdict. Both readings were transmitted, and both are true in their register: obedience would have been better for them, and given that they did not obey, death in that state is worse than the life they were trying to preserve.

The rhetorical effect of ending the verse mid-phrase is that the listener supplies the completion. The audience in the mosque, hearing the recitation, would have completed it themselves — and the completion each hearer supplies is a revelation of his own state. The one who hears "obedience would have been better" is hearing a call. The one who hears "death would be more fitting" is hearing a sentence. The verse offers both and says which.

**The Psychology of the Fainting Look**

The verse's description is not a moralistic exaggeration; it is an accurate account of a real psychosomatic event. Fear of this intensity produces visible physiological changes — pallor, fixed gaze, slowed speech, the inability to process what is being said — and the Qur'an's choice of the simile of the dying man indicates that what the Prophet ﷺ saw was a genuine collapse of composure.

What produced it? Not cowardice in the simple sense, since some of these men did fight. What produced it was the sudden collision between a self-image and a demand. The hypocrite had built a position: he was among the believers, he attended the assemblies, he said the words, he was regarded as one of them. That position was comfortable and it was profitable. The command to fight required him to place that position at risk — not only the risk of death but the risk of exposure, since a battle is a situation in which the difference between the sincere and the insincere becomes visible to everyone. Verse 31 states the mechanism: *"And We shall test you until We know those among you who strive and those who are patient, and We shall test your proclamations."* The test does not create the distinction; it displays it.

The fainting look is therefore the face of a person watching his own exposure begin. This is why the sūrah returns to it at verse 29 — *"Or do those in whose hearts is a disease suppose that God will not expose their rancor?"* — and at verse 30 — *"And you will certainly know them by their tone of speech."* The disease is not primarily about fear of the enemy; it is about fear of being seen.

Anyone who has lived in an institution where a test separates the committed from the nominal recognizes the scene. The person who wanted the promotion and does not want the responsibility. The student who wanted the degree and does not want the examination. The member of the movement who wanted the belonging and not the cost. The face at the moment the cost is announced is the face verse 20 describes, and it is the same face in every century.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:21

> **Would be obedience to God and honorable speech. Then, when the matter is resolved, were they true to God, it would be better for them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**What Would Have Been Better**

Verse 20 ended with the incomplete *fa-awlā lahum* — "more fitting for them" — and verse 21 completes it: *ṭāʿatun wa-qawlun maʿrūf*, "obedience and honorable speech." The completion is a noun phrase without a verb, which in Arabic produces a statement of what the case consists in: what was more fitting for them was obedience, and speech of the recognized kind. The men who looked at the Prophet ﷺ with the eyes of dying men should have done two things, and the verse names both.

**Ṭāʿah: The Obedience That Is the Whole of Religion**

*Ṭāʿah* is from *ṭ-w-ʿ*, to comply, to obey willingly — the root carries the sense of voluntary compliance, as distinct from *k-r-h*, compulsion. The Qur'an sets the two against each other explicitly: *"Then He turned to the heaven while it was smoke, and said unto it and unto the earth: Come, both of you, willingly or unwillingly. They said: We come willingly"* (41:11). The whole of creation obeys willingly except the human being, who is given the option and whose obedience is therefore valuable precisely because it is chosen.

The pairing of obedience with God and with the Messenger is one of the Qur'an's most repeated commands:

> *"O you who believe! Obey God and obey the Messenger, and those in authority among you. And if you differ over anything, refer it unto God and the Messenger, if you believe in God and the Last Day. That is better and fairer in outcome."* (4:59)

> *"Say: Obey God and obey the Messenger. But if they turn away, upon him is what he is charged with, and upon you is what you are charged with. And if you obey him, you will be guided. And the Messenger's duty is only plain proclamation."* (24:54)

> *"And obey God and obey the Messenger; but if you turn away, then upon Our Messenger is only what is placed upon him."* (64:12)

The source commentary lists the instances: 3:32; 4:59; 5:92; 24:54; 64:12, and the related 3:132; 4:80; 7:156–57; 8:1, 20, 46; 33:33; 49:14; 58:13. The repetition establishes that obedience is not one virtue among others but the form of the religious life itself.

What is significant in verse 21 is the placement of obedience *after* the description of the fainting look. The men described in verse 20 heard the command and were unable to receive it; the verse says what they should have done instead, and it is obedience. Not understanding — obedience. Not agreement — obedience. The Qur'an does not require that the command be found agreeable; 2:216 already concedes that fighting is hateful. It requires that it be obeyed, and the obedience performed despite the hatred is worth more than obedience performed with enthusiasm, because it costs more.

**Qawl Maʿrūf: Speech That Is Recognized as Good**

The second element is *qawlun maʿrūf*, rendered here "honorable speech." Al-Jalālayn explains it, as the source records, as words that are kind to the Prophet ﷺ rather than scoffing. The term deserves fuller treatment because it is a Qur'anic technical expression with a range of applications.

*Maʿrūf* is the passive participle of *ʿarafa*, to know — the same root as verse 6's *ʿarrafahā lahum*. A *maʿrūf* is that which is *known*, recognized, acknowledged — and in the Qur'an's usage it means what is recognized as good by the moral sense of the community and by the revelation. Its opposite is *munkar*, the unrecognized, the thing the community's conscience does not know — "evil," in the idiom of *"commanding the maʿrūf and forbidding the munkar"* (3:104, 110; 9:71).

A *qawl maʿrūf* is therefore speech of the recognized kind: appropriate, decent, kind, honest, in the register the occasion calls for. The Qur'an uses the phrase in a range of situations, and the range shows how wide it is.

To the person asking for help whom one cannot assist: *"And if thou turn away from them, seeking mercy from thy Lord which thou hopest for, then speak unto them a word of gentleness (qawlan maysūrā)"* (17:28); and *"a known word (qawlun maʿrūf) and forgiveness are better than charity followed by injury"* (2:263).

To the one being addressed about inheritance and the rights of relatives present at the division: *"And when the relatives and the orphans and the needy are present at the division, provide for them out of it, and speak unto them a known word (qawlun maʿrūf)"* (4:8).

To the Prophet's wives concerning their speech with strangers: *"Do not be soft in speech, lest he in whose heart is a disease be moved with desire, and speak a known word (qawlan maʿrūfā)"* (33:32).

In the marriage contract: *"And there is no blame upon you for what you offer by way of proposal to women, or hide in yourselves... and do not resolve on the knot of marriage until the term has reached its end"* (2:235), where the manner of the proposal is to be *maʿrūf*.

The application in verse 21 is thus specific: the men who fainted at the mention of fighting should have spoken to the Prophet ﷺ in the register of respect and honesty. Al-Jalālayn's gloss — kind words rather than scoffing — identifies what they were doing instead. The tradition records the scoffing of the hypocrites elsewhere in the Qur'an in explicit terms: *"And among them are those who harm the Prophet and say: He is an ear. Say: An ear of good for you..."* (9:61); *"And among them are those who listen unto thee — is it thou who makest the deaf hear?"* (10:42); *"And when they come unto thee, they greet thee with a greeting wherewith God greets thee not"* (58:8). The *qawl maʿrūf* is the opposite of all of this.

There is a further nuance that the commentators noted. A *qawl maʿrūf* in this context includes the honest statement of one's own weakness. The Qur'an does not require the believer to pretend he is brave; it requires him to speak appropriately. A man who said, "O Messenger of God, I am afraid, and I will go anyway" would have spoken *qawlan maʿrūfā*. A man who said, "Would that a sūrah were sent down" and then looked at the Prophet with the eyes of a dying man said nothing at all, and the silence was the scoffing. The verse prescribes the words he did not find.

**Fa-Idhā ʿAzama al-Amr: When the Matter Is Resolved**

*"Then, when the matter is resolved"* — *fa-idhā ʿazama al-amr*. The verb *ʿazama*, from *ʿ-z-m*, denotes firm resolution, the settling of a decision after deliberation. The Qur'an uses it of God's command to the Prophets: *"And be patient over what befalls thee; truly that is of the resolute affairs (min ʿazmi al-umūr)"* (31:17); of the Prophet ﷺ: *"And when thou hast resolved (ʿazamta), then rely upon God"* (3:159); and of the divorce: *"And if he resolves on divorce (ʿazama al-ṭalāqa)"* (2:227).

Al-Qurṭubī explains the phrase in this context as meaning when fighting has been decreed — when the decision is no longer hypothetical, no longer under discussion, no longer something one might be spared. The *ʿazm* is the point of no return: the order has been given, the army has been mustered, the departure is fixed.

The placement of this clause is the verse's most searching feature. It comes *after* the prescription of obedience and honorable speech, and it describes the moment when the cost becomes concrete. The hypothetical obedience of verse 20's first clause — "would that a sūrah were sent down" — was cheap. The obedience at the moment of *ʿazm* is expensive. The verse does not allow the reader to remain in the cheap register; it advances him to the expensive one and asks the question there.

**Law Ṣadaqū Allāh: Being True to God**

*"Were they true to God, it would be better for them"* — *fa-law ṣadaqū Allāha la-kāna khayran lahum*. The verb *ṣadaqa*, from *ṣ-d-q*, means to be truthful, to be sincere, to keep one's word — the same root as *ṣiddīq*, the one who never lies, and *taṣdīq*, the act of affirming the truth of something. The construction *ṣadaqa Allāh* — literally "he was true to God" — is unusual and forceful: the truth is directed at God as its recipient, so that the sense is to make good what one said to God, to be sincere with Him, to match the promise with the performance.

The Qur'an uses the phrase of the highest category of believers:

> *"Among the believers are men true to what they promised God (ṣadaqū mā ʿāhadū Allāh ʿalayhi); and among them are those who have fulfilled their pledge, and among them are those who await. And they have not changed in the least."* (33:23)

The verse was revealed about the men who had pledged themselves at the Battle of the Trench and kept the pledge, and the tradition identifies among them Anas ibn al-Naḍr, who was not present at Badr and said: "O Messenger of God, I was absent from the first battle in which you fought the polytheists. If God grants me to witness a battle with the polytheists, God will see what I do." At Uḥud, when the Muslims broke, he said: "O God, I apologize to You for what these did" — meaning his Companions' retreat — "and I am free of what these did" — meaning the polytheists. He advanced and was killed, and his sister recognized him only by his fingertips. More than eighty wounds were counted on him. Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ said: "We held that this verse was revealed about him and his companions" (al-Bukhārī 2805; Muslim 1903).

Anas ibn al-Naḍr is the *ṣadaqa Allāh* of verse 21 in a human body. He said a thing to God when it cost nothing, and he performed it when it cost everything, and the sūrah's phrase *"they have not changed in the least"* describes him.

The conditional *law* — "were they" — with the past tense indicates a counterfactual: they were not true, and had they been, it would have been better for them. The comparative *khayran* — better — is deliberately understated. Better than what? Better than the fainting look, better than the survival they were trying to secure by their reluctance, better than the position among the believers they were trying to hold without paying for it. The verse does not spell out the comparison, and the reader is left to complete it — as he was left to complete verse 20's *fa-awlā lahum*.

**The Reward of Truthfulness to God**

Muslim records the Prophet's ﷺ statement of the mechanism:

> *"Whoever asks God for martyrdom sincerely (ṣidqan), God will bring him to the ranks of the martyrs even if he dies upon his bed."* (Muslim 1909, from Sahl ibn Ḥunayf)

The ḥadīth is the positive counterpart of verse 21. The hypocrites of verse 20 asked for a sūrah without sincerity and received it without obedience. The believer who asks for martyrdom with sincerity receives the rank of the martyr even if he never reaches a battlefield, because the sincerity is itself the thing God accepts. The reward attaches to *ṣidq*, not to outcome — which is the same principle verse 7 states as a conditional and verse 21 states as a counterfactual.

Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr also connect verse 21 to the account in Sūrah al-Tawbah of the man who made a covenant with God and then broke it:

> *"And among them are those who covenanted with God: If He gives us of His bounty, we will surely give in charity and be among the righteous. Then when He gave them of His bounty, they were miserly with it, and turned away while they were averse. So He requited them with hypocrisy in their hearts, unto the day they meet Him, because they broke their promise to God and because they used to lie."* (9:75–77)

The sequence is the same as verse 21's, in the register of wealth rather than battle: the promise made to God, the arrival of the condition, the failure to perform, and the result — hypocrisy installed in the heart as a permanent condition, *until the day they meet Him*. The verse 9:77 is the most severe statement in the Qur'an about the consequences of failing *ṣadaqa Allāh*, and it explains why verse 21's conditional is spoken in the past tense: had they been true, it would have been better — and since they were not, the alternative is what 9:77 describes.

**The Verse as a Command to the Reader**

Verse 21 is spoken about the hypocrites but it is spoken to everyone. Its three elements form a program that any believer can apply at any moment of demand.

*Obedience* — when the command arrives, comply. Not after you have understood it fully, not after you have felt like it, not after you have negotiated its terms. Comply, and let the understanding follow the compliance, which is the order verse 17 establishes: those who are guided are increased in guidance.

*Honorable speech* — speak in the register the occasion deserves. To God, in the words of reverence and honesty. To His Messenger, in the words of respect. To the community, in the words that build rather than corrode. And about yourself, in words that are true: if you are afraid, say so; if you are unwilling, say so; if you need help, ask for it. The *qawl maʿrūf* is not flattery; it is appropriateness.

*Truthfulness when the matter is resolved* — the test is not the wish but the arrival. Everyone can want a sūrah. The measure of a person is what he does at the *ʿazm*, when the decision has been made and the cost is on the table and no further deliberation is possible. The sūrah asks its reader to locate himself at that moment, and to notice whether the person who said "would that a sūrah were sent down" is the same person standing when it is.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the practical rule for the interval between the wish and the arrival:

> *"If you ask, ask God; and if you seek help, seek help from God. And know that if the community gathered to benefit you, they could not benefit you except with what God has written for you..."* (al-Tirmidhī 2516, from Ibn ʿAbbās)

The one who asks God for a command and then seeks help from God to perform it has been true to Him. The one who asks and then faints has not. Verse 21 states which is better, and it does not need to state by how much.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:22

> **Were you to turn away, would you perchance work corruption upon the earth and break your family relations?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Put to Every Generation**

Verse 22 shifts address. The preceding verses have been about the hypocrites; this one speaks to the hearers in the second person plural — *fa-hal ʿasaytum in tawallaytum an tufsidū fī al-arḍi wa-tuqaṭṭiʿū arḥāmakum*: "Were you to turn away, would you perchance work corruption upon the earth and break your family relations?" The question is put to the Muslim community, and through it to every reader of the sūrah.

The verb *ʿasā* expresses expectation or probability — "would you perhaps," "is it to be expected of you." The construction *fa-hal ʿasaytum* is a rhetorical question with a negative force: surely you would not?
