And the two things it names are what the community would do if it turned away from the religion and the Prophet ﷺ — the source commentary identifies these as the objects of the turning.

The verse's logic is a prophecy about the consequences of apostasy, and it is stated in general terms that make it applicable far beyond its occasion. If you abandon this religion, the verse says, two things will follow: you will spread corruption in the earth, and you will sever your kinship ties. Not perhaps. Will. The verse treats both outcomes as the natural sequel of the turning away, and it invites the hearer to consider whether the sequel is one he wants.

**Tufsidū Fī al-Arḍ: Corruption Upon the Earth**

*Fasād* — corruption, spoilage, disorder, mischief — is one of the Qur'an's central moral terms, and its opposite is *ṣalāḥ*, the soundness that produces the *ṣāliḥāt* of verse 2. Al-Rāghib explains *fasād* as the departure of a thing from its proper state of balance, whether little or much, and it is used of the soul, the body, the property, the family, and the land.

The Qur'an's usage is extensive and the source commentary lists the principal references: arrogance (7:74), oppression (26:183; 28:4), and the general prohibition at 7:56:

> *"And work not corruption upon the earth after its rectification, and call upon Him in fear and longing. Truly the mercy of God is near unto the good-doers."* (7:56)

> *"And when he turns away, he strives upon the earth to work corruption therein, and to destroy the tilth and the progeny; and God loves not corruption."* (2:205)

> *"Corruption has appeared on land and sea because of what the hands of men have earned, that He may let them taste something of what they have done, that haply they may return."* (30:41)

The last of these is the Qur'an's most comprehensive statement on the subject, and it establishes that corruption in the world is not an independent phenomenon but an effect of human action, and that its appearance is itself a form of divine pedagogy — a tasting of the consequence, so that the return may happen.

What is distinctive about verse 22's use of the term is its connection to *turning away*. The corruption is not described as caused by wealth, or power, or ignorance, but by apostasy — by the abandonment of the religion. The verse thereby makes a claim about the function of religion in the moral order: it is what restrains corruption. Remove it and the restraint goes with it, and the corruption that follows is not a coincidence but a consequence.

Ibn Khaldūn developed this claim into a historical theory in the *Muqaddimah*, arguing that the religious impulse is the only force capable of uniting a group beyond the limits of blood and interest, and that where it is absent, group cohesion (*ʿaṣabiyyah*) serves only the ambition of the strong and decays into the oppression the Qur'an calls *fasād*. The theory is a working out of verse 22 at the scale of civilizations.

**Tuqaṭṭiʿū Arḥāmakum: The Severing of the Wombs**

The second consequence is stated in one of the Qur'an's most concrete idioms. *Arḥām* is the plural of *raḥim*, the womb — and by extension, the kinship that proceeds from the womb. *Taqqīṭ al-arḥām*, "the cutting of the wombs," is the fixed phrase for the severing of family relations; its opposite, *ṣilat al-arḥām*, "the joining of the wombs," is the fixed phrase for maintaining them.

The choice of the word *raḥim* rather than a general term for family (*ālah*, *qarābah*, *dhū al-qurbā*) is deliberate and it carries a theological weight that the Islamic tradition drew out extensively. The word is related to the divine Name *al-Raḥmān*, the All-Merciful, and the tradition makes the relation explicit. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim record the ḥadīth qudsī the source commentary quotes:

> *"God created the creation, and when He finished, the womb stood up and said: This is the place of one who seeks refuge in You from severance. He said: Yes — will you not be pleased that I keep good relations with whoever keeps good relations with you, and sever relations with whoever severs relations with you? It said: Yes, my Lord. He said: Then that is for you."* (al-Bukhārī 4830, 5987; Muslim 2554, from Abū Hurayrah)

Abū Hurayrah added: *"Read if you wish the statement of God: 'Were you to turn away, would you perchance work corruption upon the earth and break your family relations?'"* — and the source commentary notes that the Prophet ﷺ himself cited this verse in connection with the report.

Muslim also records:

> *"The womb is suspended from the Throne, saying: Whoever joins me, God joins him; and whoever severs me, God severs him."* (Muslim 2555, from ʿĀʾishah)

The image in both reports is remarkable: the *raḥim* is personified, it stands before God, it asks for a covenant, and the covenant is granted. Kinship is thereby given a status in the created order that no merely social arrangement possesses. It is not a convention that a society may reorganize at will; it is a creature with a standing petition before its Maker, and the petition has been answered.

**Why the Two Sins Are Paired**

The verse joins corruption upon the earth and the severing of the womb in a single clause, and the joining is not accidental. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn ʿAṭiyyah both note that although corruption here is linked specifically to breaking family relations, it is more generally linked to all manner of iniquity.

The link between the two is structural, and it is worth spelling out. The family is the smallest unit of moral formation and the only one that is universal. Every human being belongs to one; most human beings are formed by one; and the obligations one learns there — to give without return, to bear with the difficult, to maintain a bond that has stopped being pleasant, to keep faith with someone who cannot compel you — are the obligations that make social life beyond the family possible at all. A person who cannot keep a tie of blood will not keep a tie of contract.

This is why the Qur'an treats the severing of kinship as an index of general corruption rather than as one vice among many. And it is why the two appear together in the description of the people who break God's covenant:

> *"Those who break the covenant of God after its ratification, and sever what God has commanded to be joined, and work corruption upon the earth — those are the losers."* (2:27)

The three elements — broken covenant, severed joining, worked corruption — are the same three that verse 22 compresses into two. And the "what God has commanded to be joined" is understood by the commentators primarily as the ties of kinship, and secondarily as every bond God has ordained: the bond of faith between believers, the bond of the family, the bond of the community.

The Qur'an's own ranking of the virtue is stated at its opening:

> *"O mankind! Be reverent toward your Lord, who created you from a single soul, and created from it its mate, and spread from the two of them many men and women. And be reverent toward God, by whom you ask one another, and the wombs. Truly God is ever Watchful over you."* (4:1)

The source commentary notes the parallel: reverence is linked to maintaining family relations in 4:1 in the same way that obedience is linked to it here. The oath formula of the Arabs — "I ask you by God and by the womb" — is invoked in the verse, which means that the ties of kinship were already sacred in pre-Islamic Arabia, and Islam did not introduce the obligation but confirmed and deepened it.

**The Reading That Makes Kinship Mean Brotherhood**

The source commentary raises an important interpretive difficulty. Al-Jalālayn and al-Ṭabarī understand breaking family relations in this context as returning to the ways of paganism and idolatry before Islam. But maintaining family relations was also an esteemed virtue among the Arabs before Islam — the pre-Islamic poetry is full of it, and the Quraysh were famed for it. For the interpretation to hold, *arḥām* would need to be understood metaphorically, as the bonds of brotherhood between Muslims:

> *"The believers are but brothers; so make peace between your brethren, and be reverent toward God, that haply you may receive mercy."* (49:10)

The metaphorical reading is not a weakening of the verse but an extension of it. The community of faith creates relations that carry the obligations of blood, and severing them is severing a *raḥim* in the extended sense. The Prophet ﷺ's statement of the principle is in al-Bukhārī:

> *"The believers, in their mutual love, mercy, and compassion, are like a single body: when one part of it suffers, the whole body responds with wakefulness and fever."* (al-Bukhārī 6011; Muslim 2586, from al-Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr)

Both readings — the literal kinship and the metaphorical brotherhood — are valid, and the verse applies to both. The one who turns away from the religion will find himself unable to maintain his family and unable to maintain his community, and the two failures will proceed together.

**The Accelerated Punishment**

The tradition attaches to the severing of kinship a warning that appears nowhere else in the same form: it is among the sins whose punishment is hastened in this life. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"There is no sin more deserving that God hasten its punishment for its doer in this world, in addition to what is stored for him in the Hereafter, than rebellion (al-baghy) and the severing of the ties of kinship (qaṭīʿat al-raḥim)."* (Abū Dāwūd 4902; al-Tirmidhī 2511, from Abū Bakrah; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

The two sins named together in this report are the two named together in verse 22 — corruption upon the earth (which the commentators explain as rebellion and oppression) and the severing of the wombs. The convergence suggests that verse 22 is not describing two separate faults but a single complex of social breakdown.

And the reward for the opposite practice is stated in equally worldly terms:

> *"Whoever would love that his provision be expanded for him and his trace be lengthened in the land, let him join his ties of kinship."* (al-Bukhārī 5986; Muslim 2557, from Anas ibn Mālik)

Provision and lifespan — the two things human beings most want — are attached to the maintenance of the *raḥim*. The tradition has understood the "lengthening of the trace" both literally, through the blessing in one's years, and figuratively, through the continuation of one's memory in the family one kept together.

**The Modern Application**

Verse 22 speaks to a condition that is characteristic of modernity. The severing of kinship is not an individual failing in the modern world; it is a structural outcome. Mobility separates children from parents by continents. Work schedules make the weekly meal impossible. Individualism reframes family obligation as optional emotional preference. The elderly die in institutions attended by paid strangers while their children live an hour away. Divorce produces step-relations in which the categories of *raḥim* no longer have obvious referents. And beneath all of it runs the specific modern innovation of the *chosen* estrangement: the cutting off of a parent, a sibling, or an adult child as a therapeutic act, described in the vocabulary of self-protection.

The verse's judgment on all of this is stated as a question, and the question is the more forceful for that: *were you to turn away, would you perchance work corruption upon the earth and break your family relations?* The turning away and the severing are presented as one movement with two effects — one upon the earth, one upon the womb — and the modern world displays both effects in a form its inhabitants do not recognize as related.

The practical obligation the verse and the reports attached to it produce is specific and it is difficult, which is why it is rewarded. To initiate contact with the relative who has not called. To attend the funeral of the cousin you did not like. To give to the sibling who has borrowed before and not repaid. To answer the parent whose conversation is a repetition of the same complaints. To keep the tie when the tie has stopped giving anything back — because the *raḥim* is not a contract of mutual benefit but a covenant whose guarantor is God, and the report says that He keeps relations with whoever keeps relations with it.

The Prophet ﷺ defined the standard in the report preserved by al-Bukhārī:

> *"The one who maintains kinship is not the one who reciprocates. The one who maintains kinship is the one who, when his relations are cut, joins them."* (al-Bukhārī 5991, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr)

Reciprocity is trade. The *raḥim* is maintained by the one who keeps his end while the other end is broken — and this is the only form of the practice that carries the promise, because it is the only form that requires the covenant with God rather than the agreement with the relative.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:23

> **They are those whom God has cursed, making them deaf and blinding their sight.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict and Its Two Deprivations**

Verse 22 asked a question; verse 23 answers with a verdict. *Ulāʾika alladhīna laʿanahumu Allāhu fa-aṣammahum wa-aʿmā abṣārahum* — "They are those whom God has cursed, making them deaf and blinding their sight." The demonstrative *ulāʾika* — "those" — points back at the people described in verse 22: the ones who would turn away, work corruption, and sever their kinship ties. The verse names them, and it names what has been done to them.

Three acts are attributed to God, and the sequence between them is marked by the particle *fa-*, which indicates consequence: He cursed them, *and so* He made them deaf and blinded their sight. The curse precedes and produces the deprivations. This order is theologically important and it is the key to reading the verse without misrepresenting it.

**Laʿnah: Removal From Mercy**

*Laʿana*, from *l-ʿ-n*, means to drive away, to expel, to banish — and in religious usage, to remove from mercy. Al-Rāghib explains *al-laʿnah* as removal and distancing from God, and the *laʿīn* as the one removed from all good. The Qur'an uses the term of Iblīs: *"And truly upon you is My curse until the Day of Requital"* (38:78); of the one who kills a believer deliberately: *"God has cursed him and is angry with him and has prepared for him a great punishment"* (4:93); of those who conceal the revealed proofs: *"Truly those who conceal what We have sent down of clear signs and guidance, after We made it clear for mankind in the Book — those, God curses them and the cursers curse them"* (2:159).

The curse is therefore not a divine emotional outburst but a change of position. The cursed person has been placed outside the sphere in which mercy operates effectively. He is not deprived of God's general providence — the Qur'an is explicit that God provides for all creatures, believer and disbeliever, and that His mercy encompasses everything (7:156) — but he is deprived of the specific mercy that guides, forgives, and admits. The distinction is the difference between being fed and being received.

Al-Qurṭubī notes that the curse in this verse is directed at those who would turn away from the religion and sever their ties, and that the severity of the verdict reflects the severity of what verse 22 describes: apostasy combined with social dissolution. The person who abandons the religion and breaks his family is not committing a private error; he is dismantling the two structures — faith and kinship — upon which the moral order rests, and the verdict matches the act.

**Fa-Aṣammahum: Made Deaf**

*"Making them deaf"* — *fa-aṣammahum*. The verb is the fourth form of *ṣ-m-m*, to be deaf, and the fourth form makes it causative: He made them deaf. The object is the whole person — *aṣammahum*, "He deafened them" — rather than a specific organ, which is the first of the verse's two asymmetries.

*Wa-aʿmā abṣārahum* — "and blinded their sights." Here the verb *aʿmā*, the causative of *ʿ-m-y*, is given a specific object: *abṣārahum*, their sights, the plural of *baṣar*. The sight is named; the hearing is not. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī both noticed the difference, and the traditional explanation is that hearing is named as the faculty of receiving and sight as the faculty of perceiving, so the verse deafens the receiver and blinds the perceiver — the two channels by which truth enters a human being.

The Qur'an uses this pairing constantly, and always to describe a spiritual rather than a physical condition:

> *"Deaf, dumb, blind — they do not return."* (2:18)

> *"And the likeness of those who disbelieve is the likeness of one who shouts at what hears nothing but a call and a cry — deaf, dumb, blind; they do not understand."* (2:171)

> *"And if We willed, We would surely blind their eyes, then they would rush upon the path — but how would they see?"* (36:66)

> *"Truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but it is hearts within breasts that go blind."* (22:46)

The last of these is the interpretive key, and the source commentary cites it directly: the deafness and blindness of verse 23 are of the heart, which is the seat of the intellect. Al-Qurṭubī's explanation, as the source records, is that they continue to hear and to see, but because the heart is hardened and therefore spiritually dead, they are like the cattle mentioned in verse 12 in that they have no intellect.

**The Sequence: Curse, Then Deafness, Then Blindness**

The verse's ordering — *laʿanahum* then *fa-aṣammahum* then *wa-aʿmā abṣārahum* — describes a process rather than a single event, and the process is the one the sūrah has been tracing since verse 14.

The person turns away (v. 22). The turning produces the curse — the removal from the sphere of effective mercy. The removal from mercy produces the deafness: the revelation reaches the ear and does not reach the heart. The deafness produces the blindness: without the received word, the person's perception of reality loses its reference point, and what he sees he can no longer interpret.

The Qur'an states the same sequence in the opposite direction at 6:110:

> *"And We turn their hearts and their sights, as they did not believe in it the first time, and We leave them in their rebellion, wandering blindly."* (6:110)

Note the causal clause: *as they did not believe in it the first time.* The turning of the hearts and sights follows the refusal to believe at the first opportunity. The divine act is real and it is consequent upon the human act, and the Qur'an holds both statements together without resolving them into one.

This is the theological position the Sunni tradition settled upon, and Ibn Taymiyyah articulated it with his characteristic precision: God's decree encompasses the servant's choice, and the servant's choice is genuinely his. The person is deaf because God deafened him, and God deafened him because the person turned away. Both statements are true, neither cancels the other, and the practical consequence for the servant is that he is responsible for the turning and not for the deafness — which means his responsibility lies entirely at the beginning, when the choice was still open.

**The Cattle of Verse Twelve**

The verse's connection to verse 12 — *"those who disbelieve enjoy themselves and eat as cattle eat"* — is made explicit by al-Qurṭubī, and the connection completes the sūrah's diagnosis. The cattle have functioning ears and eyes. They hear the shout and they see the trough. What they lack is not sensation but *intellect* — the faculty by which sensation becomes understanding, and by which understanding becomes choice.

The deaf and blind of verse 23 are in the same position with respect to revelation. Their organs work. Their intellect, in its relation to the truth, does not. This is why the verse is not describing a punishment inflicted upon innocent faculties; it is describing the end-state of a faculty that has been misused for a long time. The ear that has heard the recitation a thousand times without attending has become an ear that cannot attend. The eye that has looked at the evidence and refused to see has become an eye that no longer sees.

The tradition describes the process in the image of rust and stain, and the Qur'an supplies the word:

> *"No indeed; rather what they have earned has covered (rāna) their hearts."* (83:14)

*Rāna* is from *r-w-n*, and it describes a film that gathers over a surface through repeated deposition — the scale inside a kettle, the tarnish on silver, the deposit on a lens. The verb is in the perfect tense with *ʿalā*, and the effect is of something that has settled and hardened. The report attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās and transmitted through the commentators explains it as the sin upon the sin until the heart is covered, and the Prophet's ﷺ description of the heart's condition is in Muslim:

> *"Trials are presented to hearts as a mat is presented strand by strand, and any heart that absorbs them has a black spot placed in it, and any heart that rejects them has a white spot placed in it — until the hearts become two: a white heart like polished stone, which no trial harms so long as the heavens and the earth endure; and a black heart, murky, like a vessel turned upside down, which recognizes no good and rejects no evil, except what its caprice dictates."* (Muslim 144, from Ḥudhayfah ibn al-Yamān)

The report is the fullest description in the tradition of the mechanism verse 23 names in three words. The heart that has absorbed the trials strand by strand becomes the inverted vessel — it can no longer receive anything, because its opening faces the ground. That is the deafness and the blindness.

**The Verse as Warning Rather Than Sentence**

The verse speaks of people in the third person and in the past tense — *ulāʾika alladhīna laʿanahumu Allāh*, "those are they whom God has cursed." It is a description of a category, not a naming of individuals, and the sūrah's own verse 34 confirms that the category is closed only by death: *"then die while they are disbelievers."*

This distinction is what makes the verse usable by a believer rather than merely informative about others. Read as a description of a category, it invites the question the sūrah has been asking throughout: am I in it? The test is available and it is the one verse 24 supplies immediately: *do they not contemplate the Qur'an?* The heart that still receives meaning from the recitation has not been deafened. The heart that still sees the evidence and is moved by it has not been blinded. The curse of verse 23 describes a condition the reader can check for, and the checking is itself an act of the hearing the verse describes as lost.

The tradition's practice around this verse is instructive. The Companions and their successors recited descriptions of the hypocrites and applied them to themselves — this is recorded of Umar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, who asked Ḥudhayfah ibn al-Yamān, the keeper of the Prophet's ﷺ confidential knowledge of the hypocrites, whether he had been named among them. Ibn Abī Mulaykah said:

> *"I met thirty of the Companions of the Prophet, every one of whom feared hypocrisy for himself."* (al-Bukhārī, *taʿlīq*, in the Book of Faith)

The fear is not pathology; it is the hearing functioning. The person who reads verse 23 and feels nothing is closer to its description than the person who reads it and is frightened, and the fright is the evidence of life. This is why al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī is reported to have said: *"No one fears it but a believer, and no one feels safe from it but a hypocrite."*

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:24

> **Do they not contemplate the Qur'an? Or do hearts have their locks upon them?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Central Command**

Verse 24 is six words in Arabic and it is the pivot of the entire chapter. *Afa-lā yatadabbarūna al-Qurʾāna am ʿalā qulūbin aqfāluhā* — "Do they not contemplate the Qur'an? Or do hearts have their locks upon them?" The first half is a command in the form of a question; the second is a diagnosis in the form of an alternative.

The verse answers verse 23. The deaf and blind of that verse are described there as a consequence of the curse; here the mechanism is named, and the naming is a summons. Contemplate the Qur'an, and the deafness ends. Do not contemplate it, and the locks are what you have. The sūrah has been describing the sealed heart since verse 16; verse 24 tells the reader what to do about it, and the instruction is not a ritual, an invocation, or a pilgrimage. It is an act of attention.

**Tadabbur: Looking Behind the Thing**

*Yatadabbarū* is the fifth form of *d-b-r*, the root denoting the back, the rear, the end of a thing. *Dabar* is the back, as *qubul* is the front; *dubur* is what follows; *tadbīr* is management, because the manager considers the ends of things. The fifth form *tadabbara* means to look into a matter, to consider its consequences, to reflect upon it — literally, to look at its *dubur*, what lies behind it and after it.

The word therefore denotes a specific kind of thinking. It is not the first glance (*naẓar*), not the surface reading (*qirāʾah*), not the memorization (*ḥifẓ*). It is the looking-through and looking-beyond: what does this verse imply, where does it lead, what follows from it, how does it connect to what came before and after, what is it doing to me? A person who *tadabbara* the Qur'an does not merely receive its sentences; he follows them into their consequences.

Al-Rāghib defines *tadabbur* as the consideration of the ends of affairs and their outcomes, and he notes that it is used in the Qur'an of both the revelation and the world. The Qur'an commands it four times, and the four instances together define the practice:

> *"Do they not contemplate the Qur'an? Had it been from other than God, they would have found in it much discrepancy."* (4:82)

> *"Do they not contemplate the Qur'an, or do hearts have their locks upon them?"* (47:24)

> *"Have they not contemplated the Word, or has there come unto them that which came not unto their forefathers?"* (23:68)

> *"A blessed Book We have sent down unto thee, that they may contemplate its signs, and that those of understanding may take heed."* (38:29)

The last of these is the most comprehensive: the Book is described as *mubārak*, blessed, and its purpose is stated as *li-yatadabbarū āyātihi* — that they may contemplate its signs. The purpose of the revelation is not its recitation, its preservation, or its veneration, though all three are commanded elsewhere; the purpose is *tadabbur*. The verse thereby makes contemplation the reason the Book exists.

The instance at 4:82 attaches to contemplation a specific argument: the reader who actually looks into the Qur'an will find it internally consistent, and internal consistency across a text produced over twenty-three years by an unlettered man in conditions of war, migration, and legislation is itself evidence of its origin. This is not a demand for blind assent; it is an invitation to inspect. Al-Qurṭubī notes that no other book of its length survives that test, and that the challenge is addressed to anyone willing to do the work.

**Am ʿAlā Qulūbin Aqfāluhā: The Locks**

*"Or do hearts have their locks upon them?"* The alternative is presented with the particle *am*, which in this construction introduces a rhetorical option whose reality is affirmed: the sense, as Ibn al-Jawzī reads it, is "but hearts have their locks upon them."

The noun *aqfāl* is the plural of *qufl*, a lock — specifically the padlock, the kind that is applied to a door from outside and requires a key. The word appears in the Qur'an only here, and its grammatical form deserves attention because the commentators found it significant. The phrase is *ʿalā qulūbin aqfāluhā* — "upon hearts, their locks." The noun *qulūb* is indefinite and the noun *aqfāl* is definite, with the definite article *al-* and the attached pronoun *-hā* referring back to the hearts. The construction is read as an intensification: not "are there locks upon hearts?" in general, but "do *the* hearts have *their own* locks upon them?" — as though each heart carries the lock made for it, its own particular closure.

Al-Zamakhsharī remarked on the construction as one of the Qur'an's most eloquent: the definite article on the locks with the pronoun referring to indefinite hearts produces the sense that these are locks specifically theirs, fitted to them, belonging to them. The image is of a padlock with a name on it.

The gradation the source commentary records from Ibn al-Jawzī is important: some understand the locked heart to refer to the heart of the most severely afflicted, whom no guidance reaches, while hearts with rust upon them (83:14) and hearts that are sealed (v. 16) are less diseased. The hierarchy — rust, seal, lock — describes three stages of closure, each worse than the last. Rust is a deposit that can be polished; a seal is an impression that can be broken; a lock is a mechanism that requires a key the person does not possess.

**The Key: What Opens the Locked Heart**

If the heart is locked, and the lock requires a key, and the person does not have the key, then the verse's opening command — contemplate the Qur'an — appears impossible. This is the objection the commentators faced, and their answer is that the command is possible precisely because the verse issues it: the contemplation of the Qur'an *is* the key, and the fact that it is commanded means it is available.

The tradition states this in a report of the Prophet ﷺ preserved by al-Bukhārī and Muslim in slightly different forms:

> *"Hearts are like vessels, and the best of them is the most retentive."* — and in the version of al-Tirmidhī: *"Verily these hearts rust as iron rusts when water touches it."* He was asked, "O Messenger of God, what is their polishing?" He said: *"Remembering death and reciting the Qur'an."* (al-Bayhaqī, *Shuʿab al-Īmān*; al-Tirmidhī, from Ibn ʿUmar and Abū Hurayrah in related wordings; the polishing report is graded ḥasan by al-ʿIrāqī and others)

The pairing — remembrance of death and recitation of the Qur'an — is exactly the pairing Sūrah Muḥammad supplies at verses 18 and 24: the suddenness of the Hour, and the contemplation of the Book. The two together polish the rust, and the polishing is the beginning of the unlocking.

The practical instruction the tradition derived is specific. Ibn Masʿūd, whose method of teaching the Companions the source commentary records at this verse, is reported to have said:

> *"The Prophet used to recite for them ten verses, and they would not move on to the next ten until they had learned everything about the first ten — the knowledge and the action. Thus he taught them the Qur'an and the deeds based upon it together."* (Aḥmad, *al-Musnad*; Abū Nuʿaym, *al-Ḥilyah*)

Ten verses at a time, and not forward until the ten have been understood *and acted upon*. This is *tadabbur* as a method, and it explains why the Companions took years to memorize the Qur'an while later generations memorize it in two. The verse 24 command is not to read more; it is to look further into less.

**The Fourth Imam's Statement on the Verse as Treasure**

The source commentary records the saying of ʿAlī Zayn al-ʿĀbidīn, the fourth Imam of the Shīʿah (d. 94/713), transmitted across the traditions:

> *"The verses of the Qur'an are stores of treasure. So whenever you open one of them, you must see what lies inside."*

The image is of a locked container holding something valuable — the same image as the locked heart, inverted. The verse is locked in the sense that its contents are not visible from outside, and the reader's task is to open it and look inside. This is what *tadabbur* means as a practice: not the accumulation of verses but the opening of them.

Ibn Masʿūd's parallel statement, also recorded in the source, gives the reason:

> *"When you intend to acquire knowledge, study the Qur'an in depth, for in it lies the knowledge of those of old and those of later times."*

And the Prophet's ﷺ own statement of the value of the practice:

> *"The best of you is the one who learns the Qur'an and teaches it."* (al-Bukhārī 5027, from ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān)

The ḥadīth's pairing of learning and teaching is the same pairing the verse implies: the one who contemplates receives, and the one who teaches passes on what he received. Neither is complete without the other, and the sūrah's verse 16 describes the failure of the first — the man who heard and received nothing — while verse 30 will describe the failure of the second, the man whose tone of speech revealed what he had not taken in.

**Al-Ghazzālī's Four Obstacles, Applied**

The source commentary records al-Ghazzālī's four obstacles to understanding the Qur'an at verse 16, and they belong equally here, since verse 24 names the practice those obstacles prevent. Restated against the command to contemplate:

The person preoccupied with the correctness of his pronunciation is reading the letters and not the meanings. His *tadabbur* has been consumed by his *tajwīd*, and while the tajwīd is a virtue, its substitution for contemplation is the obstacle.

The person whose school has already told him what every verse means cannot contemplate, because contemplation requires the possibility of discovering something. His reading is a verification, not an inquiry.

The person persisting in sin, or characterized by pride and worldly passion, finds that the verse does not reach him. He reads and is unmoved, and the unmoved reading is the report of the rust.

The person who assumes the transmitted commentaries exhaust the Book has closed it while holding it open. He has read Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd and concluded that there is nothing further to find, and the conclusion prevents the finding.

Each of these is a lock, and each has a key: attention to meaning alongside pronunciation, openness to the text alongside the tradition, repentance alongside reading, and the expectation of discovery alongside the received commentary.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Answer to Its Own Diagnosis**

Sūrah Muḥammad has, by verse 24, described the failure of understanding in five separate images: the man who listens and asks what was said (v. 16), the one whose evil deeds are beautified (v. 14), the follower of caprice (v. 14), the sealed heart (v. 16), the deaf and blind (v. 23). A reader who has followed the sūrah this far may reasonably ask whether the condition is curable, and whether he is in it.

Verse 24 answers both questions in one sentence. The condition is curable, because contemplation is commanded, and nothing is commanded that is impossible. And the reader can determine whether he is in it, by the simple test of whether the Qur'an still reaches him. If a verse he has read a hundred times strikes him suddenly with a meaning he did not have before, his heart is not locked. If nothing in the Book moves him and he has stopped expecting that anything will, the locks are the diagnosis.

The verse's own form is a mercy in this respect. It is a question, not an accusation. *Do they not contemplate?* — the question leaves the answer open, and an open question is an invitation. The sūrah has pronounced curses, sealed hearts, and deafened ears; and then, at the center of its examination of hypocrisy, it asks a question that anyone can still answer. The asking is the proof that the door is not yet closed.

Ibn al-Qayyim wrote in *al-Fawāʾid* that the Qur'an is a letter from the Beloved to those who love Him, and that a letter is read differently by the one who loves its sender than by the one who does not. The lover reads a single line for an hour. The other reads the whole letter in a minute and puts it down. The difference between the two readings is not intelligence; it is the condition of the heart, and verse 24 names the condition by naming its symptom: whether the reading produces anything.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:25

> **Truly those who turn their backs after guidance has been made clear to them—Satan has seduced them, yet He grants them respite.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Irtaddū ʿAlā Adbārihim: Turning Back Upon the Heels**

Verse 25 describes a specific movement, and the Arabic makes it physical. *Inna alladhīna rtaddū ʿalā adbārihim min baʿdi mā tabayyana lahumu al-hudā* — "truly those who turn their backs after guidance has been made clear to them." The verb *irtadda* is the eighth form of *r-d-d*, to return, to turn back; *adbār* is the plural of *dubur*, the back, the rear — the same root that produces *tadabbur* at verse 24. The phrase pictures a person who has been walking forward and then turns around on his heels and walks back the way he came.

The image is not the sūrah's invention; it is a Qur'anic idiom with a fixed force:

> *"Muhammad is only a messenger; messengers have passed away before him. If he dies or is killed, will you turn back upon your heels (tanqalibū ʿalā aʿqābikum)? And whoever turns back upon his heels will not harm God in the least, and God will reward the grateful."* (3:144)

The verse was revealed at Uḥud when the rumor spread that the Prophet ﷺ had been killed, and some of the Muslims did in fact turn back — the report in al-Bukhārī describes men sitting down and saying, "if only we could send to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy to obtain a guarantee for us from Abū Sufyān." The verse's rebuke established the idiom: *turning back on the heels* is the specific sin of the one who abandons the faith at the moment it becomes costly.

And:

> *"And We appointed the direction thou wast facing only that We might know him who follows the Messenger from him who turns back upon his heels (yanqalibu ʿalā ʿaqibayh); and truly it was a hard thing, except for those whom God guided."* (2:143)

The change of the qiblah from Jerusalem to the Kaʿbah was itself a test of exactly this kind, and the Qur'an describes it as difficult except for the guided. Turning back on the heels is therefore not always a rejection of doctrine; sometimes it is an inability to follow a change in practice, and the sūrah treats both cases alike.

**Min Baʿdi Mā Tabayyana Lahumu al-Hudā: After It Became Clear**

The clause *"after guidance has been made clear to them"* is the verse's central qualification, and it establishes the gravity of the offense. *Tabayyana* is the fifth form of *b-y-n*, to become clear, evident, manifest — the same root as *bayyinah* at verse 14. The guidance did not merely arrive; it became *clear*. The person who turned back did not turn back in confusion but in full sight.

The Qur'an distinguishes sharply between the one who never received the message and the one who received it clearly and left. Of the latter it says:

> *"And whoever opposes the Messenger after guidance has been made clear to him, and follows other than the way of the believers, We shall turn him unto what he has turned unto, and We shall roast him in Hell — and wretched is the destination."* (4:115)

Note the divine response: *We shall turn him unto what he has turned unto.* The one who turns away is turned — the same principle as verse 23's deafening and blinding, stated here as a proportionate response. And at 61:5: *"And when they turned away, God turned their hearts away."*

This is why the tradition has always treated apostasy after clarity as a graver matter than persistent unbelief. The disbeliever who never understood has an excuse the Qur'an recognizes: *"Lest you should say: There came to us no bringer of glad tidings and no warner"* (5:19). The one who understood and left has no such excuse, and verse 25 supplies the explanation for his leaving — which is not reason but seduction.

**Al-Shayṭānu Sawwala Lahum: The Making-Easy**

*"Satan has seduced them"* renders *al-shayṭānu sawwala lahum*. The verb *sawwala*, from *s-w-l*, is one of the Qur'an's most precise psychological terms and it is usually undertranslated. Its root sense is to make something seem easy, agreeable, attainable; to smooth the way to it; to present it attractively and to encourage the person with false hopes about its consequences. The Qur'an uses it several times:

> *"Satan made their deeds fair-seeming unto them and barred them from the way, though they were endowed with keen perceptions."* (29:38)

> *"And when their deed was made fair-seeming unto them (zayyana), and they thought they were able — Satan said: I am free of you; I see what you do not see."* (8:48)

> *"And when Moses came unto Our appointed time and his Lord spoke to him, he said: My Lord, show me, that I may look upon Thee... [and later] Samiri said: I saw what they did not see, and I took a handful from the trace of the messenger, and I cast it in. Thus my soul made it fair-seeming unto me (sawwalat lī nafsī)."* (20:96)

The mechanism *taswīl* describes is a threefold operation. First, the act is made to look easy — the difficulty is minimized. Second, the act is made to look reasonable — the objections are answered in advance with plausible arguments. Third, the consequences are made to look distant or negligible — the cost is deferred, discounted, or denied. The person who falls for *taswīl* does not experience himself as being seduced; he experiences himself as having worked out a sensible plan.

The Qur'an's account of Satan's own method is stated in his own words:

> *"He said: Because Thou hast led me astray, I will surely sit in ambush for them upon Thy straight path. Then I will come upon them from before them and from behind them, and from their right hands and from their left hands, and Thou wilt not find most of them grateful."* (7:16–17)

The four directions — before, behind, right, left — describe an encirclement. The straight path is the only direction not named, and the reason is that Satan cannot attack from God's direction; he attacks from every other side. Ibn ʿAbbās explained the four approaches in the report transmitted by al-Ṭabarī: from before them — by making them doubt the Hereafter; from behind them — by making them attached to this world; from their right — by confusing them about their good deeds (making them proud of them); from their left — by making their evil deeds attractive.

Verse 25's *sawwala* is the operation described from the left and from behind. The person is told that his desires are reasonable, that the cost of the straight path is excessive, and that there will be time to return later.

**Wa-Amlā Lahum: Respite or False Hope**

The final clause is the verse's most discussed. *Wa-amlaa lahum* — and the subject of the verb is grammatically ambiguous, and the ambiguity produces two readings the source commentary records.

On the first reading, the subject is God: "yet He grants them respite." The verb *amlā* is from *m-l-y*, to give time, to extend a period — the same root as *imhāl* and *amlā*, used at 3:178: *"And let not those who disbelieve think that Our granting them respite (imlāʾanā) is better for their souls; We grant them respite only that they may increase in sin."* On this reading the verse describes God's extension of their time — a respite that is either an opportunity to repent before death or, if unrepented, a further proof of their sinful nature.

On the second reading, recorded from al-Ṭabarī, the verb derives from *amala*, to hope, and the subject is Satan: "and he gives them false hope." The two readings produce different images but the same diagnosis. Satan either secures for his victim an extension of time — through the respite God grants — or fills him with false hopes about what the time contains.

The Qur'an's treatment of respite is unambiguous about its danger:

> *"Do they think that what We provide them of wealth and children — We hasten unto them in good deeds? No, but they do not perceive."* (23:55–56)

> *"So when they forgot that by which they were reminded, We opened unto them the gates of every thing, until when they rejoiced in what they were given, We seized them suddenly, and lo, they were in despair."* (6:44)

> *"And I grant them respite; truly My plan is firm."* (7:183)

The *istidrāj* — being led on by degrees — is the theological name for this process, and verse 25 places it squarely in the sūrah's argument. The hypocrites and the apostates of Madinah were not struck down. They continued to live, to attend the assemblies, to prosper, to be treated as members of the community. Their respite was the evidence they used to conclude that they were right, and the respite was in fact the mechanism of their seduction.

**Who Are They?**

The commentators identified the referents of *"those who turn their backs"* along three lines, all recorded in the source.

*First*, the hypocrites who turned away when fighting was enjoined upon them — the men of verse 20 who fainted at the mention of battle and whose retreat was a turning back upon the heels in the sense of 3:144. Ibn al-Jawzī and al-Qurṭubī favor this reading, and it fits the sūrah's immediate context: verses 20–24 have been about the reaction to the command to fight, and verse 25 explains it.

*Second*, the Jews of Madinah who rejected the Prophet ﷺ and turned away from part of what was revealed to them, as at 2:85: *"Do you, then, believe in part of the Book and disbelieve in part?"* The verse 2:85 describes a community that accepted some of the revelation and rejected the rest, and that "turned away" from the part it did not accept — a partial *irtidād* that the Qur'an treats as complete in its consequences.

*Third*, the disbelievers who knew the Prophet's ﷺ trustworthy character well before the coming of Islam and nonetheless rejected him afterward. This is the reading al-Qurṭubī notes, and it is supported by the historical record: the Quraysh called him *al-Ṣādiq al-Amīn*, the truthful, the trustworthy, for forty years before the revelation, and Abu Jahl is reported to have said to him privately that they did not accuse him of lying but rejected what he brought. The Qur'an records the distinction:

> *"Truly We know that what they say grieves thee. For they do not accuse thee of lying, but the wrongdoers deny the signs of God."* (6:33)

The third reading gives verse 25 its most uncomfortable application: the person who had every reason to believe, who knew the character of the messenger firsthand, who had no doubt about his honesty — and turned back anyway. The clarity of the guidance (*tabayyana*) was in his case complete, and the turning was therefore inexcusable.

**The Verse's Function in the Sūrah**

Verse 25 sits at a turning point. Verses 20–24 have described the hypocrites' reaction to the command to fight, the alternative they should have chosen, the corruption their turning away would produce, the curse and deafness that follow, and the command to contemplate. Verse 25 explains the mechanism underneath all of it: Satan's *taswīl* and the respite that permits it.

The explanation serves two purposes. It removes the excuse — the turning back was not produced by confusion but by seduction, and seduction succeeds only where the appetite consents. And it locates the danger — the respite means the seduction is ongoing, and the person under it will not feel it. The one who is being led on by degrees experiences the leading as prosperity.

The verse therefore ends where the sūrah's whole argument has been pointing: at the necessity of self-examination in conditions where self-examination feels unnecessary. Verses 26 to 30 will describe the exposure that is coming — the secret consultations, the tone of speech, the rancor that God will bring out — and verse 25 explains why the exposed did not see it coming. They were given time, and the time was used against them.

The practical remedy the tradition draws from this verse is the practice of *muḥāsabah* — the daily reckoning with oneself. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb's instruction, transmitted by al-Tirmidhī and quoted in every work of Islamic spirituality:

> *"Reckon yourselves before you are reckoned, and weigh yourselves before you are weighed; for it is easier for you to reckon yourselves today than tomorrow. And adorn yourselves for the greatest display: on that day you shall be exhibited, and nothing of you shall be hidden."* (al-Tirmidhī 2459, from ʿUmar; also in Aḥmad)

The reckoning is the antidote to *taswīl*, because the seduction depends on the person not looking at his own trajectory. The one who examines himself weekly cannot be led on by degrees, because he notices the degrees.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:26

> **That is because they say to those who are averse to what God has sent down, "We shall obey you in some matters." And God knows their keeping of secrets.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Secret Alliance**

Verse 25 explained the turning back as the work of Satan's seduction; verse 26 identifies the human instrument. *Dhālika bi-annahum qālū li-lladhīna karihū mā nazzala Allāhu sa-nuṭīʿukum fī baʿḍi al-amr* — "That is because they say to those who are averse to what God has sent down: We shall obey you in some matters."

The *dhālika bi-anna* formula — "that is because" — has appeared at verses 3, 9, and 11, and it always supplies the reason for a preceding statement. Here the reason given for the seduction and the respite is a specific act of speech: the hypocrites approached the open disbelievers and offered them partial obedience.

The verse is thus a report of a conspiracy, and it names the parties precisely. The speakers are the hypocrites — the diseased hearts of verses 20 and 29, the men who fainted at the mention of fighting. The addressees are *"those who are averse to what God has sent down"* — *alladhīna karihū mā nazzala Allāh* — which is the same description verse 9 gave of the disbelievers whose deeds God made to come to naught. The two groups are distinct, and the verse records their convergence: the people inside the community and the people outside it, meeting in secret, with the insiders offering compliance.

**Sa-Nuṭīʿukum Fī Baʿḍi al-Amr: Obedience in Some Matters**

The offered obedience is qualified, and the qualification is the whole of the verse's insight. *"We shall obey you in some matters"* — *fī baʿḍi al-amr*, in part of the affair. Not in everything. The hypocrites are not proposing to leave Islam; they are proposing to serve its enemies selectively, while remaining within it.

The Qur'an identifies this partiality elsewhere as a defining feature of hypocrisy, and it addresses it directly to the People of the Book:

> *"Then you are those who kill one another, and expel a party of you from their homes, backing one another against them in sin and aggression; and if they come to you as captives, you ransom them — while their expulsion was forbidden to you. Do you, then, believe in part of the Book and disbelieve in part? What is the reward of those who do so among you, but disgrace in the life of this world, and on the Day of Resurrection they shall be returned unto the most severe punishment? And God is not heedless of what you do."* (2:85)

The verse describes a community that accepted one commandment (ransoming captives) and rejected another (not expelling their own), and it asks the question that applies equally to 47:26: *do you believe in part of the Book and disbelieve in part?* The partial acceptance is not a lesser form of belief; it is a form of disbelief, because it makes the believer's own preference the criterion of which commands to accept — and a criterion other than God is the definition of an *ilāh*.

The mechanism is the same in 47:26. The hypocrites are not rejecting revelation wholesale; they are selecting from it and selecting against it, and the selection is made in consultation with those who hate it. The obedience they offer *"in some matters"* is the mirror image of the belief they hold *"in some matters,"* and both amount to the installation of a private criterion in the place of the revealed one.

**The Historical Occasion**

The commentators identify the conspiracy with specific events in Madinah. Ibn al-Jawzī and al-Qurṭubī report that it refers either to the hypocrites or to the Jews turning to the disbelievers who were averse to what God has sent down and offering to follow them. The historical record supplies the candidates.

Before the Battle of Uḥud, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy ibn Salūl — the chief of the Khazraj and the leader of the Madinan hypocrites, a man who had been on the point of being made king of Yathrib before the Prophet's ﷺ arrival displaced him — withdrew three hundred men from the army on the way to the battle, saying that the Prophet had taken the opinion of others over his. The withdrawal was a military act with the character of a betrayal, and the Qur'an addresses it at 3:121–123 and 3:155.

At the Battle of the Trench, the Banū Qurayẓah — who had a defensive treaty with the Muslims — were approached by Ḥuyayy ibn Akhṭab of the Banū Naḍīr and persuaded to break the treaty while the Muslim army was besieged, and the moment chosen was the moment of maximum danger. Ibn Isḥāq's account records the Prophet's ﷺ reaction when the news was brought: the Companions were told to be of good heart, and he sent Saʿd ibn Muʿādh and Saʿd ibn ʿUbādah to verify, and they returned with a coded report so as not to break the army's morale.

Between these events ran a continuous traffic of secret communication, and the Qur'an describes it repeatedly:

> *"And when they come unto thee, they greet thee with a greeting wherewith God greets thee not, and say within themselves: Why does God not punish us for what we say?"* (58:8)

> *"Have you not seen those who were forbidden from private conversation (al-najwā) and then returned to what they were forbidden, and converse together in sin, aggression, and disobedience to the Messenger? And when they come unto thee they say: We greet you — a greeting God does not greet you with. And they say within themselves: Why does God not punish us for what we say?"* (58:8)

Sūrah al-Munāfiqūn describes the same conspiracy in its own terms:

> *"They are the enemy, so beware of them. God's curse upon them! How they are deluded! And when it is said unto them: Come, the Messenger of God will ask forgiveness for you — they turn their heads aside, and thou seest them turning away while they are arrogant."* (63:4–5)

> *"It is they who say: Do not spend upon those who are with the Messenger of God, until they scatter. And unto God belong the treasuries of the heavens and the earth, but the hypocrites do not understand. They say: If we return to Madinah, the mightier will surely expel the weaker therefrom. And unto God belongs might, and unto His Messenger and unto the believers, but the hypocrites do not know."* (63:7–8)

The last of these — the statement of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy that the source commentary cites at verse 30 — is the clearest instance of what verse 26 describes: the secret plan, formulated among the hypocrites, aimed at the expulsion of the Prophet ﷺ and his Companions from Madinah. It was overheard by Zayd ibn Arqam, a young boy who reported it, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy denied it under oath — until the Qur'an confirmed Zayd's report by revelation, at which point, Ibn Isḥāq records, the man's own people became his harshest critics and he was reduced to silence.

**Wa-llāhu Yaʿlamu Isrārahum: The Knowledge of What Is Concealed**

The verse closes with the declaration that runs through the sūrah like a refrain: *"And God knows their keeping of secrets"* — *wa-llāhu yaʿlamu isrārahum*. The source commentary records the alternate reading: *asrārahum*, "their secrets," and notes that both readings refer to their secret consultations with the disbelievers.

*Isrār* is the verbal noun of *asarra*, the fourth form of *s-r-r*, to conceal, to keep secret, to speak in confidence — the root also produces *sirr*, a secret, and *sarīrah*, the inner state, the plural of which appears in the Qur'an's description of the Day: *"On the day when the secrets (al-sarāʾir) are tested"* (86:9). The fourth form emphasizes the deliberate act of concealment: not a thing that happens to be hidden but a thing actively hidden.

The verse's declaration is the answer to the conspiracy's method. The hypocrites' entire strategy was secrecy — the private conversation (*najwā*), the whispered agreement, the words said *"within themselves."* Against that strategy the verse sets one statement: God knows their concealing. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr both note that the declaration is placed immediately after the report of their speech, so that the reader is left in no doubt that the speech was heard.

The Qur'an makes the same declaration repeatedly and in escalating terms:

> *"It is the same to Him whether one of you conceals his speech or declares it, and whether he hides by night or goes forth by day."* (13:10)

> *"And God knows what is in your hearts, and God is Knowing, Forbearing."* (33:51)

> *"Say: If you hide what is in your breasts or manifest it, God knows it. And He knows whatever is in the heavens and whatever is in the earth, and God is powerful over all things."* (3:29)

> *"He knows the treachery of the eyes and what the breasts conceal."* (40:19)

The last of these is the most exacting: *khāʾinat al-aʿyūn* — the treachery of the eyes — the stolen glance, the look exchanged between conspirators in a room, the momentary expression that reveals what the face is composed to hide. The verse 30 that follows in this sūrah will describe God's ability to have shown the hypocrites to the Prophet ﷺ by their *mark* — *sīmā* — and by their *tone of speech* — *laḥn al-qawl*. Together the two verses describe a surveillance that includes the glance.

**Why God Does Not Expose Them Immediately**

The verse states that God knows, and the following verse (29) asks whether the hypocrites suppose God will not bring out their rancor, and the verse after that (30) says that God could have shown them to the Prophet ﷺ and did not. The sequence raises a question the tradition addressed directly: if God knows and could expose, why the delay?

The commentators offer several answers, all consistent with the sūrah's own argument. The delay is respite (v. 25), and the respite permits repentance — the door that verse 1's past tense leaves open and that verse 34 closes only at death. The delay also permits the community's own test (v. 31): *"We shall test you until We know those among you who strive and those who are patient."* The presence of hypocrites within the community is itself part of the testing of the sincere, who must learn to recognize, to be patient with, and not to become what they oppose. And the delay preserves a mercy for the believers: had every hypocrite been exposed by name, the community would have been consumed by suspicion, and the Prophet ﷺ did not act upon knowledge he was given of specific individuals — Ḥudhayfah ibn al-Yamān was given the list and instructed to keep it, and Umar spent years asking whether he was on it.

There is a further reason, and it is the one verse 30 states: *"And you will certainly know them by their tone of speech."* The exposure that matters is not the divine announcement but the community's own recognition, and recognition requires time, repeated contact, and the observation of pattern. A conspiracy exposed by miracle produces obedience without understanding; a conspiracy exposed by its own contradictions produces a community that can detect the next one. God chose the second, and the cost was borne by the first generation, who had to live beside men they came gradually to know.

**The Modern Form of the Secret Alliance**

Verse 26 describes an event in seventh-century Madinah, and its structure recurs wherever a religious community is under pressure from a hostile culture. The partial obedience — *"we shall obey you in some matters"* — is the characteristic compromise of the accommodated believer. He does not leave the faith; he negotiates with its critics, conceding point by point in the hope of retaining acceptance, and each concession is described as strategic.

The concession pattern is visible in every age. The scholar who softens a ruling because it will be reported badly. The parent who stops mentioning a practice because the neighbors find it strange. The institution that removes a phrase from its statement of belief to preserve an accreditation. The individual who agrees with the criticism of his own tradition at a dinner party, in order not to spoil the evening. None of these is apostasy. All of them are *"we shall obey you in some matters."*

The verse's judgment on the practice is not that it fails; it is that it is *known*. God knows the concealing, and the knowledge is stated here not as a threat of exposure but as a statement of fact that reorders the calculation. The hypocrite's plan depended on the assumption that the conversation was private — that what he said to those averse to revelation would never reach the believers, and that what he said to the believers would never reach those averse to revelation. Verse 26 removes the assumption. There is no private channel. The two conversations are being held in the same room, and the room is not his.

The practical consequence for a believer living under cultural pressure is not paranoia but simplification. If nothing is secret, there is nothing to manage. The person who says the same thing in every room has no conspiracy to maintain, no inconsistency to conceal, and no tone of speech to be detected by. Verse 30 will describe God's ability to know the hypocrites by their *laḥn al-qawl* — the tenor of their speech, the way they say things — and the only reliable protection against that detection is to have only one way of saying things. This is what the tradition calls *ṣidq*, truthfulness, and verse 21 has already made it the criterion: *"were they true to God, it would be better for them."*

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:27

> **Then how will it be when the angels seize them, striking their faces and their backs?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question About a Moment**

Verse 26 ended with the declaration that God knows their secrets; verse 27 advances to the moment when the knowledge will be acted upon. *Fa-kayfa idhā tawaffathumu al-malāʾikatu yaḍribūna wujūhahum wa-adbārahum* — "Then how will it be when the angels seize them, striking their faces and their backs?"

The construction *fa-kayfa idhā* — "then how, when..." — is an elliptical interrogation that the Qur'an uses at moments of extremity. The verb that would complete the sentence is left unspoken: how will it be *for them*, how will they *fare*, how will they *manage*. The omission is more powerful than any completion, because it forces the hearer to imagine the scene himself and to notice that no adequate answer exists. The same construction appears at 40:16:

> *"On the day they come forth, nothing of them is hidden from God. To whom belongs the dominion today? To God, the One, the Overpowering."* (40:16)

and at 47:27's closest parallel, 8:50, which supplies the fuller version of the same scene.

**Tawaffathumu al-Malāʾikah: The Taking of the Souls**

*Tawaffā* is the third form of *w-f-y*, to fulfill, to complete, to pay in full — the root of *wafāʾ*, loyalty, and *wāfī*, complete. The verb's use for death is precise and it is not a euphemism: to *tawaffā* a person is to take him in full, to collect him completely, the way a debt is collected in full. The soul is not lost at death; it is *collected*. The Qur'an uses the verb of God and of the angels:

> *"God takes the souls at the time of their death, and those that have not died, in their sleep. Then He withholds those upon whom death has been decreed, and sends the others until an appointed term."* (39:42)

> *"Say: The angel of death who has been entrusted with you will take you (yatawaffākum); then unto your Lord you will be returned."* (32:11)

> *"Those whom the angels take (yatawaffāhum) in a state of purity, saying: Peace be upon you; enter the Garden for what you used to do."* (16:32)

The last two establish the two modes of the taking, and the Qur'an describes both with a specificity that verse 27 belongs to. For the believer:

> *"Those whom the angels take while they are pure, they say: Peace be upon you; enter the Garden for what you used to do."* (16:32)

For the disbeliever:

> *"And if thou couldst see when the angels take the souls of those who disbelieve, striking their faces and their backs: Taste the punishment of the burning! That is for what your hands sent forward, and God is not unjust to the servants."* (8:50–51)

And:

> *"If thou couldst see when the angels take the souls of the disbelievers, striking their faces and their backs, and: Taste the punishment of the burning."* (8:50)

Verse 47:27 is thus the sūrah's own version of a scene the Qur'an describes at least four times (6:93; 8:50; 16:28; 47:27), and 6:93 supplies the most detailed:

> *"And if thou couldst see when the wrongdoers are in the agonies of death, and the angels stretch forth their hands: Bring out your souls! Today you shall be recompensed with the punishment of humiliation for what you used to say against God other than the truth, and for the signs you used to scorn."* (6:93)

*"Bring out your souls"* — *akhrijū anfusakum* — is a command addressed to the dying person by the angels, and the tradition explains it: the soul of the disbeliever does not come out willingly; it clings, it scatters into the body, and the angels beat it out. Al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib's famous report, transmitted in the *Musnad* of Aḥmad and in Abū Dāwūd, and graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Ḥākim, describes the two deaths at length:

> *"The soul of the believer comes out flowing as a drop flows from the mouth of a waterskin... and the soul of the disbeliever is scattered in his body, and the angels descend upon him with a garment of fire, and they say: Come out, O foul soul, come out to what you used to hate."* (Aḥmad, *al-Musnad* 18534; Abū Dāwūd 4753, from al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib)

**Yaḍribūna Wujūhahum Wa-Adbārahum: The Faces and the Backs**

The striking is of two places, and the commentators found meaning in the specification. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī note that the face is struck because it is the front of the person — the direction he faced — and the back because it is the direction he turned toward when he turned away. The verse 25's *irtaddū ʿalā adbārihim* — they turned back upon their backs — is answered here by the striking of the *adbār*. The instrument of the turning becomes the site of the blow.

Another reading takes the phrase as covering the whole person: from front and behind, on every side, without an unstruck part remaining. This reading is supported by the Qur'an's parallel at 16:28: *"Those whom the angels take while they are wronging themselves"* and at 8:50's identical phrase.

The face in particular carries a symbolic weight in the Qur'anic vocabulary. The face is what one turns toward a direction: *"So turn thy face toward the Sacred Mosque"* (2:144, 149, 150); it is what remains when everything else perishes: *"All that is upon it perishes, and the Face of thy Lord abides"* (55:26–27); it is what will be radiant or darkened on the Day: *"Faces that day will be radiant, laughing, rejoicing; and faces that day will be dust-covered, covered by darkness"* (80:38–41); *"On the day when some faces will be whitened and some faces blackened"* (3:106). To strike the face is therefore not only to inflict pain but to dishonor the organ of direction and identity. The Prophet ﷺ forbade striking the face even in corporal punishment and even in war:

> *"If one of you fights, let him avoid the face."* (al-Bukhārī 2559; Muslim 2612)

And he forbade it in discipline: *"Do not strike the face, and do not revile"* (Abū Dāwūd 2142). The prohibition in the earthly case makes the divine case more stark: the striking of the faces described in verse 27 is not a human act governed by human limits. It is the reception of a soul by those who have no instruction to spare it.

**How Will It Be: The Verse's Real Question**

The question *"then how will it be?"* is not asking about the mechanics of the scene, which the Qur'an has already described in detail elsewhere. It is asking about the state of the person to whom it happens — and the answer it implies is that he will have nothing. No patron (v. 11), no help (v. 13), no remembrance that avails (v. 18), no deeds that arrive (vv. 1, 8, 9), no secret that stays concealed (v. 26). The accumulation of the sūrah's negatives reaches its point here, and the question is the sum of them.

Al-Rāzī observes that the verse's interrogation is directed at the reader rather than at the hypocrites, who will not hear it. Its function is to make the living hearer imagine himself in the scene — and the imagining is the mercy, because the person who can imagine it can still avoid it. Verse 18 established that the Hour comes suddenly and that late remembrance is useless; verse 27 establishes that the personal hour — the taking of the soul — comes with the same suddenness and the same uselessness, and that it comes before the cosmic Hour, so that no one can defer his own arrival by deferring the end of the world.

The tradition's practice in the face of this verse was the preparation of the deathbed. The Prophet ﷺ instructed:

> *"Mention the death to your sick ones, for no one is in a state and remembers death, except that it becomes a comfort to him."* — and the general instruction: *"Visit the sick and follow the bier; it reminds you of the Hereafter."* (Aḥmad; Muslim 2162, on following the bier)

And his own description of the believer's death is the counterpart to verse 27:

> *"When the believer is about to depart from the world and turn toward the Hereafter, angels descend upon him from heaven with faces as though they were suns, and they sit from him as far as the eye can see..."* (Aḥmad 18534, from al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib)

The two descriptions were held together in the Islamic imagination deliberately. The tradition did not allow the believer to contemplate the striking of the faces without also contemplating the faces like suns, and the purpose of holding both was not to frighten but to orient.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:28

> **That is because they followed that which angers God and were averse to His good pleasure; so He made their deeds come to naught.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reason Restated at the Level of the Will**

Verse 27 described the scene of the angels striking faces and backs; verse 28 explains it, and the explanation is not new — it is verse 9 returned in a more penetrating form. *Dhālika bi-annahumu ttabaʿū mā askhaṭa Allāha wa-karihū riḍwānahu fa-aḥbaṭa aʿmālahum* — "That is because they followed that which angers God and were averse to His good pleasure; so He made their deeds come to naught."

Compare verse 9: *"That is because they were averse to that which God sent down, so He made their deeds come to naught."* The two verses have the same skeleton — *dhālika bi-anna* ... *fa-aḥbaṭa aʿmālahum* — and the same conclusion. What differs is the middle. Verse 9 located the cause in aversion to revelation: *karahū mā anzala Allāh*. Verse 28 locates it in the orientation of the will itself: *ittabaʿū mā askhaṭa Allāha wa-karihū riḍwānah* — they followed what angers God and were averse to what pleases Him.

The movement from verse 9 to verse 28 is a movement from the object to the subject. Verse 9 said what they rejected; verse 28 says what they loved and what they hated. And the answer is symmetrical and complete: they loved what God hates, and they hated what God loves. There is no residue. The whole of their moral orientation has been described in two clauses, and the description leaves nothing that could be corrected by argument, since argument addresses belief and this is about love.

**Ittabaʿū Mā Askhaṭa Allāh: The Following of What Angers**

The verb *ittabaʿa* — to follow — is the sūrah's governing verb, appearing at verse 3 (*the disbelievers followed falsehood, the believers followed the truth*), at verse 14 (*those who follow their caprices*), and at verse 16 (*who follow their caprices*). Its reappearance here confirms the sūrah's structure: everything comes down to what a person is walking behind.

*Askhaṭa* is the fourth form of *kh-ṭ*, wrath, anger — the causative meaning "that which causes anger." The phrase *mā askhaṭa Allāh* — "that which angers God" — is a category rather than a list: whatever produces divine displeasure. The Qur'an's counterpart is *riḍwān*, and the two are set as the only alternatives, exactly as verse 3 set *al-ḥaqq* and *al-bāṭil*. There is no neutral category. Every act either angers or pleases, and the one who does not know which is doing has not been left without means of knowing.

The divine attributes of anger and pleasure require a word, because the tradition has been careful about them. The Sunni position, articulated by Ibn Taymiyyah against both the anthropomorphists and the deniers, is that God's *ghaḍab* and *riḍā* are real attributes affirmed by revelation, not reducible to a metaphor for reward and punishment, and not resembling the anger and pleasure of created beings, which are passions arising from a disturbance in the one who feels them. God's displeasure is not a disturbance in God; it is the settled opposition of His will to what is contrary to His command and His wisdom. The affirmation without likeness (*bi-lā tashbīh*) and without stripping (*bi-lā taʿṭīl*) is the classical formula, and it allows the Qur'an to speak of God's anger and pleasure as real while refusing to describe them as emotional states.

The practical consequence of the distinction is that the believer's relation to God's pleasure is a matter of conduct rather than of mood-management. One does not placate God; one does what pleases Him, and the Qur'an tells him what that is.

**Karihū Riḍwānah: Aversion to the Good Pleasure**

*Riḍwān* is the intensive form of *riḍā*, from *r-ḍ-w* (and *r-ḍ-y*), to be pleased, content, accepting. The intensive form denotes the fullness of the pleasure — God's complete acceptance and good will. The Qur'an presents it as the greatest of the rewards of Paradise, greater than the Garden itself:

> *"God has promised the believing men and the believing women Gardens with rivers running below, abiding therein, and dwellings of goodness in Gardens of perpetuity — and good pleasure from God is greater. That is the great triumph."* (9:72)

*"And good pleasure from God is greater"* — *wa-riḍwānun mina Allāhi akbar*. The rivers, the dwellings, the eternity — all of it is exceeded by the pleasure. And the inhabitants of the Garden are given the statement directly:

> *"And God will call unto them: Did I not give you your Lord's promise? They will say: Yes. And a caller will call in the midst of them: The curse of God be upon the wrongdoers!"* — and elsewhere: *"I have made My pleasure incumbent upon you, and I will never be angry with you after this."* (Muslim 2837, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī, in the description of the Garden: God says to its inhabitants, *"Will you not be pleased?"* They say, "How should we not be pleased, O Lord, when You have given us what You have given no one of Your creation?" He says, *"Shall I not give you better than that?"* They say, "What is better than that?" He says: *"I will cause My pleasure to descend upon you, and I will never be angry with you afterward."*)

Verse 28's statement is therefore the inversion of the highest reward: they were *averse* to it. The word *karihū* is the same verb used at verse 9 of their aversion to revelation and at verse 26 of those *"averse to what God has sent down."* The sūrah has made *karāhah* — aversion — its central diagnosis, and verse 28 carries it to its furthest point: aversion not merely to a text but to the pleasure of God.

Al-Rāzī's analysis of the clause is worth recording. He observes that the aversion to God's pleasure necessarily follows from the following of what angers Him, because a person cannot love the thing and love the displeasure of its owner; the two clauses are not two faults but one fault described from two sides. And Ibn al-Qayyim developed the same point in *Madārij al-Sālikīn*: the heart's love and God's love cannot coexist in the same direction, and the person who follows what angers God has thereby made his own preference a rival to divine pleasure — which is the definition of *shirk al-hawā*, the association of caprice, named at 45:23: *"Have you seen him who takes his caprice as his god?"*

**Fa-Aḥbaṭa Aʿmālahum: The Third Occurrence**

The conclusion — *"so He made their deeds come to naught"* — is the third use of *aḥbaṭa* in the sūrah (after vv. 9 and 28; the fifth will be at v. 32). The verb's etymology was discussed at verse 9: the animal that grazes on a poisonous plant, swells, and dies. The deed looks substantial and is rotten.

Its placement here, at the end of the sūrah's longest diagnosis of hypocrisy, gives it a specific application. The deeds being nullified are not only the deeds of open disbelief; they are the deeds of the people described in verses 20 through 27 — the ones who asked for a sūrah, who fainted at fighting, who plotted in secret, who will be struck by the angels. These people prayed. They attended the assemblies. They said the testimony. They may have given charity and fasted and fought occasionally. All of it is nullified, and the reason given is not the absence of deeds but the direction of the will: they followed what angers God and were averse to His pleasure.

Al-Rāzī notes the distinction between this nullification and the one at verse 9: here the deeds that come to naught are understood as a reference to acts done in defiance of the Prophet ﷺ, and the use of the future tense at verse 32 conveys a promise that they will not prevail against him. The three occurrences of *aḥbaṭa* in the sūrah therefore cover three objects: the deeds of general disbelief (v. 9), the deeds of the hypocrites' conspiracy (v. 28), and the deeds of those who oppose the Messenger after guidance became clear (v. 32).

**The Two Directions of the Will**

Verse 28's structure gives the sūrah its clearest statement of the moral psychology underlying everything else. The human will has two directions: toward what God loves and toward what He hates. There is no third direction and no neutral position, because the will is always directed at something, and whatever it is directed at is either pleasing to God or angering to Him.

The person who follows what angers God is not thereby a monster; he is usually a person doing ordinary things — pursuing wealth by an unlawful means, maintaining a relationship God has forbidden, seeking approval from people whose approval God does not value, avoiding an obligation because it is inconvenient. Each act is small. The verse's warning is that the acts accumulate into an orientation, and the orientation is what is judged.

The person averse to God's pleasure is not someone who hates God in a felt sense; he is someone who finds the divine approval unappealing. He would rather be admired by his peers than accepted by God. He would rather have the wealth than the blessing on it. He would rather be comfortable than be right. The aversion is not dramatic; it is a matter of preference, and preferences are what a life is made of.

The Qur'an's remedy for the misdirected will is not an argument but a reordering of love, and it is stated in the report preserved by al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

> *"None of you believes until I am more beloved to him than his father, his child, and all mankind."* (al-Bukhārī 15; Muslim 44, from Anas ibn Mālik)

The condition of belief is a ranking of loves.
