And in the version recorded by ʿAbd Allāh ibn Hishām, ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb said: "O Messenger of God, you are more beloved to me than everything except myself." The Prophet ﷺ said: *"No, by Him in Whose Hand is my soul — until I am more beloved to you than yourself."* ʿUmar said, "Then by God, you are now more beloved to me than myself." He said: *"Now, ʿUmar."* (al-Bukhārī 6632)

The report is the practical content of verse 28 read in the positive: the one who has reordered his loves follows what pleases God and is averse to what angers Him, and the nullification does not touch his deeds. The one who has not will find, on the day the angels come, that the direction of his will was the direction of his whole life, and that his deeds went the same way.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:29

> **Or do those in whose hearts is a disease suppose that God will not expose their rancor?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question the Hypocrites Did Not Ask Themselves**

Verse 29 is a single question, and it is the sūrah's most direct address to the hidden interior of its opponents. *Am ḥasiba alladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍun an lan yukhrija Allāhu aḍghānahum* — "Or do those in whose hearts is a disease suppose that God will not expose their rancor?"

The particle *am* at the head, following the preceding verses, introduces a question that carries a negative expectation: *or do they really think...?* The verb *ḥasiba* means to suppose, to reckon, to calculate — and in the Qur'an it is frequently used of a mistaken calculation: *"Does he suppose that none sees him?"* (90:7); *"Do those who do evil reckon that We will make them like those who believe and perform righteous deeds?"* (45:21); *"Does man suppose that We shall not assemble his bones?"* (75:3). The verse therefore identifies not an act but a *miscalculation*: the diseased hearts have worked out that their condition will remain undetected, and the calculation is wrong.

The clause they are supposed to have reasoned is *an lan yukhrija Allāhu aḍghānahum* — "that God will not bring out their rancors." The verb *yukhrija*, from *kh-r-j*, to bring out, to extract, to cause to emerge — the same verb used of the dead emerging from the graves and of plants emerging from the earth. The rancors are pictured as buried things, and the verse asks whether the people who buried them have concluded that the burial is permanent.

**Aḍghān: The Buried Hatreds**

*Aḍghān* is the plural of *ḍighn*, and the word is more specific than "hatred." Al-Rāghib defines *al-ḍighn* as hatred combined with enmity that has settled in the heart and been concealed there — a grudge that has been kept down long enough to harden. It is not the flash of anger (*ghaḍab), which passes; it is not the open hostility (*ʿadāwah*), which declares itself. It is the resentment that has been swallowed and retained, and the retention is what makes it a *ḍighn*.

The Qur'an uses the word and its relatives in a small number of carefully chosen places, and each use describes the removal or the exposure of what was buried:

> *"And We shall remove whatever is in their breasts of rancor (ghill) — brothers upon couches, facing one another."* (15:47)

> *"And We shall remove whatever of rancor (ghill) is in their breasts — rivers running below them."* (7:43)

> *"Our Lord, forgive us and our brothers who preceded us in faith, and place not in our hearts any rancor (ghill) toward those who believe. Our Lord, truly Thou art Kind, Merciful."* (59:10)

> *"Say: Shall I inform you of better than that? For those who are reverent, with their Lord are Gardens..."* — and in the same context, of the believers who spend *"in ease and in hardship, who restrain their anger and pardon mankind"* (3:134).

*Ghill* and *ḍighn* are near-synonyms, and the Qur'an's treatment of both is consistent: they exist in the heart, they are concealed there, they are either removed by God (in Paradise, through the prayer of 59:10 and the promise of 15:47) or exposed by God (at verse 29). There is no third outcome. The buried hatred either comes out as forgiveness or it comes out as accusation, and the person does not control which.

The image of the Garden in 15:47 is instructive because it names what the removal produces: *brothers upon couches, facing one another*. The facing is significant — the inhabitants of the Garden look at each other directly, with nothing concealed and nothing withheld, because the *ghill* has been taken out. The hypocrites of verse 29 are the inverse: they sit in the assembly facing the Prophet ﷺ with the *ḍighn* still inside, and the verse announces that the interior will be opened.

**The Exposure That Is Coming**

The verb *yukhrija* is in the future within a negation that the verse denies: *an lan yukhrija Allāhu* — "that God will not bring out." *Lan* is the particle of absolute future negation, and the verse's whole content is the denial of that negation: yes, He will bring it out.

The Qur'an describes the manner of the bringing-out in several places, and the descriptions are of increasing comprehensiveness.

The exposure to the community in this life: verse 30 immediately follows, and it states that God could have shown the hypocrites to the Prophet ﷺ by their marks, and that he will certainly know them by their tone of speech. This is a partial exposure, in this world, to a limited number of people — and the sūrah notes at verse 30 that even this was not done completely (*"and if We willed"*), which is itself a mercy.

The exposure at death: verse 27 has already described the angels striking faces and backs, and the striking is the physical form of the exposure — the soul taken in full, with nothing left inside.

The exposure on the Day: *"On the day when the secrets (al-sarāʾir) are tested"* (86:9); *"and the secrets of the breasts are brought out"* (100:10); *"Does he not know that when what is in the graves is poured out, and what is in the breasts is obtained, that day their Lord will be fully Aware of them?"* (100:9–11). The verb at 100:10 is *ḥuṣṣila* — obtained, extracted, distilled — the same image as *yukhrija*: what was inside is taken out and held.

The cumulative effect of these verses is that concealment is temporary in a technical sense: it has an expiry, and the expiry is fixed, and the person under it does not know the date. Verse 29's question is therefore not a threat of possible exposure but a correction of a mistaken calculation about certain exposure.

**Why the Verse Is Addressed to the Believers Too**

The question is put in the third person — *do those in whose hearts is a disease suppose* — but the sūrah's readership includes people who are not hypocrites, and the tradition has consistently read such verses as addressed to everyone who can hear them. The question becomes: do *I* suppose that God will not bring out what is in my heart?

The application is immediate and uncomfortable. Every person carries resentments he has not spoken. The colleague passed over, the relative who said something unforgivable twenty years ago, the friend who did not return the favor, the parent whose criticism was never answered, the spouse whose indifference accumulated silently. Most of these are not acted upon; most are not even consciously reviewed. They are stored, and the storage is exactly what *ḍighn* denotes.

Verse 29 states that the storage is not permanent. What is buried will be brought out — either through the removal God performs for those who ask Him (59:10: *"place not in our hearts any rancor toward those who believe"*) or through the exposure God performs for those who do not (47:29). The believer's practical option is therefore not whether to have the *aḍghān* but what to do with them while they are still inside and still reachable.

The tradition prescribed three instruments, and all three are Qur'anic. The first is prayer against the rancor itself, in the exact words of 59:10 — a supplication that asks God to remove a feeling rather than to change the person who caused it. The second is the restraint of anger and the pardoning of mankind (3:134), which the Prophet ﷺ defined in the report preserved by al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

> *"The strong man is not the good wrestler. The strong man is the one who controls himself when he is angry."* (al-Bukhārī 6114; Muslim 2609, from Abū Hurayrah)

The third is the act of reconciliation, which the Qur'an makes a communal obligation: *"There is no good in much of their private conversation, except one who commands charity or justice or reconciliation between people"* (4:114); *"The believers are but brothers, so make peace between your brethren"* (49:10).

The three together form the practice that prevents the *ḍighn* from hardening. A resentment prayed about, restrained in expression, and addressed through reconciliation does not become a buried hatred. A resentment nursed in silence becomes one, and verse 29 is spoken over it.

**The Disease Named Again**

The verse repeats the phrase *"those in whose hearts is a disease"* from verse 20, and the repetition is the second of only two occurrences in the sūrah (with v. 20), which marks verses 20 through 29 as a unit. The disease named at verse 20 was diagnosed by a visible symptom — the fainting look at the mention of fighting. The disease named at verse 29 is diagnosed by an invisible one — the buried rancor. The two symptoms are the outside and the inside of the same condition, and the sūrah's method is to show both, so that the reader who cannot see another's interior may recognize it by its exterior, and the reader who can see his own interior may recognize it there.

Ibn al-Qayyim's description of the diseased heart in *al-Dāʾ wa-l-dawāʾ* is the classical elaboration of verse 29: the heart that carries a concealed hatred toward the truth or toward its bearers is in a state of internal inflammation, and the inflammation spreads from the object of the hatred to the capacity for love generally, until the person finds himself unable to love what is good and unable to rejoice at another's good fortune. The diagnosis is available to anyone willing to make it: notice what you resent hearing, and notice whose success you cannot celebrate. Those two observations locate the *ḍighn* more accurately than any self-examination of one's beliefs.

The verse's question is the sūrah's last word on the subject of concealment before it turns, at verse 30, to the means by which the concealed becomes visible. Between the two stands the simple fact that God knows — declared at verse 26 (*God knows their keeping of secrets*) and again at verse 30 (*God knows your deeds*). The exposure is not a matter of whether the rancor will be found. It is a matter of when, and by whom, and whether the one who carries it will have done anything about it first.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:30

> **And if We willed, We would show them to you so that you would know them by their mark. And you will certainly know them by their tone of speech. And God knows your deeds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exposure That Was Withheld, and the One That Was Given**

Verse 29 asked whether the diseased hearts supposed their rancor would stay buried; verse 30 describes the two forms of exposure, one of which God chose not to exercise. *Wa-law nashāʾu la-araynākhum fa-la-ʿaraftahum bi-sīmāhum* — "and if We willed, We would show them to you so that you would know them by their mark." The conditional *law* with the emphatic *lām* in both verbs — *la-araynākum*, *la-ʿaraftahum* — states a real possibility that was not taken: God could have made the hypocrites visible to the Prophet ﷺ, individually and unmistakably, and He did not.

*Wa-la-taʿrifannahum fī laḥni al-qawl* — "and you will certainly know them by their tone of speech." Here the *lām* of oath is followed by the *nūn* of emphasis, the doubled form that produces the strongest assertion Arabic has: *you will absolutely know them.* What was withheld in the first clause is granted in the second. The mark will not be shown; the speech will be sufficient.

The verse thus describes a deliberate limitation of divine disclosure and a deliberate provision of human means. God did not hand the Prophet ﷺ a list. He gave him something better for the community's formation: a faculty of recognition that the community could learn.

**Why the List Was Not Given**

The commentators addressed the question directly, and the answers converge on several reasons.

*First*, mercy toward the hypocrites themselves. Exposure by divine designation would have ended any possibility of repentance; a man named by God as a hypocrite in the presence of the community has no road back. The sūrah's own verse 25 describes the respite God grants, and verse 1's past tense leaves repentance open. The tradition records that the Prophet ﷺ was given the names of certain hypocrites — Ḥudhayfah ibn al-Yamān was made the keeper of the list, known as *ṣāḥib al-sirr*, the keeper of the Messenger's secret — and the Prophet ﷺ did not act upon the knowledge. When ʿUmar became Caliph he would watch Ḥudhayfah at funerals: if Ḥudhayfah prayed over the deceased, ʿUmar prayed; if Ḥudhayfah withdrew, ʿUmar withdrew. The list existed and was not used, and the restraint was itself a ruling.

*Second*, the protection of the community from suspicion. Had every hypocrite been marked, the community would have lived in a state of surveillance, and the marking would have generated a class of accusers and a class of the accused. Islam's legal principles run in the opposite direction: the presumption of the sincerity of the profession, the prohibition of spying (*wa-lā tajassasū*, 49:12), the command to think well of other believers (*"O you who believe, avoid much suspicion; truly some suspicion is sin"*, 49:12), and the report:

> *"Beware of suspicion, for suspicion is the most lying of speech. Do not spy, do not compete, do not envy, do not hate, do not turn away from one another — and be, O servants of God, brothers."* (al-Bukhārī 6064; Muslim 2563, from Abū Hurayrah)

*Third*, and most significantly for the verse's argument, the formation of the believers' own perception. A community told who its hypocrites are learns nothing. A community required to recognize them by their tone of speech develops a faculty it keeps. Verse 31 states the principle immediately after: *"And We shall test you until We know those among you who strive and those who are patient."* The testing requires that the answers not be given in advance.

**Bi-Sīmāhum: The Mark on the Face**

*Sīmā* — mark, sign, distinguishing trace — is a Qur'anic term with a specific application to the face. The word is used of both the believers and the guilty, and the two usages together define it.

Of the believers:

> *"Their mark (sīmāhum) is in their faces, from the trace of prostration."* (48:29)

> *"They do not ask of people insistently; thou knowest them by their mark (bi-sīmāhum) — they do not ask people with persistence."* (2:273)

Of the guilty:

> *"The guilty shall be known by their marks (bi-sīmāhum), and they shall be seized by the forelocks and by the feet."* (55:41)

And of the people of the Heights:

> *"And upon the Heights are men who know all by their marks (bi-sīmāhum), and they call unto the people of the Garden: Peace be upon you... And they call unto the people of the Fire..."* (7:46–48)

The *sīmā* is therefore a visible trace produced by an interior state and legible to those who know how to read it. In 48:29 it is produced by prostration — the years of placing the face on the ground leave something in the face that other people can see. In 2:273 it is produced by dignity in poverty — the poor who do not beg are recognizable by the quality of their restraint. In 55:41 it is produced by guilt, and the recognition is immediate and terrifying: no questions are needed, and the seizing follows the seeing.

The verse 30 states that God could have produced such a mark on the hypocrites — a visible sign by which the Prophet ﷺ would have known each of them — and that He chose not to. The refusal is one of the Qur'an's clearest indications that God's purpose in dealing with human beings is not the efficient identification of the guilty but the formation of the community. The efficient method was available. It was declined.

**Fī Laḥni al-Qawl: The Tone of Speech**

The means actually given is the second: *laḥn al-qawl*. The word *laḥn* is from *l-ḥ-n*, and it has a range of related meanings the commentators gathered. Al-Rāghib explains *laḥana* as turning speech from its straight course — which produces, on one side, the meaning of a mistake in grammar (a *laḥn* is a solecism), and on the other, the meaning of oblique, indirect, allusive speech that conceals its real intent. The same root produces *talḥīn*, the art of arguing skillfully and of speaking in a way that carries a hidden meaning for those who catch it.

The commentators, as the source records, understand *laḥn al-qawl* either as the *tenor* with which they said things — the tone, the manner, the inflection — or as the actual *words* they chose. Both readings are supported by the historical record, and both were operative.

The clearest instance the source commentary supplies is the statement of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy at the expedition of Banū al-Muṣṭaliq, when a dispute arose between a Muhājir and an Anṣārī at the water and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy said to those around him:

> *"Do not spend on those who are with the Messenger of God until they scatter... If we return to Madinah, the mightier will surely expel the weaker therefrom."* (63:7–8)

The statement was overheard by Zayd ibn Arqam, a young man who reported it to the Prophet ﷺ. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy denied it under oath, and some of the Companions inclined to believe the denial against the boy's report. Then Sūrah al-Munāfiqūn was revealed, confirming the words exactly, and ʿUmar said: "O Messenger of God, let me strike off the neck of this hypocrite." The Prophet ﷺ said: *"Leave him; let it not be said that Muhammad kills his companions."* And ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy — the sincere Muslim son of the hypocrite father — offered to kill his own father, and the Prophet ﷺ refused and told him to be gentle with him.

The episode is a complete demonstration of *laḥn al-qawl*. The words were ordinary; the tenor was not. What was detectable in the statement was not its content but its direction — the assumption that the Prophet ﷺ and his Companions were a faction among factions, that they could be expelled, that their presence was a matter of political convenience. A person who believed the revelation could not have framed the sentence that way, and the framing revealed what the profession concealed.

**What the Tone Reveals**

The verse's claim is that the interior is audible. This is not a mystical assertion but an observation about speech that any experienced listener recognizes. People reveal themselves in the way they say things: the pause before an affirmation, the qualifier that limits a commitment, the joke that tests a proposition and withdraws it if challenged, the change of register when a subject becomes costly, the enthusiasm for a cause that requires nothing and the flatness toward one that requires something.

The sūrah has already described the phenomenon at verse 20 — the men who asked for a sūrah and then looked at the Prophet ﷺ with the eyes of dying men. The face is a form of *laḥn*. Verse 16 described another — the men who listened and then asked what had been said, and the asking had a tone. Verse 26 described a third — the words spoken to those averse to revelation, which were not overheard by the believers but were heard by God.

The Islamic tradition developed the observation into a discipline. The jurists made the *laḥn* of a witness's speech relevant to the assessment of his testimony; the scholars of *rijāl* assessed transmitters partly by the manner of their speech; the spiritual writers made attention to one's own tone a part of self-examination. Ibn al-Qayyim wrote in *al-Fawāʾid* that the tongue is the interpreter of the heart and that a person who wishes to know what is in his heart should listen to what his tongue says when he is not managing it. The observation runs in both directions: you will know them by their tone, and you may know yourself by yours.

The Prophet ﷺ stated the general principle in the report preserved by Muslim:

> *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him say good or be silent."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47, from Abū Hurayrah)

The alternative to good speech in this formulation is not bad speech but silence, and the reason is that unmanaged speech discloses. The hypocrite's problem at verse 30 is not that he said a forbidden thing; it is that he could not keep his interior out of his words.

**Wa-llāhu Yaʿlamu Aʿmālakum: The Turn to the Reader**

The verse closes with a declaration that shifts the address: *"And God knows your deeds"* — *wa-llāhu yaʿlamu aʿmālakum*. The pronoun is plural and it is addressed to the believers, not to the hypocrites. Having described the exposure of the diseased hearts, the verse turns the same knowledge upon the audience.

The turn is one of the Qur'an's characteristic moves and it prevents the verse from becoming an instrument of judgment against others. God knows their secrets (v. 26), He will bring out their rancor (v. 29), He will show them by their tone (v. 30) — *and He knows your deeds.* The believer who has been following the exposure of the hypocrites arrives at the last clause and finds himself under the same declaration.

This is the third of the sūrah's four declarations of divine knowledge (vv. 19, 26, 30, 31), and its placement here is deliberate. The exposure of hypocrisy is not a spectator sport. The same God who knows the *laḥn* of the hypocrite knows the *laḥn* of the believer who is listening, and the deeds referred to include the deed of listening to a description of hypocrisy and applying it only to other people.

The practical result is that verse 30 gives the believer two instructions at once. Learn to recognize hypocrisy by its tone — in others, since the community requires the faculty and verse 30 promises it will be given. And listen to your own tone — since the same faculty, turned inward, is the most reliable self-examination available. The person who can detect the qualifier in another's commitment can detect it in his own; the person who hears the flinch in another's enthusiasm can hear it in his.

Verse 31 then supplies the context in which both recognitions will be tested: *"And We shall test you until We know those among you who strive and those who are patient, and We shall test your proclamations."* The tone of speech is tested at the moment of demand, and the moment is coming. The verse has told the reader how to hear it — in others, and, if he is honest, in himself.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:31

> **And We shall test you until We know those among you who strive and those who are patient, and We shall test your proclamations.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Oath of Testing**

Verse 31 is the sūrah's statement of the principle that has been operating silently throughout: nothing has been described here without being tested. *Wa-lanabluwannakum* — "and We shall surely test you." The Arabic form is the strongest assertion the language provides: the *lām* of oath followed by the doubled *nūn* of emphasis attached to the verb, and the whole preceded by the conjunctive *wa*. There is no ambiguity, no condition, and no exception. Testing will occur, and it will occur to the addressees — the believing community.

*Balā*, from *b-l-w*, means to test, to try, to put through an ordeal — the root also produces *balāʾ*, a tribulation, and *mubtalā*, one being tested. The Qur'an uses it of both the good and the bad as instruments of trial: *"And We shall test you with evil and with good as a trial, and unto Us you will be returned"* (21:35). The test is not identified with hardship; hardship is one of its materials, and ease is another.

The verse's nearest parallel in the Qur'an is at 3:142, and the parallelism is so close that the commentators read the two together:

> *"Or did you suppose that you would enter the Garden while God had not yet known those among you who strive, and known the patient?"* (3:142)

The verse was revealed after Uḥud, when the Muslims had been defeated and the question inside the community was why. The answer given is that entry into the Garden is preceded by the display of striving and patience, and that the display is the purpose of the difficulty. And at 29:2–3:

> *"Do people suppose that they will be left to say, 'We believe,' and that they will not be tested? And We tested those before them, so God will surely know those who are truthful, and He will surely know the liars."* (29:2–3)

The construction in Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt is nearly identical to verse 31's, including the emphatic particles. The principle is established across the Qur'an: the profession of belief is not accepted without verification, and the verification is the test.

**Ḥattā Naʿlama: The Knowledge That Is Made Manifest**

The clause *"until We know those among you who strive and those who are patient"* — *ḥattā naʿlama al-mujāhidīna minkum wa-l-ṣābirīn* — raises the classical theological question the source commentary notes: how can God come to know what He already knows?

The answer given unanimously by the commentators — Ibn al-Jawzī, al-Rāzī, al-Ṭabarī, and before them the whole tradition — is that *"until We know"* here means *"in order to make manifest"* in existence. God already knows the true nature of every soul from eternity; the test does not inform Him. The test brings into the realm of actual, witnessed, historical fact what was known in the divine knowledge as future, so that it exists as a deed performed, a choice made, a patience demonstrated. The knowledge that is realized by the test is not God's information but the servant's actuality.

The distinction is not a dodge; it is a real one, and the Qur'an uses both forms of the same verb in a way that clarifies it. At 2:143:

> *"And We appointed the direction thou wast facing only that We might know (li-naʿlama) him who follows the Messenger from him who turns back upon his heels."* (2:143)

The commentators explain that the knowing at issue is the knowing of the thing as it occurs — *ʿilm al-shayʾ kāʾinan* — as distinct from the knowing of it as it will be. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said of such verses: *"until We know"* means *"until We see"* — that is, until the matter is displayed. And al-Rāzī's formulation is that the divine knowledge encompasses the thing before its existence, at its existence, and after it, and that the verse refers to the middle: the knowledge of the thing as actualized, which requires the actualization.

Ibn Taymiyyah's treatment of the question, developed across his writings on divine attributes, insists on both facts without dissolving either: God's knowledge is complete and eternal and is not increased by anything that happens; and the human act is real, chosen, and consequential, and the test genuinely produces something that was not there before — namely, the act itself. The purpose of the test is not epistemic but ontological: courage that has never been exercised does not exist as an actuality, and a world in which no test occurs is a world in which the moral qualities do not exist either.

This is the same principle verse 4 stated about the fighting: *"And if God willed, He would take vengeance upon them, but that He may test some of you by means of others."* Verse 31 generalizes it: the testing is not confined to the battlefield; it extends to the whole life of the community, and its objects are named.

**Al-Mujāhidīn Wa-l-Ṣābirīn: The Two Qualities Tested**

The two qualities the test will display are striving and patience — *al-mujāhidīn* and *al-ṣābirīn*. The pairing is not arbitrary, and it appears together elsewhere in the Qur'an: 3:142; 9:16; 2:153 (*"O you who believe, seek help through patience and prayer"*); and in the description of the highest rank of believers at 49:15:

> *"The believers are only those who believe in God and His Messenger, then doubt not, and strive with their wealth and their selves in the way of God. Those are the truthful ones."* (49:15)

*Mujāhid* is the active participle of *jāhada*, the third form of *j-h-d*, to strive, to exert oneself, to struggle. The third form carries the sense of mutual or intense exertion, and the noun *jihād* covers every form of maximal effort in God's cause. The source commentary notes correctly that *"those among you who strive"* refers not only to those who fight, but also to those who strive in the way of God with their property and themselves (49:15; cf. 2:218; 4:95; 5:54; 8:74; 9:20, 41, 44, 81). The Qur'an's ranking of the two forms is explicit at 4:95:

> *"Not equal are those of the believers who sit at home, without injury, and those who strive in the way of God with their wealth and their selves. God has preferred in degree those who strive with their wealth and their selves over those who sit at home; and to each God has promised the good, but God has preferred the strivers over the sitters with a great reward."* (4:95)

*Ṣābir* is the active participle of *ṣ-b-r*, patience — the root's literal sense is to hold, to restrain, to bind. A *ṣābir* is one who holds himself in place when the impulse is to flee, who restrains his tongue when the impulse is to complain, who continues the act when the impulse is to abandon it. The Qur'an's estimation of the virtue is unmatched: *"God is with the patient"* (2:153; 8:46, 66); *"Be patient; truly God does not let the reward of the good-doers be lost"* (11:115); *"And be patient, and thy patience is only by God"* (16:127); *"Truly the patient will be given their reward without reckoning"* (39:10).

The last of these — *bi-ghayri ḥisāb*, without reckoning — means without limit, without calculation, beyond the scale that applies to every other deed. And the reason the two qualities are paired in verse 31 is that they are the two halves of the religious life: striving is the expenditure of effort toward a good, and patience is the maintenance of effort against a difficulty. Every act of obedience requires both, and the test displays which of the two a person lacks.

The Prophet ﷺ described the range of the test:

> *"The most severely tested of people are the prophets, then the next best, then the next best. A man is tested according to his religion: if his religion is firm, his test is severe; and if there is weakness in his religion, he is tested according to his religion. Trials do not cease from the servant until they leave him walking upon the earth with no sin upon him."* (al-Tirmidhī 2398; Aḥmad, from Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ; graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**Wa-Nabluwa Akhbārakum: The Testing of the Proclamations**

The final clause is the verse's sharpest: *"and We shall test your proclamations"* — *wa-nabluwa akhbārakum*. *Akhbār* is the plural of *khabar*, a report, a piece of news, a statement about a state of affairs. In this context the commentators understand it as the claims people make about themselves — their professions of belief, their declared willingness, their statements of readiness.

The sūrah has already supplied the case study. Verse 20 described those who said *"would that a sūrah were sent down"* — a proclamation — and then fainted when it was sent. Verse 21 named the alternative: *"were they true to God, it would be better for them."* Verse 31 now states the general rule: the proclamations will be tested, and the testing is what distinguishes them.

The verse is thus the sūrah's answer to the problem of religious speech. In any community, talk is cheap and everyone talks. The person who says he would do anything, who declares his readiness, who professes his love for the cause, who announces his willingness to sacrifice — cannot be distinguished from the person who means it, by the speech alone. The distinction requires a test, and the test is what verse 31 promises.

The Qur'an's own description of the false proclamation is at 9:75–77, the man who covenanted with God to give charity if given bounty, and then was miserly when the bounty arrived — and the verse's conclusion, quoted at verse 21's commentary, is that God *"requited them with hypocrisy in their hearts, unto the day they meet Him, because they broke their promise to God and because they used to lie."* The proclamation tested and failed became a permanent condition.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the practical instruction for the interval between proclamation and test:

> *"Do not ask to meet the enemy, and ask God for well-being. But if you meet them, be patient, and know that Paradise is beneath the shadow of the swords."* (al-Bukhārī 2965; Muslim 1742, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Awfā)

The instruction not to seek the encounter is a discipline against the proclamation: the person who asks for the test is often the one who will fail it, and the believer's proper posture is to ask for well-being and to be patient if the test comes. The one who says "would that a sūrah were sent down" has asked for the encounter. Verse 31 warns him that he will get it.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Purpose Statement**

Verse 31 stands at the head of the sūrah's final movement (vv. 31–35) and retrospectively explains everything that has come before. Why does the sūrah spend fifteen verses describing the hypocrites' interior? Because the interior must be displayed. Why does it legislate the battlefield, with all its severity? Because striving is one of the two qualities being tested. Why does it describe the four rivers and the boiling liquid? Because patience is sustained by the vision of the destination. Why does it declare God's knowledge four times? Because the test's outcome is known and will nonetheless be actualized. Why does it end with the command to spend? Because wealth is the material of the last and most universal test.

The verse also resolves the sūrah's apparent harshness toward the hypocrites. The exposure of verses 25 through 30 is not an exercise in condemnation; it is the necessary condition of a test in which the tested are also the observers. The community needed to know that its own members could be diseased, that the disease was detectable, and that detection was a duty — because the community was being tested too, and one of the things it would be tested on was whether it could recognize what was happening inside itself.

For the individual reader, the verse is a statement about his own life that he can verify. He has made proclamations — in prayer, in moments of emotion, in conversation, in the private commitments nobody knows about. Verse 31 tells him that those proclamations are on a schedule. The test is coming, and it will come in the form of an ordinary demand at an inconvenient moment: the money that has to be given, the truth that has to be said, the obligation that has to be kept, the injury that has to be borne, the pleasure that has to be refused. The proclamation will be tested then, and not by God's discovery of the answer but by the person's own performance of it — and everyone else's observation.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:32

> **Truly those who disbelieve and turn from the way of God, and oppose the Messenger of God after guidance has been made clear to them will not harm God in the least. And He will make their deeds come to naught.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Opening Words Return**

Verse 32 begins with the same words that opened the sūrah: *inna alladhīna kafarū wa-ṣaddū ʿan sabīli Allāh* — "truly those who disbelieve and turn from the way of God." The exact phrase of verse 1 is repeated, and to it the verse adds a third element: *wa-shāqqū al-rasūla min baʿdi mā tabayyana lahumu al-hudā* — "and oppose the Messenger after guidance has been made clear to them."

The repetition is the sūrah's own structural device, and it performs the closing of an argument. Verse 1 named two faults and one consequence. Verse 32 names three faults and two consequences — and between them lie thirty verses of evidence. The reader who has followed the sūrah arrives at verse 32 having seen the faults described in operation: the aversion (v. 9), the conspiracy (v. 26), the buried rancor (v. 29), the tone of speech (v. 30). The restatement at verse 32 is therefore not a summary but a verdict delivered after the presentation of the case.

**Shāqqū al-Rasūl: Being on the Opposite Side**

*Shāqqa* is the third form of *sh-q-q*, to split, to cleave, to divide. The third form carries a sense of mutual opposition: *shāqqa* means to be in the opposite *shiqq* — the other half, the other side of the split. The one who *shāqqa* the Messenger has taken up a position on the far side of a division from him, and the Qur'an uses the verb in exactly this sense:

> *"That is because they opposed God and His Messenger; and whoever opposes God — truly God is severe in punishment."* (8:13)

> *"And whoever opposes God and His Messenger — truly God is severe in punishment."* (59:4)

> *"Do they not know that whoever opposes God and His Messenger — for him is the Fire of Hell, abiding therein?"* (9:63)

The image is spatial rather than merely verbal. Opposition to the Messenger is not disagreement about a proposition; it is taking a position, occupying ground, standing on the other side of a line. And the Qur'an's treatment of it is consistently severe because the position is chosen after the line has been made visible — which is what the clause *"after guidance has been made clear to them"* specifies.

*Min baʿdi mā tabayyana lahumu al-hudā* — the same phrase that appeared at verse 25 of those who turned back on their heels. *Tabayyana* is the fifth form of *b-y-n*: the guidance did not merely arrive, it became clear, evident, unmistakable. Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī both note that the qualification aggravates the offense: the person who opposes after clarity is not the person who opposes in confusion. The Qur'an states the same aggravation at 4:115:

> *"And whoever opposes the Messenger after guidance has been made clear to him, and follows other than the way of the believers, We shall turn him unto what he has turned unto, and We shall roast him in Hell — and wretched is the destination."* (4:115)

The historical referents include, as the commentators note, those who knew the Prophet ﷺ before the revelation — the Quraysh who called him *al-Ṣādiq al-Amīn* for forty years, who trusted him with their deposits, who referred their disputes to him — and who opposed him after. The Qur'an addresses this case directly:

> *"Truly We know that what they say grieves thee. For they do not accuse thee of lying, but the wrongdoers deny the signs of God."* (6:33)

The denial is not about the messenger's honesty, which they had verified over decades. It is about the message, and the opposition is therefore a matter of will rather than of evidence. This is the *tabayyun* of verse 32: the guidance had become as clear as forty years of personal experience could make it.

**Lan Yaḍurrū Allāha Shayʾan: They Will Not Harm God at All**

The first consequence is a negation, and it is absolute: *"they will not harm God in the least"* — *lan yaḍurrū Allāha shayʾan*. The *lan* denies the future absolutely and the *shayʾan* is the accusative of generalization: not any thing, not in any amount, not at all.

The Qur'an repeats this declaration in a series of places, and the repetition establishes it as a fixed doctrine:

> *"And let not those who disbelieve think that they have outstripped. Truly they cannot escape."* (8:59)

> *"O Messenger, let not those who race into disbelief grieve thee... they will not harm God in the least."* (3:176)

> *"And whoever disbelieves — his disbelief is not upon him but upon himself."* — and: *"Whoever does righteousness, they do so for their own souls; and whoever does evil, they do so against them. And thy Lord is not unjust to the servants."* (41:46)

> *"If you disbelieve — truly God is Independent of you. And He is not pleased with disbelief for His servants."* (39:7)

> *"O mankind, you are the poor, in need of God; and God is the Rich, the Praiseworthy. If He wills, He will take you away and bring a new creation, and that is not difficult for God."* (35:15–17)

The last of these is the theological ground of the whole declaration, and Sūrah Muḥammad returns to it at its final verse: *"God is the Rich, and you are the poor"* (47:38).

The declaration answers a question the disbelievers' behavior implicitly raises: does their opposition matter to God? The Qur'an's answer is no, and the "no" is not a dismissal of the opposition's seriousness but a statement about God's sufficiency. Their opposition matters enormously — to them, to their victims, to the community they attack, to history. It does not diminish God, whose perfection is not affected by anything creatures do.

There is a pastoral consequence to the verse that the earliest community drew immediately. The Muslims of Madinah were being opposed by a coalition that included the Quraysh, several Jewish tribes, the hypocrites inside their own city, and the bedouin tribes who joined whichever side appeared to be winning. The opposition caused real damage: lives, property, security, morale. Verse 32 tells them what the opposition does not do: it does not harm God. And the fact that it does not harm God means that the opposition cannot succeed in the dimension that ultimately matters — the establishment of the truth. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the future tense in the second consequence conveys a promise: they will not prevail against him.

**Wa-Sayuḥbiṭu Aʿmālahum: The Promise of Nullification**

The second consequence is in the future tense, and the tense changes its force: *"and He will make their deeds come to naught"* — *wa-sayuḥbiṭu aʿmālahum*. At verses 9 and 28 the nullification was stated in the past (*aḥbaṭa*); here the *sīn* of futurity marks it as a promise. Al-Rāzī notes that the deeds nullified here are understood as acts done in defiance of the Prophet ﷺ, and that the future tense conveys the assurance that their opposition will not achieve its purpose.

The difference between the past and the future statements is worth marking. The past tense at verses 9 and 28 describes an accomplished fact about the deeds of the averse: they have already come to naught, in the sense that they were never going to arrive anywhere. The future tense at verse 32 describes the demonstration of that fact: their campaigns, their conspiracies, their alliances, their expenditure of energy against the Messenger — all of it will be shown to have been void, and the showing is still to come.

The historical demonstration was complete within the sūrah's own decade. The coalition that opposed the Prophet ﷺ at Uḥud and at the Trench was, ten years later, gone. Makkah had surrendered without a battle. The tribes that had fought him were sending delegations to accept Islam — the Year of Delegations, 9/630–631. Abū Sufyān, who had led the opposition, was given a position of honor in the new order. Khālid ibn al-Walīd, who had broken the Muslim line at Uḥud, became one of Islam's commanders. The deeds of the opposition had come to naught in the most literal sense: nothing they built remained standing, and much of what they built was rebuilt in the opposite direction.

Al-Qurṭubī's principle applies here as at verse 4: the nullification is not the destruction of the persons but of the works, and the works of opposition to a truth that God has willed to establish cannot succeed, because they are opposed to the direction of reality. The image at verse 9's etymology — the animal that grazes on the poisonous plant, swells, and dies — fits the political campaigns of the opposition precisely. They looked formidable. They were fatal to those who undertook them.

**The Verse's Place in the Final Movement**

Verses 31 through 35 form the sūrah's closing exhortation, and verse 32 functions within it as the removal of an obstacle. Verse 31 announces the test; a reader may ask why the test should be borne, given the strength of the opposition. Verse 32 answers: the opposition cannot harm God and its works will be void. Verse 33 then gives the command that follows from the answer: obey God and obey the Messenger and let not your deeds be in vain. Verse 34 closes the door on the alternative: those who die in that opposition will not be forgiven. Verse 35 gives the practical conclusion: do not falter.

The sequence is a complete moral argument. You are being tested (31). What you are being tested against cannot win (32). Therefore obey, and guard your own deeds (33). The opposition's door closes at death (34). Therefore do not show weakness while you have the upper hand (35). Every verse is a premise for the next, and the conclusion is the sūrah's final three verses on spending — the most universal and least dramatic form the striving takes.

For the reader in any age who is opposing something true, the verse states his position with an accuracy that is itself an invitation. His opposition will not harm God. It will harm him. His deeds will be nullified — not as a punishment inflicted from outside but as the natural fate of works directed against the grain of reality. And the alternative is available until the moment named at verse 34: the door closes at death, and not before.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:33

> **O you who believe! Obey God and obey the Messenger and let not your deeds be in vain.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Verb of Nullification**

Verse 33 is addressed to the believers, and it contains the sūrah's third term for the ruin of deeds. The chapter has used *aḍalla* — to cause to stray, to lose (vv. 1, 8) — and *aḥbaṭa* — to spoil, to swell and perish (vv. 9, 28, 32). Here it uses *abṭala*: *wa-lā tubṭilū aʿmālakum* — "and let not your deeds be in vain." The verb is the fourth form of *b-ṭ-l*, the root of *al-bāṭil*, falsehood and voidness, discussed at verse 3. To *abṭala* a deed is to render it *bāṭil* — void, without effect, as though it had not been.

The shift in addressee is the crucial feature. Verses 1, 8, 9, 28, and 32 all describe God nullifying the deeds of the disbelievers. Verse 33 addresses the *believers* and tells them not to nullify their own. The agent of the voiding changes: in the earlier verses it is God, acting upon deeds that were already void by their direction; here it is the believers themselves, acting upon deeds that were sound. The command is therefore a warning that the ruin of a deed is not only a punishment for disbelief but a possibility for faith — and that the possibility is in the believer's own hands.

**Aṭīʿū Allāha Wa-Aṭīʿū al-Rasūl**

*"Obey God and obey the Messenger"* — the command is repeated in the Qur'an so often that the source commentary calls it an underlying theme of the whole Book: 3:32; 4:59; 5:92; 24:54; 64:12; and the related 3:132; 4:80; 7:156–57; 8:1, 20, 46; 33:33; 49:14; 58:13.

The repetition of the verb — *aṭīʿū* twice — has been examined by the commentators, and the observation is standard: obedience to God and obedience to the Messenger are named separately because they are two objects of one duty, not two duties. The Qur'an states the relation explicitly at 4:80:

> *"Whoever obeys the Messenger has obeyed God; and whoever turns away — We have not sent thee as a watcher over them."* (4:80)

And the necessity of the Messenger's role in determining the content of the obedience is stated at 16:44:

> *"And We sent down unto thee the Reminder, that thou mayest make clear unto mankind what has been sent down unto them, and that haply they may reflect."* (16:44)

The Sunnah is thus not an addition to the Qur'an but its specification, and the command to obey the Messenger is the command to accept the specification. This is why the Qur'an treats the refusal of the Messenger's judgment as a matter of faith:

> *"But no, by thy Lord, they do not believe until they make thee judge in what disputes among themselves, and then find within themselves no discomfort over what thou hast judged, and submit in full submission."* (4:65)

The three conditions in this verse — referring the dispute to him, finding no inner resistance to the ruling, and submitting fully — describe the obedience verse 33 commands, and they identify the internal obstacle to it: the *ḥaraj*, the discomfort, the inward reservation. The believer who obeys while resenting the command has obeyed in form and not in the sense verse 33 requires, and the resentment is precisely the *karāhah* the sūrah diagnosed at verses 9 and 28.

**Wa-Lā Tubṭilū Aʿmālakum: The Two Readings**

The final clause has been read by the commentators along two principal lines, both recorded in the source, and both are supported by the Qur'an elsewhere.

*The first reading*: do not undo your good deeds through disobedience. On this reading the voiding is caused by subsequent sin — the good deed performed and then cancelled by an act that removes its effect. The Qur'an's clearest instance is at 2:264:

> *"O you who believe! Do not render void your charities (lā tubṭilū ṣadaqātikum) with reproach and injury, like one who spends his wealth to be seen by mankind and does not believe in God and the Last Day."* (2:264)

The charity given and then followed by a reminder of the favor, or by an injury to the recipient, is void — the same verb, *abṭala*, and the voiding is caused by the giver's own subsequent conduct. The Qur'an states the same principle at 49:2:

> *"O you who believe! Do not raise your voices above the voice of the Prophet, and do not speak loudly to him as you speak loudly to one another, lest your deeds be void (an taḥbaṭa aʿmālukum) while you do not perceive."* (49:2)

Here the voiding is caused by a manner of speaking — raising the voice in the Prophet's presence — and the verse adds the warning that the voiding happens *while you do not perceive*. This is the most frightening feature of the doctrine: the deed can be voided without the person noticing. He continues to feel he has done it; the record says otherwise.

*The second reading*: the clause is an injunction to purify one's intentions. On this reading the voiding is caused not by later sin but by the corruption of the act at its origin — performing it for other than God. The Qur'an's statement of the principle is at 39:65:

> *"And it was revealed unto thee and unto those before thee: If thou associate partners with God, thy deed shall surely come to naught (la-yaḥbaṭanna ʿamaluka), and thou shalt surely be among the losers."* (39:65)

The two readings are not alternatives but complements, since a deed can be voided at its beginning by a corrupt intention or at its end by a corrupt act, and the verse's prohibition covers both.

**Al-Tustarī on the Void of Self-Attribution**

The source commentary records the reading of Sahl al-Tustarī (d. 283/896), which pushes the second interpretation to its furthest point:

> *"Let not your deeds be in vain, that is, by seeing them as coming from yourselves and by seeking recompense from your Lord, for sincere action is that which seeks no recompense."*

Al-Tustarī identifies two voiders. The first is *ruʾyat al-ʿamal* — seeing the deed as one's own, the attribution of the act to the self rather than to the enablement of God. The second is *ṭalab al-ʿiwaḍ* — seeking compensation, doing the deed in order to be paid. The second sounds paradoxical, since the Qur'an promises reward constantly; but al-Tustarī's point is the one the whole spiritual tradition made: the reward promised is for the one who acts for God, and the person who acts *for the reward* has made the reward his object and God his means, which inverts the relation.

The tradition's own statement of the same principle is the most famous ḥadīth in Islam:

> *"Deeds are only by intentions, and every person has only what he intended. Whoever's emigration was to God and His Messenger, his emigration was to God and His Messenger; and whoever's emigration was for a world he would attain or a woman he would marry, his emigration was to what he emigrated for."* (al-Bukhārī 1; Muslim 1907, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb)

The report's occasion gives it weight: the phrase about the woman refers to a man known as Muhājir Umm Qays — the Emigrant for Umm Qays — who emigrated to Madinah to marry a woman who had made her emigration a condition, and the Companions used the name for him. His deed was formally identical to the deeds of every other emigrant. Its value was different because its object was different.

And the ḥadīth qudsī recorded by Muslim states the divine response to the mixed deed:

> *"I am the most independent of partners from partnership. Whoever does a deed in which he associates with Me other than Me, I leave him and his association."* (Muslim 2985, from Abū Hurayrah)

**Riyāʾ: The Lesser Shirk**

The voiding of deeds by intention acquired a technical name in the tradition: *riyāʾ*, ostentation, from *raʾā*, to see — doing a deed to be seen. The Prophet ﷺ named it the danger he most feared for his community:

> *"What I fear most for you is the lesser shirk."* They said, "What is the lesser shirk, O Messenger of God?" He said: *"Ostentation (al-riyāʾ). God will say on the Day of Resurrection, when people are recompensed for their deeds: Go to those for whom you used to show off in the world, and see whether you find any recompense with them."* (Aḥmad, *al-Musnad* 24043; al-Ṭabarānī, from Maḥmūd ibn Labīd; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī)

The report describes the exact mechanism of verse 33's voiding: the deed was performed for an audience, the audience is directed to provide the payment, and the audience has nothing. The deed is not merely unrewarded; it has been misdirected, and the misdirection is the voiding.

Muslim also records the account of the three brought to judgment first — the one who died fighting, the one who acquired knowledge and taught it and recited the Qur'an, and the one to whom God gave wealth and who spent it — each of whom is told that he did it to be described as brave, learned, and generous, and that the description was granted in the world, and each of whom is then dragged into the Fire. The three are the highest forms of religious achievement, and the report's function is precisely verse 33's: do not let your deeds be void.

**How the Deed Is Protected**

The verse's prohibition implies a practice, and the tradition supplied it. The Companions' method of guarding the intention is reported in several forms. Ibn Abī Mulaykah's statement, recorded by al-Bukhārī, has been quoted already at verse 23: *"I met thirty of the Companions of the Prophet, every one of whom feared hypocrisy for himself."* The fear was not morbidity; it was the monitoring verse 33 requires.

Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī (d. 131/748) is reported to have said: *"Purifying the intention is harder for the workers than all their work."* And al-Fuḍayl ibn ʿIyāḍ's formulation, already cited at verse 1, defined the two conditions of acceptance as sincerity and correctness.

The practical discipline the tradition developed has three parts. Before the act: examine why you are doing it, and if a mixed motive is found, do the act anyway and ask God to accept it. During the act: keep the attention on the act rather than on the observers. After the act: do not recount it, do not seek its acknowledgment, and ask forgiveness for whatever entered into it — the practice of *istighfār* after the prayer, which the tradition established immediately after the salutation, is the institutional form of this.

The Prophet ﷺ's own supplication for the intention is preserved:

> *"O God, I seek refuge in You from associating anything with You knowingly, and I ask Your forgiveness for what I do not know."* (Aḥmad, *al-Musnad* 19606; al-Bukhārī, *al-Adab al-Mufrad*, from Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī; graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī)

The two clauses of the supplication cover the two possibilities: the deliberate mixture, from which one seeks refuge, and the unnoticed mixture, for which one seeks forgiveness. Verse 33's *"while you do not perceive"* at 49:2 makes the second clause necessary, and the supplication is the practice that answers the verse.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:34

> **Truly those who disbelieve and turn from the way of God, then die while they are disbelievers, God will not forgive them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Closing of the Door**

Verse 34 is the sūrah's third restatement of its opening sentence, and it adds the one element that determines everything: death. *Inna alladhīna kafarū wa-ṣaddū ʿan sabīli Allāhi thumma mātū wa-hum kuffārun fa-lan yaghfira Allāhu lahum* — "truly those who disbelieve and turn from the way of God, then die while they are disbelievers, God will not forgive them."

Verse 1 named the same two faults — *kafarū wa-ṣaddū ʿan sabīli Allāh* — and stated the consequence in the past tense, which the commentators noted left repentance open. Verse 32 added the third fault — opposing the Messenger after clarity — and stated the consequence as a promise. Verse 34 adds the temporal limit: *thumma mātū wa-hum kuffārun*, "then die while they are disbelievers." The particle *thumma* indicates sequence with a delay, and the delay is the whole of a life. During the delay, the door is open. At the end of the delay, it closes.

The verb *fa-lan yaghfira Allāhu lahum* uses *lan*, the particle of absolute future negation: God will *never* forgive them. The negation is unqualified and it is the strongest in the Qur'an's grammatical inventory. There is no exception clause, no qualification by category, and no opening for the interpretation that the forgiveness is merely deferred.

**Why Death Closes the Door**

The doctrine requires explanation, because the modern reader's first objection is usually that an infinite God ought to forgive infinitely, and that a finite life ought not to determine an infinite outcome. The Islamic answer is not that God's mercy is limited but that the meaning of the act is exhausted.

Forgiveness is a relation between God and a person, and the relation requires that the person be in a state capable of receiving it. Repentance (*tawbah*) is that state: the turning, the recognition, the resolution not to return. The Qur'an describes repentance as a real movement and requires it as a condition: *"Except those who repent and mend and hold fast to God and are sincere in their religion toward God — those are with the believers"* (4:146); *"And turn unto your Lord, and submit unto Him, before the punishment comes upon you; then you will not be helped"* (39:54).

Death ends the possibility of the movement, because it ends the possibility of choice. The person who dies has completed his direction; he has arrived at his orientation and cannot change it, since the changing requires time, body, and the capacity to act — all of which the death has removed. The Qur'an's own description of the state after death is of fixation: *"Until, when death comes to one of them, he says: My Lord, return me, that I may do righteousness in what I left behind. No indeed! It is a word he speaks. And behind them is a barrier (barzakh) until the day they are resurrected"* (23:99–100). The request to return is itself evidence that the returner knows what he would do — and the refusal is evidence that the knowledge arrives too late to be a choice.

Verse 18 of this sūrah already stated the principle from the side of the Hour: *"Then when it has come upon them, what will their remembrance do for them?"* Verse 34 states it from the side of the individual death, which is the Hour that arrives for every person and usually without warning.

**The Two Verses That Define the Limit**

The Qur'an states the limit twice, in the two verses the Islamic tradition treats as the definitive statements on the subject:

> *"Truly God forgives not that any partner be ascribed unto Him, but He forgives what is less than that for whomsoever He wills. And whoever ascribes partners unto God has indeed gone far astray."* (4:48)

> *"Truly God forgives not that any partner be ascribed unto Him, but He forgives what is less than that for whomsoever He wills. And whoever ascribes partners unto God has forged a mighty sin."* (4:116)

The two verses are identical in their first clause and are addressed to two different situations — the second is understood in the tradition as concerning the one who dies in *shirk*, the first as concerning the one who lives in it. The doctrine the Sunni tradition drew from them, and that verse 34 confirms, is that the only unforgiven sin is the one a person dies in, and that everything short of it is within the divine will to forgive.

And the door's openness before death is stated in the verse the tradition places against all despair:

> *"Say: O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of the mercy of God. Truly God forgives all sins. Truly He is the Forgiving, the Merciful."* (39:53)

*"All sins"* — *al-dhunūba jamīʿā*, with the article and the adverb of totality. The verse addresses the person who has transgressed against himself, and the Islamic tradition has consistently read it as applying to every sin including *shirk*, provided the repentance occurs before death. Al-Bukhārī records the report of the man who killed ninety-nine people and then asked whether repentance was possible for him, and was told:

> *"Who stands between him and repentance?"* (al-Bukhārī 3470; Muslim 2766)

The report is the tradition's standing answer to every calculation about the size of a sin. The size is irrelevant; the timing is everything.

**Khālid ibn al-Walīd: The Verse's Own Counter-Example**

The source commentary supplies the illustration that makes the doctrine concrete, and it is the strongest possible one. Khālid ibn al-Walīd (d. 19/642) was, before his conversion, the most damaging opponent the Muslims faced. He commanded the cavalry that turned the Muslim flank at Uḥud and inflicted the defeat in which seventy Companions were killed, including Ḥamzah, the Prophet's ﷺ uncle. He led the Qurayshī cavalry at the Trench. He commanded against the Muslims at Muʾtah. Every one of the sūrah's descriptions applied to him in those years: he disbelieved, he turned people from God's way, he opposed the Messenger after guidance had been made clear to him — he had known the Prophet ﷺ personally since childhood, they were both Quraysh, and the guidance could not have been clearer.

Then, in 7/628 — after Ḥudaybiyah, alongside ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ and ʿUthmān ibn Ṭalḥah — he rode to Madinah and embraced Islam. Verse 34 did not apply to him, because he did not die while he was a disbeliever. Verse 2 applied to him instead: *"He has absolved them of their evil deeds and set their state aright."* And verse 1's promise — that God made the deeds of the disbelievers go astray — was gathered back, in his case, and redirected.

He became the commander who broke the Byzantine and Sāsānian armies, the general whom the tradition regards as undefeated in more than fifty engagements, the one the Prophet ﷺ called *"a sword among the swords of God"* (al-Tirmidhī 3846; Aḥmad). When ʿUmar dismissed him from command — for reasons of protecting the community's reliance on God rather than on his genius — Khālid accepted the dismissal and continued to fight as a common soldier. He died in his bed, and the report of his dying words is preserved:

> *"There is no part of my body in which there is not a wound from a sword, a spear, or an arrow — and here I am, dying upon my bed like an old camel. So let the eyes of the coward not sleep."* (Ibn ʿAsākir, *Tārīkh Dimashq*; al-Bayhaqī)

The verse 34 that could have been spoken over him in the third year of the Hijrah was not spoken over him in the nineteenth. The difference was one decision, taken at one moment, before one death. The sūrah records the case — the source commentary explicitly mentions Khālid at verse 34 — so that no reader of the verse concludes that it describes anyone's fixed fate.

**The Verse's Function After Verse Thirty-Three**

Verse 33 commanded the believers to obey and not to void their deeds. Verse 34 states what happens to those who refuse. The sequence is characteristic of the Qur'an's method: the command is followed by the description of the alternative, and the alternative is described in its final form so that the command can be weighed against it.

The verse also completes the sūrah's treatment of the disbelievers. It has described their lost deeds (v. 1), their wretchedness (v. 8), their nullified works (v. 9), the destruction of their predecessors (vv. 10, 13), their lack of a master (v. 11), their cattle-like consumption (v. 12), their beautified evil and followed caprices (v. 14), their boiling drink (v. 15), their sealed hearts (v. 16), the uselessness of their late remembrance (v. 18), their curse and deafness and blindness (v. 23), their locked hearts (v. 24), their seduction and respite (v. 25), their conspiracy (v. 26), the angels striking them (v. 27), the voiding of their works (v. 28), their exposed rancor (v. 29), and their inability to harm God (v. 32). Verse 34 adds the one thing that makes all of it final: the death in that state.

For the reader, the verse performs two services at once. It closes the door on the disbeliever who dies in his disbelief, and in doing so it opens the door to everyone still alive — because the closing is specified as occurring *at death* and not before. Every person reading the verse is, by the fact of reading it, on the near side of the limit. The limit is real, and it is not yet reached. Verse 34 is therefore not only a warning; it is, in the only way such a verse can be, an announcement that there is still time.

The Prophet ﷺ's instruction on how to use that time is the shortest possible:

> *"Be in this world as though you were a stranger or a traveler along a road."* (al-Bukhārī 6416, from Ibn ʿUmar)

The stranger does not delay his departure preparations indefinitely, and the traveler does not build at a rest stop. Verse 34 tells the reader where the road ends for those who never set out; verse 18 tells him the arrival is sudden; and the whole of the sūrah between them is the instruction for the journey.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:35

> **So do not falter and call for peace while you have the upper hand. God is with you and will not deprive you of your deeds.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command Against Weakness**

Verse 35 begins with *fa-* connecting it to what precedes: because the opposition cannot harm God and its deeds will be void (v. 32), because you are commanded to obey and not to void your own deeds (v. 33), because the door closes at death (v. 34) — *therefore*: *fa-lā tahinū wa-tadʿū ilā al-salmi wa-antum al-aʿlawna*, "so do not falter and call for peace while you have the upper hand."

*Tahinū* is from *w-h-n*, weakness, frailty, loss of vigor — the root that produces *wahn*, the weakening, and appears in the Qur'an's description of the mother: *"His mother bore him in weakness upon weakness (wahnan ʿalā wahnin)"* (31:14) and *"she bore him with hardship and gave birth to him with hardship"* (46:15). Ibn al-Jawzī, as the source records, glosses *lā tahinū* as "do not show weakness." The prohibition is not against tiredness, which is not chosen, but against the *assumption* of weakness — the posture, the tone, the negotiation conducted from a crouch.

*Wa-tadʿū ilā al-salmi* — "and call for peace." *Salm* (also read *silm*) is peace, reconciliation, the state of non-hostility. The verb *tadʿū* — you call, you invite, you summon — indicates initiation. The prohibition is on *asking* for peace, on making the overture, on being the party that seeks the cessation.

*Wa-antum al-aʿlawna* — "while you are the upper ones," the higher, the ascendant, the party with the advantage. The comparative *aʿlā* is from *ʿ-l-w*, height and elevation. The clause is the condition that makes the prohibition operative, and it is the key to the verse's meaning: the command addresses the specific situation of a community that has the upper hand and is nonetheless inclined to seek terms out of fatigue, fear, or the desire for quiet.

**Why the Upper Hand Changes the Ruling**

The verse does not prohibit peace. It prohibits seeking peace from a position of strength when the seeking itself would be an act of weakness. The distinction is a real one in both law and strategy, and the commentators worked it out carefully.

A party that has the upper hand and seeks peace sends a signal that it does not believe in its own advantage — and the signal changes the other party's calculation. What might have been a durable settlement becomes an opportunity; what might have been a genuine conversion of the opponent into a neighbor becomes a regrouping. The community that seeks peace while ascendant purchases quiet at the price of its position, and usually loses both.

A party that is losing and accepts peace does something entirely different, and the Qur'an commands it:

> *"And if they incline toward peace, incline thou toward it, and trust in God. Truly He is the Hearing, the Knowing. And if they intend to deceive thee — truly God is sufficient for thee."* (8:61–62)

The two verses describe two situations and issue two rulings. When the enemy inclines to peace, incline to it — this is a command, and 8:61 makes it obligatory. When you have the upper hand and are inclined to seek peace out of weakness — do not. Al-Qurṭubī records that some held 8:61 to abrogate 47:35, but he prefers, as at verse 4, the reading that the two describe different conditions rather than one cancelling the other. This is the same hermeneutic the sūrah's jurisprudence has used throughout: differing rulings on one subject usually indicate differing circumstances, and the reflex to declare abrogation should be the last resort.

**Ḥudaybiyah: The Verse Tested by the Prophet's Own Practice**

The most important evidence for interpreting verse 35 is the Prophet's ﷺ own conduct, and it is complex in a way that requires careful handling. In Dhū al-Qaʿdah 6/628 the Prophet ﷺ set out for ʿumrah with about fourteen hundred Companions, unarmed except for the traveler's sword, and was stopped at Ḥudaybiyah by the Quraysh. The negotiations were long; the Companions were anguished; ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb said he had never doubted as he doubted that day; the Muslims gave the pledge of al-Riḍwān under the tree, prepared to fight to the death; and then the treaty was concluded on terms that appeared humiliating — the Muslims would return that year and come back the following year for three days only; any Qurayshī who came to the Muslims without his guardian's permission would be returned; and any Muslim who went over to Quraysh would not be returned; the document would not carry the phrases "in the name of God, the Merciful" or "Muhammad the Messenger of God" but simply "Muhammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh."

Was this a violation of verse 35? The tradition's answer is no, and the reasoning is instructive. The treaty was not sought by the Muslims out of weakness — the Quraysh initiated the negotiations, sending Suhayl ibn ʿAmr with terms, and the Prophet ﷺ accepted what was offered after assessing the situation. The Muslims were not in the "upper hand" position the verse describes; they were unarmed pilgrims in enemy territory, blocked from the sanctuary, facing a coalition, and with a covenant-breaking risk from within. The Prophet's ﷺ acceptance was *incline toward peace when they incline* (8:61), not *falter and seek peace while ascendant*.

And the outcome vindicated the judgment: the treaty produced ten years of security during which the Muslims multiplied several-fold, delegations came from across Arabia, and Khālid ibn al-Walīd and ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ embraced Islam during it. On the road back the sūrah was revealed that begins:

> *"Truly We have given thee a manifest victory, that God may forgive thee what has advanced of thy sin and what has been delayed."* (48:1–2)

The Companions asked: "Is this a victory, O Messenger of God?" and the answer was that it was the greatest of victories. The sūrah then describes the believers at the pledge: *"God was pleased with the believers when they pledged to thee under the tree; He knew what was in their hearts, and sent down tranquility upon them, and rewarded them with a near victory"* (48:18). The sequence — the pledge, the tranquility, the near victory — is the practical exegesis of 47:35: the community did not falter, it did not seek terms from weakness, and when terms came from the other side it accepted them with trust in God, and the acceptance was itself the victory.

Two years later the Quraysh's allies violated the treaty, and Makkah was entered with ten thousand men and no resistance. The verse 35 command — do not falter — had been obeyed at Ḥudaybiyah in a form nobody in the army recognized at the time.

**Wa-llāhu Maʿakum: The Divine Companionship**

*"God is with you"* — *wa-llāhu maʿakum*. The *maʿiyyah* — being-with — is one of the Qur'an's most frequent assurances and the tradition distinguished two kinds of it. The general companionship (*maʿiyyah ʿāmmah*) extends to all creatures: *"And He is with you wherever you are"* (57:4); *"There is no private conversation of three but He is their fourth"* (58:7). The special companionship (*maʿiyyah khāṣṣah*) is granted to specific categories and denotes help, support, and protection: *"Do not grieve; truly God is with us"* (9:40, the Prophet ﷺ to Abū Bakr in the cave); *"Truly God is with the patient"* (2:153; 8:46); *"Truly God is with those who are reverent and those who are good-doers"* (16:128); *"Fear not; truly I am with you both, Hearing and Seeing"* (20:46, to Moses and Aaron).

Verse 35's *maʿakum* is the special companionship, and Ibn Taymiyyah's treatment of the concept insists that it must be affirmed as a real divine attribute of being-with without being reduced to mere knowledge and without being understood as spatial presence. The practical content is what matters for the verse's argument: the party that has God with it is not, in fact, the weaker party, and the faltering the verse prohibits arises from a miscalculation of the balance.

Moses and Aaron were sent to Pharaoh with this exact assurance, and the verse's command to not falter is the same command given to them:

> *"Go, both of you, unto Pharaoh; truly he has transgressed. And speak unto him a gentle word, that haply he may take heed or fear. They said: Our Lord, we fear that he may hasten against us or transgress. He said: Fear not; truly I am with you both, Hearing and Seeing."* (20:43–46)

And the same assurance at Badr: *"Truly I am with you; so make firm those who believe"* (8:12) — which is verse 7's *"make firm your steps"* in the voice of God addressing the angels on the believers' behalf.

**Wa-Lan Yatirakum Aʿmālakum: He Will Not Deprive You**

The verse closes with a promise that returns the sūrah to its opening: *"and He will not deprive you of your deeds"* — *wa-lan yatirakum aʿmālakum*. The verb *yatira* is from *w-t-r*, to diminish, to detract, to deprive of a portion — the root also produces *witr*, the odd number, and *watara*, to wrong someone of a right. The phrase means: He will not wrong you of your deeds, He will not let any portion of them go unpaid.

The *lan* makes the negation absolute: He will *never* deprive you. Against verse 1 — *He made their deeds go astray* — and verses 9, 28, and 32 — *He made their deeds come to naught* — this is the fourth and final term of the sūrah's vocabulary of deeds, and it is the only one addressed to the believers. Their deeds will not stray, will not spoil, will not be voided, and will not be diminished. The whole of the sūrah's concern with the fate of human action reaches its assurance here.

Al-Qurṭubī connects the promise to the verse's own command: the community is told not to falter because faltering would cost them, and the cost is not the loss of God's help but the loss of the deeds they might have performed. The one who gives up in a difficulty loses the deeds the difficulty would have produced; the one who holds on accumulates them, and verse 35 guarantees that none of the accumulation will be deducted.

**The Verse as a Rule for Ordinary Endurance**

The verse's occasion is military, and its language is military, but its structure applies wherever a person holds a position that is difficult and is tempted to abandon it out of fatigue rather than out of principle.

The person who has been honest in a dishonest trade and is losing money as a result, and is considering whether to adjust his standards. The parent who has maintained a boundary with a difficult adult child and is exhausted. The spouse who has kept a promise made in better circumstances. The student who has refused to plagiarize and is falling behind. The employee who has declined to falsify a report and has become unpopular. The believer in a workplace where his practice is a subject of commentary.

In each case the temptation is not to do something wrong; it is to *seek terms* — to negotiate a smaller commitment, to ask for peace, to accept a settlement that costs the position. And in each case the verse's condition applies: *wa-antum al-aʿlawna.* The person holding onto an obligation for God's sake is in fact in the upper hand, whether or not it feels so, because the *maʿiyyah* of verse 35 is real and the balance it describes is the true one. The faltering would be a misreading of the balance.

The Qur'an's own summary of the posture is at 3:139:

> *"Do not falter (lā tahinū) and do not grieve; you are the upper ones (al-aʿlawna), if you are believers."* (3:139)

The verse was revealed after Uḥud — after the defeat, after the seventy dead, after the moment when faltering would have seemed not weakness but realism — and it uses exactly the words of 47:35. The command to not falter is therefore not issued from a position of victory; it is issued from a position of defeat, and it states that the believer is nonetheless the upper one. That is the whole of verse 35: the balance is not what it appears to be, God is with you, and nothing you do for Him will be deducted.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:36

> **The life of this world is but play and diversion. If you believe and are reverent, He will give you your rewards and not ask you for your wealth.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Turn From the Battlefield to the Purse**

Verse 36 opens the sūrah's final movement, and the opening is a change of register so complete that it can be missed. The preceding thirty-five verses have dealt with fighting, captives, conspiracies, the angels striking faces, the exposure of rancor, and the command not to falter. Verse 36 says: the life of this world is play and diversion. And then it asks for something much smaller than a life: belief, reverence — and, in the verses that follow, a portion of the wealth.

The transition is deliberate and it is one of the Qur'an's characteristic methods. Having established that the highest form of striving may cost a person his life (vv. 4–7), the sūrah descends to the form of striving that costs him his money (vv. 36–38). The descent is not a diminishment; it is an inclusion. Everyone is called to spend; not everyone is called to fight.
