The sūrah's final three verses are therefore addressed to the whole community and to every subsequent reader, and they open with the premise that makes the demand reasonable: what you are being asked to give is not worth what you think it is.

**Laʿib Wa-Lahw: Play and Diversion**

*"The life of this world is but play and diversion"* — *innamā al-ḥayātu al-dunyā laʿibun wa-lahwun*. The restrictive particle *innamā* — "only, nothing but" — makes the statement exclusive: this life is *nothing but* play and diversion. Not partially; wholly.

*Laʿib* is play, sport, the activity undertaken for its own enjoyment with no end beyond itself — a child's game, an amusement, a thing done and then done with. *Lahw* is diversion, amusement, distraction — the root *l-h-w* denotes the turning of the heart away from something important toward something that occupies it without advancing it. Al-Rāghib explains *lahw* as whatever occupies a person and diverts him from what he ought to be attending to.

The pairing is precise. *Laʿib* describes the activity's character — it has no product, no accumulation, no result that persists. *Lahw* describes its effect on the person — it diverts his attention from the thing that matters. The first says the game produces nothing; the second says the game absorbs everything. A life that is *laʿib wa-lahw* is a life spent on activities that leave nothing behind and prevent attention to what would.

The Qur'an states the same assessment in a series of places, and the source commentary lists them:

> *"And the life of this world is naught but play and diversion; and the abode of the Hereafter is better for those who are reverent. Will you not then understand?"* (6:32)

> *"And the life of this world is naught but play and diversion, and the abode of the Hereafter is truly the Life, if they but knew."* (29:64)

> *"And the life of this world is naught but the enjoyment of delusion (matāʿu al-ghurūr)."* (3:185; 57:20)

> *"Know that the life of this world is but play and diversion, and adornment, and mutual boasting among you, and vying for increase in property and children — the likeness of a rain whose vegetation impresses the farmers; then it withers such that you see it turn yellow; then it becomes chaff."* (57:20)

The last is the fullest statement, and it expands the two terms into five: play, diversion, adornment (*zīnah*), mutual boasting (*tafākhur*), and vying for increase (*takāthur*). The five are the complete inventory of what human beings actually do with their unexamined hours — amuse themselves, decorate themselves, compare themselves, and accumulate. The agricultural simile at the end is the assessment: it looks like a harvest, it yellows, it becomes chaff.

**The Distinction the Verse Does Not Make**

It is essential to read verse 36 correctly, because the misreading has caused a great deal of unnecessary misery. The verse does not say the world is evil. It does not say enjoyment is forbidden. It does not command asceticism. Islam rejects monasticism explicitly — *"monasticism they invented; We prescribed it not for them"* (57:27) — and the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"But I fast and I break my fast, I pray and I sleep, and I marry women. Whoever turns away from my Sunnah is not of me."* (al-Bukhārī 5063; Muslim 1401, from Anas ibn Mālik)

What the verse says is that the world's life is *laʿib wa-lahw* in relation to the Hereafter — that is, its activities are of the kind that do not persist and that divert attention from what does. The same activity can be either *laʿib* or worship depending on its direction. The meal eaten with gratitude and the strength spent in earning lawfully for one's family are not play; the meal eaten in heedlessness and the wealth accumulated for boasting are. The Prophet ﷺ made even the permitted pleasures into acts of reward:

> *"In the sexual act of each of you there is charity."* They said, "O Messenger of God, does one of us fulfill his desire and have a reward for it?" He said: *"Do you not see that if he placed it in the unlawful he would bear a sin? So likewise, if he places it in the lawful, he has a reward."* (Muslim 1006, from Abū Dharr)

And in the same report: *"In the morning, charity is due from every joint of a person's body... and the two rakʿahs of the forenoon prayer suffice for all of that."*

The verse's assessment of the world is therefore not a verdict on its contents but a statement about their durability and their direction. The Prophet ﷺ stated the durability in the report preserved by al-Tirmidhī:

> *"This world is accursed — accursed is what is in it — except the remembrance of God and what is connected to it, and a scholar and a student."* (al-Tirmidhī 2322, from Abū Hurayrah; graded ḥasan)

The exceptions are the criterion: what remains in a life is the remembrance of God, the knowledge that serves it, and the teaching that transmits it. Everything else is the *laʿib wa-lahw* of verse 36 — not evil, but perishable.

**In Tuʾminū Wa-Tattaqū: The Two Conditions**

*"If you believe and are reverent"* — *in tuʾminū wa-tattaqū*. The two conditions are the sūrah's recurring pair: belief, which verse 2 defines as including the acceptance of what was sent down to Muhammad ﷺ; and *taqwā*, which verse 15 makes the qualification for the Garden and verse 17 makes the thing God gives to those who are guided.

The conditional form is important. The verse does not say "believe and be reverent and you will be rewarded"; it says "*if* you believe and are reverent, He will give you your rewards." The reward is certain and the condition is real, and the condition is in the reader's hands. This is the same structure as verse 7 — *if you help God, He will help you* — and the two conditionals together frame the sūrah's practical teaching: the divine response is guaranteed and the human act is required.

**Yuʾtikum Ujūrakum: The Rewards Are Given, Not Deducted**

*"He will give you your rewards"* — *yuʾtikum ujūrakum*. The phrase *ujūrakum* — *your* rewards — with the possessive pronoun, is significant. The rewards are described as belonging to the believers before they are given, as though they were already deposited and awaiting collection. The Qur'an uses the same construction repeatedly: *"Truly the patient will be given their reward without reckoning"* (39:10); *"And to each are degrees from what they did, and that He may repay them in full for their deeds, and they will not be wronged"* (46:19); *"That day every soul will be requited for what it earned; no injustice that day"* (40:17).

The promise answers the fear that the verse's first clause might produce. If this life is play and diversion, and I have spent my life in it, then I have lost — and the loss is total. The second clause responds: no, the reward is yours and it will be given, and the giving will be in full. The play and diversion are not the whole account; they are the material from which the account is made, and the belief and reverence convert them.

**Wa-Lā Yasʾalkum Amwālakum: He Does Not Ask for Your Wealth**

The final clause is the sūrah's most surprising and the transition to its closing argument: *"and He will not ask you for your wealth"* — *wa-lā yasʾalkum amwālakum*. Al-Qurṭubī explains it, as the source records, in two ways. The first: God will allow people to keep most of their wealth, requiring only that they pay the mandatory alms (*zakāh*). The second: God is not asking for people's wealth but for *His* wealth, since all things ultimately belong to Him (cf. 25:57).

Both readings are supported by the Qur'an. The first by the specification of the *zakāh* rate — two and a half percent on monetary wealth held for a year, a tenth or a twentieth on agricultural produce, a fixed number from livestock — which is a small fraction of what most people hold. The second by the declaration:

> *"And they do not worship besides God... Say: I ask of you no reward for it; it is only a reminder for the worlds."* (6:90 in related sense) — and: *"Say: Whatever reward I have asked of you is yours; my reward is only upon God."* (34:47)

> *"I do not ask you for any provision, nor do I ask that you feed Me. Truly God is the Provider, the Possessor of Strength, the Firm."* (51:57–58)

The Prophets make the same declaration individually: Noah (11:29), Hūd (11:51), Ṣāliḥ (26:109), Shuʿayb (26:180), and Muhammad ﷺ (38:86: *"Say: I do not ask of you any reward for it, nor am I of those who pretend"*).

The theological point is that God's demand for wealth is not a need but a test, and the test is calibrated to be passable. Verse 37 will make the calibration explicit: were God to ask for everything, you would be miserly, and your rancor would be exposed. He asks for a fraction, and the fraction is a mercy as well as a command — mercy because the demand is small, and mercy because the small demand preserves the relation between the servant and his Lord that an impossible demand would destroy.

**The Verse's Argument in Sequence**

Read as an argument, verse 36 has three steps, and each prepares the next.

*Step one*: this life is play and diversion — it does not persist and it diverts. The premise deflates the object of attachment. The person who has understood this premise no longer regards his wealth as an ultimate possession; it is a temporary holding in a game whose rules are set elsewhere.

*Step two*: if you believe and are reverent, you will be given your rewards in full. The premise of impermanence is answered by a promise of permanence, and the promise is specified as *full* — no deduction, no injustice, no loss.

*Step three*: and He will not ask you for your wealth. The demand that follows is therefore small in relation to both the world's impermanence and the reward's completeness. What God asks is a fraction of what does not last, in exchange for what does.

The three steps make the miserliness of verses 37 and 38 inexplicable. The person who refuses the small demand has either not accepted step one (he thinks the wealth is permanent) or not accepted step two (he does not trust the reward) — and either failure is a failure of belief, which is why the sūrah places the whole discussion under the conditional *"if you believe and are reverent."*

The Prophet ﷺ stated the resulting calculation in the report preserved by Muslim:

> *"O son of Adam, spend — and I will spend upon you."* And he said: *"God's Hand is full, and the night and the day do not diminish it. Have you considered what He has spent since He created the heavens and the earth? It has not diminished what is in His Hand."* (al-Bukhārī 4684; Muslim 993, from Abū Hurayrah)

And:

> *"Charity does not decrease wealth, and God increases the one who pardons in nothing but honor, and no one humbles himself for God except that God raises him."* (Muslim 2588, from Abū Hurayrah)

The verse's premise and the report's promise are the same arithmetic. The world is play; the reward is real; the demand is small; and the spending does not decrease what is spent.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:37

> **Were He to ask you for it, then implore you, you would be miserly, and He would expose your rancor.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counterfactual That Reveals the Heart**

Verse 37 continues verse 36's statement that God will not ask for your wealth, and explains why the limitation is a mercy — by describing what would happen if it were removed. *In yasʾalkumūhā fa-yuḥfikum tabkhalū wa-yukhrij aḍghānakum* — "Were He to ask you for it, then implore you, you would be miserly, and He would expose your rancor."

The construction is a counterfactual conditional: *in* with the present tense, describing a hypothetical that is not the case. God has *not* asked for all of it; He has asked for a portion. The verse asks the reader to imagine the alternative and to notice his own reaction to the imagining — because the reaction is the diagnosis.

**Fa-Yuḥfikum: The Pressing Demand**

*Yuḥfikum* is the fourth form of *ḥ-f-y*, and it is the verse's most important word. Al-Rāghib explains *aḥfā* as pressing someone hard in a demand or a question, insisting, pursuing the matter until the other is worn out — the root also produces *ḥafāʾ*, barefootness, and the notion of scraping away, of reducing something to nothing by persistence. Ibn al-Jawzī, as the source records, glosses the phrase as "were He to ask you for all that you own."

The word therefore describes not the size of the demand but its *manner*: the insistent, repeated, exhaustive pressing that leaves the person no room. A demand that asks for a portion and stops is a demand that can be met. A demand that asks for everything and presses — *yuḥfī* — is a demand that exposes what is under the willingness.

The commentators note that the two conditions in the verse are cumulative: *in yasʾalkumūhā* (were He to ask you for it) *fa-yuḥfikum* (and then press you). The first alone might be bearable; the second alone would have nothing to press about. Together they produce the reaction the verse predicts.

**Tabkhalū: You Would Be Miserly**

*Tabkhalū* is from *b-kh-l*, miserliness, stinginess, the withholding of what ought to be given. The noun *bukhl* and the adjective *bakhīl* appear throughout the Qur'an, and the tradition treats miserliness as one of the destructive qualities:

> *"Beware of oppression, for oppression will be darkness upon darkness on the Day of Resurrection. And beware of miserliness (al-shuḥḥ), for miserliness destroyed those before you: it commanded them to be unjust and they were unjust, it commanded them to sever their ties and they severed them, and it commanded them to commit immorality and they committed it."* (Muslim 2578, from Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh)

The report connects miserliness to the two sins verse 22 of this sūrah named — corruption upon the earth and the severing of kinship — and it makes miserliness the cause of both. The miser is unjust because he takes what is not his; he severs ties because maintaining them costs money; he commits immorality because the restraint that would prevent it costs something he is unwilling to pay.

The Prophet ﷺ also asked his Companions:

> *"What do you consider to be the property of the miser among you?"* They said, "The one who does not spend anything." He said: *"The property of the miser is what he sends forward [to the Hereafter], and the property of the generous is what he leaves behind."* — and in another wording: *"Which of you loves the wealth of his heir more than his own wealth?"* They said, "O Messenger of God, there is none of us but loves his own wealth more." He said: *"Then your wealth is what you send forward, and the wealth of your heir is what you leave behind."* (al-Bukhārī 6442, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd)

The report inverts the ordinary calculation of ownership and it is the practical content of verse 36's premise: the world is play and diversion, so what you are holding is not, in the accounting that matters, yours.

**Wa-Yukhrij Aḍghānakum: The Rancor Exposed**

The verse's final clause returns to the word from verse 29: *aḍghān*, the buried rancors. *"And He would expose your rancor"* — *wa-yukhrij aḍghānakum*. Ibn al-Jawzī explains it as "make manifest your enmity toward God and His Messenger."

The verse thereby makes a claim of extraordinary precision about the human heart: beneath the willingness to give a portion lies a resentment of the demand itself, and a sufficiently pressing demand will bring the resentment to the surface. The miserliness is not the cause of the rancor; the miserliness is what the rancor looks like when it is acted upon. The rancor was already there, buried, and the demand was the instrument that extracted it.

This is why the verse pairs the two clauses. *Tabkhalū* — you would withhold. *Wa-yukhrij aḍghānakum* — and He would bring out your rancors. The withholding is the visible act; the exposure is the invisible consequence, and the consequence is the point. God would not be obtaining the wealth; He would be disclosing the heart. And since the disclosure will happen in any case — verse 29 has already promised it, and verse 30 has described the means — the limitation of the demand is the mercy that allows the disclosure to occur in a form the person can still do something about.

Al-Rāzī draws the theological conclusion: the calibrated demand is itself evidence of divine mercy, because an exhaustive demand would produce an exhaustive refusal, and the refusal would fix the person's state beyond repair. By asking for a portion, God makes obedience possible, and by making obedience possible He keeps the door of verse 34 open.

**The Calibration of the Demand**

The Islamic law of wealth is the practical expression of this calibration, and its structure shows the verse's principle at work throughout.

The *zakāh* is two and a half percent of monetary wealth held above the minimum threshold (*niṣāb*) for a lunar year. It is not a progressive tax on income; it is a levy on accumulated, idle wealth, which means it falls most lightly on the person whose wealth is in use and most heavily on the person whose wealth is hoarded. Agricultural produce is assessed at a tenth if watered by rain and a twentieth if watered by labor — the rate varies with the effort expended, which is a calibration of a different kind. Livestock is assessed by head-count thresholds. Trade goods are assessed at their market value. Personal effects — the house one lives in, the animal one rides, the tools of one's trade, the furniture, the clothing — are exempt entirely.

The result is a demand that is real, that cannot be evaded without dishonesty, and that almost everyone can meet. The verse's counterfactual — were He to ask for all of it and press you — describes a demand the tradition consistently declined to construct. When Abū Bakr fought the tribes that refused the *zakāh* after the Prophet's ﷺ death, the dispute was about the payment of the established rate, not about the size of it; ʿUmar initially objected to fighting people who said *lā ilāha illā Allāh*, and Abū Bakr's answer became one of the most quoted statements in Islamic history:

> *"By God, I will fight whoever separates the prayer from the zakāh, for the zakāh is the right of wealth. By God, if they withhold from me a hobble for a camel that they used to give to the Messenger of God, I will fight them for it."* (al-Bukhārī 1399; Muslim 20)

The hobble — *ʿiqāl*, the rope used to tie a camel's leg — is the smallest unit of the levy, and Abū Bakr's willingness to fight over it demonstrates that the calibration is not about the amount being trivial. The amount is small and the obligation is absolute, and verse 37 explains why the arrangement takes that form: a small absolute obligation preserves the heart, and a large exhaustive one destroys it.

**The Verse Applied to Non-Monetary Demands**

The verse's principle is not confined to money, and the tradition applied it widely. God does not ask for the whole of a person's time — He asks for five prayers, which the jurists calculated as occupying a small fraction of the day, with the rest left free. He does not ask for the whole of a person's appetite — He asks for one month of fasting and permits every lawful thing at every other hour, and the nights of Ramadan included. He does not ask for the whole of a person's capacity for hardship — *"God intends ease for you and does not intend hardship"* (2:185); *"He has placed no hardship upon you in the religion"* (22:78); *"God does not task a soul beyond its capacity"* (2:286).

The Prophet ﷺ stated the governing rule:

> *"Religion is easy, and no one overburdens himself in religion except that it overcomes him. So be moderate, or come close, and be of good cheer, and seek help in the morning and the evening and a portion of the night."* (al-Bukhārī 39, from Abū Hurayrah)

And:

> *"When I command you to do something, do of it as much as you are able; and when I forbid you something, leave it."* (al-Bukhārī 7288; Muslim 1337, from Abū Hurayrah)

The asymmetry in the second report is instructive: the positive command is limited by capacity, and the negative prohibition is absolute. This is the same calibration verse 37 describes — the demand is set at what the person can bear, and it is the *yuḥfī* demand, the pressing exhaustive one, that the tradition consistently warns against, in the reports about the three men who vowed to fast every day, pray all night, and abstain from marriage, and were told that the Prophet's ﷺ own practice was otherwise.

**The Reader's Own Reaction to the Verse**

Verse 37 asks the reader to notice something about himself, and the noticing is uncomfortable. When a religious obligation is presented as demanding, the immediate internal response in most people is not gratitude for the calibration but resentment of the demand. The person who hears that the *zakāh* is due feels, for a moment, that something is being taken from him — and the feeling is precisely the *ḍighn* the verse describes. It surfaces in a fraction of a second, in a person who has never refused to pay, and it is the rancor that a pressing demand would have extracted in full.

The verse's mercy lies in the fact that the extraction has not occurred. The demand is small enough that the rancor can be noticed, named, and asked forgiveness for, rather than acted upon and thereby fixed. The person who feels the resentment at paying the *zakāh* and pays it anyway is doing what verse 33 commands — obeying and not voiding the deed — and what verse 21 prescribes — the truthfulness to God that would have been better for the hypocrites.

The practice the tradition derived from this verse is the examination of one's reaction to small demands, on the principle that the small demand reveals what the large one would produce. ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz is reported to have said: *"Take account of yourself in the small matter, for the large matter is only an accumulation of small ones."* And Ibn al-Qayyim's formulation in *al-Fawāʾid* is that the heart's condition is disclosed not in the great tests but in the ordinary ones, because in the great tests a person performs and in the ordinary ones he reveals.

Verse 37 is the sūrah's own ordinary test, set at the end of a chapter that began with the battlefield. After everything the reader has been told about fighting, martyrdom, the angels striking faces, and the four rivers of Paradise, the final examination is about money — the thing he actually has, and the demand on which is actually small. The verse asks him to imagine the demand being large, notices what the imagining produces, and tells him what it produced.

---
## Sūrah Muḥammad 47:38

> **Behold! You are those who are called upon to spend in the way of God; yet among you some are miserly. And whosoever is miserly is only miserly toward himself. God is the Rich, and you are the poor. And if you turn away, He will cause a people other than you to take your place, and they will not be the likes of you.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Hā Antum Hāʾulāʾ: The Pointing**

The final verse of Sūrah Muḥammad begins with a double demonstrative that has no exact equivalent in English. *Hā antum hāʾulāʾ tudʿawna li-tunfiqū fī sabīli Allāh* — "Behold! You are those who are called upon to spend in the way of God." The *hā* of alerting appears twice, and the combination produces an address of unusual directness: look here; you; you are the ones being described; this is you.

The Arabic construction is what the grammarians call *al-tanbīh* — the drawing of attention — doubled for emphasis, and the Qur'an uses it at moments of confrontation: *"Behold, you are those who are called to spend in the way of God"* (47:38); *"Behold, you are those who disputed about them in the life of this world"* (3:66 in related form); *"Behold, you are those who love them, and they love you not"* (3:119). In each case the construction seizes the reader's attention and refuses the possibility of applying the statement to someone else.

The verse thereby ends the sūrah the way the sūrah began — with a direct statement about the addressee's own condition. Verse 1 spoke about "those who disbelieve" in the third person; verse 38 speaks to "you" in the second. The whole of the chapter's argument has moved from description of others to confrontation with the self, and the final verse completes the movement.

**Tudʿawna Li-Tunfiqū: Called to Spend**

*Tudʿawna* — you are called, you are summoned, you are invited — is the passive of *daʿā*, to call. The passive voice is significant: the caller is not named, because the caller is God, and the calling is what the entire sūrah has been. Every verse the reader has passed through was part of the call, and verse 38 names what the call is for: *li-tunfiqū fī sabīli Allāh* — that you spend in the way of God.

*Anfaqa*, from *n-f-q*, to spend, to expend — the same root as *nafaq*, the hole from which the gerbil emerges, and *munāfiq*, the hypocrite, so called because he enters the religion through one door and leaves through another, the way the animal has two exits from its burrow. The root's basic sense is expenditure and passage through, and its religious use denotes spending wealth in God's cause.

*Fī sabīli Allāh* — in the way of God — is the sūrah's governing phrase, appearing at verses 1, 4, 32, and 34. The spending is therefore not charity in the general sense; it is expenditure upon the road, and the road is the one the sūrah has described: the road of the fighting (v. 4), of the helping of God's religion (v. 7), of the striving with wealth and self (v. 31). The *zakāh* is its obligatory core; the *ṣadaqah* is its voluntary extension; and everything spent to keep the road open — the mosque, the school, the well, the book, the traveler, the fighter, the orphan, the widow, the student of knowledge, the person in debt — is *infaq fī sabīl Allāh*.

**Fa-Minkum Man Yabkhal: Some of You Are Miserly**

The verse does not accuse the whole community. *Fa-minkum man yabkhal* — "yet among you some are miserly." The *min* is partitive and the *man* is indefinite: a portion of you, someone among you. The Qur'an's precision here is characteristic: it describes the community honestly, including its failures, without condemning it wholesale. The same precision appears at verse 16 (*"among them are those who listen unto thee"*) and verse 30 (*"and you will certainly know them"*).

The effect on the reader is the effect the verse intends. Since the miserly are *among you* and are not identified by name, every member of the community must ask whether he is one of them. The indeterminacy is the diagnostic. The person who reads the verse and thinks of someone else has not yet read it; the person who reads it and asks whether it means him has.

**Wa-Man Yabkhal Fa-Innamā Yabkhalu ʿAn Nafsihi: The Reflexivity**

*"And whosoever is miserly is only miserly toward himself"* — *wa-man yabkhal fa-innamā yabkhalu ʿan nafsih*. The preposition *ʿan* with *bakhila* means to be miserly *with respect to*, to withhold from — and the object of the withholding, the verse states, is the self. The miser's withholding does not harm the recipient; it harms the withholder.

The source commentary identifies this as a principle the Qur'an states repeatedly, and calls it the moral and spiritual reflexivity of actions: one's actions ultimately affect oneself more than anyone else. The instances it lists are 4:131; 5:54; 6:133; 14:19, and the wider group includes 2:9, 272; 3:69; 4:111; 6:123; 8:60; 9:34–35; 10:23; 29:6; 35:18; 36:19. The clearest statements are:

> *"Whoever does righteousness, it is for his own soul; and whoever does evil, it is against it. And thy Lord is not unjust to the servants."* (41:46)

> *"If you do good, you do good for yourselves; and if you do evil, it is for it."* (17:7)

> *"O mankind, be reverent toward your Lord... Whoever is guided is guided only for his own soul, and whoever goes astray goes astray only against it. And no bearer of burdens bears the burden of another."* (17:15)

> *"And whoever is protected from the miserliness of his own soul — those are the triumphant."* (59:9; 64:16)

The last of these is the verse's closest parallel, and it states the principle positively: protection from one's own miserliness *is* triumph.

Ibn al-Jawzī explains the reflexivity in this context as meaning that refusal to pay the mandatory alms is in fact to deprive oneself of the benefits that accrue from it, since God has no need of one's money. Al-Qurṭubī states the same. And the Qur'an supplies the counterpart:

> *"And whatever good you spend, it is for yourselves, when you spend only seeking the Face of God. And whatever good you spend, it will be repaid to you in full, and you will not be wronged."* (2:272)

The logic is not difficult, and it is the same logic verse 35 used about the deeds: nothing spent for God is lost, and nothing withheld for God is kept. The withheld portion remains in the estate, and the estate passes to heirs who did not earn it and will not be grateful for it, and the withholder arrives at the reckoning with the deed undone. The Prophet ﷺ's statement of the calculation, already cited at verse 37, is the plainest form: *"your wealth is what you send forward, and the wealth of your heir is what you leave behind"* (al-Bukhārī 6442).

**Wa-llāhu al-Ghaniyyu Wa-Antumu al-Fuqarāʾ: The Two Descriptions**

*"God is the Rich, and you are the poor"* — *wa-llāhu al-ghanīyyu wa-antumu al-fuqarāʾ*. The two nouns are both definite and both in the emphatic position, and the contrast is the theological ground of the whole verse.

*Ghanī* is from *gh-n-y*, sufficiency, freedom from need, independence. *Al-Ghanī* is one of the divine Names, and it denotes absolute self-sufficiency: God needs nothing, depends on nothing, and is diminished by nothing. The Qur'an states it repeatedly: *"O mankind, you are the poor, in need of God; and God is the Rich, the Praiseworthy. If He wills, He will take you away and bring a new creation, and that is not difficult for God"* (35:15–17); *"And Moses said: If you disbelieve, you and all who are in the earth — truly God is Independent, Praiseworthy"* (14:8); *"Whoever disbelieves — truly God is Independent of the worlds"* (3:97).

*Fuqarāʾ* is the plural of *faqīr*, from *f-q-r*, the vertebra of the spine, the breaking of the back — a *faqīr* is one whose back is broken, who has nothing, who is in need. The Qur'an uses the word of the human being's essential condition, not only of his economic one: *"O mankind, you are the poor (al-fuqarāʾ) toward God."* The poverty is ontological: the human being is dependent in his existence, in his continuation, in every moment of his being.

The source commentary notes that in this context the phrase also refers to the fact that all of one's wealth is from God, so to refuse to pay the alms is to refuse to give back to God what is rightfully His; and it can be understood as a reference to the fact that one's very existence is from God, so that one must submit fully and completely to Him. Sahl al-Tustarī's statement, recorded in the source, compresses the whole doctrine:

> *"Knowledge of poverty before God is the knowledge that wealth is in God."*

The saying is a paradox that resolves on inspection. The person who knows he is poor knows where the wealth is; the person who thinks he is wealthy does not know either. The *faqr* is not a deficiency to be overcome but a knowledge to be attained, and it is the precondition of every act of worship, since the one who does not know his need cannot ask and the one who cannot ask cannot receive. This is why the Sufi tradition made *faqr* its central virtue and why the Prophet ﷺ is reported to have said: *"Poverty is my pride"* — a saying transmitted in the collections of Ibn ʿAsākir and al-Daylamī whose chain the ḥadīth scholars regard as weak, but whose meaning is supported by the report in Muslim that the Prophet ﷺ chose to be *"a servant and a messenger"* rather than *"a king and a prophet"* (Muslim 2276 style reports; also al-Tirmidhī 2353).

**Wa-In Tatawallaw Yastabdil Qawman Ghayrakum: The Replacement**

The verse's final movement is its most severe and its most quoted: *"And if you turn away, He will cause a people other than you to take your place, and they will not be the likes of you."* *Wa-in tatawallaw yastabdil qawman ghayrakum thumma lā yakūnū amthālakum.*

*Tatawallaw* is from *t-w-l*, to turn away, to be in charge, to be a patron — the same verb used at verse 22 (*in tawallaytum*, "were you to turn away") and at verse 25 (*irtaddū*, turning back). The turning away here is the refusal of the spending that the verse has just described.

*Yastabdil* is the tenth form of *b-d-l*, to exchange, to substitute — to take one thing in place of another. The verse states that God will exchange the community that refuses for a community that does not, and the Qur'an repeats the declaration in a series of places the source commentary lists: 4:133; 5:54; 6:133; 9:39; 11:57; 14:19; 35:16. The fullest statement is at 5:54:

> *"O you who believe! Whoever among you turns back from his religion — God will bring a people whom He loves and who love Him, humble toward the believers, stern toward the disbelievers, striving in the way of God, not fearing the blame of any blamer. That is the bounty of God; He gives it to whom He wills, and God is Embracing, Knowing."* (5:54)

The four qualities of the replacement people are worth setting against verse 38's context: love of God and being loved by Him (against the miserliness that reveals the absence of love); humility toward the believers (against the arrogance that makes a person unwilling to give); sternness toward the disbelievers (against the faltering verse 35 prohibited); and striving in God's way without fearing blame (against the concern for others' opinion that verse 33 identified as voiding the deed).

And at 9:39 the warning is attached to the same context of spending and mobilization:

> *"Go forth, light and heavy, and strive with your wealth and your selves in the way of God. That is better for you, if you but knew. If you do not go forth, He will punish you with a painful punishment and replace you with another people, and you will not harm Him in the least. And God is powerful over all things."* (9:38–39)

**Thumma Lā Yakūnū Amthālakum: They Will Not Be Your Likes**

The final clause is the verse's last word and the sūrah's: *"and they will not be the likes of you"* — *thumma lā yakūnū amthālakum*. Ibn al-Jawzī explains it as meaning they will be better people; al-Ṭabarī as meaning not miserly and refusing to spend in the way of God.

The word *amthāl* is the same word that appeared at verse 3 — *"Thus does God set forth for mankind their likenesses"* (*amthālahum*) — and at verse 10 — *"and the disbelievers shall have the like thereof"* (*amthāluhā*). The sūrah's final word is thus the word with which its argument began, and the return is not accidental. Verse 3 announced that God strikes likenesses; verse 38 announces that God replaces people, and the replacement will not be *your* likeness. The community that refuses is not necessary to the religion; the religion is necessary to the community.

There is a mercy in the clause as well as a threat, and the tradition drew both. The threat: you can be removed, and the removal will not be noticed by the cause you abandoned. The mercy: you have not yet been removed, and the verse's conditional — *in tatawallaw* — leaves the alternative open. The replacement occurs only upon the turning away, and the turning away has not yet happened for anyone who is still reading.

The Prophet ﷺ stated the principle in the report preserved by al-Bukhārī:

> *"Truly God will support this religion with a man who has no share in it."* (al-Bukhārī 3062, from Abū Hurayrah — in the context of the man who fought fiercely at Khaybar and was thought to be a Muslim, and who then killed himself, and the Prophet ﷺ said the words)

And in the version at al-Bukhārī 6606: *"God supports this religion by the wicked man."* The religion does not depend on the virtue of its current adherents; it depends on God, who supplies adherents as He wills. The honor of being among them is therefore a gift, and the gift can be transferred.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Conclusion**

Sūrah Muḥammad ends where it began, and the ending completes the argument. Verse 1: those who disbelieve and turn from God's way have their deeds made to go astray. Verse 38: those who are called to spend in God's way and are miserly are miserly against themselves, and if they turn away they will be replaced. The same two faults — disbelief and turning from God's way — appear in both verses; the same two consequences — the loss of the deeds and the loss of the position — appear in both.

Between them lie thirty-six verses that have covered the battlefield and the assembly, the angels striking faces and the four rivers of Paradise, the sealed heart and the increased guidance, the conspiracy whispered in secret and the rancor that God will bring out, the test of the proclamations and the promise that no deed will be deducted. And at the end, the demand: spend. Not die — spend. The smallest act of the striving, available to everyone who has anything, and the act on which the whole of the sūrah's verdict turns.

The verse's final clause is the last sentence of the chapter, and it is spoken over every generation that reads it. *They will not be the likes of you.* The sentence is not a threat about the future; it is a statement about the present that the reader can still change. The people who would take the place are not yet called, and the calling is still open, and the amount asked for is small, and the One asking is the Rich and the one asked is the poor — and the poor man's only wealth is what he sends forward.

---
**[End of the commentary on Sūrah Muḥammad]**
